Their verses preserve stories of struggle and renunciation, but also something more important: **the possibility of completion of the path of liberation**.

The *Theragāthā* is, ultimately, a collection of voices from the Path.

Listen to them not merely as verses from the distant past, but as the recorded voices of yogis who undertook the training and reached its goal.

**These are the voices of liberation.**

### Ekanipāto --- Chapter of the Ones 

#### 1.1. Subhūti

**Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā, Vassa deva yathāsukhaṁ;\
Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ, Ātāpī viharāmi vassa devā ti.**

*Covered is my kuti (little hut), comfortable, Wind-sheltered; O rain deva, do as you like. My Citta (mind-heart) is well-concentrated (susamāhitaṁ) and liberated (vimuttaṁ); I live (viharāmi) with the primordial spiritual force: tapas (ātāpī), O rain deva.*

**Commentary**: Much like a kuti (little hut) is thatched (*channā*), comfortable (*sukhā*), and sheltered from wind (*nivātā*), so the person lives in the physical shelter (kuti) is entirely secure and unbothered by external storms. A bhikkhu's c*itta* is well-concentrated (*susamāhitaṁ*), established in *samādhi,* and liberated (*vimuttaṁ*) from worldly bondage, so the soul-spirit (**Ātmā**) lives in the *citta* is well protected and unaffected by the storms of the outside world (loka), live with ātāpī (adjective: \"possessing *tapas*\", the primordial spiritual force).

**Citta-Ātmā:** The Mind-Heart (*Citta*) and the Soul-Spirit (*Ātmā*)

While *citta* is the total sphere of consciousness, spanning from its most superficial external projections down to its deeper essence *ātmā* is the inner core depth.

**1). *Citta* as the Total Sphere of Consciousness**

*Citta* is the complete matrix of mind-heart awareness. It is the entire domain of conscious experience.

- **The Spectrum of Mind-Heart:** *Citta* encompasses the entire spectrum of awareness---from the outer peripheral layers (thoughts, feelings, sensory projections, past memory impressions) down to its own absolute center.

- **The Exterior Surface:** The outer layer of *citta* is what forms a person\'s visible **outlook** and interaction with the world. It is dynamic, easily disturbed by external impacts, and prone to surface turbulence.

**2). *Ātmā* as the Core Depth of *Citta***

*Ātmā* is **the deep layer, the essence of *citta* itself**---its essential ground (*svabhāva*).

- **The Unshakable Foundation:** *Ātmā* is *citta* in its primary, essential existential state---unmoved by external impressions (*vṛttis*), luminous.

- **Core vs. Surface:** The distinction between *citta* and *ātmā* becomes a distinction between the **surface ripples** of consciousness (*citta*\'s exterior outlook) and the **oceanic depth** of consciousness (*ātmā*).

**Citta Susamāhitaṁ Vimuttaṁ**

**(1). Susamāhitaṁ:** *\"Thoroughly and completely established in **Samādhi**.\"*

- **Prefix su- :** Well, thoroughly, perfectly, auspiciously.

- **Prefix saṁ- :** Together, completely, in union, perfectly integrated.

- **Prefix ā- :** Towards, fully, completely (intensive prefix).

- **Root dhā :** To put, place, set, hold, fix, establish.

- **Suffix -ta :** Past passive participle suffix.

- **Form:** su + sam + ā + dhā + ta = *susamāhita* (Neuter nominative: *susamāhitaṁ*). Literally: *\"Thoroughly and completely placed/established together.\"*

In the *Ṛgveda* and early Upaniṣads, the root **dhā** with the prefix **sam-ā-** (*samādhā*) refers to the act of setting something firmly in its proper place or assembling parts into a harmonious, unbroken whole.

- In ritual contexts, *samāhita* describes the sacrificial fire deva (*agni*) or ceremonial offerings when they are perfectly set, centered, and stabilized without turbulence or disruption (*Ṛgveda* 10.56.1).

- In early Upaniṣadic thought (e.g., *Chāndogya Upaniṣad* 8.1.5), a *samāhita-citta* or *samāhitātmā* denotes an inner state where the senses and mental faculties are pulled back from outward dispersion and fixed firmly in the center---unshaken by external disturbances.

In the **Pali Canon** Suttas, **samāhita** is the past participle form of the state reached in ***Samādhi***. The addition of **su-** elevates this to the highest, unbroken degree of concentration:

- In the *Sāmaññaphala Sutta* (DN 2), when the Citta enters the four jhānas, it is described as *samāhite citte parisuddhe pariyodāte* (\"when the Citta is concentrated (*samāhite*), purified (*parisuddhe*), bright (*pariyodāte*)\").

- **Susamāhitaṁ** goes beyond temporary suppression of thought; it signifies that the Citta is immovably fixed, perfectly centered, and fully anchored within *Samadhi*. Because it is thoroughly concentrated, external disturbances---like the storming rain outside the *kuṭi*---cannot shake or scatter it.

**(2). Vimuttaṁ:** *\"Completely unbound, released,* or *separated from all ties.\"*

- **Prefix vi- :** Away from, un-, dis-, complete detachment/ separation.

- **Root muc / mokṣ:** To release, untie, unbind, discharge, set free.

- **Suffix -ta:** Past passive participle suffix (muc + ta -\> *mutta*).

- **Form:** vi + mutta = *vimutta* (Neuter nominative: *vimuttaṁ*). Literally: *\"Completely unbound,\" \"thoroughly released,\"* or *\"separated from all ties.\"*

In the *Ṛgveda*, the root **muc** is prominently used in prayers to the gods (especially Varuṇa and Agni) requesting untying or releasing from bonds (*pāśa*), debt (*ṛṇa*), or illness.

- In *Ṛgveda* 1.24.15, a verse attributed to the seer Śunaḥśepa---we encounter one of the oldest structural representations of human bondage and release in Vedic literature. "ud uttamam varuṇa pāśam asmad avādhamaṁ vi madhyamaṁ śrathāya \| athā vayam āditya vrata tavānāgaso aditaye syāma \|\|" (\"O Varuṇa, unloose from us the upper bond, untie the middle, loosen the lowest\").

- In the Vedic sacrificial view, liberation (*mukti*) meant unbinding the physical or subtle body from snares, evil spirits, or death (*mṛtyu*).

In the **Pali** Suttas, ***vimutta*** is the complete unbinding of the citta from the worldly fetters (*saṁyojana*) of ***rāga***, ***dosa***, and ***moha***.

- The prefix **vi-** emphasizes **transcendence through separation**: the Citta is not merely quieted; it is fundamentally disconnected and unhitched from the world (*loka*).

- The liberation triad of *nibbidā*/*nibbindati* (becomes disenchanted from the worldly) -\> *viraga*/*virajjati* (fades away worldly passion) -\> *vimutti*/*vimuccati* (released from the worldly bondage). Thus, ***vimutta*** represents the ultimate state of liberation where the citta, having abandoned all worldly phenomena, transcends the world entirely.

**Ātmā** **Ātāpī Viharāmi**

**(3). Ātāpī**

- **Prefix ā- :** Towards, fully, intensively.

- **Root tap:** To burn, shine, radiate heat, exert effort, undergo ascetic yogic effort *tapa*.

- **Suffix -in:** Possessive suffix indicating \"one who possesses or practices\" (Nominative singular masculine: *ātāpī*).

- **Form:** ā + tap + in = *ātāpī*. Literally: *\"One who burns,\" \"possessing intense yogic tapa practices/ardence,\"* or *\"one who vigorously burns away impurities.\"*

The root **tap** is central to Vedic cosmology and spiritual discipline under the concept of **Tapas** (तपस्).

- In the *Ṛgveda* (e.g., 10.190.1, the *Aghamarṣaṇa Hymn*), *tapas* is the primordial, creative consciousness / energy of the source of the universe. Human soul-spirit (*ātmā*) as a fractal of this source also contains this creative primordial consciousness: *\"ṛtaṁ ca satyaṁ cābhīddhāt tapaso \'dhyajāyata\"* (\"Divine Order (*ṛta*) and Truth (*satya*) were generated / manifested (adhyajāyata) from glowing / intensely kindled (abhīddhāt) Tapas\").

- In the **Ṛgveda 10.129 Nāsadīya Sūkta**, the primordial One (*Tad Ekaṁ*) arises from a dark, formless void through the intense power of **Tapas**. Verse 3 explicitly names this cosmic emergence.

  **The Core Verses: RV 10.129.1--3:** *nāsad āsīn no sad āsīt tadānīṁ nāsīd rajo no vyomā paro yat \| tama āsīt tamasā gūḷham agre \'praketaṁ salilaṁ sarvam ā idam \|\| tuchyena ābhu apihitaṁ yad āsīt **tapasas tan mahinājāyata ekam** \|\|\|*

  *\"There was neither non-existence (asat) nor existence (sat) then; there was no realm of space, nor the sky beyond. In the beginning was darkness hidden by darkness; all this was unmanifest, indistinguishable water. That empty void which was covered by husk---**that One (Ekam) was born / manifested (ajāyata) by the greatness (mahinā) of Tapas!**\"*

Mainstream academia claims that "Before the Buddha, contemporary movements like Jainism and various ascetic sects used *tapas* to mean severe physical self-mortification---such as starvation, holding painful postures, or exposing oneself to extreme weather---to burn up bad karma. The Buddha tried these extreme practices for six years and nearly died. Consequently, throughout the Pali Canon, he **redefines Tapa from external physical torture to internal mental discipline**."

This is completely ignorant, stupid and wrong accusation.

The textual evidence across the Pali sutta demonstrates that Gautama Buddha never rejected **tapas**. Rather, he rejected physical torture (*attāntāpana* or *dukkarakārikā*), which is precisely **NOT tapas**.

He explicitly used the term *tapas* (and its verbal/adjectival forms *tapati*, *tāpaso*, *ātāpī*) to describe the radiant, primordial, concentrated spiritual energy of the citta that incinerates mental defilements (*kilesa*) and powers the attainment of **Arahantship** and **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

**(1). Direct Affirmation: Gautama Buddha Declares His Own Path as Tapas**

In the **Bhaya-bherava Sutta (MN 4)**, Gautama Buddha directly refutes the notion that he is an opponent of *tapas*. He affirms that he lives possessing genuine *tapas* (*tapasī*): *So kho ahaṁ, brāhmaṇa, tapassī ceva homi paramo ca tapassīnāṁ \|*

*\"Indeed, brāhmaṇa, I am one who has **tapas** (tapassī), and I am the foremost among those who possess **tapas**.\"*

He does not abandon the word or distance himself from it---he claims the supreme mastery of it.

**(2). True Tāpasa*:* One who Practise *Tapas***

In the **Sutta Nipāta (verse 492, Māgha Sutta)**, Gautama Buddha described *tāpaso* (one who practices *tapas*): *Ye vedha pāpāni nudanti hitvā, pariggahaṁ amamā cerayanti \|Te tādisā tāpasā sādhurūpā, etehi havyāny anumodayetha \|\|*

*\"Those here who drive away evil (pāpāni), who have abandoned worldly possessions (amamā)---such ones are true tāpasā of good nature.\"*

Here, one who practices *tapas* is to have good nature by abandoning evil*,* unwholesome states (*pāpāni*) of the mind, one abandons worldly possessions, ownership (*amamā*), and becomes spiritual oriented, such person is one could induce the primordial spiritual force *tapas*, that can purify the citta, lead to liberation from worldly bondage.

**(3). Manifestation of** ***Tapas*: Patient Endurance (*khantī*)**

In the **Dhammapada (verse 184, Ovāda-Pātimokkha)**, Gautama Buddha defines the supreme manifestation of *tapas*: *Khantī paramaṁ tapo titikkhā, nibbānaṁ paramaṁ vadanti buddhā \|*

*\"Patient endurance (khantī) is the supreme **tapas** (paramaṁ tapo); \'Nibbāna is supreme,\' say the Buddhas.\"*

*Tapas* as the inner spiritual power of the mind-heart, manifest externally as **patient endurance** (*khantī*) for any worldly sufferings, for such a person with *Tapas* already sets their eyes on the supreme *Nibbāna.*

**(4). *Tapas* Ignited Through Jhāna, *Tapas* as Spiritual Fire/Power (*tejas*)**

In the **Dhammapada (verse 387)**, Gautama Buddha explicitly ties the verbal root of *tapas* (*tapati* = to shine forth/ radiate) to the practice of **jhāna**: *Divā tapati ādicco, rattim ābhāti candimā \| Sannaddho khattiyo tapati, jhāyī tapati brāhmaṇo \| Atha sabbam ahorattiṁ, buddho tapati tejasā \|\|*

*\"The sun shines (tapati) by day; the moon glows by night. The warrior shines (tapati) in armor; the brāhmaṇa shines (tapati) by absorption in* **Jhāna** *(jhāyī). But day and night, all through, the Buddha shines (tapati) by his spiritual fire/ power (tejas).\"*

**(5) Tapas** as the internal ignition of primordial consciousness innate to the soul-spirit (*ātmā*) can burn away defilements and purify the Citta.

**SN 12.70 (Susima Sutta): Tapas as Internal Ignition for Liberation**

When Susīma asks about the spiritual life, the Buddha explicitly affirms *tapas* as: *\"Susīma, **tapas** is for the purpose of direct knowledge, for the purpose of full understanding, for the purpose of the complete destruction of the taints (āsava).\"*

In the *Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta* (MN 10), the formula for establishing mindfulness opens with: *\"ātāpī sampajāno satimā\"* (\"ardent, fully aware, mindful\").

**Mainstream Academia's Wrong Accusation and Attack on *Tapas***

Academic Indology and Buddhist Studies routinely translate *tapas* as \"austerity,\" \"penance,\" or \"self-mortification,\" equating it with physical self-torture. This translation is a fundamental distortion of the early Pali Suttas. It misreads the critique of bodily self-infliction, missing how Gautama Buddha distinguished external physical torment from internal spiritual exertion.

**The Fundamental Flaw: Confusing *Paritāpana* with *Tapas***

Mainstream scholarship accuses early Buddhism of either maintaining a contradiction---rejecting *tapas* in principle while demanding \"asceticism\" in practice---or presenting the Buddha as a reformer who simply softened harsh self-mortification into a milder form of physical restraint.

Both assertions rest on a category error. Across the early Suttas (e.g., *Kandaraka Sutta* \[MN 51\], *Māgaṇḍiya Sutta* \[MN 75\], *Devadaha Sutta* \[MN 101\]), the Buddha does **not** reject *tapas*. He rejects **paritāpana** (the deliberate infliction of affliction, torment, and physical pain upon the body), which has nothing to do with ***tapas***.

When the Buddha critiques those who starve their stomachs, bake under the summer sun, or sleep on thorns, he labels them **attāntapo attāparitāpanānuyogamanuyutto** (\"self-tormentors devoted to the pursuit of afflicting themselves\"). The critique targets the **paritāpana**---the useless physical torment that leaves *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* completely untouched within the citta.

**Root of the Academic Misinterpretation**

Mainstream academia\'s erroneous translation and accusation on *tapas* stems from three methodological failures:

**(1). Indiscriminate Application of Hinduism Frameworks**

Mainstream academic Indology has fundamentally misunderstood the ancient meaning of *tapas*, reducing a diverse field of spiritual disciplines to physical mortification. Ancient practitioners employed numerous methods to cultivate spiritual power, including profound meditative states such as *jhāna* and *samādhi*, as well as demanding bodily austerities such as prolonged fasting. Jesus\' forty-day fast in the wilderness provides another familiar example. Such practices were understood as means of inducing profound spiritual transformations and extraordinary capacities. Accounts of near-death experiences, in which individuals sometimes report unusual perceptions or psychic powers, further illustrate the complexity of this subject. By ignoring this broader spiritual context and indiscriminately equating *tapas* with bodily self-mortification, mainstream academics expose the limitations of their historical understanding and their reductionism methodology. Their sweeping judgments reveal not scholarly precision, but a fundamental ignorance of the very spiritual traditions they presume to explain.

**(2). Victorian Theological Framing**

Nineteenth-century colonial translators (such as T.W. Rhys Davids and V. Trenckner) viewed Indian spiritual practices through the lens of medieval Christian monasticism. They mapped the Latinate concepts of **\"penance\"** and **\"mortification of the flesh\"** directly onto *tapas*. This linguistic choice loaded the term with connotations of self-punishment for sin, a concept entirely alien to the early Pali framework of mind-purification.

**Ātāpī Viharāmi**

Here, **Ātāpī** signifies the active, burning energetic primordial spiritual force, needed to prevent unwholesome states from arising and to burn up existing defilements (*kilesa*). It is not passive relaxation inside the hut; it is an active, dynamic internal spiritual energy (*vīriya*) that one dwells with it (*viharāmi*), keeping the citta completely sheltered from external worldly conditions.

The bhikkhu abides (*viharāmi*) in a state of continuous flow of active, burning energetic primordial force. The practice of Brahmacariya is maintained with unwavering diligence.

**What Is the Release, *Mukti* / *Mokṣa in Ṛgveda*? (The Nature of Liberation)**

In the *Ṛgveda*, the root **muc** is prominently used in prayers to the gods (especially Varuṇa and Agni) requesting untying or releasing from bonds (*pāśa*), debt (*ṛṇa*), or illness. In the Vedic sacrificial view, liberation (*mukti*) meant unbinding the physical or subtle body from snares, evil spirits, or death (*mṛtyu*).

In this early Vedic, release (*mukti* / *mokṣa*, from muc) is understood concretely as **unbinding, acquittal, and restoration to Wholeness**.

In *Ṛgveda* 1.24.15: ud uttamam varuṇa pāśam asmad \| avādhamaṁ vi madhyamaṁ śrathāya \| athā vayam āditya vrata tavānāgaso aditaye syāma \|\|

*\"Loosen the upper bond from us, O Varuṇa, untie the middle, loosen the lowest.\"*

To understand what the worshipper is bound to and what release signifies here, we must examine the specific nature of **Varuṇa**, his **pāśa** (fetters), and the Vedic cosmological view of human existence.

**(1). What Are Humans Bonded To? (The Nature of the Fetters)**

In the *Ṛgveda*, **Varuṇa** is the guardian of **Ṛta**---the divine order. When a human violates Ṛta, he suffers **pāśa** (bonds or snares).

**The Three Bonds (Uttama, Madhyama, Adhama)**

The verse explicitly divides the bondage into a threefold spatial and somatic hierarchy:

- **The Upper Bond (*Uttamam Pāśam*):** at the head or crown of the individual. Cosmologically, it links to the heavenly sphere (*svar/dyu*). Somatically/psychically, it represents mental confusion, spiritual affliction, blindness to Ṛta, and heavy guilt that weighs down consciousness from above.

- **The Middle Bond (*Madhyamaṁ Pāśam*):** at the chest or midsection. Cosmologically, it links to the atmospheric realm (*antarikṣa*). Somatically, it binds the life energy (*prāṇa*), the heart (*hṛd*), physical vitality, and emotional balance, causing anxiety, disease, and shortness of life.

- **The Lower/Lowest Bond (*Adhamaṁ Pāśam*):** at the feet or lower limbs. Cosmologically, it links to the earth (*pṛthivī*) and the underworld. Somatically, it represents physical paralysis, total immobility, decay, entangling karmic consequences, and the gravitational pull toward **Mṛtyu** (death) and decay.

**What Caused the Bondage?**

The bondage is caused by **Enmity/Sin (*Āgas* or *Enas*)**. This verse explicitly asks to be made **anāgas** (*an-* \[without\] + *āgas* \[sin/transgression\] = guiltless/faultless). In the Vedic worldview, a person in bondage is physically or psychically ill, sick, unwholesome and is \"trapped\" within realms of existence.

**The Unbinding and Transition to *Aditi* (Freedom)**

The verb forms used---**ut-śrathāya** (loosen upward), **vi-śrathāya** (untie outward/apart), and **ava-śrathāya** (loosen downward)---describe an act of surgical release. The three knots are untied in their respective locations so that the human being is no longer constricted at the head, heart, or feet.

The ultimate purpose of unbinding the three fetters is: *\"So that we, abiding in your sacred law (vrata), sinless/guiltless (anāgaso), may belong to **Aditi*** **(Freedom)***.\"*

**Aditi** (literally *a-diti* = \"unbound,\" \"undivided,\") represents boundlessness and primordial freedom.

**Release (*Mukti*)** in this Vedic context means: **Exoneration:** The removal of sin/guilt (*āgas*) so that one aligns with the divine order (Ṛta). **Somatic/Life Restoration:** Freedom from sickness/decaying, death (*mṛtyu*), allowing one to become wholesome. **Entry into Boundlessness and Primordial Freedom (*Aditi*):** Moving from a state of being trapped (*diti*) to a state of unrestricted, free existence (*aditi*).

**Parallel Confirmation: RV 1.25.21-** The exact same threefold structure appears in the very next sūkta, RV 1.25.21, demonstrating that this was an established liturgical formula in early Vedic thought: *ud uttamaṁ mumugdhi no vi madhyamaṁ cṛṣa \| avādhamāni jīvase \|\| \"Loosen upward the highest bond from us, untie asunder the middle bond, \[loosen\] downward the lowest bonds---so that we may live (jīvase)!\"*

**The Three Fetters: Head-Binding (*Śīrṣataḥ*)**, **Heart-Trembling (*Hṛtkampa*)**, And **Foot-Shackling (*Padbīṣa*):** are regarded as devastating problems in the Vedic tradition, because human body was understood not as an isolated physical object, but as a microcosm (*puruṣa*) woven directly into the cosmic order (*Ṛta*).

**(1). The Highest Bond: Head-Binding (*Śīrṣataḥ*)**

The head (*śīrṣa*) is defined in the *Atharvaveda* (10.2.27) as the **Devakośa**---the \"closed treasury of the deva-gods.\" It is the seat of *manas* (mind-will), spiritual vision, and the gateway connecting the individual to *Dyu* (the upper celestial realm of light). When bound around the head (*śīrṣataḥ*): The \"treasury\" is sealed shut; Mental light is replaced by confusion, delusion, and spiritual blindness; The individual experiences heavy cognitive disorientation, existential sin/guilt (*āgas*), and alienation from divine order (*Ṛta*).

- **Loss of Cosmic Alignment:** A person whose head is bound cannot perceive truth or act with wisdom. They lose their moral compass because their connection to the cosmic mankind Brahma realm is severed.

- **Inability to Perform Right Action:** In the Vedic worldview, all purposeful human action begins with clear mental focus (*manas*). If the head is bound, every intention is corrupted at its source level, trapping the individual in continual ignorance and spiritual suffering.

**(2). The Middle Bond: Trembling of the Heart (*Hṛtkampa*) & Constriction of Life Force (*Prāṇa*)**

The middle region---the chest, heart (*hṛdaya*), and lungs---is the realm of **Prāṇa** (vital energy, life force, foundation for psychic powers) and emotional equilibrium, corresponding to *Antarikṣa* (the atmospheric middle space). When bounded, it manifests physically and psychically as: **Hṛtkampa:** Violent trembling, palpitations, and terror in the heart. **Prāṇa-Constriction:** Suffocation, shortness of breath, and chest tightness. **Internal Agony:** Acute emotional dread, anxiety, and panic.

- **Disruption of the Life-Engine:** *Prāṇa* is the fuel of existence. When the chest is constricted, life-force cannot circulate. The body loses its vital warmth and resilience, making it vulnerable to severe disease.

- **Paralysis of Emotional Stability:** The heart is the center of courage and composure. *Hṛtkampa* destroys inner calm, leaving a person in a constant state of mortal terror and helplessness.

**(3). The Lowest Bond: Foot-Shackle (*Padbīṣa*) & Physical Paralysis**

The lowest region---the legs, feet (*pādau*), and base---anchors the human being to *Pṛthivī* (the earth). The term **Padbīṣa** specifically refers to a heavy wooden or metal shackle used to immobilize animals or prisoners. When bounded: The legs are locked, resulting in physical paralysis or severe lameness. The body loses its capability to move forward, walk, or act upon the earth. It drags the individual down toward **Mṛtyu** (death) and physical decay.

- **Loss of Human Agency:** A human who cannot walk cannot engage in life, perform daily duties, or walk the path of virtue. Physical immobility turns a living person into a passive victim of decay.

- **The Gravitational Pull of Death:** In Vedic thought, mobility represents life (*jīva*), while absolute immobility represents death (*mṛtyu*). The foot-shackle is the direct grip of Yama (death) pulling the body down into the dirt before its natural time.

When an individual is liberated from the three bonds---the highest at the head (*uttama*), the middle at the chest (*madhyama*), and the lowest at the feet (*adhama*)---the Vedic texts describe a profound, total restoration. The individual transitions from a state of paralysis, sin/guilt, and sickness to **Wholeness (*Sarvatāt*)**, **Boundlessness/Freedom (*Aditi*)**, **Divine Light (*Svar/Jyoti*)**, and **Life (*Āyus*)**.

**(1). Entry into Boundlessness (*Aditi*) and Freedom from Sin (*Anāgas*)**

Upon the loosening of the three fetters, one ceases to be constricted (*diti*) and enters the state of **Aditi**---the infinite, undivided, wholesome and unblemished state of existence.

**Ṛgveda 1.24.15:** *ud uttamaṁ varuṇa pāśam asmad avādhaman vi madhyamaṁ śrathāya \| athā vayam āditya vrata tavānāgaso aditaye syāma \|\|*

\"O Varuṇa, loosen the highest bond upward from us! Unloose the lowest \[bond\] downward, and the middle \[bond\] asunder! Then, O Āditya, remaining in thy sacred vow, may we, sinless/blameless (*anāgasaḥ*), belong to Aditi (*Boundlessness*)!\"

**(2). Restoration of Life (*Āyus*) and Overcoming Death (*Mṛtyu*)**

**Ṛgveda 1.25.21:** *ud uttamaṁ mumugdhi no vi madhyamaṁ cṛṣa \| avādhamāni jīvase \|\|*

\"Unbind the highest bond upward from us! Untie the middle bond asunder! \[Unbind\] the lowest bonds downward, that we may live (jīvase)!\"

**Atharvaveda 19.44.4 --- Re-standing and Strength:** *muñcāmi tvā haviṣā jīvano madhyamato pāśāt \| ut te sthāmāyatan pṛthivyām \|\|*

\"I release thee by this consecrated offering, that thou mayest live, from the middle fetter! Upward is thy firm footing (sthāman) established upon the earth (pṛthivyām)!\"

**(3). Emergence from Darkness into Light (*Svar / Jyoti*)**

When the highest bond (*uttama pāśa*) is lifted upward (*ud*) from the head, the dark veil over the mind (*manas*) dissolves. The individual steps out of suffering and beholds the supreme light of truth (*Satya*).

**Ṛgveda 1.50.10 --- Rising Beyond Darkness:** *ud vayaṁ tamasas pari jyotiṣ paśyanta uttaram \| devaṁ devatrā sūryam aganma jyotir uttamam \|\|* \"Beholding the higher light beyond the darkness, we have reached Sūrya, the god among gods---the supreme Light (jyotir uttamam)!\"

**Atharvaveda 7.83.3 --- Awakening from Deathly Slumber:** *vi varha śirasaḥ pāśaṁ vi granthiṁ śrathayā krimeḥ \| ut tiṣṭha jīva mā mṛto mā te prāṇaṁ mayīdharam \|\|* \"Tear away the bond/snare (pāśa) from the head! Untie asunder the knot (granthi) of the worm (krimi)! Rise up (uttiṣṭha)! Live (jīva)! Do not die! Let not thy vital breath (prāṇa) be taken away!\"

**(4). Attainment of Total Wholeness (*Sarvatāt*) and Soundness**

When all three levels are unchained, the human being reaches **Sarvatāt**---complete wholesome of body, breath, and mind, where no part of the microcosm remains diseased or restricted.

**Ṛgveda 10.100.1--11 (Recurring Refrain):** *ā sarvatātim aditiṁ vṛṇīmahe\... \"for **Wholeness (Sarvatāt)** and **Aditi (unbound state)**\...\"*

**Atharvaveda 6.96.3 --- Freedom from All Evil and Affliction**

*muñcantu mā varuṇasya pāśād yad uttamaṁ yad adhamaṁ yan madhyamam \| sarvaṁ tad asmad duritaṁ sujātā sarvair aṅgaiḥ saha lokam ema \|\|* \"May \[the pure deities\] release me from Varuṇa\'s fetter---that which is highest, that which is lowest, and that which is middle! May all that sin / spiritual affliction (duritam) be driven away from us! O well-born ones (sujātāḥ), with all our parts whole, may we enter the world (lokam ema)!\"

In the *Ṛgveda*, **Āditya** is a **patronymic title** meaning *\"Son/Offspring of Aditi\"* (*a-diti* = the unbound, absolute freedom, *sugati*, and wholeness).

In the earliest layers of the Veda, the **Ādityas** are a group of solar, celestial, and Brahma deities who uphold divine order (**Ṛta**). **Varuṇa is prominent member of the Ādityas.** The *Ṛgveda* frequently speaks of the Ādityas in the plural. While later Puraṇic texts expand their number to 12 (one for each solar month), the earliest Vedic texts name 6, 7, or 8 chief Ādityas:

i.  **Varuṇa** (The King and Guardian of Ṛta)

ii. **Mitra** (The Lord of Alliances, Contracts, and Light)

iii. **Aryaman** (The Lord of Hospitality and Nobility)

iv. **Dakṣa** (The Power of Will / Conscious Capability)

v.  **Bhaga** (The Dispenser of Due Shares / Fortune)

vi. **Aṁśa** (The Apportioner)

vii. **Sūrya / Mārtaṇḍa** (The Solar Orb)

In **RV 1.24.15**, calling Varuṇa *\"Āditya\"* is addressing him by his divine family/functional title: **\"O Son of Aditi!\"**

**Bondage in the Pali Canon**

The concepts of bondage and being bonded means the deep psychological and cosmological mechanisms that bind the **Citta** (mind) to **saṃsāra** and the world (**loka**). The state of being bonded is defined as being trapped in involuntary reincarnation, subject to birth, aging, sickness, and death.

**1). The Primary Classifications of Bondage**

The Pali Canon categorizes bondage under several frameworks:

**A. The Ten Fetters (*Samyojana*)**

The *Samyojanas* are the primary bonds that tie an uninstructed worldly person (*puthujjana*) to the wheel of rebirth (*samsāra*), in two sets:

- **Five Lower Fetters (*Orambhāgiya-saṁyojana*)** --- Bind the Citta to the lower realms of existence (sensual sphere):

**Sakkāya-diṭṭhi:** Real/True (**Sa**) Body (**kāya**) view (**diṭṭhi**) (believing the body is true, or ultimately real).

**Vicikicchā:** Skeptical doubt regarding the ultimate deathless reality and the validity for the ancient path of liberation from the world.

**Sīlabbata-parāmāsa:** Wrong, misapprehension, blind attachment to (**parāmāsa**) the vow / observance (**bata/vata**) of **Sīla**: merit hunter or overly fear for observance-failures, abused by the Jataka stories beyond moral teaching, not knowing Sīla is purely a training place for gradual withdrawal from the world.

**Kāmacchanda:** Sexual and sensual desire / craving for sensory stimulation.

**Byāpāda:** fear, anger, ill-will, resentment, or aversion (*dosa*).

- **Five Higher Fetters (*Uddhambhāgiya-saṁyojana*)** --- Bind the Citta to the higher rupa and arupa realms:

**Rūparāga:** attachment to *rūpa* realm (*rūpa-dhatu*).

**Arūparāga:** attachment to *arūpa* realm (*arūpa-dhatu*).

**Māna:** Ego, conceit, worldly pride.

**Uddhacca:** Restlessness, mental agitation.

**Avijjā:** Ignorance of the brahmana three knowledges (**tisso** **vijjā**): 1) knowing what reality is (cosmology), 2) knowing how the universe operates across the history of the cosmic mankind race---including the Brahma source---and 3) knowing how these processes are brought to final resolution or completion: that is, liberation from worldly entrapment, specifically for the mankind race, into the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**B. The Three Root Fires / Poisonous Bonds**

At the core of all bondage to the world lie the three roots of defilements:

- **Rāga:** Sexual craving, lust, passion, worldly attachment.

- **Dosa:** Fear, anger, violence, hatred, ill-will.

- **Moha:** Delusion, ignorance, spiritual blindness. Not knowing the path to liberation.

**C. The Four Bondages / Ties (*Yoga* & *Gantha*)**

The Suttas often use the word **Yoga** (literally \"yoke\" or \"bond\") and **Kāyaganthi** (\"bodily tie/knot\"):

a.  **Kāmayoga:** The bond of sexuality and sensuality.

b.  **Bhavayoga:** The bond of worldly existence/becoming/manifestation.

c.  **Diṭṭhiyoga:** The bond of wrong views and distorted understanding: such as the wrong-awakening to non-dual knowledge or non-dual reality, which leads to dissolution back into the source of the universe; and the wrong understanding of *Anattā* as \"no self\" or \"no soul,\" or the reductionist view that non-self implies the five aggregates constitute the entirety of human existence.

d.  **Avijjāyoga:** The bond of ignorance, not knowing the three knowledges: not knowing the cosmology, true nature or laws of the world. Not knowing the conflict between the civilizational laws and values of cosmic mankind race (from Brahma), and the natural way of the universal laws (from the source of universe). Not knowing what it truly means to be liberated from worldly bondage and universe.

**2). The State of Being Bonded (*Baddha*)**

In the Pali Suttas, an individual who is bonded (*baddha*) is described through vivid structural and existential characteristics:

**I. Subjection to Māra's Snares (*Māra-pāsa*):**

**Māra** is the **personification** of **Death**, the universal law of decay and dissolution. The ancient Vedic metaphor of Varuṇa's noose (*pāśa*) evolves in the Theravada Pali Suttas into **Māra's snare** (*Māra-pāsa*). Anyone bound by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* is caught in Māra\'s net: *Kāmesu giddhā pasutā pamuttā, Anayaṃ samāpannā na hi jānamānā; Nandirāgabaddhā mārapāsena baddhā, Na te pamuccanti mārabandhanā.*

Greedy for sexual and sensual pleasures (kāmesu giddhā), intent upon (pasutā) and immersed in them (pamuttā), Fallen into ruin (anayaṃ samāpannā), truly without knowing (na hi jānamānā); Bound by delight in worldly craving and attachment (nandi-rāga-baddhā), bound (baddhā) by Māra\'s snare (māra-pāsena), They are not (na te) released (pamuccanti) from Māra\'s fetter (māra-bandhanā). (Sutta Nipāta 4.13).

**II. Misapprehension of *Anattā***

The state of worldly bondage is fundamentally perpetuated by a double-sided misunderstanding of *Anattā*, further amplified in the modern era by secular, psychological mistranslations:

a)  **The Modern Translational Distortion (\"No-Self\" as Psychological Reductionism):**

Under the influence of 20th-century secular academia and Western psychology, the Pāḷi term *attā* has been flattened into the abstract psychological concept of \"self\" or \"ego identity.\" This mistranslation distorts *Anattā* into the secular doctrine of \"no-self,\" misleading seekers into believing that ancient spiritual realization is merely a psychological theory claiming human beings possess no soul-spirit (*attā*) or spiritual essence---reducing the entirety of human existence to a temporary, mechanical cluster of five physical and cognitive aggregates (*pañcakkhandha*) that completely disintegrates upon physical death.

b)  **The Materialist/Annihilationist Fallacy (*Ucchedadiṭṭhi*):**

Building upon this translational mistake, this reductionist view assumes that because the five aggregates are *Anattā*, there is no transcendent spiritual entity whatsoever. To justify this claim, proponents of secular reductionism attempt to anchor their \"no-soul\" theory in the teaching of Conditioned Dependability (*Paṭiccasamuppāda*). They argue that human existence is nothing more than a closed, self-contained loop of interdependent mental and physical phenomena---an empty stream of cause and effect where \"impersonal processes give rise to impersonal processes,\" with no underlying soul-spirit (*attā*) or enduring essence behind them. From this premise, they assert that liberation is simply the mechanical halting of this continuous chain of becoming, treating ultimate liberation as total annihilation (*ucchedavāda*)---the exact materialist error explicitly rejected throughout the Pāḷi Canon.

**The True Meaning of Dependent Origination (*Paṭiccasamuppāda*)**

This reductionist interpretation fundamentally misconstrues the purpose and scope of Dependent Origination:

i.  **A Law of the World (*Loka*), Not the Boundless Citta:**

*Paṭiccasamuppāda* does not describe the absolute nature of reality or the totality of existence. Rather, it specifically maps the causal architecture of **worldly bondage, friction, and involuntary reincarnation** (*dukkha*). It demonstrates precisely how the Citta, when blinded by ignorance (*avijjā*), becomes entangled in the worldly apparatus of cause and effect through worldly craving (*taṇhā*) and worldly clinging (*upādāna*).

ii. **Diagnosing the Entrapment Mechanism:**

The twelve links of *Paṭiccasamuppāda* are not an ontological proof that \"nothing exists except conditions.\" Instead, they serve as a diagnostic blueprint showing how the Citta generates worldly becoming (*bhava*) and worldly birth (*jāti*). It shows that as long as the Citta identifies with conditioned phenomena (by the universal law of decay and dissolution), it remains trapped in this samsāric worldly existence.

iii. **Conditioned Cessation vs. Absolute Annihilation:**

When the teaching speaks of the cessation of Dependent Origination (*avijjāyatveva asesavirāganirodhā saṅkhāranirodho\...*), it does not mean the annihilation of a living being or the destruction of the soul-spirit. It signifies the **complete collapse of the worldly causal chain**.

By shattering ignorance through Paññā and burning out latent passion kamma seeds through the primordial force of *tapas*, the Citta severs the fuel of worldly becoming. It drops the five aggregates---recognizing them as worldly burdens belonging strictly to the laws of cause and effect of the world (*loka*)---withdraws its interest, leaves worldly phenomena behind, and transcends them. Rather than dissolving into non-existence, the purified, untrammeled Citta is freed from the closed circuit of conditionality altogether, realizing its true, unshakeable sanctuary in the deathless realm of *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

c)  **The Pseudo-Monistic/Non-Dual Entrapment:**

On the opposite extreme, pseudo-awakening to a non-dual view mistakes the non-duality for the divine, leading to dissolution back into the conscious source of the universe rather than true transcendence out of *saṁsāra*.

To truly understand *Anattā* according to the early Pāḷi Canon is not to proclaim the secular slogan \"there is no soul.\" Rather, *Anattā* serves as a rigorous analytical tool of **disenchantment and dis-identification**. It explicitly targets the five world-bound aggregates (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*) to declare:

*\"This is not mine; I am not this; this is not my soul-spirit.\"*

The fundamental error lies in mistaking the *objects being discarded* (the five aggregates) for the *totality of what exists*. Those bound by this view fail to comprehend that the true spiritual essence (*attā*)---the purified, untrammeled Citta---is entirely beyond, independent of, and uncontained by the five worldly aggregates.

**Textual Proofs & Citations from the Pāḷi Canon**

**The Triad Formula of Dis-identification (*Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta*, SN 22.59 / Vin I 13):** In this sutta, *Anattā* is defined not as a categorical denial of spiritual reality, but as a systematic methodology to dis-identify from conditioned, worldly phenomena: *\"Rūpaṁ\... vedanā\... saññā\... saṅkhārā\... viññāṇaṁ anattā. Taṁ kiṁ maññatha, bhikkhave, rūpaṁ niccaṁ vā aniccaṁ vā\'ti? --- Aniccaṁ, bhante. Yaṁ panāniccaṁ dukkhaṁ vā taṁ sukhaṁ vā\'ti? --- Dukkhaṁ, bhante. Yaṁ panāniccaṁ dukkhaṁ vipariṇāmadhammaṁ, kallaṁ nu taṁ samanupassituṁ: \'Etaṁ mama, eso\'hamasmi, eso me attā\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, bhante.*

*"Form\... feeling\... perception\... mental formations\... consciousness is not the soul-spirit (anattā). What do you think, monks, is form permanent or impermanent?" --- "Impermanent, Venerable Sir." --- "Is that which is impermanent suffering or happiness?" --- "Suffering, Venerable Sir." --- "Is it fitting then to regard that which is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change as: \'This is mine, this I am, this is my soul-spirit\'?" --- "Definitely not, Venerable Sir."*

**Analysis:** The discourse does not state \"there is no soul-spirit.\" It rigorously instructs the seeker to examine each of the five aggregates, recognize their impermanent (*anicca*) and unsatisfying (*dukkha*) nature, and systematically reject them as \"not my soul-spirit\" (*na me so attā*).

**The Five Aggregates Are Not the Whole Being (*Yamaka Sutta*, SN 22.85):** In SN 22.85, bhikkhu Yamaka holds the harmful, reductionist view (*pāpakaṁ diṭṭhigataṁ*) that a liberated being is completely annihilated at death because human existence consists *only* of the five aggregates that break apart. Venerable Sāriputta corrects this exact misunderstanding: *\"Taṁ kiṁ maññasi, āvuso yamaka, rūpaṁ tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso. Vedanā\... Saññā\... Saṅkhārā\... Viññāṇaṁ tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso. Aññatra rūpā\... vedanāya\... saññāya\... saṅkhārehi\... viññāṇā tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso.*

*"What do you think, friend Yamaka: Do you regard physical form as the Tathāgata?" --- "No, friend." --- "Do you regard feeling\... perception\... choices\... consciousness as the Tathāgata?" --- "No, friend." --- "Do you regard the Tathāgata as existing apart from form, feeling, perception, choices, or consciousness?" --- "No, friend." --- "Since the Tathāgata cannot be pinned down or trapped as real and true even here in this very life within the five aggregates, is it proper for you to declare that a realized being is annihilated upon death?"*

**Analysis:** Sāriputta proves that equating a liberated being (*Tathāgata,* literally means having resolved to go there) strictly with the five aggregates---or assuming that nothing remains once the aggregates dissolve---is a fundamental delusion born of materialist reductionism.

**Rejection of Both \"No Soul-Spirit\" and \"Soul-Spirit is the Aggregates\" (*Sabbāsava Sutta*, MN 2):** In MN 2, the Buddha explicitly classifies the dogmatic assertion \"I have no soul-spirit\" (*natthi me attā*) as a thicket of views (*diṭṭhigahana*) and a fetter (*diṭṭhibandhana*) that locks a person into worldly bondage: *\"Tassa evaṁ ayoniso manasikaroto channaṁ diṭṭhīnaṁ aññatarā diṭṭhi uppajjati: \'Atthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati; \'Natthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati\... Ayaṁ vuccatābhikkhave, diṭṭhigahaṇaṁ diṭṭhikantāraṁ\... diṭṭhisaṁyojana saṁyutto.\"*

*"When one attends unwisely in this way, one of six views arises as true and established: The view \'I have a soul-spirit\' arises\... or the view \'I have no soul-spirit\' arises\... This is called a thicket of views, a wilderness of views, a contortion of views\... bound by the fetter of views."*

**Analysis:** The canonical text explicitly rejects the annihilationist/ materialist stance (\"I have no soul-spirit / there is no soul\") as an unwise, speculative trap. Denying spiritual reality entirely keeps the mind trapped within the worldly framework.

To uncover why the statement **\"I have a soul-spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)** is classified as an unwisely (not touching the source/yoni) attended view (*ayoniso manasikāra*). It boils down to **who the \"I\" is** and **the direction of ownership**.

**The Trap of \"I Have a Soul-Spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)**

When someone says **\"I have a soul-spirit,\"** the sentence grammatically and conceptually sets up a false subject-object relationship: **The False \"I\":** The \"I\" claiming ownership in this statement is actually the **ego/personality rooted in the physical reality and five aggregates** (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*). **The False \"Object\":** It treats the true spiritual essence (*attā*) as a secondary possession---a \"thing\" that this worldly body-mind complex *owns* or *possesses* (like saying \"I have a car\" or \"I have a house\").

This is an inverted, upside-down understanding. It places the worldly manifestation in the driver\'s seat and treats the transcendent soul-spirit as an attribute or accessory belonging to the physical person.

**Correct Understanding: The Citta / Soul-Spirit as the True Subject**

To correct this inversion we need to place the true subject back where it belongs: **The True Subject:** **I am the soul-spirit (the purified Citta). The Manifestation / Entrapment:** **I have a temporary manifestation / vehicle in this physical reality (the five aggregates).**

Under this understanding: **The Physical/Mental Equipment is the Burden:** The physical body and cognitive machinery are physical/mental phenomena manifested in this physical world (*loka*). They are subject to cause, effect, decay, and death (*dukkha*). **The Identification Error:** Delusion occurs when the true subject (the Citta) misidentifies with its temporary manifestation, thinking: *\"This physical body/mind is me, this is mine, this is my self.\"* **The Trap:** Thinking \"I (this person) have a soul-spirit\" keeps you grounded in the physical identity. Recognizing \"I am the soul-spirit trapped in/manifesting through this temporary world-bound equipment\" restores the true scale of spiritual reality.

**How the Pāḷi Canon Frames This Correction**

This exact distinction is illustrated in the **Bhāra Sutta (SN 22.22)** through the analogy of the **Burden and the Bearer**: **The Burden (*Bhāra*):** The five aggregates---the physical body, feelings, perceptions, choices, and sensory consciousness---are the worldly burden. **The Bearer (*Bhārahāra*):** The individual spiritual entity (the soul-spirit / Citta) is the bearer carrying the burden.

The error of saying *\"I (the body/mind) have a soul-spirit\"* is equivalent to the **burden claiming it owns the bearer**. The correct realization is recognizing that the bearer (the Citta / soul-spirit) is currently carrying an external, heavy, worldly burden. Through sense restraint, disenchantment, Samadhi, and Paññā, the bearer simply **drops the burden** (*bhāranikkhepanaṁ*), severs all worldly attachment, and realizes ultimate liberation in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**The Analogy of Carrying the Worldly Load (*Bhāra Sutta*, SN 22.22)**

In SN 22.22, the Buddha explicitly separates the five aggregates from the true bearer (*bhārahāra*) who carries and eventually drops them: *\"Bhārā ha pañcakkhandhā, bhārahāro ca puggalo; Bhārādānaṁ dukkhaṁ loke, bhāranikkhepanaṁ sukhaṁ.\"*

*"The five aggregates are indeed the burden (bhāra); the individual bearer (bhārahāra) is the carrier of the burden. Taking up the burden is suffering in the world; laying down the burden is supreme bliss."*

**Analysis:** The five aggregates constitute the worldly burden (*bhāra*). The spiritual essence (the Citta / person) is the bearer (*bhārahāra*). Equating the bearer directly with the burden---or claiming the burden is all that exists---is the precise reductionist mistake that prevents the Citta from dropping the load, achieving dispassion (*virāga*), and realizing ultimate liberation (*Vimutti*) in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**III. Involuntary Reincarnation (*Saṃsāra*)**

Because of worldly craving (*taṇhā*) and clinging (*upādāna*), the Citta is perpetually yoked to new worldly existence. Upon the dissolution of one body, the Citta is propelled by its own worldly attachments into another womb, another world, and another cycle of birth and death.

**3). The Nature of Liberation (*Vimutti*)**

Just as bondage is the internal tying of the Citta to the world through *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, liberation (*vimutti*) is the complete untying and unbinding from the world:

**Cessation of Worldly Clinging:** It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, that we can escape involuntary reincarnation and gain true freedom from the trap of the world.

**Transcendence of the World (*Loka*):** We are trapped into the world, that results in the involuntary reincarnation in this world, and all the worlds in the universe. We call the pulling force of the world is the defilements of the mind-heart Citta, namely *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. Our goal of practice is to purify the Citta, sever the attachment to the world. The purified Citta will disenchant from the world (*nibbida*), fading off world passion (*viraga*) and be liberated from the world (*vimutta*).

**The Exit Threshold:** The exit point from *saṃsāric* worlds to the deathless realm of *Nibbāna* is specifically through the Pure Abodes Brahma heavens. Upon the dissolution of the final body of an Arahant (a fully purified mankind), the purified mind-heart Citta of the Arahant enters the rupa realm, and liberation is realized through the exit of this world to the outside of the universe to go to the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*. The Pure Abodes Brahma heavens of the rupa realm serves as final threshold, the \"gateway\" through which this transcendence occurs.

**The Unconditioned Realm (*Asaṅkhata-Dhātu*), Deathless (Amata), Pure Wholesomeness (Sugata) with Ultimate Peace (Santi)**

Everything in the world is conditioned (*saṅkhata*) by the universal laws of the universe which contains billions of worlds---including the laws of decay and dissolution. Nibbāna-Dhātu is a realm that is completely unconditioned (*asaṅkhata*) by the laws we observe in this universe because it is outside of the jurisdiction of this universe. That is why mankind in the realm of Nibbāna has no death, they can enjoy permanent blissful life, ultimate Peace (Santi), and without sufferings as we experienced here in this world.

In short, the liberated life in the realm of Nibbāna is not nothingness, annihilation or empty voidness; it is a new life with the perfect freedom. In the Pāli Canon, specifically within the **Asaṅkhata Saṁyutta** (SN 43.1--44), the Buddha delivers a series of short discourses outlining the **epithets, characteristics, and synonyms of Nibbāna**. This definitive canonical list contains **35 characteristics** describing what the realm of Nibbāna is like including:

Asaṅkhata (unconditioned) ananta (infinite) anāsava (taintless, undefiled), Sacca (truth, real) pāra (beyond, the other shore) nipuṇa (perfect) sududdasa (hard to be seen); Ajajjara (un-aging, undecaying) dhuva (permament, constant) apalokita (undisintegrating), Anidassana (non-manifestive) nippapa (free from proliferation/diffuseness) santa (calmed, peace).

Amata (deathless) paṇīta (sublime, excellent) siva (auspicious) khema (secure, safe), Taṇhākkhaya (destruction of craving) acchariya (wondrous, extraordinary) abbhuta (un-precedented, marvellous); Anītika (free from harm/ plague) anītikadhamma (of a nature free from harm/ plague), Nibbāna (unbinding) sugata (pure wholesomeness) desita (shown, pointed out).

Abyābajjha (without oppression, un-afflicted) virāga (dispassion), Suddhi (purity, cleanness) mutti (release, unbinding) anālaya (un-clinging, free from attachment); Dīpa (island) leṇa (cave) tāṇa (guard, protection), saraṇa (refuge, shelter) parāyana (final destination, ultimate goal).

#### 1.2. Mahākoṭṭhita

**Upasanto uparato, mantabhāṇī anuddhato;\
Dhunāti pāpake dhamme, dumapattāṁva māluto ti.**

*Calmed (Upasanto), restrained (uparato), speaking with measured wisdom (Mantabhāṇī), un-agitated (Anuddhato)---he shakes off unwholesome mental states (pāpake dhamme), just as the wind (māluto) shakes off a leaf from a tree (dumapattāṁ).*

**1. Upasanto:** \"He who has reached supreme peace or appeasement\"

- **Prefix *Upa-***: Derived from PIE *\*upo* (\"up, high, supreme, toward\"). It denotes **elevation, supreme attainment, heightenings, or complete convergence** (e.g., *upagacchati* = to reach fully; *upajjhāya* = higher preceptor). Here, *upa-* signifies **supreme, total, or heightened**

- **Santi** *(feminine noun, from Sanskrit śānti)*: **Peace itself** as an ultimate state, reality, or quality. It is the actual cessation of agitation, the stillness, and the tranquility realized when defilements (*kilesa*) are extinguished. In early text, *Santi* is a direct epithet for Nibbāna.

<!-- -->

- **Santo** *(masculine nominative singular adjective/participle, from Sanskrit śānta)*: **The person or mind that IS peaceful**. It describes one who has reached that peace---someone who is calmed, stilled, pacified, or thoroughly appeased (e.g., *Upasanto*). It can also refer collectively to \"the peaceful ones\" or \"the virtuous/tranquil ones.\"

**Canon & Vedic Lineage**

- **Pāḷi Suttas:** In the *Upasanta Sutta* (AN 10.8), *upasanta* describes one who has completely stilled the fires of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*: *\"Kathañca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu upasanto hoti? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno rāgo pahīno hoti, doso pahīno hoti, moho pahīno hoti\...\"*

  \"And how, bhikkhus, is a bhikkhu supremelypeaceful? Here, his rāga, dosa, and moha are abandoned\...\"

- **Vedic Antecedent:** In the *Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad* 6.11, it denotes ultimate transcendental peace beyond the physical world: *śāntaṁ niravadhyaṁ nirañjanaṁ\...*

  \"The peaceful, blameless, unblemished\...\"

- **Ṛgveda 10.34.14**, *śam* is invoked as divine appeasement: *mṛḍā no viśvā bhuvanābhi śaṁ naḥ*

  \"Be gracious to us, bring supreme peace to our whole world\"

**2. Uparato:** \"He who has elevated above worldly delight.\"

- **Prefix *Upa-***: Functioning as an intensifier indicating **transcendence, stepping above, or supreme turning away**.

<!-- -->

- *Rataḥ*(Sanskrit) and *Rato* / *Rata* (Pāḷi), literally means**:** \"Delighted,\" \"engrossed,\" \"pleased,\" \"attached,\" or \"resting upon.\"

  **The Two Directions of *Rato* in early Texts**

  In early literature, **rato** acts as a direction indicator for the *Citta* (mind-heart). It is not inherently negative or positive; rather, its quality depends entirely on its object:

1)  **Unwholesome Direction:**

    When directed toward external objects (*loka*), it leads to craving, attachment, and continued *dukkha*. Example: *Pappoti visamaṁ kāme **rato*** --- \"He falls into trouble who is **engrossed** in sensual pleasures.\"

2)  **Wholesome Direction:**

    When directed toward *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, or *Paññā*, it serves as a necessary stepping stone (*Samādhi*) on the path. Example: *Bhāvanāya **rato** mano* --- \"The mind that finds **delight** in mental cultivation/meditation (*bhāvanā*).\"

**Rata is NOT exclusively worldly.** In the Pali Canon, *rata* is frequently used to describe the highest transcendent joy, liberation, and Nibbāna.

**(1). Transcendent *Rata* in the Canon**

The Suttas repeatedly use *rata* (and the noun *rati*) to describe the Citta resting in the highest spiritual realities:

- **Delighting in the Destruction of Craving (Dhammapada 187):** *Taṇhākkhaya-rato hoti, sammāsambuddhasāvako.*

\"The disciple of the Rightly and Fully Awakened One **delights (*rato*)** in the destruction of craving.\"

- **Delighting in Renunciation & Peace (Dhammapada 181):** *Ye jhānapasutā dhīrā, nekkhammūpasame ratā.*

\"The wise who are devoted to jhāna, who **delight (*ratā*)** in the peace of renunciation.\"

- **Delighting in the Dhamma (Theragāthā 1003):** *Dhammarāmo dhammarato\...*

\"One who finds joy in the Dhamma, who **delights (*rato*)** in the Dhamma\...\"

**(2). The True Secret of root ram (To Rest)**

To understand why *rata* can apply to both sensual pleasure and transcendental spirituality, we have to look at the oldest Vedic layer of the root **ram**.

Mainstream academia usually translate ram only as \"to delight\" or \"to enjoy.\" But in the ancient *Ṛgveda*, the primary physical meaning of ram is **\"to come to a standstill, to stop wandering, to rest, to abide.\"**

Pleasure and delight are *secondary* meanings. Why? Because **delight is simply the experience of the mind coming to rest on an object.**

- When the mind stops wandering and rests on a sensual object, it experiences *rata* as sensual delight.

- When the mind stops wandering and rests in the unconditioned truth, it experiences transcendent *rata* of spiritual delight.

**(3). The Symmetry of** ***Upasanto* and *Uparato***

Mainstream scholars translate *Upasanto uparato* as \"Calmed and ceased.\" This makes the Arahant sound dead and ascetic. But applying the vector of **upa-** (\"supreme, to the ultimate limit\") to the true roots reveals a state of majestic completion:

- **śam (to still/pacify) + upa- -\> Upasanto:**

The Citta is **supremely stilled** (all fires of *rāga*, *dosa*, *moha* extinguished).

- **ram (to rest/delight) + upa- -\> Uparato:**

The Citta is **supremely rested**. It has come to its supreme abiding place. The exhausting wandering through worldly objects has ended, and the mind now rests in the high, wholesome, spiritual delights.

By forcing the definition of *rata* to mean only \"worldly attachment,\" mainstream academics created a false paradox that forced them to translate *uparato* as the death of all joy. We need to cut through that: *rata* is the Citta finding its resting place of joy. **Uparato** is the Citta finding its *ultimate* resting place, transcending the world entirely.

**(1). World-Transcending Delights**

When the prefix **upa-** elevates the root ram, it signifies a functional shift: the Citta steps out of base, unwholesome pleasures (*kāma-rati*) and engages with **higher, wholesome, liberation-oriented delights**.

In the Canon, this elevated spectrum of delights includes:

- **Delight in Seclusion & Renunciation (*Nekkhammūpasame ratā*):** The intense, unblemished joy that arises when stepping away from household entanglements and sensory noise (**Dhp 181**).

- **Delight in the Jhāna (*Jhānarato*):** The refined, unworldly bliss (*niramisa-sukha*) of the jhāna states, where the mind is unified and free from sensory disturbance.

- **Delight in the Dhamma (*Dhammarato*):** The deep joy of reflecting upon, understanding, and practicing the Truth (**Theragāthā 1003**).

- **Delight in the Destruction of Craving (*Taṇhākkhaya-rato*):** The sublime satisfaction of seeing the defilements fall away (**Dhp 187**).

- **The Supreme Anchorage (*Nibbānaratana*):** The absolute culmination, where the Citta rests permanently in the deathless realm (**Nibbāna-dhātu**).

**(2). Updated Definition of *Uparato***

**Uparato** (upa- + ram) is the Citta **elevated above base, worldly pleasures**, actively delighting in the higher, world-transcending qualities of liberation:

- **Negative Vector (What is Left Behind):** Complete desisting from unwholesome, world-bound, feverish craving (*kāma-rati* / *nandī*).

- **Positive Vector (What Abide on):** Heightened engagement with wholesome, noble, and liberating delights (*dhammarati* / *nekkhammarati*), leading up to the supreme peace of Nibbāna.

**(3). The Direct Alignment of Upasanto uparato**

With this updated framework, the dual qualities of the yogi in this verse stand in perfect balance:

- **Upasanto (upa- + śam):** Supremely peaceful---the internal fires of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* are completely extinguished.

- **Uparato (upa- + ram):** Supremely elevated in delight---turned away from base worldly attractions and resting fully in the noble, transcendent joy of the Dhamma and ultimate unbinding.

In conclusion, we must correct the mainstream academia's wrong understanding of **uparato**, that they think the yogi "no longer takes delight (*nandī*) in any phenomenon, and the engagement (*rata*) has not been elevated into a higher pleasure; it has been **completely outgrown and left behind**."

**Canon & Vedic Lineage**

**1). Pāḷi Suttas: MN 1 (Mūlapariyāya Sutta):** *\"Nandī dukkhassa mūlanti---iti viditvā\... uparato hoti\...\"*

\"Having known that delight (*nandī*) is the root that leads to *dukkha*\... he becomes *uparato*\...\"

In early texts, **nandī** (delight/rejoicing) functions as an energetic motor of the mind (*citta*). Mainstream academia treats *nandī* as an inherently evil word creates logical gaps in the texts, because the Suttas themselves describe noble forms of rejoicing (*dhammanandī*, *pīti-sukha*) as well.

Instead, **nandī is structurally neutral at its core**---it is dynamic psychological energy. The critical factor is whether *nandī* anchors itself in worldly phenomena or points toward world-transcending liberation.

**The Mechanics: How *Nandī* Becomes the Root of *Dukkha***

When the Sutta states: **\"Nandī dukkhassa mūlaṁ\"** *(\"Delight is the root of dukkha\")*

It is declaring a structural law of cause and effect. The mechanism works in three distinct stages:

**a) Neutral *Nandī* (Raw Delight/Satisfaction):** The mind experiences gratification or pleasure (*assāda*) in a phenomenon (whether physical elements, high states of absorption, or intellectual insights).

**b) The Worldly Clinging Pivot (*Upādāna*):** As detailed in MN 1 (*Mūlapariyāya Sutta*), the uninstructed mind delights in the worldly (*loka*)---anchoring itself to worldly phenomena.

**c) The Result (*Dukkha*):** Because every worldly phenomenon (*saṅkhata*) is subject to change, decay, and death, delighting in worldly fuel guarantees *dukkha* when those conditions inevitably shift.

**Examining: What Leads to *Dukkha* vs. What Liberates**

**A. Worldly *Nandī* (Leads Directly to *Dukkha*)**

- **Target:** Sensual objects, worldly elements (*pathavī*, *āpo*, *tejo*, *vāyo*), beings, devas when perceived and functioned as worldly.

- **Nature:** Intoxicating, worldly, dependent on conditioned phenomena (*saṅkhata dhamma*).

- **Mechanism:** Generates worldly *upādāna* (clinging) and feeds the cycle of involuntary rebirth (*saṃsāra*).

**B. World-Transcending *Nandī* (Destroys *Dukkha*)**

- **Target:** The Dhamma, renunciation (*nekkhamma*), seclusion (*viveka*), the destruction of defilements (*taṇhākkhaya*), Jhāna/Samādhi, and the unbinding of the mind.

- **Nature:** Cool, unblemished, world-transcending (*lokuttara*), completely unattached to mundane fuel.

- **Mechanism:** Functions as a spiritual stepping stone (*samādhi-parikkhāra*) that lifts the mind entirely out of worldly attachments.

As stated in **SN 35.156**: *\"Nandikkhayā rāgakkhayo\...\" \"Through the destruction of \[worldly\] delight comes the destruction of lust\...\"*

The destruction of *nandī* here means the depletion of the mind\'s fuel for clinging to worldly phenomena, not the destruction of noble spiritual joy.

**Meaning of *Uparato***

With this neutral baseline established, **uparato** can be defined as: Having clearly discerned (*viditvā*) that worldly delight (*lokiya nandī*) in any conditioned phenomenon inevitably generates *dukkha*, the yogi becomes **uparato**: **He steps above and desists** from seeking gratification in worldly fuel (*loka*). **He disengages** the motor of *nandī* from worldly and constructed objects (*saṅkhata dhamma*). **He anchors the mind** in world-transcending, unconditioned delights (*dhammarati* / *nekkhammarati*) that do not lead to *dukkha*, culminating in the absolute security of Nibbāna.

**Pāḷi Canon: Theragāthā 626 & Sutta Nipāta 787**

- **Theragāthā 626:** *Uparato khīṇasīnāvesano\...:* Describing an Arahant as one who is **uparato**---meaning he has completely stepped out of, desisted from, and elevated himself above the habits (*āvesana*) of worldly attachment.

- **Sutta Nipāta 787 (Māgandiya Sutta):** The Sutta contrasts those bound by worldly views with the sage who is *uparato*---withdrawn from worldly disputation and sensual longing, resting instead in inner peace.

**2). Vedic: Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad 4.4.23**

This is an exceptional historical parallel. The phrase in 4.4.23 forms the direct ancient foundation for the saintly ideal: *\...śānto dānta uparatastitikṣuḥ samāhito bhūtvāyĀtmanyevĀtmanaṁ paśyati\...*

(\"Having become peaceful \[***śānta***\], controlled \[***dānta***\], elevated/withdrawn from worldly delights \[***uparata***\], patient \[***titikṣu***\], and concentrated/ ***samāhita***\...\")

- Classical Vedānta commentary (e.g., Śaṅkara) correctly defines *uparati* in this formula as the sustained withdrawal from worldly rituals, sensory duties, and external desires.

- **The Parallel:** Notice the identical pairing of roots: **śānta** (śam) and **uparata** (ram). This proves that long before the Pāḷi Canon, Indian contemplative tradition paired **peaceful** (śam) with **elevating/desisting from worldly noise** (ram) as complementary noble qualities.

**Vedic: Kaṭha Upaniṣad 1.2.24:** *nāvirato duścaritān nāśānto nāsamāhitaḥ \| nāśāntamānaso vāpi prajñānenainam āpnuyāt \|\|*

\"One who has not **desisted/elevated from bad conduct** \[*nā-avirato*\], who is not peaceful \[*nā-śānto*\], who is not concentrated, cannot attain liberation through mere intellectual knowledge.

Here, **avirato** (the negative of *virato/uparato*) explicitly means failure to abandon base, worldly misdeeds. True realization requires turning away from unwholesome actions to allow the mind to rest in higher, pure mental states and deeds.

**Conclusion**

- **What *Uparata* leaves behind:** Worldly *nandī*, sensory indulgence, unwholesome compulsions, and object-bound attachments.

- **What *Uparata* achieves:** Elevation above the mundane world, anchoring the Citta in higher, transcendent, liberation-path-directed delights (*dhammarati*, *nekkhammarati*, *jhānarati*) that culminate in ultimate peace.

**3. Mantabhāṇī**: \"One who speaks from wisdom, reflective, truth.\"

- **Manta**: Derived from root man (\"to think, reflect, discern\") + suffix *-ta*. It translates as **thought, wisdom, counsel** (cognate with Sanskrit *mantra* / *mati*).

- **Bhāṇī**: Agent noun from root bhā (\"to speak, articulate\").

**Canon & Vedic Lineage**

- **Pāḷi Suttas:** In **AN 4.159**, the Buddha specifies that a genuine yogi speaks not with superficial chatter (*vikkhippavācā*), but with *manta*:

  *\"Manta-bhāṇī anuddhato, attha-maṅga-samāhito\...\"*

  \"Speaking with wisdom, un-agitated, concentrated/unified in other's welfare/benefits and auspicious sign \...\"

- **Vedic:** In **Ṛgveda 10.71.2**, speech (*vāc*) is refined through the mind-will (*manas*) by the wise (*dhīrāḥ*):

  *saktumiva titauunā punanto yatrā dhīrā manasā vācamakrata\...*

  \"Where the wise, sifting with the mind as through a sieve, made speech\...\"

**4. Anuddhato:**

- **Prefixes *An- + Ud-***: An- (\"not\") + ud- (\"upward, bursting forth\").

- *Uddhata* literally means \"puffed up, tossed around, agitated, blown upward like dust.\"

- **Literal Meaning:** \"Not blown upward\" / \"Un-agitated\" / \"Grounded and untossed by mental restlessness (*uddhacca*).\"

**Canon & Vedic Lineage**

- **Pāḷi Suttas:** *Uddhacca* (restlessness) is the 8th fetter (*saṁyojana*). In **SN 47.10**, *anuddhato* marks the Citta that is grounded and free from fluctuation: *\"So anuddhato hoti avikkhippo, ekaggacitto\...\"* \"He is un-agitated, un-distracted, with unified Citta\...\"

- **Vedic Antecedent:** In **Ṛgveda 10.94.11**, *uddhata* describes excited, roaring ritual elements or winds. The negative state---being free from wild upward agitation---is praised in **Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa 14.7.2** as the mark of a Mind that has settled into its natural depth rather than being whipped up by external sense-winds.

**5. Dhunāti pāpake dhamme, dumapattāṁva māluto**

"He shakes off unwholesome mental states (pāpake dhamme), just as the wind (māluto) shakes off a leaf from a tree (dumapattāṁ)"

**(1). Unwholesome Mental States (*Pāpake Dhamme*) and the Defilement of the *Citta,***

***Dhammā* as Part of *Citta* and Manifestation of *Ātmā the Soul-Spirit***

***Citta*** is the complete matrix of mind-heart awareness---the entire domain of conscious experience. Within this framework, ***dhammā*** (mental phenomena) operate both as the internal components of ***citta*** and as the outer, functional manifestations of the underlying ***ātmā*** (soul-spirit).

Because ***citta*** spans from its superficial exterior outlook down to its deepest center, all mental phenomena (*dhammā*) are contained within its domain.

**Internal Contents:** Thoughts, feelings, sensory projections, perceptions, and memory impressions are not external entities; they are the active, dynamic parts that constitute the surface layer of the ***citta***. Unwholesome mental states (*pāpake dhamme*)---such as the turbulence born of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---occupy the outer, peripheral layer of the ***citta,*** reflects a defiled citta down to its core essence ***ātmā*** (soul-spirit).

While ***ātmā*** is the essential ground (*svabhāva*), the self-luminous core, and the innermost depth of ***citta***, it is not an abstraction isolated from existence.

- **The Expression of the Core:** Just as the five aggregates (*pañcakkhandhā*) serve as the manifested complex (*lakkhaṇa*) or incarnation of ***citta--ātmā*** in the world, mental phenomena (*dhammā*) are the direct psychological manifestations radiating from the ***ātmā***.

- **The Mechanism of Defilement:** When the outer *dhammā* generated by the ***citta*** are unwholesome (*pāpake dhamme*), they do not merely float on the surface---they pollute, cloud, and defile the state of the ***ātmā*** itself. The unwholesome *dhammā* smother the inner soul-spirit, binding the being to worldly *dukkha*, and involuntary reincarnation.

**(2). Purification: Shaking Off the Defiling *Dhammā***

To restore the ***ātmā*** to its pristine, pure state, the defiled ***Dhammā,*** occupying the outer layer of the ***citta*** must be actively detached: *Dhunāti pāpake dhamme, dumapattāṁva māluto.* \"He shakes off unwholesome **Dhammā** (pāpake dhamme), just as the wind (māluto) shakes off a leaf from a tree (dumapattāṁ).\"

Shaking off the leaves (*pāpake dhamme*) clears the current surface pollution, but if the tree itself---the **Citta**---remains saturated with latent defilement, the soil and sap will continually produce new unwholesome leaves. To stop the growth of unwholesome leaves entirely, the purification must go beyond the peripheral manifestations and transform the root matrix of the tree, restoring the **Citta** to its essential depth: the purified **Ātmā** (soul-spirit).

To purify the tree (the ***citta***) and completely stop it from sprouting unwholesome leaves (*pāpake dhamme*), the practice must go deeper than surface-level shaking off (*dhunāti*). It requires the internal transformational engine of **Nibbidā** (disenchantment from the world) and **Virāga** (fading off worldly passion).

**A. *Nibbidā* (Disenchantment toward Worldly Fuel)**

- **The Turnaround:** *Nibbidā* occurs when the ***citta*** sees through the conditioned world (*loka*) and the five aggregates (*nāma-rūpa*). It recognizes that all worldly phenomena generate *dukkha*.

- **Breaking the Enchantment:** The ***citta*** becomes thoroughly weary of feeding on worldly delight (*lokiya nandī*). *Nibbidā* is the active turning away---the moment the ***citta*** feels a deep, wholesome disenchantment toward the worldly fuel that previously nourished its unwholesome states.

**B. *Virāga* (Dispassion / Fading Away of Worldly Passion)**

- **Draining the Toxic Sap:** *Virāga* literally means the \"fading away of worldly coloration/passion.\" It is the direct destruction and fading out of existing *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* within as kamma seeds in ***citta***.

- **Starving the Roots:** While *Nibbidā* is the decision to stop drinking worldly poison, *Virāga* is the actual drying up of the latent tendencies (*anusaya*). Without the \"color\" of passion to stain it, the internal sap of the ***citta*** is thoroughly cleansed.

**From Leaf-Shaking (*Dhunāti*) to Root-Purification**

The complete practice integrates every level of mind-restraint, moving from outer control to total inner transformation:

**Sīla/ Brahmacariya (Virtuous Conduct):** Seals the exterior boundaries, preventing coarse worldly actions from disturbing the Citta.

**Concentration (*Samādhi*):** Unifies and steadies the Citta, generating the intense inner primordial force/energy (*tapas*) required to burn out the subtle, latent defilements (*anusaya*).

**Paññā (Wisdom):** The sharp insight that clearly discerns the difference between the worldly, changing aggregates (*nāma-rūpa*) and the essential ground/core (*svabhāva*). Penetrates the true nature of worldly phenomena, disenchant the soul-spirit from worldly entanglement (*nibbida*), fading of worldly passion and attachment (*viraga*), this leads to liberation of the soul-spirit from the world (*vimutti*).

**The Purified *Ātmā* (Soul-Spirit) :** While *dhammā* form the surface ripples and active contents of the ***citta***, ***ātmā* (soul-spirit)** is its essential core (*svabhāva*). When the Citta\'s latent defilements are completely eradicated through wisdom and intense concentration, its internal nature changes permanently: **Zero Unwholesome Output:** The tree no longer produces *pāpake dhamme*. The Citta\'s output consists only of wholesome, noble expressions (*kusala/lokuttara dhamme*). **Collapsing the Surface Turbulence:** The surface ripples of the Citta become peaceful (**upasanto**). **Uncovering the True Core:** With the outer surface cleared and the internal matrix cleansed, the Citta rests entirely in its primary, self-luminous depth---the purified, unblemished **Ātmā / Attā**.

**Dhutaṅga** (*compound*: *dhuta* + *aṅga*): *Dhuta* = Shaken off / Cleansed; *Aṅga* = Factor / Practice / Limb; **Literal Meaning:** \"The practices for shaking off\" or \"the factors of cleansing.\"

Before the Gautama Buddha, the *śramaṇa* tradition practiced various forms of *tapas* to shake off unwholesome karma (*pāpa*). **The Śramaṇa Milieu:** Renunciates (*paribbājakas*), Jains, and forest-dwellers practiced various forms of *tapas* include applying intense physical friction to \"shake out\" (*dhū*) deep-seated impurities embedded in the subtle body/mind. **Gautama Buddha's inheritance:** Gautama Buddha and some of his early disciples inherited this traditional practice as an **active mental restraint and non-clinging**.

In the early Pāḷi Sutta Pitaka and Vinaya Pitaka, the term *dhutaṅga* as a fixed list of 13 rules was not yet fully standardized, but the individual practices were widely performed by the early Sangha including Venerable **Mahākassapa Thera:** Designated by Gautama Buddha as the foremost in *dhuta* practices (*dhutavādānaṁ*). Mahākassapa explicitly states in the *Kassapa Saṁyutta* (SN 16.5) that he undertook these practices for two reasons: For his own personal well-being (*ditthadhamma-sukhavihāra*) and out of compassion for future generations, to leave an unyielding model of austere discipline. However, the *dhutaṅga* practices were **voluntary, individual vows** designed to support *samādhi* practices, it is not rigid institutional requirements. The practices were later formally systematized into the **13 Dhutaṅgas**:

**1) Paṁsukūlik\'aṅga:** Wearing rag-robes. 2) **Tecīvarik\'aṅga:** Owning only three robes. 3) **Piṇḍapātik\'aṅga:** Eating only almsfood. 4) **Sapadānapātik\'aṅga:** Walking house-to-house without skipping any on the almsround. 5) **Ekāsanik\'aṅga:** Eating in a single sitting. 6) **Pattāpiṇḍik\'aṅga:** Eating directly out of the almsbowl. 7) **Khalupacchābhattik\'aṅga:** Refusing food offered after starting to eat. 8) **Āraññik\'aṅga:** Living in the forest. 9) **Rukkhamūlik\'aṅga:** Living at the foot of a tree. 10) **Abbhokāsik\'aṅga:** Living in the open air. 11) **Sosānik\'aṅga:** Living in a charnel ground/cemetery. 12) **Yathāsanthatik\'aṅga:** Accepting whatever dwelling is assigned. 13) **Nesajjik\'aṅga:** The sitter\'s practice (never lying down to sleep).

#### 1.3. Kaṅkhārevata

**Paññaṁ imaṁ passa tathāgatānaṁ, Aggi yathā pajjalito nisīthe;\
Ālokadā cakkhudadā bhavanti, Ye āgatānaṁ vinayanti kaṅkhan ti.**

*Behold (passa) this (imaṁ) wisdom (paññaṁ) of the Tathāgatās (tathāgatānaṁ), Just as (yathā) a fire (aggi) blazing brightly (pajjalito) in the deep of night (nisīthe); Light-givers (ālokadā) and eye-givers (cakkhudadā) they become (bhavanti), Who (ye) dispel (vinayanti) the doubt (kaṅkhaṁ) of those who come (āgatānaṁ) \[to them\].*

**Commentary**: The Wisdom (*pañña*) of the Tathāgatās

*Paññā* of the Tathāgatās is like *fire* (*aggi*), that consumes fuel (defilements) and generates immediate, unyielding brightness. The wisdom of a Tathāgata is functional and energetic---it destroys darkness on contact.

Tathāgatas are light-givers and eye-givers (**Ālokadā cakkhudadā**). **Light and Vision (*Āloka* & *Cakkhu*)** grants immediate seeing the truth, the liberation path toward the deathless, dispel the doubt (*Kaṅkhā*), guide them to practise toward ultimate liberation through his teaching (*dhamma*) and provides the precise methodology (Sīla, Samādhi, Paññā).

**Tathāgata: The Pre-Buddhist Śramaṇa Origin**

**Tathāgata** does **not** appear anywhere in the primary Vedic texts---it is completely absent from the *Rig Veda*, *Sāma Veda*, *Yajur Veda*, *Atharva Veda*, as well as the early *Brāhmaṇas* and early *Upaniṣads*. It is a term that originated exclusively within the **Greater Magadha Śramaṇa substrate** before Gautama Buddha's time and was canonized by Gautama Buddha.

**1). Tathāgata Is Absent from the Vedas**

The term *Tathāgata* belongs to a specific philosophical vocabulary designed to describe a being who has completely transcended the cycle of rebirth (*saṃsāra*) and worldly existence (*loka*).

- **The Vedic Framework:** As established in the *Rig Veda*, the highest spiritual goal was achieving long life on earth and ascending to the ancestral heaven (*Pitṛ-loka*). The early Vedic bards had no term describing someone who had gone beyond the cosmic universe entirely.

- **Grammatical Formation:** The compound is built from early Middle Indo-Aryan/Pāḷi roots (**tathā + gata**):

  - ***tathā*** functions as a demonstrative pointing to a specific location, condition, or state (*\"there\"* / *\"that place\"*).

  - ***gata*** means **\"gone\"** (or **\"has gone\"**).

  - Compound Meaning: ***tathā-gata*** = **\"gone there\"** / **\"one who has gone to that place\"**.

**2). Jainism (Ardhamāgadhī Canon)**

In the Jain Agamas (written in Ardhamāgadhī Prākrit), the exact linguistic equivalent **Tathāgaya** (*Tathāgata*) appears occasionally, though it is not a central title for Mahāvīra.

- **Epithet for Liberated Beings:** When it does appear in Jain texts (such as passages in the *Ayyāraṁga Sutta* or late commentaries), it functions as an adjectival epithet meaning \"one who has reached such a place or level (*Tatha*).\"

- **Secondary Status:** Jainism overwhelmingly preferred titles like **Tīrthaṅkara** (\"Ford-maker\"), **Jina** (\"Conqueror\"), **Kevalin** (\"Omniscient One\"), and **Arhat** (Arahant, \"Purified One\"). *Tathāgata* remained a peripheral term rather than a primary title.

In the context of the term Tīrthaṅkara (\"Ford-maker\"), a ford is a shallow, safe crossing place across a river or body of water. Linguistically and metaphorically:

- **Tīrtha (Ford):** A physical place where a river is shallow enough to wade across safely without drowning. Metaphorically, it refers to a spiritual pathway or bridge created across the treacherous ocean of worldly existence (*saṃsāra*).

- **Kara (Maker / Builder):** Derived from the root kṛ (*to make/do*), meaning one who constructs or establishes something.

<!-- -->
