- ***Tīrtha*** comes from the root **tr**, which literally means *\"to cross over\"* or *\"to reach the other side.\"*

- From this same root comes the word ***Tāraka*** (*\"one who carries across\"*) and ***Avatāra*** (*\"one who descends down\"*).

Thus, a **Tīrthaṅkara** is literally *\"one who makes a crossing place\"* across the river of worldliness so that others may safely cross over to liberation. In ancient Indian spiritual traditions, worldly existence (*saṃsāra*)---with its endless cycle of birth, death, decay, and karmic bondage---is routinely envisioned as a vast, dangerous, and turbulent river or ocean that living beings are drowning in.

A **Tīrthaṅkara** (\"Ford-maker\") is a spiritual pioneer who:

a.  **Finds the Shallow Crossing:** Discovers the exact spot where the turbulent river of worldliness can be crossed safely.

b.  **Builds the Path:** Establishes the spiritual order and teachings (the Dhamma/Dharma) that allow others to cross over.

c.  **Leads Beings to the Other Shore:** Guides seekers across the waters of existence to reach the safe \"other shore\"---the deathless realm of total liberation (*Nirvāṇa* / *Mokṣa* / *Nibbāna-dhātu*).

**3). Canonized by Gautama Buddha**

While the word belonged to the broader Magadhan ascetic vernacular, Gautama Buddha uniquely elevated **Tathāgata** into his primary title to signify one who has transcended worldly existence and is ready to go to the realm (*dhātu*) of *Nibbāna.* The word appears prominently in the **Pāḷi Canon** (and early Śramaṇa literature), where Gautama Buddha routinely uses it as his primary self-designation instead of using personal pronouns:

- **Sutta Nipāta & Dīgha Nikāya:** The Buddha frequently refers to himself in the third person as *The Tathāgata*.

- **Pāḷi Definition:** *Tathā tathā gato\'ti Tathāgato*---\"There, to that place \[he has\] gone, therefore \[he is called\] Tathāgata.\"

#### 1.4. Puṇṇa (1st)

**Sabbhireva samāsetha, paṇḍitehatthadassibhi;\
Atthaṁ mahantaṁ gambhīraṁ, duddasaṁ nipuṇaṁ aṇuṁ;\
Dhīrā samadhigacchanti, appamattā vicakkhaṇā ti.**

*With the virtuous ones (sabbhi) only (eva) one should associate (samāsetha), and with the wise ones (paṇḍitehi) who see the supreme goal (liberation from the world) (atthadassibhi mahantaṁ); The goal (atthaṁ) \[which is\] profound (gambhīraṁ), hard to see (duddasaṁ), subtle (nipuṇaṁ), \[and\] heavenly (aṇuṁ);*

*The steadfast ones (dhīrā, those anchored in unwavering mental stability and virtue) attain the goal with samadhi (samadhi-gacchanti), \[being\] zealous (appamattā) \[and\] clear-sighted (vicakkhaṇā, possessing sharp, analytical vision).*

**Commentary**: **1). Exclusivity of Association (*Sabbhireva Samāsetha*)**

One should associate (samāsetha) with the virtuous ones (sabbhi) and the wise ones (paṇḍitehi) who see the supreme goal (liberation from the world) (atthadassibhi mahantaṁ) only (eva).

- **Exclusive Alignment (*Eva*):** The strict inclusion of *eva* (\"only/alone\") establishes that spiritual development requires total boundary control over one\'s influences. Engaging with those bound to worldly delight (*lokiya nandī*) feeds the surface turbulence of the ***citta***, whereas associating *exclusively* with the virtuous (*sabbhi*) closes off unwholesome inputs.

- **The Virtuous (*Sabbhi*) & The Wise (*Paṇḍitehi*):** Association (*samāsetha*) is not casual companionship; it is proximity, alignment, and receptive listening. The *sabbhi* provide the moral foundation (*sīla*), while the *paṇḍitehi* possess the analytical clarity needed to guide the mind toward spiritual progress.

- **Seers of the Supreme Goal (*Atthadassibhi Mahantaṁ*):** True teachers are not merely scholars of text; they are *atthadassibhi*---those who directly behold (*dassī*) the supreme, ultimate purpose (*atthaṁ mahantaṁ*), which is complete liberation from the world.

**2). The Nature of the Transcendent Goal (*Atthaṁ*)**

The supreme goal (liberation from the world) (atthaṁ) \[is\] profound (gambhīraṁ), hard to see (duddasaṁ), subtle (nipuṇaṁ), \[and\] heavenly (aṇuṁ).

- **Profound (*Gambhīraṁ*):** The ultimate goal is deep and unfathomable by ordinary sensory logic or surface-level intellection. It cannot be reached through worldly conceptual frameworks.

- **Hard to See (*Duddasaṁ*):** Because the unpurified ***citta*** is clouded by ignorance (*avijjā*) and occupied by unwholesome mental states (*pāpake dhamme*), the true liberation to conquer aging and death to have permanent life remains hidden behind worldly delusion.

- **Subtle (*Nipuṇaṁ*):** The path and the target are exceptionally refined, requiring the complete stilling of mental restlessness to be perceived.

- **Heavenly / Exalted (*Aṇuṁ*):** Transcending coarse physical limitations, this domain belongs to the elevated, world-transcending sphere---pointing toward the exalted realms and ultimate liberation from *saṃsāric* worlds.

**3). The Engine of Realization (*Samadhi-gacchanti*)**

The steadfast ones (dhīrā, those anchored in unwavering mental stability and virtue) attain the goal with samadhi (samadhi-gacchanti), \[being\] zealous (appamattā) \[and\] clear-sighted (vicakkhaṇā, possessing sharp, analytical vision). 

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Factor**                                          **Operational Role**
  --------------------------------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **The Steadfast (*Dhīrā*)**                         Mind-hearts anchored in unshakable stability and virtue, refusing to be swayed by worldly attractions.

  **Zealous (*Appamattā*)**                           Living with relentless, vigilant effort; refusing to compromise with sensory distraction or sloth.

  **Clear-Sighted (*Vicakkhaṇā*)**                    Possessing sharp, penetrating vision (*paññā*) that discerns conditioned phenomena from the unconditioned core depth.

  **Attaining via *Samādhi* (*Samadhi-gacchanti*)**   Penetration of the supreme goal occurs directly through the power of ***Samādhi***, which acts as the purifying force that elevates the ***citta*** to higher dimensions and reveals its pristine essence.
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

By associating exclusively (*eva*) with those who see the true exit from the world, the practitioner gathers the instructions needed to navigate a goal that is profound, subtle, and hard to see. Through unflagging zeal (*appamattā*) and sharp insight (*vicakkhaṇā*), the steadfast practitioner relies on the power of **Samādhi** to transcend to the Rupa realm, allowing the ***citta*** to exit the universe and realize ultimate freedom and permanent life.

#### 1.5. Dabba

**Yo duddamiyo damena danto, Dabbo santusito vitiṇṇakaṅkho;\
Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Dabbo so parinibbuto ṭhitātto.**

*He who (yo) was difficult to tame (duddamiyo), disciplined (danto) through self-control (damena); Dabba \[the Capable One\] (dabbo), fully contented (santusito), having crossed over (vitiṇṇa) doubt (kaṅkho); A conqueror (vijitāvī), free from fear (apetabheravo) indeed (hi); That (so) Capable One (dabbo), completely unbound (parinibbuto), is steadfast (ṭhita) in the Soul-Spirit (attā).*

**Commentary**: The Taming Engine

1.  **Taming the Wild (*Damena Danto*):** The unpurified ***citta*** in its natural worldly state is inherently *duddamiyo*---wild, untamed, and continually generating unwholesome mental states (*pāpake dhamme*). Taming occurs through *dama* (internal restraint, self mastery, self control).

The Pāḷi term **Damena** is the Instrumental Singular form of the masculine noun **Dama**, meaning *\"through self-mastery,\" \"by restraint,\"* or *\"through internal discipline," "*subduing of the senses*.\"*

Rooted directly in the ancient Indo-European verbal root **dam** (cognate with the Sanskrit dam, Greek *damáō*, and Latin *domare* / English *tame* or *dominate*), it signifies the direct, energetic application of force to subdue wild, unrefined nature.

**Dynamic Role in Ancient Texts**

Across Vedic Sanskrit and Pāḷi Canonical literature, **Dama** represents the necessary initial friction applied to an untamed, wild entity to make it fit for higher purpose.

**A. The Equine / Yoke Metaphor**

In early texts, the mind before spiritual training is repeatedly compared to a wild, unbroken stallion (*assadammayutta* or *duddamiyo*). Left to itself, its unrestrained power leads to self-destruction. **Damena** is the act of putting on the bit, bridle, and yoke---forcing the wild energy into a single direction.

**B. Dama vs. Daya / Dāna (Vedic & Upaniṣadic Foundations)**

In the famous passage of the *Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad* (5.2.3), the divine voice of thunder utters the syllable *\"Da\"*, which is interpreted three ways:

a)  **Damyata:** Practice **Dama** (self-control/taming).

b)  **Dattā:** Practice **Dāna** (generosity).

c)  **Dayadhvam:** Practice **Dayā** (compassion).

Here, **Dama** is identified as the prime directive for human beings (*manusya*) to control their sensory drives and raw impulses before higher wisdom can manifest.

2.  **Contented (Santusito) and Eradication of Hesitation (*Vitiṇṇa-kaṅkho*):** Having received both light and vision (*ālokadā cakkhudadā*) from the Tathāgata, the practitioner is contented (santusito) and crosses over (vitiṇṇa) all existential doubt (*kaṅkhā*). No longer wavering, they become a true conqueror (*vijitāvī*) over worldly attachments (*loka*).

The liberation path instructed from our Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, is a precise, step-by-step methodology (*anupubbikathā / anupubbasikkhā*) designed to pull a practitioner out of worldliness (*loka*) and lead them straight out of universal *saṃsāra*.

**A. Santusito (Contented / Fully Satisfied)**

Deep, unshakeable contentment; complete freedom from the inner itch of wanting something else or something more.

In the gradual training, *santusito* is not passive apathy. It is the immediate consequence of **renunciation (*nekkhamma*)**. The unpurified *citta* seeks satisfaction in external sensory objects (*kāmaguṇa*), which are inherently unstable (*anicca*) and *dukkha*. As a practitioner moves from 5 to 8 to 10 precepts and into the *bhikkhu* life, outer needs are stripped down to the bare minimum (clothing, food, shelter, medicine). True *santosa* (contentment) arises because the worldly craving (*taṇhā*) is no longer being fed; the mind becomes self-contained, delighting in quietude rather than external consumption.

**B. Kaṅkhā (Doubt / Hesitation / Wavering)**

Existential doubt, spiritual hesitation, double-mindedness, or wavering at the crossroads.

*Kaṅkhā* is one of the primary hindrances (*nīvaraṇa*) that paralyzes spiritual progress. It stems directly from a lack of knowledge and vision (*aññā*). When a disciple has not yet tasted the truth of the Dhamma, they vacillate between worldly pursuits and the transcendent path.

**Crossing Over (*Vitiṇṇakaṅkho*):** When Gautama Buddha's instruction is seen clearly---that all conditioned phenomena are worldly, belonging to the world (*loka*), subject to decay and death---the disciple crosses over all wavering. The path ceases to be a theoretical debate and becomes an exact, unambiguous directional exit.

**The Gradual Training of Detachment (*Anupubbasikkhā*)**

Gautama's training systematically alters the practitioner\'s relationship to the world, progressively severing the cords of worldly attachment:

- **5 Precepts (Ethical Human):** Restrains destructive physical/verbal actions; prevents birth in lower realms by establishing basic non-harming (*ahiṃsā*).

- **8 Precepts (Uposatha / Part-Time Practice):** Cuts into sensory gratification (celibacy, fasting past noon, abstaining from worldly entertainment and luxuries) to give householders periodic tastes of renunciation.

- **10 Precepts (Full-Time Renunciate):** Complete abandonment of money, personal property, and worldly commerce; full entry into the life of a renunciate.

- **Bhikkhu / Bhikkhunī Discipline:** Full monastic integration designed to refine every act, habit, and thought, keeping the practitioner entirely dedicated to purification of residue worldly attachments.

**The Ultimate Exit: Beyond the Universe to Nibbāna-Dhātu**

The purpose of this progressive detachment is not to repair or settle down within the world, but to escape it entirely.

a.  **The Worldly Sphere (*Loka*):** The universe consists of billions of worlds and realms (*loka-dhātu*), all governed by universal law of decay and death. They offer no permanent life.

b.  **Disenchantment (*Nibbidā*) and Dispassion (*Virāga*):** As the disciple sees clearly through wisdom (*paññā*) that the *citta,* the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā,* can exist independent of the world and universe *(anissito),* one becomes thoroughly disenchanted from the world. It lets go of all worldly clinging (*upādāna*), fades off all worldly passions (*virāga*).

c.  **The Threshold of Escape:** The exit point from *saṃsāra* to **Nibbāna-dhātu** operates specifically through the Pure Abodes Brahma heavens. Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant, the purified *citta* transcends the confines of the physical universe entirely, realizing ultimate liberation in **Nibbāna-dhātu**---the deathless realm completely outside the universe.

<!-- -->

3.  **Conqueror (vijitāvī),** **Freedom from Fear (*Apetabheravo*):**

**Physiological & Psychological Union of Fear and Anger**

In Pāḷi Sutta analysis, both fear (*bhaya/bherava*) and anger (*kodha/dosa*) belong to the same root category: **Dosa-mūla Citta** (consciousness rooted in dosa: fear, anger, violence, aversion, hate etc.).

- Anger is the active, aggressive outward projection of ***dosa***, while fear is the passive, defensive inward contraction of the exact same ***dosa***.

- **Somatic Manifestation:** Because both stem from ***dosa*** to an unpleasant object or threat, they trigger identical physical heat, tightness, and visceral agitation in the physical body.

**Confidence (*Saddhā*) as the Direct Antidote to Fear**

Fear arises primarily from a lack of unwavering confidence (*saddhā*) in the Tathāgata and his ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

- **The Shift in Value:** When a practitioner clearly understands the step-by-step training, their concern shifts away from worldly loss (*loka*). They realize what belongs to the world is not important, not worthy of attention, just *dukkha*.

- **Abandoning Worldly Benefits:** Recognizing that material possessions, status, and even the physical body are not our true self or soul-spirit, the practitioner ceases to fear their loss.

- **Focus on Mental Qualities:** The sole priority becomes the cultivation of internal virtues: **Sīla** (virtue), ***Samādhi*** (concentration), and **Paññā** (wisdom).

**The Role of This Book \"Voices of Liberation\"**

The authentic utterances (*udāna*) and verses (*gāthā*) of early *bhikkhus* and *bhikkhunīs*---Gautama\'s direct spiritual children---serve as precise, battle-tested evidence for modern practitioners.

By reading their direct experiences:

A.  **Unwavering Clarity:** The practitioner receives direct operational instructions on how to practice, train, and tame the *citta*.

B.  **True Conquest (*Vijitāvī*):** Seeing that human beings of the past successfully traversed this exact path generates the unshakable conviction that allows one to cast off all terror (*apetabheravo*), conquer the world (*loka*), and march steadily toward liberation in **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

To become a true conqueror (**vijitāvī**), the battle is shifted entirely from the external world to the internal arena of the mind. The world (*loka*) is not conquered by ruling over land or defeating external enemies, but by conquering oneself through the purification of the ***citta***.

This internal conquest proceeds through four sequential stages: **Taming**, **Disenchantment (*Nibbidā*)**, **Dispassion (*Virāga*)**, and **Ultimate Deliverance (*Vimutti*)**.

**(1). The Nature of the True Conqueror (*Vijitāvī*)**

In the early texts, a true conqueror is defined by self-mastery rather than external dominion. As stated in the *Dhammapada* (verse 103):

*\"Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, he who conquers himself alone is indeed the supreme victor in battle.\"*

The external world (*loka*) holds power over an individual only as long as the mind responds to it with craving (*taṇhā*). By taming the mind, the practitioner neutralizes the world\'s influence, becoming a true *vijitāvī*.

**(2). The Fourfold Sequence of Internal Conquest**

**Stage 1: Taming & Purification of the Citta**

- **Arresting Turbulence:** Through ethics (*sīla*) and ***Samādhi* (**Concentration**)**, the practitioner subdues the noise of the *citta*.

- **Self-Recognition:** As concentration deepens, the practitioner recognizes that the worldly does not belong to our true self, the soul-spirit ***ātmā***.

**Stage 2: Disenchantment from the world (*Nibbidā*)**

- **Seeing Through the World:** *Nibbidā* is the natural psychological shift that occurs when wisdom (*paññā*) sees worldly phenomena as inherently unstable (*anicca*) and *dukkha*.

- **Turning Away:** Like a child who tires of playing with painted clay toys once their true nature is understood, the ***citta*** becomes thoroughly disenchanted with all worldly states, sensory attractions, and material gains.

**Stage 3: Fading of worldly passion (*Virāga*)**

- **Starving the Roots:** *Virāga,* the fading away of worldly passion, acts as the engine that starves the roots of craving (*taṇhā*), *rāga* (attachment), *dosa* (aversion), and *moha* (delusion).

- **Severing the Cords:** Without fuel, the unwholesome states (*pāpake dhamme*) lose their ability to take root, creating the severance of worldly bondage required for ultimate liberation.

**Stage 4: Deliverance from the world (*Vimutti*)**

- **Complete Unbinding from the World:** *Vimutti* is the final release of the ***citta*** from all worldly ties.

- **The Exit from the World:** Reaching total release from bondage to the world, the practitioner knows: *\"Destroyed is birth again in this world, the holy life (brahmacariya) has been fulfilled, what had to be done is done, there is no more returning to this world.\"*

**(3). The Final Triumph**

By conquering oneself, meaning the worldly bondage to our true self, the soul-spirit **Ātmā,** the practitioner is no longer subject to involuntary reincarnation within the world systems that comprise *saṁsāra*.

Passing through the final threshold of the Pure Abodes Brahma heavens, the purified ***citta*** exits the universe entirely, realizing supreme, unshakeable permanent life in the deathless **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

4.  **Completely Unbound from the World, Established in the Soul-Spirit (*Attā / Ātmā*) (*Parinibbuto Ṭhitatto*):**

**(1). Parinibbuto: Completely Unbound from the world / Cessation of Worldly Becoming**

**Pari- (Complete / Fully):** Denotes total completion with no remaining trace. **Nibbuto** literally means **unbound**, **unfettered**, or **disentangled**.

**Stop Arising from the World / Worldly Becoming:** *Parinibbuto* means that the process of worldly becoming (*bhava*) has been completely halted. Because the roots of worldly craving (*taṇhā*), *rāga (worldly carving and attachment)*, *dosa (survival instincts manifested as fear, anger, aversion, violence and hatred)*, and *moha (delusion and ignorance, not knowing the liberation from worldly bondage)* have been starved through fading off worldly passion (*virāga*), no new fuel is added. The momentum of birth in the world, worldly rising, comes to a total stop.

**Completely Unbound from the World:** The soul-spirit (*attā*) is thoroughly unbound from the cords of worldly existence. It is free from worldly bondage and yoke, completely liberated from the universe, is freed from worldly trap.

**(2). Ṭhitatto: Established in the Soul-Spirit (*Attā / Ātmā*)**

**Ṭhita (Sanskrit: *Sthita*)** literally means *\"to stand, to place, to be established, to abide.\"*

**Ṭhitatto: Internal Centering**

**External Centering:** The average person anchors their life, attention, and identity externally in worldly things. This is the cause of *dukkha*. Rather than chasing external, worldly phenomena that belong to the world (*loka*), the practitioner disenchants them, leaves them behind, and grounds their life completely on the soul-spirit (*attā*).

**Internal Centering (Ṭhitatto):** **Ṭhitatto** means one shifts the entire locus of life away from the external world and centers it internally upon the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*). To cultivate and develop the mind-heart *citta* which envelops the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*) using Gautama's teachings (*dhamma*).

**(3). The Unified Reality: Parinibbuto Ṭhitatto**

**The Negative Operation (*Parinibbuto*):** The total unbinding from the world (*loka*) and the complete stopping of the arising of birth in this world, no more another becoming/life in this world.

**The Positive Realization (*Ṭhitatto*):** The state of being permanently established, rooted, and stationary in the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

Together, **Parinibbuto Ṭhitatto** describes the Arahant who has ceased all worldly becoming, completely unbound the soul-spirit from the matrix of worldly *saṃsāra*, and stood firm in ultimate, world-transcending freedom.

**Two Sides of One Transcendent Realization:** *Parinibbuto* describes what has been cast off and extinguished (worldly pollution of the soul-spirit and suffering-aging and death with the world), while *ṭhitatto* describes the resulting internal immovable abide with the soul-spirit, the true self (*attā/ātmā*). A practitioner who is **parinibbuto ṭhitatto** has fulfilled the *Brahmacariya*. Upon the dissolution of their final body, the purified *citta* enters the **Pure Abodes Brahma heavens and the** **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

#### 1.6. Sītavaniya

**Yo sītavanaṁ upagā bhikkhu, Eko santusito** **samāhitatto;\
Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Rakkhaṁ kāyagatāsatiṁ dhitimā ti.**

*That bhikkhu who has gone to the Cool/* *peaceful Forest (Sītavana), Solitary/* *alone (Eko), completely content (Santusito), centered internally in Samādhi upon the soul-spirit (attā) (samāhitatto); A conqueror (Vijitāvī), fearless (Apetaloma-haṁso), Guarding (Rakkha) mindfulness directed to the body (Kāyagatāsati), resolute/* *fortitude (Dhitimā).*

**Commentary**: **Qualities of Bhikkhu with Internal Mastery**

**Sītavanaṁ:** *Sīta* (cool / peaceful) + *vana* (forest). Specifically refers to the Sītavana (Cool Grove/Charnel Ground near Rājagaha).

**Eko:** *Solitary, alone, single-minded.*

**Santusito:** *Sṁ-* (completely) + *tuṣ* (content/satisfied) -\> *thoroughly content, needing no external worldly stimulation.*

**Samāhitatto:** One who centers life not on external worldly phenomena, but internally, collected in Concentration (Samādhi) upon the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

- *Samāhita:* Collected, centered in Samādhi (Concentration).

- *Atto / Attā:* Soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

**Vijitāvī:** a conqueror who has overcome all worldly attachments.

**Apetaloma-haṁso:** One from whom hair-raising terror and fear have completely departed; utterly fearless in the desolate charnel grounds. *Apeta:* Gone away, departed, eradicated. *Loma:* Body hair. *Haṁsa:* standing on end (goosebumps from fear or dread).

**Rakkhaṁ:** *Guarding, protecting, sustaining*.

**Kāyagatāsatiṁ:** *Kāya* (body) + *gatā* (directed to / gone into) + *sati* (mindfulness) -\> *mindfulness directed to the body, leads to **Samādhi**.*

**Dhitimā:** *Dhiti* (firmness, fortitude, resolve) + *-mant* (possessing) -\> *steadfastly resolute, endowed with unshakeable spiritual strength.*

#### 1.7. Bhalliya

**Yopānudī maccurājassa senaṁ, Naḷasetuṁva sudubbalaṁ mahogho;\
Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Danto so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.**

*He who has dispelled the army of the King of Death (Maccurājassa), like a mighty flood sweeps away a fragile bridge of reeds; A conqueror (Vijitāvī), free from fear and terror (Apetabheravo) indeed (hi),* *Tamed (Danto), he is completely unbound (Parinibbuto), centering life internally on the soul-spirit (attā) (Ṭhitatto).*

**Commentary**: The Kinetic, Uncompromising Force of the Noble Path

The army of Death (*Māra*) controls beings only as long as they are attached to the worldly (*loka*). By becoming **danto** (tamed through internal discipline and concentration), one is like a mighty flood (*mahogho*), **Death\'s army is flimsy like dried reeds.** It relies entirely on the delusion (*moha*) of the unpurified mind. The moment the disciple centers life internally on the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*), Death loses its hold completely.

**The practitioner is the mighty flood.** The momentum of the noble path---fueled by **Appamattā** (zeal) and **Samādhi**---sweeps over the weak structures of *saṃsāra* without being stopped.

Having stopped the arising of future becoming, the Arahant is **parinibbuto** (completely unbound from the world) and **ṭhitatto**---living centered firmly and permanently on the true self, the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

***Apānudī**:* *Apa-* (away) + *nud* (to push, strike, drive off, dispel) -\> *pushed away, drove off, destroyed.*

**Maccurājassa:** *Maccu* (death / King Māra) + *rāja* (king) in the genitive case -\> *of the King of Death.*

**Senaṁ:** *Army, host, forces* (accusative singular). The army of Death consists of the defilements (*kilesa*): craving, aversion, delusion, fear, and attachment to the worldly (*loka*).

**Mahogho (Maha + ogha):** *Maha* (great/mighty) + *ogha* (flood, torrent) -\> *a mighty, overwhelming flood. Metaphor:* Just as a massive flash flood effortlessly smashes and sweeps away a flimsy bridge made of dried reeds, the practitioner\'s power of wisdom and concentration completely obliterates the forces of Māra/Death.

**Vijitāvī:** *Vi-* (intense) + *ji* (to conquer) -\> *a conqueror over all worldly attachments and defilements.*

**Apetabheravo:** *Apeta* (departed, eradicated) + *bherava* (terror, fear, dread) -\> *one from whom all fear and terror have completely departed.* Fear (*dosa*) vanishes because the mind no longer clings to external worldly phenomena that are subject to decay and death.

**Danto:** Past participle of *dam -\>* *tamed, subdued, disciplined through internal restraint.*

**Parinibbuto:** *Pari-* (completely) + **nibbuto** -\> *completely unbound from the world, stopping the arising and becoming of worldly birth.*

**Ṭhitatto (Ṭhita + Attā/Ātmā):** One who centers life not on external worldly phenomena, but internally on the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

- *Ṭhita:* Established, firmly rooted, no longer swayed.

- *Attā / Ātmā:* Soul-spirit.

#### 1.8. Vīra

**Yo duddamiyo damena danto, Vīro santusito vitiṇṇakaṅkho;\
Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Vīro so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.**

*He who (yo) was difficult to tame (duddamiyo), disciplined (**danto**) through self-control (**damena**); A hero (**vīro**), fully content (**santusito**), having crossed over doubt (**vitiṇṇa-kaṅkho**); A conqueror (**vijitāvī**), **fearless** (apetalomahaṁso); That (so) hero (vīro) \[is\] completely unbound (**parinibbuto**), centering life internally on the soul-spirit (**Attā / Ātmā**) (ṭhitatto).*

#### 1.9. Pilindavaccha

**Svāgataṁ na durāgataṁ, Nayidaṁ dumantitaṁ mama;\
Saṁvibhattesu dhammesu, Yaṁ seṭṭhaṁ tadupāgamin ti.**

*Directed to wholesomeness (svāgataṁ), not (na) directed to unwholesomeness (durāgataṁ); This (idaṁ) is my way (mama) of no (na) unwholesome deliberation (dumantitaṁ), among the clearly divided (saṁvibhattesu) mental states (dhammesu); That (yaṁ) is the highest (seṭṭhaṁ), to that (taṁ) I have attained (upāgamiṁ).*

**Commentary**: **The** **Noble Dualism (*Saṁvibhattesu dhammesu*)**

All human mental states or mental phenomena (***dhammesu***) are clearly divided by (***saṁvibhattesu***) wholesomeness (**svāgataṁ**) and unwholesomeness (**durāgataṁ**).

The term *saṁvibhattesu* (\"well-divided\" or \"clearly distinguished\") highlights the fundamental necessity of ethical dualism. Gautama's teaching does not blur *kusala* (wholesome) and *akusala* (unwholesome) into a singular non-dual matrix.

Liberation requires actively discerning, developing, and perfecting *kusala* while systematically abandoning and destroying *akusala* through the discipline of Sīla (ethical alignment), Concentration (Samādhi), and Paññā (wisdom). This rigid dualistic structure is the structural engine that elevates human existence and provides a clear exit from saṃsāra.

Gautama's **Arahanism** uses **The** **Noble Dualism,** leads the soul-spirit, **ātmā**, to **permanent life in the Deathless Realm**. **Non-Dualism** leads the soul-spirit to **nothingness**---the ultimate **death** of **ātmā**.

Arahanism relies on an uncompromising ethical framework we earth humans inherited as values from our manussa (mankind) ancestral gods. Where non-dualism seeks to collapse dualities---leading to the ultimate dissolution of the soul-spirit and the death of *ātmā* into the source of universe---Arahanism maintains the sharp distinction between wholesome and unwholesome to achieve actual transcendence rather than cosmic absorption.

**Svāgata**: Directed to virtue, wholesomeness. Svā/Su-: wholesomeness and virtuousness + āgata (arrived, directed to). Refers to taking up Brahmacariya and Brahmavihara as a deliberate move toward total purity.

*Svāgataṁ* carries the underlying principle of *sugato*---meaning \"Directed to Wholesomeness and Virtuousness.\" This arrival is not a drift into non-dualism or unwholesome paths (*durāgataṁ*); it is a deliberate, uncompromising turn toward *kusala* (wholesome). The teaching always leads people directly to wholesomeness and virtuousness, never to transcend the dualism or the unwholesome (*akusala*).

#### 1.10. Puṇṇamāsa (1st)

**Vihari apekkhaṁ idha vā huraṁ vā, Yo vedagū samito yatatto;\
Sabbesu dhammesu anūpalitto, Lokassa jaññā udayabbayañcā ti.**

*Dwelling (vihari) \[free of / having set aside\] expectation (apekkhaṁ) here (idha, this life) or (vā) in the hereafter (huraṁ vā, next life); who (yo) is a master of Veda (vedagū), calmed (samito), self-controlled (yatatto); completely unstained / unpolluted (anūpalitto) among all mental phenomena (sabbesu dhammesu), one directly understand (jaññā) the arising and passing away (udayabbayañcā ti) of the world (lokassa).*

**Commentary: Transcend the World**

1). The Nature of the World (*Loka*) and the Universe

In this cosmology, the universe is a vast, recycling cosmos comprised of billions of worlds (*loka*). Every single multidimensional world---from the lowest physical to the highest celestial planes---is bound by the exact same dynamic:

**Rising and Falling (*Udayabbaya*):** Worlds come into manifestation, endure for an eon, collapse into destruction, and re-emerge.

**The Inevitability of Death:** Because the universal laws of entropy with decay and death govern every *loka*, anything born or formed within them is subject to decay and death. To remain anywhere inside the world system---even in high heavenly realms---guarantees eventual demise and involuntary reincarnation.

**2). The Profile and Choice of the *Vedagū:*** The *vedagū* (the ultimate knower, the master of Veda), a title Gautama Buddha shared, is one who has fully penetrated this reality:

**Direct Comprehension:** They do not merely observe impermanence; they realize that no worldly thing (*dhamma*) offers permanent refuge.

**Abandoning Expectation (*Apekkhaṁ*):** Recognizing that both present existence (*idha*) and future heavenly rebirths (*huraṁ*) are subject to the same decay, they abandon all lingering or dependency on any worldly outcome.

**Transcendence over Absorption:** Rather than seeking to dissolve the soul-spirit into the cosmos, the *vedagū* chooses to break attachment to the world and transcend to the entire universe.

**3). Total Exit from the World System**

Because the worlds themselves rise and fall, true freedom cannot be found *within* the world. Liberation requires an absolute exit:

It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, that we can escape our worldly bondage and gain true liberation from the world. The ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna requires leaving the entire world behind.

***Vihari: literally means \"dwelled,\" \"lived,\" or \"abode.\"***

- **Vihāra** (noun): Abode, dwelling, living, or state of being.

- **Viharati** (verb): Dwells, lives, abides.

- **Vihari** (verb): **It is simply the past tense (aorist) of *viharati*.**

**Vihari apekkhaṁ idha vā huraṁ vā:** When translated strictly according to Pali syntax without bending words:

- *Vihari* = \[He\] dwelled / lived

- *apekkhaṁ* = expectation / desire / longing

- *idha vā huraṁ vā* = here or in the hereafter

**\"Dwelling \[without\] expectation here or hereafter\"** or **\"Having lived \[free from\] expectation here or hereafter.\"**

In classical Pali verse, words are frequently compressed. Translators sometimes read *vihari apekkhaṁ* as *viharitvā apekkhaṁ pahāya* (\"having lived having abandoned expectation\"), or they treat *vihari* as describing the state of the *vedagū* who *dwells* having set aside expectations from reincarnation.

**vedagū** (*veda* + *gū*): Master of Veda

- *veda* (noun): higher knowledge, ultimate knowledge, sacred wisdom

- *gū* (root *gam*): one who has gone to the end, master.

**samito** (past passive participle of *sameti*): calmed (samito), quieted, stilled.

**yatatto** (*yata* + *attā*): Self-controlled / subdued in conduct (yatatto).

- *yata* (past passive participle of *yamati*): restrained, controlled, subdued

- *attā* (noun): self-restrained, self-controlled (yatatto).

**anūpalitto** (*an-* + *upa* + *limpati*): Unstained, completely unpolluted by worldly phenomena.

- *an-* (negative prefix): un-, not

- *upalitto* (past passive participle): smeared, stained, clinging to, entangled

**jaññā** (3rd person singular optative of *jānāti*): let him know, one should directly comprehend/understand.

**udayabbayañcā** (*udaya* + *vbaya* + *ca*): Arising and passing away

- *udaya*: arising, emergence, birth

- *vbaya* (*vaya*): passing away, dissolution, decay, death

#### 1.11. Cūḷavaccha

**Pāmojjabahulo bhikkhu, dhamme buddhappavedite;\
Adhigacche padaṁ santaṁ, saṅkhārūpasamaṁ sukhan ti.**

*A bhikkhu abundant in joy (pāmojjabahulo), in the Dhamma (dhamme) well-taught by the Buddha (buddhappavedite); can attain (adhigacche) peaceful footing/foundation (padaṁ santaṁ), the complete pacification (upasama) of worldly mental formations & actions (Saṅkhāra), bliss (sukhan).*

**Commentary:** Bhikkhu Life

**Pāmojjabahulo** (*pāmojja* + *bahula*): Abundant in spiritual joy

- *pāmojja*: gladness, joy, spiritual delight, wholesome happiness arising from moral purity

- *bahula*: full of, abundant in, devoted to

*Pāmojja* (Sanskrit: *prāmodya* / *prāmodya*) is built from three distinct linguistic layers:

- **Pra-** (prefix): forward, outward, highly intensive, forth.

- **Mud** (root *mud*): to be glad, to rejoice, to experience delight.

- **-ya / -ja** (nominalizing suffix): state or quality of.

Etymologically, *pāmojja* is not a passive or quiet contentment. It is an **active, surging forth of joy**---a \"welling up\" of clean, unpolluted delight.

Unlike *taṇhā* (craving) or *kāmacchanda* (sensual desire), which are worldly and driven by a sense of lack and seek fuel outside the mind, *pāmojja* is an **internally generated, non-sensory exhilaration**. In the Pali suttas, *pāmojja* serves a critical operational role in the progression toward Concentration (Samādhi):

Sīla (Purity) -\> Pāmojja (Joy) -\> Pīti (Rapture) -\> Passaddhi (Tranquility) -\> Sukha (Bliss) -\> Samādhi

Without *pāmojja*, the mind remains heavy, sluggish, or conflicted. *Pāmojja* provides the initial spiritual momentum that uplifts the *citta* out of worldly heaviness.

**Vedic & Pre-Pali Contexts**

In the Vedic corpus (*Ṛgveda*, *Atharvaveda*) and early *Upaniṣads*, the root *mud* and its derivatives (*muda*, *modana*, *pramoda, mudita*) carry distinct ritual and cosmological rigor:

- **Vedic Ritual & Divine Exultation (*Ṛgveda*)**:

In Vedic hymns, forms of *pramoda* describe the exultation of gods (such as Indra or Soma) and the joyful relief of sacrificers when an obstacle is overcome or light is restored. It represents a state of victory, triumph over darkness (*tamas*), and unhindered vitality.

- **Upaniṣadic Joy (*Taittirīya Upaniṣad*)**:

In the *Taittirīya Upaniṣad* (2.5), in the description of the *ānandamaya-kośa* (the sheath composed of bliss), *pramoda* is identified as one of its structural attributes: *\"Joy (priya) is its head, delight (moda) is its right wing, great delight (pramoda) is its left wing, bliss (ānanda) is its trunk.\"*

Here, *pramoda* denotes an elevated, transcendent joy that rises above raw physical pleasure, marking a shift toward subtle spiritual states.

***Pāmojja* in the Training Path**

Vedic traditions often viewed this joy as an expression of cosmic or divine power, Arahanism assigns *pāmojja* a precise, functional role within ethical dualism:

- **Wholesome Fuel vs. Unwholesome Attraction**:

The path of liberation cannot be sustained by sheer willpower alone. *Pāmojja* as inner spiritual joy arising from the mind-heart *citta* acts as the noble, wholesome (*kusala*) alternative to worldly pleasure. When a practitioner abandons *akusala* (unwholesome) habits through Sīla (ethical alignment), the immediate result is a clean conscience---free from guilt, remorse, and fear. This purity triggers *pāmojja*.

- **The Engine of Disenchantment**:

*Pāmojja* energizes the mind, making it capable of deep Concentration (Samādhi) and Paññā (wisdom). By leveraging the pure delight of *pāmojja* from within, the practitioner moves smoothly toward *nibbida*---disenchanted from the world.

It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of worldly bondage in the form of rāga, dosa, and moha, that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation and gain true liberation from the world. The ancient path to of liberation from the world into the deathless realm of Nibbāna uses this noble joy as a stepping stone to transcend the world entirely.

**Pāmojja** (spiritual joy/gladness) belongs to the **path of training (sekha)** rather than the final, fixed state of the Arahant. In the suttas, *pāmojja* is an intermediate mental phenomenon (dhamma). It is the initial spark of joy that arises when a practicing disciple (*sekha*) sees that they have abandoned defilements or fulfilled Sīla (ethical alignment). Its purpose is to energize the mind and overcome worldly sluggishness, discontent, or remorse so the practitioner can enter deep Concentration (Samādhi).

**What Happens to Pāmojja in an Arahant?**

An Arahant has fully completed the training work. Because the root causes of worldly agitation---rāga, dosa, and moha---have been completely eradicated, the Arahant no longer relies on the surging, uplifting momentum of *pāmojja* to push through practice.

- **Traded for Unshakable Peace (*Santi*):** Coarser or surging forms of joy (*pāmojja* and *pīti*) are stilled. In the higher stages of jhāna and in full liberation, excitement subsides into serene transcendental release (*upekkhā*), deep stillness (*passaddhi*), and peace (*santi*).

- **Functional Consciousness (*Kiriyā Citta*):** While an Arahant still living in a physical body can experience pleasant feeling (*sukha vedanā*) or joyful states during meditation (such as the jhānas), these mental events are purely inoperative/functional (*kiriyā*), completely free from any underlying expectation (*apekkhaṁ*) or worldly attachment.

**Pada** (root *pad*, to step or go) means foot, step, footing, foundation, or permanent realm/state. It denotes a firm standing ground upon which one stabilizes oneself.

**Santa** (present participle of *sam*, to be stilled or extinguished) means peaceful, stilled, quieted, and purified from heat/fever.

**saṅkhārūpasamaṁ** (*saṅkhāra* + *upasama* + *-aṁ*): mental and physical activities shifts from the worldly (complete pacification, not karmic) to the world-transcendened peace and bliss.

- *saṅkhāra*: worldly mental formations/fabrication and actions.

> **The Internal Engine: Mental Fabrication**

- **Intentional Shaping (*Cetanā*):** The mind takes raw perception (*saññā*) or feeling (*vedanā*) and actively constructs thoughts, desires, plans, and projections.

- **Mental Actions (*Cittasaṅkhāra* & *Vacīsaṅkhāra*):** Directed thought (*vitakka*) and evaluation (*vicāra*) that formulate ideas internally before they are spoken or acted upon.

> **The External Engine: Worldly Action & Karma**

- **Impulse to Act (*Kamma-cetanā*):** The mental fabrication doesn\'t stay dormant; it drives expression through physical body (*kāyasaṅkhāra*) and speech (*vacīsaṅkhāra*).

- **Building Future Existence:** Every volitional act---whether wholesome (*kusala*) or unwholesome (*akusala*)---constructs karmic seeds in the mind-heart.

<!-- -->

- *upasama*: complete pacification, calm, still

<!-- -->

- **Upa-** (prefix): close to, towards, completely, thoroughly.

- **Sam-** (root *śam*): to calm, pacify, extinguish, cease.

> Etymologically, *upasama* is the **thorough, total pacification or quenching** of worldly fires. In Vedic usage (*śānti*, *upaśama*), the root *śam* was used for extinguishing ritual or destructive fires. In Pali, *saṅkhārūpasama* specifically means the absolute quenching and stilling of all worldly activities (*saṅkhāra*).

- **Vedic Context (*Upaniṣads*)**: In the *Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad* (7), the unconditioned state is described as *śāntam śivam advaitam* (\"peaceful, auspicious, non-dual\"). They mistaken *Brahman* as the all-pervading cosmic origin.

> **Advaitam** (*a-* + *dvaita*) literally means **\"not-two\"**, the **complete absence of subject-object division**. It signifies that the perceived split between subject and object, self and world, observer and observed, is an illusion born of conditioned fabrication (*saṅkhāra*). All dualistic pairs of opposites (*dvandva*)---including wholesome (*kusala*) vs. unwholesome (*akusala*), good vs. bad, and subject vs. object---belong strictly to the conditioned, relative world (*loka*).

- **The Worldly Level (*Dvaita*):** Within the realm of cause and effect, the distinction between wholesome and unwholesome is essential. Wholesome actions build positive momentum, while unwholesome actions generate *dukkha*.

- **The Ultimate Level (*Advaita*):** In *Advaitam*, there is no actor to perform a deed, no deed to be performed, and no result to receive. Both \"wholesome\" and \"unwholesome\" are conditioned constructs (*saṅkhāra*). To reach ultimate transcendence, even the raft of the wholesome must eventually be set aside.

> **Vedāntic Absorption vs. Arahanism Transcendence**
>
> While *Advaita Vedānta* interprets *advaitam* as merging the individual self (*Jīva*) into a single, non-dual cosmic Self (*Brahman/Atman*), Arahanism's view is Noble Dualism and transcending the cosmos:

- **Vedānta:** Unification with a universal, all-pervading cosmic reality.

- **Arahanism:** The total disenchantment with and transcendence of all worldly phenomena (*dhammesu*). The *citta* disenchant and transcend the cosmos altogether.

<!-- -->

- **Pali Context (Arahanism):** *padaṁ santaṁ* and *upasama*

> In the *Dhammacakkappavattāna Sutta*, the Path leads directly to *upasamāya* (for complete pacification), to *abhiññāya* (for direct higher knowledge), to *sambodhāya* (for right awakening), and to *nibbānāya* (for total liberation from the worldly bondage).
>
> The sequence in the *Dhammacakkappavattāna Sutta* outlines **6 specific fruit-stages** (yielding results) because it mirrors the exact operational architecture required to transform a trapped human consciousness (*citta*) into an Arahant capable of exiting the world.

1.  **Cakkhukaraṇī** (*cakkhu* + *karaṇī*): Eye-making, giving visual clarity, producing spiritual vision. Opens the \"eye of Dhamma\" (*dhammacakkhu*), shifting the practitioner from worldly delusion (*moha*) to seeing things as they actually are.

2.  **Ñāṇakaraṇī** (*ñāṇa* + *karaṇī*): Knowledge-making, generating direct operational understanding. Converts mere belief or intellectual speculation into direct, experiential knowledge (*ñāṇa*) of the Four Noble Truths.

3.  **Upasamāya** (*upa-* + *sam-* + *-āya*): For complete pacification, for total stilling. The thorough quenching and pacification of the worldly bondage, the burning fevers of *rāga* (worldly craving), *dosa* (fear/anger), and *moha* (delusion). It brings about the complete pacification of *saṅkhāra,* mental fabrication and physical activities in the world (*saṅkhārūpasamaṁ*).

4.  **Abhiññāya** (*abhi-* + *ñā* + *-āya*): For direct higher knowledge, for supreme intuitive comprehension. Penetrating beyond surface-level worldly appearances (*loka*) to directly comprehend the fundamental laws of the world/universe.

5.  **Sambodhāya** (*sam-* + *bodhi* + *-āya*): For full awakening, for complete enlightenment. The unshakeable awakening of the *citta* where all blindness is shattered, culminating in the perfection of Paññā (wisdom).

6.  **Nibbānā** (*ni-* + *vāna*): For total liberation, for unbinding, from the world. The ultimate cessation of the cycle of involuntary reincarnation; stepping out of the dynamic machinery of the world entirely.

**Sukha (Sukhaṁ / Sukhan)**, rooted in *su-* (good, thorough, wholesome) and *-kha* (axle-hole, space, opening), literally means **\"smooth-running,\" \"unobstructed space,\"** or **\"bliss.\"** It stands in direct opposition to *dukkha* (bad, unsatisfactoriness). In both the Pali Canon and the Vedic literature, *sukha* operates at multiple levels---serving as both a vital engine **on the training path (*sekha*)** and an ultimate reality experienced **by the Arahant (*asekha*)**.

**(1). In the Pali Canon: Path Engine & Final Realization**

The Pali Canon distinguishes between three functional levels of *sukha*:

- **Sensory/Worldly Bliss (*Kāmasukha*):** Coarse, dependent on sensory inputs, and bound to decay (*dukkha*).

- **Path-Bliss (*Samādhisukha / Vivekasukha*) --- The Training Path (*Sekha*):** Non-sensory bliss born of seclusion from unwholesome states (*akusala*). This is an active, functional engine on the path. In the sequential progression toward Concentration (Samādhi), *sukha* is the essential prerequisite that stills mental restlessness: Passaddhi (Tranquility) -\> Sukha (Bliss) -\> Samādhi (Concentration). In the First, Second, and Third Jhānas, *sukha* is experienced as a refined spiritual feeling (*vedanā*). In the Third Jhāna, the practitioner abides \"happily\" (*sukhaṁ viharati*).

- **The Unconditioned Bliss of Nibbāna --- The Arahant (*Asekha*):**

> In the Fourth Jhāna and upon final liberation, refined feeling-bliss (*sukha-vedanā*) is transcended and stilled into transcendental release (*upekkhā*). However, the Arahant still experiences **Nibbāna-sukha**. When asked how Nibbāna can be called \"bliss\" (*sukha*) if there is no sensory feeling (*vedanā*) present, Venerable Sāriputta famously answered: *\"Precisely this, friend, is the bliss here---that herein there is nothing felt\"* (*Etad eva khv\' ettha, āvuso, sukhaṁ yad ettha avedayitaṁ. Anguttara Nikāya 9.34 (Navaka Sutta)*).
>
> The true bliss (*sukha*) of the Arahant is not an emotional high or a world-sustaining cosmic feeling; it is the absolute peace of a mind that has extinguished all worldly karmic fuel. It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of worldly bondage as rāga, dosa, and moha, that we can escape our involuntary worldly reincarnation and gain true liberation from the world. The ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna uses the refined joy of practice to arrive at this ultimate peace.
>
> Nibbāna-dhatu is *paramaṁ sukhaṁ* because it is the absolute end of aging and death, end of all worldly friction.

#### 1.12. Mahāvaccha

**Paññābalī sīlavatūpapanno, Samāhito jhānarato satīmā;\
Yadatthiyaṁ bhojanaṁ bhuñjamāno, Kaṅkhetha kālaṁ idha vītarāgo ti.**

*Endowed with the power of **paññā** (paññā-balī) and equipped with **sīla** (sīla-vatūpapanno), grounded in **Samādhi** (samāhito), delighted to **jhāna** (jhānarato), **mindful** (satīmā); eating **food** (bhojanaṁ bhuñjamāno) merely for its intended purpose (yadatthiyaṁ), one who is devoid of **attachment** (vītarāgo) should await his **time** (kaṅkhetha kālaṁ) here.*

**Commentary**: Bhikkhu Life

**Paññābalī** (*paññā* + *balī*): Possessed of the power of wisdom.

**sīlavatūpapanno** (*sīlavant* + *upapanna*): Endowed with, equipped with, or fully possessed of Sīla (ethical conduct+++).

**Samāhito** (nominative singular of *samāhita*): Concentrated, stilled, grounded in Samādhi.

**jhānarato** (*jhāna* + *rata*): Devoted to, delighting in, or constantly absorbed in jhāna.

**satīmā** (nominative singular of *satimant*): Mindful, endowed with unshakeable presence/awareness of mind.

**Bhojanaṁ bhuñjamāno yadatthiyaṁ**: eating food merely for its intended purpose: to sustain the physical body until its natural end. There is no craving (*taṇhā*) for flavor, no attachment to nourishment, and no karmic construction (*saṅkhāra*). Physical intake of food is reduced to a purely functional operation (*kiriyā*).

**vītarāgo** (*vīta* + *rāga*): Devoid of rāga, one who has completely cleared away lust and worldly attachment.

**Kaṅkhetha** (3rd person singular optative active of *kaṅkhati*) **kālaṁ** (*kāla* + *-aṁ*): Awaiting the Final Time. Should await, wait for, expect (without agitation or anxiety). Time (specifically referring to the time of death / physical dissolution). Having completely eradicated *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the practitioner neither longs for life nor rushes toward death. They dwell in this world (*idha*) as a completely detached observer.

**The Progressive Arc of Sīla: From Ethical Living to Total Exit**

Sīla at the 5 precepts level is ethical conduct. The 5 precepts are for humans to be ethical, to lead a wholesome life as an ethical lay person.

The 8 precepts mark the beginning of a yogi, a practitioner. The crucial distinction that separates the 8 precepts from the 5 precepts is the shift from mere sexual restraint (avoiding misconduct) to **complete celibacy** (*abrahmacariyā veramaṇī*). By cutting off sexual activity entirely, the practitioner cuts off the fundamental fuel of the sex realm (*kāma-dhātu*). Complete celibacy allows human beings to transcend their species limits---moulding and reshaping a sex realm being into a *rūpa* realm (*rūpa-dhātu*) being.

Another crucial distinction that separates the 8 precepts from the 5 precepts is that they **purposely reduce the sources of happiness derived from the external world**. By intentionally stripping away sensory entertainment, cosmetic adornment, high beds/ sleep comfort, and late-day eating / happy feelings derived form food, the 8 precepts starve the mind\'s addiction to external stimulation. This operational deprivation forces the yogi practitioner to stop seeking happiness outside and forces them to seek inner joy (*pāmojja* / *pīti* / *sukha*) from within through Samādhi and spiritual training.

When an ethical lay person starts to observe the Uposatha 8 precepts, they become a part-time yogi practitioner, observing them about once a week (on Uposatha day). When time permits and commitment deepens, the lay person starts to observe the 8 precepts every day; this makes the lay person a full-time yogi practitioner.

The 10 precepts mark the beginning of a renunciate, a *pabbajjā*. This stage forces a total severance from all worldly economic transactions, worldly wealth, and financial security. By strictly prohibiting the handling, accepting, or possessing of money, gold, and silver (*jātarūpa-rajata-paṭiggahaṇā*), the renunciate steps completely outside the economic engine of the world, relying solely on alms for survival of worldly life, forcing the yogi practitioner to foster a rich spiritual life.

The bhikkhu and bhikkhunī take full Sīla precepts (Pātimokkha), completely renounce the world, and thus become not just professional renunciates---but also Gautama's true children. As Gautama said to his bhikkhu disciples:

*\"You are my breast-born sons, born from my mouth, born of my Dhamma, formed by my Dhamma, heirs to my Dhamma, not heirs to material things.\"*

The ultimate goal of this entire Sīla training is to become an Arahant---a world-transcended being who no longer belongs to this world and is no longer subject to the universal laws of decay, aging, and death. This complete transcendence fulfills Gautama's original motive when he abandoned his palace life as a prince: to discover an unshakeable way to defeat aging and death once and for all.

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Stage of Training**   **Rule Set**           **Identity & Mode**   **Operational Purpose**
  ----------------------- ---------------------- --------------------- -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Human Ethics**        5 Precepts             Ethical Lay Person    Establishes basic wholesome living (*kusala*), restrains harmful physical/verbal actions, prevents rebirth in lower realms.

  **Part-Time Yogi**      8 Precepts- uposatha   Part-Time yogi        Enforces **complete celibacy** and **purposely cuts external sources of pleasure**, forcing the practitioner to cultivate inner joy from within; observed periodically.

  **Full-Time Yogi**      8 Precepts (Daily)     Full-Time yogi        Permanently cuts off sexual fuel, external sensory entertainment, and bodily luxuries in daily lay life, stabilizing the mind for Samādhi.

  **Novice**              10 Precepts            Pabbajjā -Sāmaṇera    **Renounces money, gold, silver, and economic transactions**; totally severs ties with worldly wealth and financial survival.

  Bhikkhu/ -nī            Pāti-mokkha            Bhikkhu / Bhikkhunī   Takes full Sīla precepts, completely renounces the world to become Gautama\'s true child; targets total conquest over aging and death to become an Arahant.
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Sīla is not a static set of social rules to make the world a better home; it is the fundamental launchpad for severance from the worldly. Worldly phenomena (*dhammesu*) and worldly formations and fabrications (*saṅkhāra*) belong to this world which contains the laws decay and death, therefore they are *dukkha*.

By stepping up from the 5 precepts to the 8, 10, and full Bhikkhu / Bhikkhunī Pāti-mokkha, the practitioner systematically starves *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (fear and anger), and *moha* (ignorance and delusion) of their worldly fuel. Sīla cleanses the mind so that Samādhi and Paññā (wisdom) can perform their ultimate work: conquering the laws of death.

It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation and gain true liberation from the world. The ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna relies on this progressive Sīla to turn the mind away from the universe.

#### 1.13. Vanavaccha (1st)

**Nīlabbhavaṇṇā rucirā, sītavārī sucindharā;\
Indagopakasañchannā, te selā ramayanti man ti.**

*Having the color of dark-blue clouds (nīlabbhavaṇṇā), beautiful/radiant (rucirā), flowing with cool waters (sītavārī), extremely clean/pure (sucindharā); Covered over / blanketed (sañchanna) with Indagopaka insects \[the vivid, bright-red scarlet velvet mites or ladybug-like insects that emerge on rocks after rain in ancient India) (indagopaka-sañchannā), on those rocky mountains (te selā), delight my mind (ramayanti maṁ).*

**Commentary**: Bhikkhu Life

A solitary *bhikkhu* dwelling in wild, secluded mountain habitats (*arañña*), far removed from human settlements and worldly affairs.

- **Seclusion as the Engine for Samādhi**: The praise of wild, dark-blue rocky mountains (*te selā*) is not romantic nature poetry or worldly aesthetic appreciation. It reflects the operational environment necessary for the practitioner. Secluded wilderness removes the friction of human society, starves the senses of worldly noise from human interactions, and provides the still, undisturbed foundation for **Samādhi** and **jhāna** practice.

- **Pristine Pure Environment (*Sucindharā / Sītavārī*)**: The cool waters (*sītavārī*) and immaculate purity (*sucindharā*) mirror the stilled state of a mind cleansed through progressive **Sīla**. Just as the mountain crags are unpolluted by human commerce and strife, the mind of the yogi practitioner becomes unpolluted by worldly craving and attachment (*rāga*), fear and anger (*dosa*), and ignorance and delusion (*moha*).

- **Mind-Delight without Worldly Clinging (*Ramayanti maṁ*)**: The delight (*ramayanti*) felt by the forest-dwelling *bhikkhu* in nature is born of seclusion (*vivekasukha* / *pāmojja*). It is an internal, wholesome joy that replaces the need for external, worldly entertainment.

#### 1.14. The Novice Sivaka

**Upajjhāyo maṁ avaca, ito gacchāma sīvaka;\
Gāme me vasati kāyo, araññaṁ me gato mano;\
Semānakopi gacchāmi, natthi saṅgo vijānatan ti.**

*My preceptor (upajjhāyo) said to me (maṁ avaca): \'Let us go from here (ito gacchāma), Sīvaka (sīvaka).\' My body (me kāyo) dwells in the village (gāme vasati), \[but\] my mind-will (me mano) has gone to the secluded place (araññaṁ gato); even while lying down (semānakopi), I go (gacchāmi); there is no attachment/ bind together (natthi saṅgo) for those who truly know (vijānatan).*

**Commentary**: **Mind-Body Severance, Seclusion and Sticky Clinging**

Complete mental severance from the worldly environment: even if his physical body (*kāyo*) must temporarily move through a village (*gāme*), his mind (*mano*) remains entirely rooted in the secluded place (*araññaṁ*), untouched by worldly engagement.

**Mind-Body Severance (*Kāyo vs. Mano*)**: The verse highlights the fundamental operational distinction between physical presence and mental inclination. While the physical body (*kāyo*) may be constrained by physical surroundings, the mind (*mano*) of a trained practitioner has already abandoned interest in worldly engagement.

**Absence of Worldly Sticky Clinging (*Natthi saṅgo*)**: For one who possesses direct penetration and higher wisdom (*vijānataṁ*), there is no internal attachment (*saṅgo*) to worldly places, social conditions, or human environments. The worldly fuel has been cut off.

**Continuous Internal Movement (*Semānakopi gacchāmi*)**: Even during physical rest or sleep (*semānakopi*), the internal trajectory of the purified mind remains firmly directed toward total liberation from the world and severance from worldly attachment.

**Araññaṁ** (accusative/locative singular of *arañña*): To the forest, wilderness, secluded wild habitat.

**Saṅgo** (nominative singular of *saṅga*): Attachment, stickiness, sticky clinging.

**1).** ***Arañña***

In classical Pāḷi and Sanskrit etymology, *arañña* / *araṇya* is built on the root ***ṛ* / *ar*** (to go, to reach, to move toward the distant or alien) combined with the primary nominal suffix ***-anya*** (*-aṇya*).

- **Derivation:** *ṛ* + *anya* \$\\rightarrow\$ *ara-ṇya* (literally: \"that which is distant,\" \"that which lies outside,\" or \"that which is foreign/other\").

- **Sanskrit Parallel:** *Araṇya* (अरण्य), related to the Vedic stem *ara* / *araṇa* (अरण), meaning "alien," \"foreign,\" \"distant,\" \"strange.\"

**Vedic & Pre-Pāḷi Usages:**

In the *Ṛgveda*, *araṇya* does not merely signify a collection of trees (which is *vrikṣa* or *vana*); it denotes the vast, uncultivated, non-domesticated realm lying beyond the boundaries of the human settlement (*grāma*).

**The Division between *Grāma* and *Araṇya*:** In Vedic texts like the *Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa* and the *Taittirīya Saṃhitā*, human reality is strictly partitioned into two domains:

**Grāma:** The inhabited, domesticated village---the domain of social obligation, worldly commerce, and social ties.

**Araṇya:** The wilderness outside---the uncultivated, undomesticated zone free from social duty, human laws, and group obligations.

**Vedic Hymn to Araṇyānī (*Ṛgveda* 10.146):** Personified as the goddess of the forest/wilderness (*Araṇyānī*), the hymn describes *araṇya* as a space that is naturally quiet, solitary, self-sustaining, and free from human cultivation. The text explicitly states that she does not kill unless a beast attack s, and she feeds without human plowing (*akṛṣīvalā*): *araṇyāny araṇyāny asau yā parāceva naśyasi \|* *kathā grāmaṁ na pṛcchasi na tvā bhīriva vindatī3 \|\| vṛṣāravo yad vadaty upacciccīkaravati \| āghāṭibhir iva dhāvayann araṇyānir mahīyate \|\| uta gāva ivādanty uta veśmeva dṛśyate \| uto araṇyāniḥ sāyaṁ śakaṭair iva sarjati \|\|*

> *O Araṇyānī, Araṇyānī, you who seem to disappear yonder into the distance! Why do you not ask for the village (grāma)? Does fear not seize you? When the cricket (vṛṣārava) chirps and the ciccika bird responds, It is as if running with cymbals---Araṇyānī exults, sounding loud. It is as if cattle are grazing yonder, and it appears like a dwelling (veśman); And at evening, Araṇyānī seems to release the carts.*
>
> *gām aṅgaiṣa ā hvayati dārv aṅgaiṣa apāvadhīt \| vasann araṇyānyāṁ sāyaṁ akrūkṣad iti manyate \|\| na vā araṇyānir hanty anyaś cen nābhighaccati \| svādoḥ phalasya jagdhvā yathākāmaṁ pra śete \|\| āñjanagandhiṁ surabhiṁ bahvannām akṛṣīvalām \| prāhāṁ mṛgāṇāṁ mātaram araṇyānim aśaṁsiṣam \|\|*
>
> Here one calls out to his cow, there another cuts down timber; Dwelling in Araṇyānī at eventide, one fancies that someone has cried out. Araṇyānī does not kill, unless some predator approaches; Having eaten sweet fruit, one takes rest according to one\'s desire. I have praised Araṇyānī, sweet-scented, fragrant, full of food though uncultivated (*akṛṣīvalā*), The mother of wild beasts.
>
> **The Acoustic Illusion** When a person stays alone in the forest (*araṇyānī*) at dusk, the vast silence and unusual rustlings tricks their mind. Every crackling branch or distant bird call sounds like familiar human activity---a cowherd calling cattle (*gām ā hvayati*) or a woodcutter chopping timber (*dārv apāvadhīt*). The solitary practitioner imagines human presence where none exists. The solitude of the wilderness forces the mind to confront its own projections.
>
> **Rooted in Isolation (*Arana* = Remote/Foreign):** The Vedic term *araṇa* directly denotes \"foreign\" or \"not one\'s own kin.\" Thus, *araṇya* is intrinsically linked to **isolation, detachment, and being removed from human domestic collectives**.

**Pāḷi & Sutta Usage Context:**

In the Pāḷi Canon (e.g., *Majjhima Nikāya* and *Sutta Nipāta*), *arañña* denotes the solitary forest dwelling (*araññagato*) that provides physical isolation (*kāyaviveka*) necessary to starve sensory distractions.

**Sutta Nipāta 1.3: *Khaggavisāṇa Sutta* (The Rhinoceros Horn)**

*\"Having discarded the household paraphernalia, like a shed-cover tree with fallen leaves, having cut off all household ties, let one live alone like a rhinoceros horn.\"* (Sn 1.3, v. 44)

*\"Seeking the secluded forest dwelling (arañña), leaving behind the entanglement of the village, let one live alone like a rhinoceros horn.\"* (Sn 1.3. 53)

**MN 39: *Mahāassapura Sutta* (The Greater Discourse at Assapura)**

*\"He seeks out a secluded dwelling: a forest (arañña), the foot of a tree, a mountain, a glen, a hillside cave, a charnel ground, a jungle thicket, an open space, or a heap of straw. After his meal, returning from his almsround, he sits down, folding his legs crosswise, holding his body erect, and setting up mindfulness before him.\"* (MN 39)

**MN 121: *Cūḷasuññata Sutta* (The Lesser Discourse on Emptiness)**

How the forest dwelling strips away the layered, collective noise of human settlements to establish a focused mind. The bhikkhu leaves behind the perceptions of the village (*grāma*) and human beings (*manussa-saññā*). By attending solely to the single perception of the forest (*arañña-saññā*), the mind calms:

*\"A bhikkhu, not attending to the perception of village, not attending to the perception of people, attends to the singleness dependent on the perception of forest\... He understands: \'Whatever disturbances there might be dependent on the perception of village, those are not present here.\'\"*

**Significance:** Physical isolation (*kāyaviveka*) systematically empties the mind of complex social noise, leaving a quiet baseline from which higher absorptions are reached.

**2). *Saṅga***

*Saṅga* is derived directly from the Verbal Root ***sañj* / *sañj*** (to stick, adhere, cling, attach, or bind together).

- **Derivation:** Root *sañj* + nominal suffix *-a* \$\\rightarrow\$ *saṅga* (literally: \"stickiness,\" \"adhesion,\" \"attachment,\" or \"clinging\").

> *Sañjana* (attaching, tying, making stick).
>
> *Saṅgata* (joined, attached).
>
> *Sakta* (stuck, attached, glued to).

- ***Saṅga* carries a \"collective\" nuance in Veda**

In early Vedic usage and later texts like the *Bhagavad Gītā*, *saṅga* refers to association or fellowship with worldly society (***saṅga* = association with objects/people**), which creates internal mental attachment (*saṅgaḥ sañjāyate kāmāt*). However, the primary operational sense of *saṅga* in spiritual contexts remains **the internal adhesive force---the sticky mental tie or attachment to worldly phenomena**. When one associates with worldly groups or objects, *saṅga* (stickiness/attachment) is generated in the mind.

**Vedic & Śrīmad Bhagavad Gītā Parallel:**

- In the *Bhagavad Gītā* (2.62):

*dhyāyato viṣayān puṃsaḥ saṅgas teṣūpajāyate* \"Dwell on objects of the senses, and attachment (*saṅga*) to them is born.\"

- In the Pāḷi Canon, *saṅga* is listed alongside *granthas* (ties) and *saṃyojanas* (fetters). It is the sticky internal glue that binds the *citta* to worldly formations (*saṅkhāra*).

**Structural Comparison**

- **Arañña:** The physical and spatial severance---moving away from the domesticated, human-populated settlement (*grāma*) into the wild, uncultivated, and solitary remote space.

- **Saṅga:** The internal, mental glue---the sticky attachment, association, and craving that ties the mind to worldly phenomena (*dhammesu*).

The structural tension between **individual sovereignty** (*svārājya*) and **deity-centered surrender** (*prapatti* / devotion) goes to the very core of ancient yogi practitioners.

**(1). The Spatial Metaphor: *Grāma* vs. *Araṇya* as States of Being**

In the Vedic worldview, the boundary between the village (*grāma*) and the wilderness (*araṇya*) was not merely physical geography---it was an ontological divide:

- **The *Grāma* (Collective / Networked Reality):** The village is the realm of collective karma, social duty (*varṇāśrama*), ancestor rituals (*pitṛ-yajna*), mutual dependence, and social consensus. In modern psychological or esoteric terms, the *grāma* represents the collective mind---a shared network of obligations, social roles, and sensory dependencies.

- **The *Araṇya* (Sovereign / Unbound Domain):** Step into the *araṇya*, and the social identity dies. There are no social ranks, no ancestral fires to maintain, and no collective consensus to lean on. The practitioner (*yogi* / *jhāyī* / *muni*) steps out of the collective realm to face individual's soul-spirit existence directly.

**(2). *Sva-rājya* (Self-Sovereignty) in the Ancient Ascetic Paradigm**

The solitary practitioner operates as their own king and kingdom---this aligns precisely with the early Upaniṣadic concept of **Svārājya** (self-rule / absolute internal sovereignty).

- **The Upaniṣadic *Svarāṭ*:** In the *Chāndogya Upaniṣad* (7.25.2), the acomplished practitioner who transcends dependency on external phenomena and worldly collectivities is termed a **Svarāṭ** (one who is sovereign unto himself): sa vā eṣa evaṁ paśyann evaṁ manvāna evaṁ vijānann Ātmāratir Ātmākrīḍa Ātmāmithuna Ātmanandaḥ sa svarāḍ bhavati tasya sarveṣu lokeṣu kāmacāro bhavati \|\|

  *\"Indeed, he---this \[practitioner\]---thus seeing, thus thinking, thus directly knowing: possessing delight in the Self, possessing play in the Self, possessing companionship in the Self, possessing bliss in the Self---he becomes a **Svarāṭ** (a self-ruler / sovereign unto himself); for him, in all worlds, there is movement according to his own free desire*/ unrestricted freedom*.\"*

  **Ātmāratiḥ** = finding delight solely in the Self/ Soul-Spirit (*Ātmā*)

  **Ātmākrīḍaḥ** = (*Ātmā* + *krīḍā*) possessing play / sport in the Self/ Soul-Spirit

  **Ātmāmithunaḥ** = (*Ātmā* + *mithuna*) possessing union / pairing / companionship in the Self/ Soul-Spirit

  **Ātmanandaḥ** = (*Ātmā* + *ānanda*) possessing bliss / joy in the Self/ Soul-Spirit

  **svarāṭ** = (*sva* + *rāj*) a self-ruler / sovereign unto himself

  **kāmacāraḥ** = (*kāma* + *cāra*) movement according to one\'s own desire / unrestricted freedom

- **The Individual as the Supreme Realm:** In the early ascetic traditions (*Samaṇa* / *Yogi*), liberation (*mokṣa* or transcendence) is not achieved by being admitted as a subject into someone else\'s kingdom. It is realized by dismantling the internal sticky attachments (*saṅga*) that bind the mind to the external something, thereby establishing the individual\'s absolute independence (*kaivalya* / detachment). The practitioner does not bow to an external ruler of a kinfdom; they tame their own mind, tame their own internal faculties (*indriya*), become independent (*anissito*) to the external world.

**Individual Sovereignty vs. Monotheistic Surrender**

Two fundamentally different spiritual architectures:

  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Dimensional Aspect**   **Yogic Sovereignty (Svārājya)**                                               **Monotheistic Subjugation**
  ------------------------ ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ --------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Primary Domain**       *Araṇya* (The autonomous, solitary space)                                      The Heavenly kingdom / Domain of a Deity

  **Primary Mechanism**    Internal purification, mastery of *Citta*, eradication of *saṅga*              External surrender (*prapatti*), grace, allegiance

  **Locus of Authority**   individual mind becomes its own sovereign ruler (*Svarāṭ*).                    The Deity holds sole sovereignty; the individual is a subject, no sovereignty.

  **Ultimate Relation**    Total independence, stepping completely outside of external worldly systems.   Permanent residency within a divine jurisdiction under a monarchical God.
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

When the yogi steps out of the collective domain (*grāma*) into the solitary space (*araṇya*), the goal is true liberation, which requires pulling the mind out of all worldly collectives---whether human settlements or heavenly kingdoms. By burning away worldly attachments manifested as *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the practitioner sever all ties (*saṅga*) to the worldly matrix.

#### 1.15. Kuṇḍadhāna

**Pañca chinde pañca jahe, pañca cuttari bhāvaye;\
Pañcasaṅgātigo bhikkhu, oghatiṇṇoti vuccatī ti.**

*Cut off five (pañca chinde), abandon five (pañca jahe), cultivate five further (pañca c\'uttari bhāvaye); a bhikkhu who has crossed beyond the five sticky attachments (pañcasaṅgātigo bhikkhu) is said to have crossed over the flood (oghatiṇṇoti vuccatī ti).*

**Commentary**: Roadmap for *Bhikkhu*

A roadmap for the *bhikkhu* aiming for complete severance from the worldly matrix and total exit from *saṃsāra*.

- **The 5 to Cut Off (*Pañca chinde*)**: The five lower fetters (*oraṁbhāgiyāni saṁyojanāni*) that bind the *citta* to the sensual realm (*kāma-dhātu*):

> *Sakkāyaditthi* (True-body view / belief in body is real/true existence)
>
> *Vicikicchā* (Skeptical doubt)
>
> *Sīlabbataparāmāsa* (Clinging to external rites and social rituals)
>
> *Kāmacchanda* (Sensual craving / lust)
>
> *Byāpāda* (Ill-will / aversion)

- **The 5 to Abandon (*Pañca jahe*)**: The five higher fetters (*uddhambhāgiyāni saṁyojanāni*) that bind the *citta* to the higher *Rūpa* and *Arūpa* realms:

> *Rūparāga* (Attachment to the *rūpa* realm / *rūpa-dhātu*)
>
> *Arūparāga* (Attachment to the *arūpa* realm / *arūpa-dhātu*)
>
> *Māna* (Conceit / implicit measurement of self / soul-spirit *ātmā*)
>
> *Uddhacca* (Restlessness)
>
> *Avijjā* (ignorance)

- **The 5 to Cultivate Further (*Pañca c\'uttari bhāvaye*)**: The five spiritual faculties (*indriyāni*) that generate the power required to destroy all fetters:

> *Saddhā* (faith / trust / confidence on the liberation path)
>
> *Viriya* (spiritual energy, vital force)
>
> *Sati* (mindfulness, awareness)
>
> *Samādhi* (concentration)
>
> *Paññā* (wisdom)

- **The 5 Sticky Attachments Transcended (*Pañcasaṅgātigo*)**: Severing the five internal adhesive forces (*saṅga*) that bind the mind to the world: *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (fear and anger), *moha* (ignorance and delusion), *māna* (conceit), and *diṭṭhi* (false views).

**Crossed Over (*tiṇṇo*) the Flood (*Ogha*).**

The flood (*ogha*) denotes the world, which operates under the universal laws of decay and death. Crossing over (*tiṇṇa*) implies leaving the world, one shore and arriving at the other shore (*param*), the deathless realm of **Nibbāna** with permanent life, peace and bliss.

#### 1.16. Belaṭṭhasīsa

**Yathāpi bhaddo ājañño, naṅgalāvattānī sikhī, gacchati appakasirena; Evaṁ rattindivā mama; gacchanti appakasirena**, **sukhe laddhe nirāmise** ti.

*Just as a very well, thoroughbred horse (yathāpi bhaddo ājañño), well-trained in harness and plowing turns with a crest/mane (naṅgalāvattānī sikhī); goes with little difficulty/effortlessly (gacchati appakasirena); So is my nights and days / the passage of time (evaṁ rattindivā mama); go effortlessly / with total ease (gacchanti appakasirena), bliss / joy having been acquired (sukhe laddhe), free from worldly bait, non-carnal, non-material, unconditioned by sensual objects (nirāmise).*

**Commentary**: A Mind Cleansed of Worldly Attachment

- **The Thoroughbred Metaphor (*Bhaddo Ājañño*)**: A noble, well-trained thoroughbred horse carries its burden without strain, stumbling, or resistance. Similarly, the practitioner who has disciplined their internal faculties through **Sīla** and **Samādhi** moves through time without psychological friction. The internal struggles of worldly life have ceased.

- **Unworldly Joy (*Nirāmisa Sukha*)**: *Āmisa* literally denotes raw flesh, bait, or worldly sensory lure. *Nirāmisa sukha* is the joy born strictly from seclusion (*vivekasukha*), Samādhi, and the progressive abandonment of sensory dependencies. By stripping away external worldly happiness---as enforced by the higher Sīla precepts---the yogi practitioner taps into an internal, independent happiness (*nirāmise sukhe*) that requires no external fuel from the world.

- **Effortless Time (*Appakasirena Gacchanti*)**: When the mind is bound by worldly craving and attachment (*rāga*), fear and anger (*dosa*), and ignorance and delusion (*moha*), days and nights are spent in turmoil, friction, and anxiety. Once those worldly fuels are systematically starved, time passes effortlessly (*appakasirena*). The practitioner remains serene, sovereign, and completely detached from the decaying world around them.

#### 1.17. Dāsaka

**Middhī yadā hoti mahagghaso ca, Niddāyitā samparivattāsāyī;\
Mahāvarāhova nivāpapuṭṭho, Punappunaṁ gabbhamupeti mando ti.**

*When (yadā) one becomes (hoti) sluggish/ lethargy or mental dullness (middhī), and a glutton / great eater (mahagghaso), a sleeper/ prone to excessive sleep (niddāyitā), who sleeps rolling from side to side / tossing lazily in bed (samparivattāsāyī); like a fat pig (mahāvarāhova) fed on grain to excess (nivāpapuṭṭho), undergoes rebirth (gabbhamupeti) again and again (punappunaṁ), dull-minded / Slow-witted (mando).*

**Commentary**: A warning against the traps of sensory over-indulgence (*mahagghasa*), using animal metaphor (*mahāvarāho*), and repeated involuntary reincarnation (*punappunaṁ gabbhamupeti*).

#### 1.18. Siṅgāla's Father

**Ahu buddhassa dāyādo, bhikkhu bhesakaḷāvane;\
Kevalaṁ aṭṭhisaññāya, apharī pathaviṁ imaṁ;\
Maññehaṁ kāmarāgaṁ so, khippameva pahissatī ti.**

*There existed (ahu) an heir (dāyādo) of the Buddha (buddhassa), a bhikkhu, in the Bhesakaḷā forest (bhesakaḷāvane, a grove near Sumsumāragira in the Bhagga country); completely (kevalaṁ) use* *the skeleton contemplation (aṭṭhisaññāya), he pervaded/ filled (apharī) this entire plane (pathaviṁ imaṁ); I think (maññehaṁ) very quickly (khippameva) he (so) will eliminate (pahissatī) sexual and sensual attachment (kāmarāgaṁ).*

**Commentary**: Asubha and Aṭṭhisaññā

Represent two widely used contemplations from the ascetic **samaṇa** tradition.

**Asubha (Repulsiveness / Unattractiveness of the Body):**
