Appears extensively across the Nikāyas as a general category of practice (asubha-bhāvanā). Used as a direct antidote to kāmarāga (sexual and sensual lust) and physical pride. Asubha covers the full spectrum of bodily impurity---the 31 or 32 parts of the body (hair, nails, teeth, skin, internal organs, bodily fluids) as well as the 10 stages of cemetery decomposition (dasasivathikā).

**Aṭṭhisaññā (The Perception of Bones / Skeleton Contemplation):**

Appears less frequently as an independent term than asubha, but holds a prominent place in specific lists of perception (saññā), such as the 7 Perceptions (Sattā Saññā) or 10 Perceptions (Dasa Saññā). It focuses exclusively on the bare white frame---the bone structure stripped of flesh, skin, and blood. Aṭṭhisaññā allows the mind to treat the steady, white skeleton as a stabilized meditation sign (nimitta). This clean, white sign can then be expanded mentally---from one\'s own frame to the room, the forest, and eventually suffusing the entire earth, the plane of existence (apharī pathaviṁ imaṁ), transforming the bone contemplation into a powerful vehicle for deep concentration (samādhi).

**Pṛthivī** (Pāḷi: *pathavī*): is not merely the physical dirt underfoot, nor is it simply a solid planet hanging in space. It denotes both the physical soil/ground and a vast, cosmic **plane of existence** (a *loka* or *dhātu*).

In early Vedic cosmology, *Pṛthivī* is continuously paired with *Dyaus* (the Heavens/Sky) to form the foundational cosmic pair: **Dyāvā-pṛthivī** (Heaven and Earth).

**A. Pṛthivī as a Cosmic Plane (Loka / Stāna)**

In the *Ṛgveda*, reality is divided into distinct planes or spaces (*lokas* or *rocanas*), typically structured as a divine triad: **Pṛthivī** (The Broad Terrestrial Plane), **Antarikṣa** (The Intermediate/Atmospheric Plane), **Dyaus** (The Celestial/Heavenly Plane).

Here, *Pṛthivī* functions as a complete **plane of existence**---the realm where mortal beings, animals, physical matter, and earthly fires (*Agni*) reside and interact.

- *Ṛgveda* 10.121.1: *\"sa dādhāra pṛthivīṁ dyam utemām\"* (\"He supported the earth-plane and yonder sky.\")

- *Ṛgveda* 1.185: The *Dyāvāpṛthivī* hymns address Earth and Sky as two vast, boundless realms that hold all worlds and creatures within them (*urvī bhūyasī*).

**B. The Atharvaveda: Pṛthivī Sūkta (12.1)**

The longest and most famous Vedic hymn to Earth, the *Bhūmi Sūkta* / *Pṛthivī Sūkta* (63 verses), demonstrates this dual nature directly:

- **As Physical Ground:** She is the bearer of rock, stone, trees, crops, and human footfalls (*verse 26: rock and soil*).

- **As Cosmic Matrix/Plane:** She is held together by truth (*satya*), cosmic order (*ṛta*), and sacred austerity (*dīkṣā*). She is called the broad realm (*urvī*), the world of men (*manuşyāṇāṁ lokaḥ*), and the base that supports all six directions of the universe (*verse 1--4*).

**Transition to Aṭṭhisaññā Meditation**

When early *samaṇa* literature uses Pāḷi **pathavī** (as in *apharī pathaviṁ imaṁ* - \"he pervaded this whole earth/plane\"), it inherits both dimensions of this ancient Vedic linguistic substrate: **The Element, the Realm and the World (*Pathavī-dhātu-loka*):** The fundamental physical plane of existence build on the Element of Earth which has the characteristics of solidity, hardness, and extension.

The **aṭṭhisaññā** meditation acts as the bridge that transitions the mind from the coarse, sensual world (*kāma-dhātu*) into the vast infinite *arūpa* realm (*arūpa-dhātu*).

Sexual and Sensual attachment (*kāmarāga*) operates strictly within the **kāma-dhātu** (sex realm). It feeds on specific visual and tactile details---skin texture, body shape, facial features, and gender markers.

- **Stripping the Lure:** Contemplating the skeleton (*aṭṭhisaññā*) systematically strips away those sensual cues. The mind no longer sees a \"man\" or a \"woman,\" but an impersonal structural assembly of bones.

- **Pervading the Plane (*Aphari Pathaviṁ Imaṁ*):** When the practitioner expands this mental sign across the entire terrestrial plane (*pathavī*), the physical world loses its solid, captivating reality. The physical body is no longer viewed as a private identity or a source of pleasure, but as mere material elements shared with the rest of the earth.

By taking the mental sign (nimitta) of the white skeleton and expanding it infinitely across space, the practitioner shifts the mind\'s baseline concentration: As the perception of bones spreads infinitely (*apharī*), the physical perception of individual forms collapses. The mind moves away from concrete material shapes and attends directly to the vastness of the space containing those forms.

**Entering Infinite Space (*Ākāsānañcāyatana*):** This spatial expansion directly transitions into the formless realm (*arūpa-dhātu*)---the Dimension of Infinite Space. By transcending bounded material forms (*rūpasaññā*), all reactive perceptions of sensory impact (*paṭighasaññā*) cease.

**Total Transcendence of Kāma-Dhātu**

Because *kāmarāga* (sensual/sexual lust) requires a coarse, form-bound object in the sensual realm to exist, it cannot survive in a mind absorbed in infinite space or pure formless abstraction.

By using **aṭṭhisaññā** to dissolve physical identity and expand awareness across the entire plane, the practitioner starves *kāmarāga* at its root. The mind rises into the higher *arūpa* realms, rendering the coarse attractions of the sex realm (*kāma-dhātu*) entirely obsolete and powerlessly falling away (*khippameva pahissatī*).

#### 1.19. Kula

**Udakañhi nayanti nettikā, Usukārā namayanti tejanaṁ;\
Dāruṁ namayanti tacchakā, Attānaṁ damayanti subbatā ti.**

*Irrigators/ canal-builders (nettikā) indeed (hi) lead/guide (nayanti) water (udakaṁ); arrow-makers (usukārā) bend/ straighten/ shape (namayanti) the arrow (tejanaṁ); Carpenters (tacchakā) bend / straighten/ shape (namayanti) the wood (dāruṁ); Those of good vows / precepts (Sīla and Damana) (subbatā) tame / discipline / train (damayanti) the **soul-spirit ātmā/attā** (attānaṁ).*

**Commentary**: Taming the mind-heart **Citta**, purifying the **soul-spirit *ātmā/attā***

The verse contrasts three external crafts with internal spiritual training of the **soul-spirit *ātmā/attā***. Irrigators channel water. Arrow-makers heat and bend crooked shafts to make straight arrows. Carpenters carve and shape rigid timber. These three craftsmen take physical material/form and shape them for certain purpose through skill and effort.

**Subbatā** (Pali), Sanskrit **subrata** (**suvrata**).

- **Su-** (Prefix): Derived from Proto-Indo-European *\*su-:* \"good\", \"well\", \"virtuous,\" \"proper,\" or \"thorough.\"

- **-vrata** (Noun): Derived from the Sanskrit root **vṛ** (Sanskrit: *वृ*), meaning \"to choose,\" \"to select,\" \"to decide,\" or \"to cover/restrain.\"

  In classical Sanskrit and Pali, *vrata* (*vata*) evolved to mean a religious vow, a holy practice, a self-imposed observance, or a moral duty.

- **-ā** (Inflection): Nominative plural masculine termination (*subvataiḥ* / *subvatā*), meaning \"those who possess good vows\" or \"those who are dedicated to virtuous observances.\"

**suvrata** denotes one who is well-disciplined, docile to spiritual training, steadfast in sacred vows, or devoted to an ordered, holy lifestyle (*Brahmacariya*).

**suvrata** (सुव्रत) is ancient, appearing directly in the earliest **Ṛgveda**. Its usage in the Vedic context illuminates how the concept evolved from divine order to spiritual self-mastery.

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Vedic Text**      **Form / Context**           **Vedic Meaning & Application**
  ------------------- ---------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Ṛgveda 1.83.1**   *Suvratásya* (Genitive)      Associated with the divine order (*Ṛta*). Refers to divine beings (like Indra or Varuṇa) or sacrificers who strictly adhere to the established order and laws.

  **Ṛgveda 3.60.3**   *Suvratāsaḥ* (Nom. Plural)   Applied to the *Ṛbhus* (divine artisans/deities) who achieved immortality through their flawless, devoted craftsmanship and righteous conduct (*sv-apatyā/su-vrata*).

  **Ṛgveda 8.25.2**   *Suvratā* (Dual/Plural)      Applied to Mitra and Varuṇa as the guardians of law (*dharmamātarā*), who are inherently \"bound to good vows\" and uphold truth against chaos (*Anṛta*).

  **Atharvaveda**     *Suvrata* (Adjective)        Extends to human initiates (sacrificers and trainees) who have taken sacred vows (*vrata*) during rites of passage, marking them as bound to truth and spiritual discipline.
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

**Semantic Evolution from Vedic to Early Buddhist Thought**

1.  **Vedic Foundation (*Ṛta* and *Vrata*):** In the Ṛgveda, a *vrata* was an inviolable command, rule, or sacred domain governed by a deity (especially Varuṇa). A person who was *suvrata* was one whose conduct aligned harmoniously with *Ṛta* (the divine order instead of chaos) by executing their sacred vows and sacrificial duties perfectly.

2.  **Buddhist Internalization (*Sīla* and *Damana*):** Early Buddhism adopted the term **subvata** (**subbatā**) but shifted its focus to internal discipline according to Gautama's ***Sīla* and *Damana***, such as self-taming in this verse (*attānaṁ damayanti*).

In this light, the *subbatā* are those who use disciplined action (*Sīla* and *Samādhi*) to align the *citta* toward wholesomeness (*Sugato*), turning the mind away from the chaotic tendencies and steering it toward ultimate liberation. The verse illustrates that if human effort can shape, redirect, and control physical matter, a yogi practitioner can certainly shape, direct, and tame their own **soul-spirit *ātmā/attā***.

**Taming Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā* through the Holy Life *Brahmacariya***

In the practice of *Brahmacariya*, taming **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā*** refers to subduing the ***Citta***\'s (as envelop of **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***) untamed tendencies through *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and *Paññā*. Left to its habitual inclinations, the mind flows toward sensual attachment, much like unchanneled water. Through deliberate effort directed toward wholesomeness and virtuousness (*Sugato*), the *bhikkhu* disenchants the mind-heart from the world (*nibbida*), purify the mind-heart of worldly passion (*viraga*), this leads to the liberation of the mind-heart from the world (*vimutti*). This process of self-taming leads directly along the ancient path to the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

#### 1.20. Ajita

**Maraṇe me bhayaṁ natthi, nikanti natthi jīvite;\
Sandehaṁ nikkhipissāmi, sampajāno paṭissato ti.**

*there is no (natthi) fear (bhayaṁ) to me (me) for bodily death (maraṇe), there is no (natthi) delight/ longing (nikanti) in body's worldly life (jīvite); I will cast off (nikkhipissāmi) both cases of the coming and going of the manifest body (sandehaṁ), with right understanding (sampajāno) and inward-recollected awareness (paṭissato) (ti).*

**Commentary**: **Transcending the Body Manifest**

***maraṇa**:* bodily death; + ***jīvita**:* body's worldly life. ***Sandehaṁ*:** *san* (both, together) + *deha* (manifest, the body), The manifest body in both cases (coming and going); the physical body that arises and passes away.

**Sampajāno:** sam (right, proper, complete) + pajāna (understanding, comprehension, knowing) **Meaning:** Possessing right understanding, having right comprehension. The understanding that the body is only the manifest (deha) of the inner **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā.***

The *bhikkhu* here declares an absolute freedom from the two great anchors of worldly existence: fear of bodily death (*maraṇe bhayaṁ natthi*) and craving for the body's worldly life (*nikanti natthi jīvite*). Both the body's worldly life and its bodily death are merely a manifest of the **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā*** in this world, which is *dukkha*.

By recognizing sandehaṁ---the body in both its coming and going---as a purely worldly manifest, the fully purified mind-heart *Citta* completely eradicates *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. Equipped with sampajāno (right understanding and right comprehension that the body is only the manifest (deha) of the inner **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***)***,*** and paṭissato (inward-recollected awareness anchored back in the inner **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***), the practitioner does not cling to the body's worldly life, nor do they fear the bodily death, as they prepare to cast off (*nikkhipissāmi*) this manifest body entirely.

**Paṭissato** (Sanskrit: **Pratismṛta**):

1)  **Paṭi- / Prati-** (Prefix): In early Vedic Sanskrit, *prati-* is a directional vector meaning **\"back towards,\" \"inward,\" \"against the current,\"** or **\"reversing the direction.\"**

2)  **Sato / Smṛta** (Participle from root *sati* / *smṛ*): **Recollection, awareness, mindfulness, holding fast in memory.**

The true meaning of **Paṭissato** is **\"Inward-recollected awareness\"**---specifically, **mindfulness anchored back to oneself, the Soul-Spirit Ātmā/Attā**. It is the deliberate act of pulling awareness back from external worldly manifest, the body, and anchoring it firmly inside the **Soul-Spirit Ātmā/Attā**.

In the Pāli Canonical texts, **paṭissato** is used exclusively in critical moments of severe physical hardship, intense meditation, or impending death to describe a *bhikkhu* whose awareness is pulled back inward and completely detached from physical sensations.

**(1). Theragāthā 230 (Kāssapa Thera):** Ukkādhārova padīpito, **paṭissato** saṁvutindriyo \| Cittaṁ vūpasame hatvā, papañcaṁ samatikkami.

*Like a burning torch held high, **inward-recollected (paṭissato)**, with guarded senses; Bringing the mind-heart to peace, he has completely transcended worldly proliferation (papañca).*

**(2). Saṁyutta Nikāya 35.202 (Kimmindasutta):** Kāye kāyānupassī viharati ātāpī **sampajāno paṭissato**, vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṁ.

*He dwells observing the body in the body, ardent, **with right understanding (sampajāno) and inward-recollected awareness (paṭissato)**, removing greed and grief (coming and going of the body).*

**(3). Dīgha Nikāya 16 (Mahāparinibbāna Sutta):** Atha kho bhagavā **sampajāno paṭissato** sato kāyasaṅkhāraṁ adhiṭṭhāya vihari.

*Then the Bhagavā, **with right understanding (sampajāno) and inward-recollected awareness (paṭissato)**, maintained his life-force through physical pain.*

Faced with severe, deadly illness before his final passing, Gautama uses *paṭissato* to anchor his awareness back inward, completely untroubled by physical bodily suffering.

**(4). Sutta Nipāta 5.14 (Posālamāṇavapucchā, Verse 1113):** Viññāṇaṭṭhitiyo sabbā, abhijañña tathāgato; Tiṭṭhantamenaṁ jānāti, vimuttaṁ tappanāyanaṁ; Evamñāṇavimuttaṁ kho, natvā **paṭissato** bhikkhu.

*Having fully comprehended all stations of consciousness, the Tathāgata knows where the liberated one stands and their final destination; knowing one thus liberated by such higher knowledge, the bhikkhu stands **inwardly recollected (paṭissato)**.*

Gautama Buddha explains how a practitioner who understands the liberation path directs the consciousness to their final destination, remains firmly anchored in inward-recollected awareness (*paṭissato*), completely unattached to any station of worldly jurisdiction.

**Sampajāno** gives the practitioner right understanding and right comprehension regarding the body and **Soul-Spirit Ātmā/Attā** relationship that the body is only the manifest (deha) of the inner **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***).

**Paṭissato** provides the inward-recollected awareness that keeps mindfulness anchored back to inner **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***), the yogi practitioner does not cling to the body's life, nor do they fear the bodily death, as they prepare to cast off (*nikkhipissāmi*) this manifest body entirely, so the **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***) can transcend to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

#### 1.21. Nigrodha

**Nāhaṁ bhayassa bhāyāmi, Satthā no amatassa kovido;\
Yattha bhayaṁ nāvatiṭṭhati, Tena maggena vajanti bhikkhavo ti.**

*I am not afraid of any fear (Nāhaṁ bhayassa bhāyāmi), our (no) Teacher (Satthā) is the expert (kovido) of the deathless (amatassa), where (Yattha) fear (bhayaṁ) does not stand (nāvatiṭṭhati), By that (Tena) path (maggena) the bhikkhus (bhikkhavo) walk (vajanti) (ti).*

**Commentary: The Path to the Deathless and Faith as the Shield for the Practitioner**

This verse serves as a direct testament and standing declaration for the Bhikkhu students of Gautama Buddha: because they are guided by a Master who is expert on how to walk to the deathless realm of Nibbāna, they have no fear. For fear is **mastered and neutralized** through unwavering conviction, trust, and faith (*saddhā*) in the Teacher (*Satthā*).

**1). Trust in the Skill of the Guide (*Amatassa Kovido*)**

When an untrained traveler enters a dangerous forest, terror arises because they do not know the terrain. But when walking behind an expert guide (*kovido*), fear dissipates---not because the forest has changed, but because the guide knows the safe path. The verse explicitly grounds their confidence in the Teacher's expertise: *\"Our Teacher is the expert of the deathless.\"*

**2). Following a Proven Path (*Tena Maggena Vajanti*)**

For a student who has not yet fully realized the deathless realm of Nibbāna, fear still exists as an underlying tendency (*anusaya*). However, their faith (*saddhā*) anchors the mind-heart *Citta*, shielding it from panic. Confidence in the path supersedes instinctive worldly fear.

#### 1.22. Cittaka

**Nīlā sugīvā sikhino, Morā kārambhiyaṁ abhinadanti;\
Te sītavātakīḷitā, Suttaṁ jhāyaṁ nibodhentī ti.**

*Blue-necked (Nīlā sugīvā) and crested (sikhino) peacocks (Morā) in the Kārambhi mountain (kārambhiyaṁ) roar (abhinadanti); Those \[peacocks\] (Te) sporting in the cool breeze (sītavātakīḷitā), awaken \[the bhikkhu\] (nibodhentī) from sleeping (suttaṁ) \[to\] jhana meditation (jhāyaṁ) (ti).*

**Commentary**: Nature as a Catalyst

This verse illustrates how a forest-dwelling *bhikkhu*, living in seclusion away from human settlements, utilizes the natural environment to rouse the mind-heart *Citta* from sloth or sleepiness (*suttaṁ*). The vibrant sight and calls of the peacocks (*morā*) sporting in the cool mountain breeze (*sītavātakīḷitā*) act as a direct alarm to wake up the yogi practitioner\'s inner vigilance.

Instead of allowing the *Citta* to slip into worldly lethargy or mental stagnation, the calling of the birds rouses the practitioner back into active meditative absorption (*jhāyaṁ*). The practitioner uses this sensory stimulus not for worldly enjoyment, but as a sharp reminder to diligently practice toward the full liberation from the world.

#### 1.23. Gosāla

**Ahaṁ kho veḷugumbasmiṁ, bhutvāna madhupāyasaṁ;\
Padakkhiṇaṁ sammasanto, khandhānaṁ udayabbayaṁ;\
Sānuṁ paṭigamissāmi, vivekamanubrūhayan ti.**

*Having eaten (bhutvāna) honeyed milk-rice (madhupāyasaṁ) in the bamboo thicket (veḷugumbasmiṁ); thoroughly and methodically (padakkhiṇaṁ) investigating (sammasanto) the arising and passing away (udayabbayaṁ) of the aggregates (khandhānaṁ); I shall return (paṭigamissāmi) to the mountain slope (sānuṁ), fostering and cultivating (anubrūhayaṁ) seclusion (vivekam).*

**Commentary**: **from Grāma to Arañña**

In ancient Indian, the **honeyed milk-rice (*madhupāyasaṁ*)** or *kheer* blended with honey and milk is an ancient, rich ritual dish composed of rice cooked in milk and sweetened with raw honey or concentrated jaggery. It was considered a supreme sacrificial offering (*havis*) to the *devas* and ancestors (*pitṛs*). It represented purity, auspiciousness, and high nutrient density.

*Madhupāyasa* was a high-status food reserved for grand ceremonial occasions, royal feasts, or major religious offerings prepared by wealthy householders (*gahapatis*).

The most famous canonical instance is Sujātā's offering of high-grade milk-rice to the Gautama under the Banyan tree right before his awakening and liberation. Receiving *madhupāyasa* on almsround meant a *bhikkhu* had received an exceptional, highly nutritious almsmeal from the village.

**Reflection in a Bamboo Thicket (*Veḷugumbasmiṁ*)**

A bamboo thicket (*veḷugumba*) held a distinct geographic and social meaning in ancient Magadha and the broader Middle Country (*Majjhimadesa*): **The Fringe Zone (*Grāmanta*):** Bamboo clumps typically grew on the outer perimeter of villages and towns---acting as living fence lines or natural barriers separating cultivated farmland from dense wilderness.

A *bhikkhu* would walk into the village or city early in the morning for alms. Upon receiving food like *madhupāyasaṁ*, he would not sit in the householder\'s domestic space, nor would he eat while walking through busy village streets. Instead, he would withdraw to the nearest quiet threshold---the bamboo thicket on the village outskirts---to consume the meal in peace.

Scrutinizing (*sammasanto*) the rise and fall (*udayabbayaṁ*) of the five aggregates (*khandhānaṁ*) signifies the truth about the law of death, the *dukkha*, and the transcendence to the deathless realm of Nibbāna. This creates an urgency for ardent practice toward liberation in **Seclusion** (***viveka***).

The contrast embedded in this verse (Supreme Honor vs. Immediate Withdrawal) highlights the core ethos of the renunciant life:

**No Entangled Gratitude or Pride:** Even after receiving an offering fit for a king or a ritual sacrifice, the *bhikkhu* does not linger in the village (*grāma*) to enjoy praise, dynamic social status, or the householders\' veneration.

**Return to Seclusion (*Sānuṁ Paṭigamissāmi*):** The higher the honor bestowed by the world, the more urgent the return to the wilderness (*arañña*). He takes the physical strength provided by the householders\' gift and directly converts it into meditative exertion (*sammasanto khandhānaṁ udayabbayaṁ*) on the mountain slope.

The verse illustrates that while the village honored the *bhikkhu* with a supreme, ritual-grade gift (*madhupāyasaṁ*), the *bhikkhu* remained entirely unattached to their veneration. He accepts the sustenance, steps out of the social domain, and uses that energy to reflect and then return to total seclusion (*viveka*) on the mountain slope to tame the *citta* and pursue ultimate liberation from the world.

#### 1.24. Sugandha

**Anuvassiko pabbajito, passa dhammasudhammataṁ;\
Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Having renounced family life (pabbajito) for a single year (anuvassiko); behold (passa) the perfect goodness of the Dhamma (dhammasudhammataṁ); the three Knowledges (tisso vijjā) have been attained (anuppattā), the teaching of the Buddha (buddhassa sāsanaṁ) has been done/* *accomplished (kataṁ).*

**Commentary**: This History of **Three Knowledges (*Tisso Vijjā*)**

In this verse, a *sāmaṇera* (novice) in his first year (*anuvassiko*) underwent intensive foundational training under a mentor (*upajjhāya*). This includes **three core knowledges/** foundational insights taught as Gautama Buddha's dispensation (*buddhassa sāsanaṁ*), and then the *sāmaṇera* verifies those principles through personal investigation (*dhamma-sudhammataṁ*) rather than blind belief.

***Vijjā* as \"Science\" or \"System of Knowledge\"**

In Vedic and early *samaṇa* contexts, the word *vijjā* (Sanskrit: *vidyā*, from root *vid*, \"to know\") meant a **formal branch of knowledge, a science, or a core body of wisdom**:

- **Vedic Precedent:** The Brahmanas held the traditional *Trayī Vidyā*---the \"Threefold Knowledge\" of the three Vedas (*Ṛg*, *Sāma*, *Yajur*).

- **Early Samaṇa Versions:** When **Early Samaṇa traditions including** Gautama Buddha used *tisso vijjā*, they inherited and updated the Vedic knowledge with their own understandings. Although historians and scholars tend to distort and demonize the traditional Vedic knowledges as rituals only, this is completely wrong. Also we need to know that the Vedic traditional knowledges are vast, with a long history and a lot of misunderstandings along the history, that is why Gautama Buddha along with his contemporary tried to establish the right version of the three knowledges, as I am doing here.

In a novice\'s first year, \"learning and realizing the three knowledges\" signifies the importance of the three fundamental insights. To uncover the original meaning of the **Three Knowledges (*Tisso Vijjā* / Sanskrit: *Trayī Vidyā*)** prior to its scholastic elaboration/wrong interpretation in later Theravāda commentary as most Buddhists understands or rather misunderstands today, we must examine how the term functioned in the **Vedic textual layer** and **Early Samaṇa's layer**.

**1). The Vedic Blueprint: *Trayī Vidyā:*** The term *Trayī Vidyā* (\"The Threefold Knowledge\" or \"The Three Sciences\") was the foundational term for complete sacred learning in Brahmanical society. **The Three Vedas:** *Trayī Vidyā* referred strictly to the mastery of the three ancient Vedic collections: **Ṛgveda,** **Yajurveda** and **Sāmaveda**. To possess the *Trayī Vidyā* was the hallmark of a fully educated, authoritative Brahmana (*tevijja*).

**The Baseline: The Three Knowledges in the Vedas**

In the *Ṛgveda*, *Sāmaveda*, *Yajurveda*, and early *Brāhmaṇas*, *trayī vidyā* refers strictly to the **threefold functional science** required to understand and operate reality:

A.  ***Ṛc* (Definition):** Knowing the inherent nature, name, and boundaries of everything in this universe.

**Ṛgveda 10.90.1** (*Ṛc* --- Defining boundaries and inherent nature): *sahasraśīrṣā puruṣaḥ sahasrākṣaḥ sahasrapāt,* *sa bhūmiṃ viśvato vṛtvātyatiṣṭhad daśāṅgulam.*

*Thousand-headed (sahasra-śīrṣā) is the Cosmic Being (puruṣas), thousand-eyed (sahasra-akṣas), thousand-footed (sahasra-pāt). He (sas), the earth (bhūmim) on every side (viśvatas) having covered (vṛtvā), extended beyond (ati-atiṣṭhat) by ten fingers\' breadth (daśa-aṅgulam).*

**Puruṣa** is explicitly **cosmic mankind**---humanoid in structural essence, not animaloid (*paśu*). The term *puruṣa* etymologically and structurally denotes the humanoid archetype expanded to cosmic proportions. While animal sacrifices (*paśu-medha*) were part of ancient ritual, the Puruṣa Sūkta (Ṛgveda 10.90) sets Puruṣa apart as the primal, conscious, anthropomorphic origin.

\"Thousand\" (*sahasra*) in Vedic Sanskrit does not mean literally 1,000; it signifies **infinitiude and complete totality**.

**Collective Consciousness:** Having all heads, eyes, and feet means that *every individual human head that thinks, every eye that sees, and every foot that walks is literally a functional organ of the one Puruṣa,* the cosmic collective.

**Immanence:** Puruṣa completely permeates and fills the material world (*bhūmi*). We earth humans are part of this cosmic mankind race.

**Transcendence Beyond the Universe:** While Puruṣa fills the entire manifestation (*bhūmi*), he extends *beyond* it (*ati-atiṣṭhat*).

**The Meaning of \"Ten Fingers\" (*daśa-aṅgulam*):**

**The Heart Center (*Hṛdaya*):** In Vedic and Upaniṣadic measurement, \"ten fingers\" is the traditional distance from the navel to the heart---the seat of the internal *citta*/spirit (***ātmā***). It signifies that Puruṣa transcends physical matter by stepping into the unmanifest realm of pure consciousness. It establishes that physical existence (*bhūmi*) is only a fraction of reality. The remaining, dominant portion of Puruṣa remains untouched by the universal laws of decay and death of the cosmos if can extend/transcend it.

This verse is the ultimate **Definition (*Ṛc*)** of the relationship between the individual, the cosmos, and transcendence:

**Microcosm = Macrocosm:** Human beings are micro-puruṣas. Our structure mirrors the cosmic Puruṣa.

**The World is Worldly (*Loka*):** The material sphere (*bhūmi*) is fully enveloped and bound by cause and effect, yet it is just the lower baseline.

**The Unmanifest Threshold:** The \"ten fingers\' breadth\" (*daśa-aṅgulam*) represents the transcendent threshold---the exit point beyond the bounded universe, pointing directly toward the unconditioned realm outside of the universe.

**Ṛgveda 10.129.1--2** (*Ṛc* --- Defining boundaries and inherent nature): *na asat āsīt no sat āsīt tadānīm, na āsīt rajas no vyomā paraḥ yat; na mṛtyuḥ āsīt amṛtam na tarhi, na rātryāḥ ahnaḥ āsīt praketaḥ.*

*Not (na) the void/non-existent state (asat) was (āsīt), nor (no) the manifest/existent state (sat) was (āsīt) then (tadānīm); Not (na) was (āsīt) the intermediate spatial realm (rajas), nor (no) the outer vault of the sky (vyomā), beyond (paraḥ) which (yat). Not (na) death (mṛtyuḥ) was (āsīt), nor (na) heavenly immortality (amṛtam) then (tarhi); Not (na) of night (rātryāḥ), nor of day (ahnaḥ) was (āsīt) any cosmic indicator/distinguishing marker (praketaḥ).*

**The Core Premise: The Universe as a Container Box**

This verse is a blueprint of what lies **outside** the universe as a container box by describing what is **absent** when we stand beyond its walls. To understand this verse, we must see the universe as a finite, structured container---a closed engine operating on four precise walls:

**(1). Spatial Boundary: The Cosmic Wall (*Rajas & Vyoman*)**

- **The Illusion:** People assume space goes on forever.

- **The Reality:** *Rajas* is the internal space where planets and realms sit. *Vyoman* is the limit/hard ceiling---the dome of the cosmos.

- **The Deep Meaning (*Paraḥ Yat*):** The verse explicitly highlights *paraḥ yat* (\"beyond which\"). It tells you that the universe is not infinite. It has an outer boundary limit/ceiling. The physical and subtle space as we observe do not exist outside that limit/ceiling.

**(2). Material Boundary: Matter vs. Void (*Sat & Asat*)**

- **The Illusion:** Liberation means dissolving into \"nothingness\" (*asat*) or expanding into \"everything\" (*sat*).

- **The Reality:** Matter/energy (*sat*) and the empty void between matter (*asat*) are just two internal states *inside* the box.

- **The Deep Meaning:** Standing outside the universe, you are neither \"a thing\" nor \"a void.\" You have stepped completely beyond the binary physics of the container. You have to transcend the box.

**(3). Temporal Boundary: Death vs. Heavenly Immortality (*Mṛtyu & Amṛtam*)**

In early Vedic culture, ***Mṛtyu* (Earthly Death):** Dying in the physical, mortal world (*mre* = to die). ***Amṛtam* (Celestial Non-Death):** Escaping physical mortality by ascending to the upper cosmic realms, where beings live for millennia on soma/nectar without physical decay, gaining **maximum lifespan** and potentially achieve **true** **deathless** afterwords.

- **The Illusion:** The ultimate goal is achieving \"immortality\" (*amṛtam*) in a high heavenly realm.

- **The Reality:** *Mṛtyu* (decay/death) rules the lower realms; *Amṛtam* in Brahma realms is only a stepping stone because Brahma realms are still trapped inside the cosmic clock.

- **The Deep Meaning:** True transcendence is exiting the universal temporal engine entirely---reaching a realm beyond this universe.

**(4). Causal Boundary: The Clockwork Engine (*Praketa*)**

- **The Illusion:** Time flows naturally everywhere.

- **The Reality:** Time only exists inside the box because cosmic markers (*praketa*---suns, moons, stars) create sequence (day and night, past and future, cause and effect).

- **The Deep Meaning:** Outside the container, there are no markers, no motion, no sequence, beyond this time matrix.

This verse is the ancient world\'s ultimate **Definition (*Ṛc*)** of the **Unconditioned**. It tells you: **Everything you can name, see, experience, or inhabit---space, time, matter, void, life, death, and heavenly rebirth---belongs strictly to this container box we call universe. It's the universal laws, including the law of decay and death, condition every aggregated existence inside the jurisdiction of the universe, at the time it birthed into an aggregated existence.**

True liberation is not about fixing, harmonizing, or expanding yourself *within* this cosmic box. It is the complete detachment of the mind-heart (*citta*) from all worldly phenomena, and stepping through the final threshold (*paraḥ yat*) **completely out of the universe into a true deathless realm of Nibbāna**.

B.  ***Yajus* (Process/Action):** Knowing the exact dynamic mechanics of cause, effect, and transformation.

**Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa 10.5.3.1:** *yad vai yat kiṃ ca prajāḥ kāmayante, tat karmaṇā kurvate, tat kāmena kṛtaṃ karmaṇo jāyate.*

Whatever (*yat vai yat kiṃ ca*) beings/creatures (*prajāḥ*) desire/crave (*kāmayante*); That (*tat*) through volitional action/movement (*karmaṇā*) they bring about/perform (*kurvate*); That (*tat*) which is performed (*kṛtaṃ*) through desire/craving (*kāmena*) is born/generated (*jāyate*) directly from action (*karmaṇo*).

This verse is the foundational **Yajus (Science of Causality)**. It outlines the exact closed-loop feedback mechanism that keeps conscious beings trapped inside the cosmic container (*loka*):

a)  **The Origin of Bondage:** Suffering and rebirth do not happen by chance or divine decree. They are driven by an internal engine: **Craving (*Kāma*) ➔ Volitional Action (*Karma*) ➔ Manifest Result (*Jāyate*)**.

b)  **The World is Cause and Effect:** Everything born from the above cycle belongs to the world (*loka*). These generated phenomena rise and fall, contain the laws of cause, effect, and death, and are **not our true self/soul-spirit *Ātmā*, nor part of our true self/soul-spirit *Ātmā*, nor belonging to our true self/soul-spirit *Ātmā***. Understanding, purifying and freeing this **soul-spirit *Ātmā*** from this **container box** (*loka*) lead to its true and ultimate freedom.

c)  **The Key to Liberation:** To break the engine, one does not stop the universe---one cuts the fuel. By eliminating *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* at the point of sensory contact, craving (*kāma*) ceases. Without craving, volitional momentum (*karma*) dies out, stopping the cycle of birth (*jāyate*) and opening the way to final transcendence through the ***Brahman* gateway**.

**Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 1.4**: *eka-nemiṃ tri-vṛtaṃ ṣoḍaśāntaṃ śatārdhāram viṃśati-pratyārābhiḥ,* *aṣṭakaiḥ ṣaḍbhiḥ viśva-rūpaika-pāśaṃ tri-mārga-bhedaṃ dvy-nimittaika-moham.*

\[We contemplate the cosmic wheel\] having a single rim (*eka-nemim*), three-folded (*tri-vṛtam*), sixteen-ended (*ṣoḍaśa-antam*), fifty-spoked (*śata-ardha-āram*), with twenty counter-spokes (*viṃśati-pratyārābhis*); With six sets of eight (*ṣaḍbhis aṣṭakais*), whose single cord (*eka-pāśam*) takes all forms (*viśva-rūpa*), divided into three paths (*tri-mārga-bhedam*), driven by two causes (*dvi-nimitta*) rooted in a single delusion (*eka-moham*).

This verse is a literal blueprint of the **Cosmic Wheel of Saṃsāra (*Brahmacakra*)**. It maps every component of the structural machine that keeps conscious beings trapped inside the universe (*loka*):

THE COSMIC MACHINE / BRAHMACAKRA

1\. RIM (Eka-Nemi): Single Underlying Primordial Reality / Maya

2\. TIRES (Tri-Vṛt): 3 Guṇas (Sattva, Rajas, Tamas)

3\. ENDS (Ṣoḍaśa-Anta): 16 Kalās (5 Elements + 10 Organs + Mind)

4\. SPOKES (Śatārdhāram): 50 Conditions of Mental Stagnation & Attachment

5\. COUNTER-SPOKES: 20 Senses & Organs of Action (10 Indriyas + 10 Objects)

6\. SIX EIGHTS: 6 Categories of 8 Structural Layers (Prakṛti, Dātu, etc.)

7\. SINGLE CORD: Unbroken Craving / Desire (Kāma/Taṇhā)

8\. THREE PATHS: Wholesome, Unwholesome, and Ignorant Karma

9\. TWO CAUSES: Wholesome & Unwholesome Volition (Punya/Apunya)

ROOT: Single Delusion (Eka-Moha / Avijjā)

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Term**           **Structural Meaning in the Causal Science (Yajus)**
  ------------------ -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Eka-Nemi**       **The Outer Rim:** The single boundary enclosing the entire manifest cosmos (*loka*).

  **Tri-Vṛt**        **The Threefold Tire:** three fundamental qualities (*sattva*, *rajas*, *tamas*) driving all worldly motion and friction.

  **Ṣoḍaśa-Anta**    **The Sixteen Terminals:** The 5 physical elements, 5 sensory organs, 5 motor organs, and the processing mind (*manas*).

  **Śatār-dhāram**   **Fifty Spokes:** The 50 psychological states (delusions, incapacities, complacencies, and attainments) that transmit force from the hub to the rim.

  **Eka-Pāśa**       **The Single Binding Cord:** The binding tether of Craving (*Taṇhā*), holds entire manifest form (*viśva-rūpa*) together.

  **Dvi-Nimitta**    **Two Causes:** Dualistic attraction (*rāga*) and aversion (*dosa*) driving every volitional action.

  **Eka-Moha**       **Single Delusion:** The foundational ignorance (*avijjā/moha*) not knowing the soul-spirit *ātmā* have the potential to be liberated from this cosmic container.
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1.  **The Universe as an Engine:** Saṃsāra is not a chaotic accident; it is an intricate, mechanical system governed strictly by cause, effect, and structural parameters.

2.  **The Root Mechanics:** All fifty spokes and sixteen ends are driven by two forces---**Attraction (*Rāga*)** and **Aversion (*Dosa*)**---which are bound together by a single cord of **Craving (*Taṇhā*)** and anchored in **Fundamental Ignorance (*Moha*)**.

3.  **The Exit Strategy:** You cannot break the wheel by fighting individual spokes. Liberation is achieved by severing the single binding cord (*eka-pāśa*) through the eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. When the fuel of craving is removed, the entire cosmic machine collapses for the practitioner, clearing the threshold for final exit through the **Brahman gateway**.

<!-- -->

C.  ***Sāman* (Resolution):** Knowing how to align or resolve contrasting forces into equilibrium.

**Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad 1.3.28:** ***asato mā sad gamaya, tamaso mā jyotir gamaya, mṛtyor mā mṛtam gamaya**.*

From unreal (*asatas*) lead (*gamaya*) me (*mā*) to real (*sat*); from darkness (*tamasas*) lead (*gamaya*) me (*mā*) to light (*jyotis*); from death (*mṛtyos*) lead (*gamaya*) me (*mā*) to immortality (*amṛtam*).

**Chāndogya Upaniṣad 1.6.1 - 1.6.7**: *iyaṁ vāvāgṛcprāṇaḥ sāmaintadetat-sāmābhyūḍham, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate; iyamaeva vāgaprāṇaḥ sāmātadetat-sāmābhyūḍham, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate.*

This \[earth/manifest matrix\] (*iyam*) indeed (*vāv*) is speech/definition (*vāc* / *ṛc*); breath/life-force (*prāṇaḥ*) is harmony/resolution (*sāma*). Upon this (*tat/etat*) *Ṛc* (*ṛci*), this *Sāman* (*sāma*) is rested/supported (*abhyūḍham*). Therefore (*tasmāt*), supported upon *Ṛc* (*ṛci adhyūḍham*), *Sāman* (*sāma*) is sung/executed (*gīyate*). This earth (*iyam*) indeed (*eva*) is speech (*vāc*); fire (*agniḥ*) is life-force (*prāṇaḥ*)\... Upon this (*tat/etat*) *Ṛc* (*ṛci*), this *Sāman* (*sāma*) is rested/supported (*abhyūḍham*).

*antarikṣameva ṛgvāyusāma tadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate; antarikṣameva ṛgvāyusāmātadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate.*

The intermediate atmosphere (*antarikṣam*) indeed (*eva*) is *Ṛc* (*ṛc*); wind/dynamic movement (*vāyuḥ*) is *Sāman* (*sāma*). Upon this (*tat/etat*) *Ṛc* (*ṛci*), this *Sāman* (*sāma*) is rested/supported (*adhyūḍham*). Therefore (*tasmāt*), supported upon *Ṛc* (*ṛci adhyūḍham*), *Sāman* (*sāma*) is sung/executed (*gīyate*).

*dyaureva ṛgādityasāma tadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate; dyaureva ṛgādityasāmātadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate.*

The sky/heavenly dome (*dyaus*) indeed (*eva*) is *Ṛc* (*ṛc*); the sun/source of light (*ādityaḥ*) is *Sāman* (*sāma*). Upon this (*tat/etat*) *Ṛc* (*ṛci*), this *Sāman* (*sāma*) is rested/supported (*adhyūḍham*). Therefore (*tasmāt*), supported upon *Ṛc* (*ṛci adhyūḍham*), *Sāman* (*sāma*) is sung/executed (*gīyate*).

*nakṣatrāṇyeva ṛgcandramāsāma tadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate; nakṣatrāṇyeva ṛgcandramāsāmātadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate.*

The stars/constellations (*nakṣatrāṇi*) indeed (*eva*) are *Ṛc* (*ṛc*); the moon/rhythmic marker (*candramāḥ*) is *Sāman* (*sāma*). Upon this (*tat/etat*) *Ṛc* (*ṛci*), this *Sāman* (*sāma*) is rested/supported (*adhyūḍham*). Therefore (*tasmāt*), supported upon *Ṛc* (*ṛci adhyūḍham*), *Sāman* (*sāma*) is sung/executed (*gīyate*).

atha yadetasya ādityasya śuklaṁ bhāḥ saiva ṛgatha yatkṛṣṇaṁ paraḥ kṛṣṇaṁ tatsāma, tadetadetasyāmṛcyadhyūḍhaṁ sāma, tasmādṛcyādhyūḍhaṁ sāma gīyate.

Now (*atha*), that which (*yat*) is the white radiance (*śuklaṁ bhāḥ*) of this sun (*etasya ādityasya*), that (*sā*) indeed (*eva*) is *Ṛc* (*ṛc*); now (*atha*), that which (*yat*) is the dark/ultraviolet depth (*kṛṣṇam*), the beyond-dark (*paraḥ kṛṣṇam*), that (*tat*) is *Sāman* (*sāma*). Upon this (*tat/etat*) *Ṛc* (*ṛci*), this *Sāman* (*sāma*) is rested/supported (*adhyūḍham*). Therefore (*tasmāt*), supported upon *Ṛc* (*ṛci adhyūḍham*), *Sāman* (*sāma*) is sung/executed (*gīyate*).

*atha ya eṣontarāditye hiraṇmayaḥ puruṣo dṛśyate hiraṇyaśmruḥ hiraṇyakeśa ā praṇakhāt sarva eva suvarṇaḥ.*

Now (*atha*), he who (*yaḥ*), this one (*eṣaḥ*) within the sun (*antar-āditye*), the golden/luminous (*hiraṇmayaḥ*) cosmic person/archetype (*puruṣaḥ*) is seen (*dṛśyate*), with golden beard (*hiraṇya-śmaśruḥ*), golden hair (*hiraṇya-keśaḥ*), unto the tip of the nails (*ā praṇakhāt*), entirely (*sarvaḥ*) indeed (*eva*) golden-hued (*suvarṇaḥ*).

*tasya yathā kapyāsaṁ puṇḍarīkamevamakṣiṇī tasyoditi nāma, sa eṣa sarvebhyaḥ pāpmabhya udita udeti ha vai sarvebhyaḥ pāpmabhyo ya evaṁ veda.*

Of that supreme entity (*tasya*), just as (*yathā*) a lotus sitting blossoming in full light (*kapyāsaṁ puṇḍarīkam*), so (*evam*) are the two eyes/vision centers (*akṣiṇī*). His name (*tasya nāma*) is \"Risen Above\" (*ud*). He (*saḥ*), this one (*eṣaḥ*), above all (*sarvebhyaḥ*) cosmic corruptions/frictions (*pāpmabhyaḥ*) has risen (*uditaḥ*). He rises (*udeti*) indeed (*ha vai*) above all (*sarvebhyaḥ*) corruptions/frictions (*pāpmabhyaḥ*) who (*yaḥ*) thus (*evam*) knows/realizes (*veda*).

Commentary: While mainstream academic and scholastic traditions read **Chāndogya Upaniṣad 1.6.1--1.6.7** either as solar mythology (personifying the sun god Sūrya) or as an internal meditation visualization (*upāsanā*), a rigorous **structural-cosmological reading** reveals something much deeper. When analyzed through pre-sectarian *samaṇa* physics and ancient Vedic cosmology, this passage is neither simple myth nor mere metaphor. It is an explicit mapping of a **dimensional portal and structural junction point** located at the core solar axis of our solar system.

**I. The Solar Boundary: The Axis of the Cosmic Container**

In ancient Indian cosmology, the sun (*āditya*) is not merely a burning ball of plasma (consciousness and life force container); it is the central **engine and outer valve** of the physical realm.

- **The Matrix and the Driver (1.6.1--1.6.4):** Verses 1 through 4 establish that the physical substrate (*Ṛc*) and the dynamic life-force (*Sāman/Prāṇa*) operate in strict pairs across all levels of the universe---Earth/Fire, Atmosphere/Wind, Sky/Sun, Stars/Moon. The physical world is a structural cage (*ṛci adhyūḍhaṃ sāma*), holding energetic force in place.

- **The Threshold of White and Black Light (1.6.5):** Verse 1.6.5 splits the sun into two distinct visual/frequency bands: *Śuklaṃ bhāḥ* (the white radiance): The manifest spectrum visible to mortal eyes within the lower cosmic matrix.

  *Kṛṣṇaṃ paraḥ kṛṣṇam* (the dark, beyond the dark): The unmanifest, high-frequency core hidden within the solar disc.

This \"beyond-dark\" core marks the exact event horizon where physical light ends and higher-dimensional structural pathways begin.

**II. The Golden Entity (*Hiraṇmaya Puruṣa*): Portal Guardian or Cosmic Threshold**

Verse 1.6.6 describes the *Hiraṇmaya Puruṣa* (\"Golden Person\") dwelling *inside* the solar disc (*antar-āditye*):

- **Not a Personal God, but an Unstained Archetype:** The \"golden\" attribute from hair to nails (*ā praṇakhāt sarva eva suvarṇaḥ*) signifies absolute resistance to physical decay and friction (*pāpman*). Gold in Vedic terminology is the element that does not rust, corrode, or react with lower physical elements.

- **The Gateway Entity:** This Golden Puruṣa represents the pristine, uncorrupted threshold state. It bridges the physical universe of gross matter (*sat*) with the subtle, higher-dimensional realms (*amṛtam*) of the celestial and Brahma worlds.

**III. The Portal Mechanics: The Name \"UD\" (*Uditaḥ*)**

The key to the esoteric mechanics lies in verse 1.6.7: **The Name is \"UD\" (Upward / Risen):** The text explicitly names this solar entity *Ud* (from *ud-ita*, meaning \"risen above\" or \"exited upward\").

**Risen Above All Friction (*Sarvebhyaḥ pāpmabhyaḥ uditaḥ*):** *Pāpman* is the binding friction of cause, effect, gravity, decay, and involuntary reincarnation that traps consciousness inside the lower planetary sphere. The solar center acts as a cosmic **gravitational and causal exit valve**. Passing through the realization of *Ud* allows consciousness to sever its tethering to physical earth-bound mechanics and ascend into the celestial and Brahma realms. When stripped of sectarian devotion, **Chāndogya Upaniṣad 1.6.1--1.6.7** describes a precise cosmological architecture:

a)  **The Sun as a Gateway:** The solar center (*āditya*) is a physical-to-subtle dimensional portal within our solar system. The visible light conceals a higher-frequency, unmanifest axis (*paraḥ kṛṣṇam*).

b)  **The Vehicle of Ascent:** By mastering the balance of *Ṛc* (structural boundaries) and *Sāman* (energetic resolution/stillness), the practitioner\'s mind-heart (*citta*) aligns with the frequency of *Ud*.

c)  **The Stepping Stone to Transcendence:** Ascending through the solar portal places consciousness into the higher celestial spheres. However, the *samaṇa* recognizes that even this golden solar portal and the realms beyond it are still within the cosmic container (*loka*). The final goal is to use this threshold to step **into Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

In the Vedic worldview, a person who mastered the *trayī vidyā* was someone who understood **what things are**, **how things work**, and **how to bring them to completion/perfection/full**.

**2). The Early Upanishadic Layer**

In early Upanishads like the *Chandogya* (3.12--3.13) and *Bṛhadāraṇyaka* (1.5), composed right before or during the early *samaṇa* period, the yogis remapped three traditional Vedic knowledges onto internal psychology, cosmology, and direct realization.

**A. Chāndogya Upaniṣad (3.12--3.13): Structural Metaphor of *Gāyatrī***

**ChUp 3.12.1: The Definition of Gāyatrī as Speech & Protection**

*gāyatrī vā idaṃ sarvaṃ bhūtaṃ yadidaṃ kiñca; vāgvai gāyatrī, vācā vā idaṃ sarvaṃ bhūtaṃ gāyati ca trāyate ca.*

Gāyatrī / structural meter (*gāyatrī*) indeed (*vai*) is all this (*idaṃ sarvaṃ*) manifest creation/existence (*bhūtam*), whatever (*yadidaṃ*) exists here (*kiñca*); Speech (*vāc*) indeed (*vai*) is Gāyatrī (*gāyatrī*), for through speech (*vācā*) indeed (*vā*) all this (*idaṃ sarvaṃ*) manifest creation (*bhūtaṃ*) is chanted/expressed (*gāyati*) and (*ca*) protected/preserved (*trāyate ca*).

**ChUp 3.12.2: The Earth as the Ground of Existence**

yā vai sā gāyatryeyaṃ vāva sā yāiyaṃ pṛthivyasyāṃ hyedaṃ sarvaṃ bhūtamabhiśritaṃ netāmatyeti.

That which (*yā*) indeed (*vai*) is that (*sā*) Gāyatrī (*gāyatrī*), that (*sā*) indeed (*vāva*) is this (*eyaṃ*) which (*yā*) is this (*iyaṃ*) earth (*pṛthivī*); for (*hi*) upon this (*asyāṃ*) all this (*idaṃ sarvaṃ*) manifest creation (*bhūtam*) is supported (*abhiśritam*), and (*ca*) it does not (*na*) extend beyond (*atyeti*) it (*etām*).

**ChUp 3.12.3: The Embodied Form (Physical Body)**

*yā vai sā pṛthīveyam vāva sā yadiadamasmin puruṣe śarīramasmiddhyete prāṇā abhiśritā etannātyenti.*

That which (*yā*) indeed (*vai*) is that (*sā*) earth (*pṛthivī*), that (*sā*) indeed (*vāva*) is this (*iyaṃ*) which (*yā*) is this (*idaṃ*) physical body (*śarīram*) within this (*asmin*) person (*puruṣe*); for (*hi*) in this (*asmin*) these (*ete*) vital forces (*prāṇāḥ*) are supported (*abhiśritāḥ*), and they do not (*na*) extend beyond (*atyenti*) it (*etat*).

**ChUp 3.12.4: The Heart as the Inner Core**

*yadvai tat puruṣe śarīramidaṃ vāva tad yadidamasminnantaḥpuruṣe hṛdayamasmiddhyete prāṇā abhiśritā etannātyenti.*

That which (*yad*) indeed (*vai*) is that (*tat*) physical body (*śarīram*) in the person (*puruṣe*), that (*tat*) indeed (*vāva*) is this (*idaṃ*) which (*yat*) is this (*idaṃ*) heart (*hṛdayam*) within this (*asmin*) inner (*antaḥ*) person (*puruṣe*); for (*hi*) in this (*asmin*) these (*ete*) vital forces (*prāṇāḥ*) are supported (*abhiśritāḥ*), and they do not (*na*) extend beyond (*atyenti*) it (*etat*).

**ChUp 3.12.5: The Four-Footed, Sixfold Structure**

*saiṣā catuṣpadā ṣaḍvidhā gāyatrī tadetadṛcābhyanūktam.*

This (*sā eṣā*) Gāyatrī (*gāyatrī*) is four-footed (*catuṣpadā*) and sixfold (*ṣaḍvidhā*); this (*tat etat*) is declared (*abhyanūktam*) by a Rigveda verse (*ṛcā*).

**ChUp 3.12.6: The Cosmic Ratio (Quoting Puruṣa Sūktam / RV 10.90.3)**

*tāvānāsya mahimā tato jyāyāṃśca pūruṣaḥ, pādo\'sya sarvā bhūtāni tripādasyāmṛtaṃ divi.*

Such (*tāvān*) is its (*asya*) grandeur/greatness (*mahimā*), and (*ca*) greater than that (*tataḥ jyāyān*) is the Cosmic mankind race (*pūruṣaḥ*); one foot/quarter (*pādaḥ*) of it (*asya*) is all (*sarvā*) manifest beings/creation (*bhūtāni*), while three feet/quarters (*tripād*) of it (*asya*) are the immortal (*amṛtaṃ*) in heaven realm (*divi*).

**(1). Etymological Decomposition of *Gāyatrī (Verse 3.12.1)***

Verse 3.12.1 provides a definition of *Gāyatrī* (*gāyati ca trāyate ca*) to demonstrate how speech constructs and stabilizes reality:

**Expressive Aspect (*Gāyati* / Speech --- *Vāc*):** Speech is the primary mechanism of manifestation. It provides the names, mental projections (*vitakkā*), definitions, and designations that delineate unarticulated phenomena into recognizable world-objects. Without *vāc*, the world remains an unformed, ungraspable expanse. *Vāc* creates order.

**Protective Aspect (*Trāyate* / Preservation):** By classifying and structuring raw cognitive and sensory inputs into structured meaning, *vāc* acts as a protective container. It anchors human experience, preventing the mind from collapsing into chaos or unvarnished perceptual overwhelm. Thus, *Gāyatrī* as *Vāc* both projects the world and preserves the cognitive boundary within it.

**(2). The Nesting Architecture (Verses 3.12.1--4)**

The text establishes a concentric model of spatial and metaphysical containment. Each successive layer rests upon (*abhiśritam*) and is bounded by (*na atyenti* --- does not extend beyond) the next, moving from the macrocosmic structural matrix down to the innermost locus of the spirit:

- **Level 1 (Cosmic Meter & Speech --- *Gāyatrī / Vāc*):** Speech is the primary enclosure containing all created or materialized entities (*bhūta*).

- **Level 2 (Macrocosmic Ground --- *Pṛthivī*):** The Earth is the concrete, physical foundation upon which all living beings rest and beyond which physical creatures cannot extend.

- **Level 3 (Microcosmic Container --- *Śarīram*):** The individual physical body encapsulates the person; within this physical frame, all vital faculties (*prāṇāḥ*) are grounded and bounded.

- **Level 4 (Innermost Locus --- *Hṛdaya / Antaḥpuruṣa*):** The heart (*hṛdayam*) is the core locus within the inner person or soul-spirit (*antaḥpuruṣa* / *ātmā*). It is here, at this innermost anchor, that the vital life-forces (*prāṇāḥ*) are ultimately supported and bounded.

**(3). The Structural Mechanics of \"Sixfold\" (*Ṣaḍvidhā*, Verse 3.12.5)**

In 3.12.5, *Gāyatrī* is defined as **four-footed** (*catuṣpadā*) and **sixfold** (*ṣaḍvidhā*). The \"four feet\" correspond to the four 6-syllable feet of the 24-syllable meter (also mirroring the 4 levels of containment listed above). The \"sixfold\" nature arises from the interaction of its six structural dimensions:

a.  **Bhūta (Manifest Beings):** The totality of created, physical, or objectified phenomena.

b.  **Gāyatrī (Cosmic Matrix):** The underlying structural meter that frames reality.

c.  **Vāc (Speech / Naming):** The expressor that formulates names, concepts, and labels.

d.  **Pṛthivī (Physical Earth):** The foundational macrocosmic plane of existence.

e.  **Śarīra (Bodily Enclosure):** The physical vessel housing the individual life-process.

f.  **Hṛdaya (Inner Heart Locus):** The central point within the inner person where life-forces (*prāṇāḥ*) are anchored.

**(4). The Exit Ratio & Transcendence (Verse 3.12.6)**

Verse 3.12.6 invokes the **Puruṣa Sūktam** (Rigveda 10.90.3) to establish a strict cosmological ratio between the manifest world and ultimate liberation:

- **One-Fourth (*Eka-pāda / Pādo\'sya sarvā bhūtāni*):** The totality of the visible universe---including speech (*vāc*), the earth plane (*pṛthivī*), the physical body (*śarīram*), and the heart locus (*hṛdayam*)---comprises merely **one quarter** of total reality. This entire manifest quarter operates strictly under cause and effect, impermanence, and decay.

- **Three-Fourths (*Tripād / Tripādasyāmṛtaṃ divi*):** The remaining **three quarters** exist in the immortal realm (*amṛtaṃ divi*).

**B: ChUp 3.13.1--6: How Things Works - The Operational Mechanics of Mind & ChUp 3.13.7: Senses & Bringing Them to Completion - The Ultimate Realization - ChUp 3.13.1 --- The Eastern Gate: Prāṇa, Sight, and the Sun:** *tasya ha vā etasya hṛdayasya pañca devasuṣayaḥ; sa yo\'sya prāṅ suṣiḥ sa prāṇastaccakṣuḥ sa ādityaḥ; tadetattejo\'nnādamityupāsīta, tejasvī annādo bhavati ya evaṃ veda.*

Of that (*tasya*) indeed (*ha vai*) this (*etasya*) heart (*hṛdayasya*) there are five (*pañca*) divine openings/apertures (*devasuṣayaḥ*); that which (*sa yaḥ*) is its (*asya*) eastern (*prāṅ*) aperture (*suṣiḥ*), that is *prāṇa* (*sa prāṇaḥ*), that is the eye/sight (*tat cakṣuḥ*), that is the sun (*sa ādityaḥ*); one should meditate on (*upāsīta*) that (*tat etat*) as radiance (*tejaḥ*) and as the eater of food (*annādam*); radiant (*tejasvī*) and an eater of food (*annādaḥ*) becomes (*bhavati*) he who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) thus (*evaṃ*).

**ChUp 3.13.2 --- The Southern Gate: Vyāna, Hearing, and the Moon:** *atha yo\'sya dakṣiṇaḥ suṣiḥ sa vyānastacchrotraṃ sa candramāḥ; tadetacchrīśca yaśaścetyupāsīta, śrīmān yaśasvī bhavati ya evaṃ veda.*

Now (*atha*) that which (*yaḥ*) is its (*asya*) southern (*dakṣiṇaḥ*) aperture (*suṣiḥ*), that is *vyāna* (*sa vyānaḥ*), that is the ear/hearing (*tat śrotraṃ*), that is the moon (*sa candramāḥ*); one should meditate on (*upāsīta*) that (*tat etat*) as prosperity (*śrīḥ*) and fame (*yaśaḥ*); prosperous (*śrīmān*) and famous (*yaśasvī*) becomes (*bhavati*) he who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) thus (*evaṃ*).

**ChUp 3.13.3 --- The Western Gate: Apāna, Speech, and Agni:** *atha yo\'sya pratyaṅ suṣiḥ sa apānastāgvāk sa agniḥ; adetadbrahmavarcasamannādamityupāsīta, brahmavarcasī annādo bhavati ya evaṃ veda.*

Now (*atha*) that which (*yaḥ*) is its (*asya*) western (*pratyaṅ*) aperture (*suṣiḥ*), that is *apāna* (*sa apānaḥ*), that is speech (*tat vāk*), that is fire (*sa agniḥ*); one should meditate on (*upāsīta*) that (*tat etat*) as spiritual radiance (*brahmavarcasam*) and as the eater of food (*annādam*); spiritally radiant (*brahmavarcasī*) and an eater of food (*annādaḥ*) becomes (*bhavati*) he who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) thus (*evaṃ*).

**ChUp 3.13.4 --- The Northern Gate: Samāna, Mind, and Parjanya:** *atha yo\'sya udan suṣiḥ sa samānastanmanaḥ sa parjanyaḥ; tadetatkīrtiśca vyuṣṭiścetyupāsīta, kīrtimān vyuṣṭimān bhavati ya evaṃ veda.*

Now (*atha*) that which (*yaḥ*) is its (*asya*) northern (*udan*) aperture (*suṣiḥ*), that is *samāna* (*sa samānaḥ*), that is the mind (*tat manaḥ*), that is the rain-god (*sa parjanyaḥ*); one should meditate on (*upāsīta*) that (*tat etat*) as renown (*kīrtiḥ*) and grace/splendor (*vyuṣṭiḥ*); renowned (*kīrtimān*) and splendid (*vyuṣṭimān*) becomes (*bhavati*) he who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) thus (*evaṃ*).

**ChUp 3.13.5 --- The Upper Gate: Udāna, Touch/Wind, and Ākāśa:** *atha yo\'sya ūrdhvaḥ suṣiḥ sa udānastadvāyuḥ sa ākāśaḥ; tadetadojaśca mahaścetyupāsīta, ojasvī mahīyān bhavati ya evaṃ veda.*

Now (*atha*) that which (*yaḥ*) is its (*asya*) upper (*ūrdhvaḥ*) aperture (*suṣiḥ*), that is *udāna* (*sa udānaḥ*), that is the wind/touch (*tat vāyuḥ*), that is space/ether (*sa ākāśaḥ*); one should meditate on (*upāsīta*) that (*tat etat*) as vital strength (*ojaḥ*) and greatness (*mahaḥ*); strong (*ojasvī*) and great (*mahīyān*) becomes (*bhavati*) he who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) thus (*evaṃ*).

**ChUp 3.13.6 --- The Five Gatekeepers of the Heart:** *te vā ete pañca brahma-puruṣāḥ svargasya lokasya dvārapāḥ; sa ya etānevaṃ pañca brahma-puruṣān svargasya lokasya dvārapān vedāsya kule vīro jāyate, jayati svargaṃ lokaṃ ya etānevaṃ pañca brahma-puruṣān svargasya lokasya dvārapān veda.*

These (*te*) indeed (*vai*) these five (*ete pañca*) are the guardians of the Brahman Soul-Spirit *ātmā* (*brahma-puruṣāḥ*), the gatekeepers (*dvārapāḥ*) of the heavenly world (*svargasya lokasya*); he (*saḥ*) who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) these (*etān*) five (*pañca*) guardians of the Brahman Soul-Spirit *ātmā* (*brahma-puruṣān*), the gatekeepers (*dvārapān*) of the heavenly world (*svargasya lokasya*) thus (*evaṃ*), in his (*asya*) lineage (*kule*) a hero/spiritual champion (*vīraḥ*) is born (*jāyate*); he conquers (*jayati*) the heavenly world (*svargaṃ lokaṃ*), he who (*yaḥ*) knows (*veda*) these (*etān*) five (*pañca*) guardians of the Brahman Soul-Spirit *ātmā* (*brahma-puruṣān*) as the gatekeepers (*dvārapān*) of the heavenly world (*svargasya lokasya*) thus (*evaṃ*).

**ChUp 3.13.7 --- The Light Beyond Heaven (*Paro Divo Dyotate*):** *atha yadataḥ paro divo dyotate viśvataḥ pṛṣṭheṣu sarvataḥ pṛṣṭheṣvanuttameṣūttameṣu lokeṣvidaṃ vāva tad yadidamasminnantaḥ puruṣe jyotiḥ.*

Now (*atha*) that which (*yat*) shines (*dyotate*) beyond (*paraḥ*) this (*ataḥ*) heaven (*divaḥ*), beyond all (*viśvataḥ pṛṣṭheṣu*), beyond everything (*sarvataḥ pṛṣṭheṣu*), in the highest (*uttameṣu*) unsurpassed (*anuttameṣu*) worlds (*lokeṣu*); that (*tat*) indeed (*vāva*) is this (*idaṃ*) light (*jyotiḥ*) which (*yat*) is this (*idaṃ*) inside person - the Soul-Spirit *ātmā* (*antaḥ puruṣe*) within (*asmin*).

**(1). The Micro-Macro Structural Alignment (*AdhyĀtmā & Adhidaiva*)**

The five apertures (*devasuṣayaḥ*) of the heart serve as the operational interface connecting the internal soul-spirit *Ātman (adhyĀtmā*) residing within the heart with cosmic deities and powers (*adhidaiva*). The senses and vital breaths do not function autonomously; they are functional rays extending directly from the inner *Ātman*:

- **Eastern Gate (*Prāṅ*)** --- Governed by the in-breath (*Prāṇa*), expressing the inner soul-spirit (*AdhyĀtmā*) through sight (*Cakṣu*), aligned with the Sun (*Āditya*), manifesting as radiance and food (*Tejas / Annāda*).

- **Southern Gate (*Dakṣiṇa*)** --- Governed by the pervading breath (*Vyāna*), expressing the inner soul-spirit (*AdhyĀtmā*) through hearing (*Śrotra*), aligned with the Moon (*Candramāḥ*), manifesting as prosperity and fame (*Śrī / Yaśas*).

- **Western Gate (*Pratyaṅ*)** --- Governed by the out-breath (*Apāna*), expressing the inner soul-spirit (*AdhyĀtmā*) through speech (*Vāk*), aligned with Fire (*Agni*), manifesting as spiritual radiance (*Brahmavarcasa*).

- **Northern Gate (*Udan*)** --- Governed by the equalizing breath (*Samāna*), expressing the inner soul-spirit (*AdhyĀtmā*) through the mind (*Manas*), aligned with the Rain-God (*Parjanya*), manifesting as renown and splendor (*Kīrti / Vyuṣṭi*).

- **Upper Gate (*Ūrdhva*)** --- Governed by the ascending breath (*Udāna*), expressing the inner soul-spirit (*AdhyĀtmā*) through touch and wind (*Vāyu*), aligned with Space (*Ākāśa*), manifesting as vital strength and greatness (*Ojas / Mahas*).

**(2). The Mechanics of the Heart as the Fortress (*Brahmapura*):** The text designates these five gates as the **guardians of Brahman Soul-Spirit** ***ātmā*** (*brahma-puruṣāḥ*) and the **gatekeepers of the heavenly realm** (*svargasya lokasya dvārapāḥ*):

- **Locus of the Inner Soul-Spirit:** The inner soul-spirit (*antaḥpuruṣa* / *Ātman*) is enthroned within the heart chamber (*hṛdayam*). The mind-will (*manas*), senses (*indriya*), and vital breaths (*prāṇāḥ*) act as outward-projecting channels originating from this central *Ātman*.

- **Gateway Governance:** The five vital breaths govern the intake and projection of physical and mental energy. By governing these five directional apertures, the *Ātman* prevents cognitive pollution and preserves the purity of the inner sanctum.

**(3). Transcendence and Beyond the Cosmic Container (Verses 3.13.6--7)**

Verses 3.13.6--7 map the relationship between the five internal gateways, the dual nature of the cosmic container, and the true exit of liberation:

> **The Conditioned One-Quarter (*Pāda*):** The one-quarter visible, manifested, and aggregated part of existence---including the five directional gates, the mind, the senses, and the physical vessel (*śarīram*)---belongs to the world (*loka*). Universal laws were conditioned into every existence when they aggregated. They rise and fall, are subject to death, and are **not our true self -- soul-spirit *ātmā***.

- **The Three-Fourths Realm (*Tripād*) as the Unmanifest Container:** The three-fourths realm (*tripād* in *divi*) represents the unmanifest, invisible, and unaggregated layer of the universe---the realm of raw consciousness units, cosmic dust, non-duality, and the death/dissolution of the soul-spirit *ātmā*. This three-fourths realm is **still contained within the cosmic container box** (*loka*); it is merely the unmanifest substrate of the world, not the home of true liberation.

- **The Light Beyond Heaven (*Paro Divo Dyotate*):** Verse 3.13.7 points to that which shines **beyond** (*paraḥ*) this heaven (*divaḥ*) and beyond all worlds (*sarvataḥ pṛṣṭheṣu*). True liberation requires transcending both the one-quarter manifest world and the three-fourths unmanifest cosmic container.

- **The Threshold of Complete Transcendence:** Real freedom is realized when the **envelop of soul-spirit *ātmā, the*** ***citta***, through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, turns away from the five gatekeepers, disenchants itself from both manifest aggregations and unmanifest non-dual existence, leaves the entire container box universe behind, and transcends to the permanent deathless realm outside.

**2). Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad (1.5)**

**BU 1.5.1--2 --- The Creation of the Seven Foods (*Saptānnāni*):** *yat saptānnāni medhayā tapasājanayat pitā \| ekam asya sādhāraṇam, dve devān akalpayat \| trīṇi Ātmane\'kuruta, paśubhya ekaṃ prāyachat\...*

When (*yat*) the Father (*pitā*) produced (*ajanayat*) the seven foods (*saptānnāni*) through intelligence (*medhayā*) and primordial force (*tapasā*): One (*ekam*) was common (*sādhāraṇam*) to all beings, two (*dve*) he assigned to the gods (*devān akalpayat*), three (*trīṇi*) he made for the soul-spirit *Ātman* (*Ātmane akuruta*), and one (*ekam*) he gave to the animals (*paśubhyaḥ prāyachat*)\...

**BU 1.5.3 --- The Three Foods of the Soul-Spirit Ātman (*Trīṇi Ātmane*):** *trīṇi Ātmane\'kuruteti mano vācaṃ prāṇaṃ tānyĀtmane\'kuruta; 'anyatramanā abhūvaṃ nādarśam', 'anyatramanā abhūvaṃ nāśrauṣam' iti, menasā hyeva paśyati menasā śṛṇoti\...*

Three (*trīṇi*) he made for the soul-spirit *Ātman* (*Ātmane akuruta*)\" --- these are Mind-will (*Manas*), Speech (*Vāk*), and Vital lifeforce (*Prāṇa*); these (*tāni*) he made for the soul-spirit *Ātman* (*Ātmane akuruta*). \[People say:\] \"My mind-will was elsewhere (*anyatramanāḥ*), I did not see (*nādarśam*); my mind-will was elsewhere (*anyatramanāḥ*), I did not hear (*nāśrauṣam*).\" Indeed, through the mind-will *mana* (*manasā hi eva*) one sees (*paśyati*), through the mind-will *mana* one hears (*śṛṇoti*)\...

**BU 1.5.4--13 --- The Structural Triads of the Manifest Container**

BU 1.5 systematically categorizes the entire universe into fundamental **triads**, demonstrating how the three foods made for the soul-spirit *Ātman* (*Vāk*, *Manas*, *Prāṇa*) correspond directly to the realms, the Vedas/Knowledges, and cosmic domains:

- **Speech (*Vāk*)** --- Earthly Realm (*Loka*), Rigveda / Earthly Knowledge, Fire (*Agni*), Present Time / Manifest Form.

- **Mind-will (*Manas*)** --- Intermediate Heavenly Realm (*Antarikṣa*), Yajurveda / Sacrificial Knowledge, Moon (*Candramas*), Past Time / Unmanifest Processing.

- **Vital Lifeforce (*Prāṇa*)** --- Upper Heavenly Realm (*Dyauḥ*), Sāmaveda / Celestial Knowledge, Sun (*Āditya*), Future Time / Ascending Energy.

BU 1.5 describes a Father/Progenitor (*Prajāpati*) creates beings and assigning foods. The three internal foods for the soul-spirit *Ātman* (*Vāk*, *Manas*, *Prāṇa*) directly mirror the external:

- **Speech (*Vāk*)** --- Represents the manifest, the gross physical world (*bhūr*), the aggregates.

- **Mind-will (*Manas*)** --- Represents the subtle mental world (*bhuvaḥ*), internal processing, power of the mind.

- **Vital Lifeforce (*Prāṇa*)** --- Represents the life energy (*svar*), ascending power, and foundation for psychic power.

Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad 1.5.14--23: tracing the cosmic contest between the organs, the *Prāṇa* survives physical death, and its ultimate limits.

**BU 1.5.14--15 --- Prajāpati as Time and the Sixteen Digits (*So\'yaṃ saṃvatsaraḥ*):** *sa eṣa saṃvatsaraḥ prajāpatiḥ ṣoḍaśakalaḥ\... tasya rātraya eva pañcadaśa kalāḥ, dhruvaivāsya ṣoḍaśī kalā; sa rātribhir evā ca pūryate \'pa ca kṣīyate\...* This (*saḥ eṣaḥ*) Father/Progenitor (*prajāpatiḥ*) is the year (*saṃvatsaraḥ*), consisting of sixteen digits (*ṣoḍaśakalaḥ*)\... His fifteen digits (*pañcadaśa kalāḥ*) are the nights (*rātrayaḥ*); his sixteenth digit (*ṣoḍaśī kalā*) is fixed/enduring (*dhruvā*). He waxes (*āpūryate*) and wanes (*apakṣīyate*) by means of the nights\...

**BU 1.5.21 --- The Cosmic Contest and the Exhaustion of Senses (*Athāto vratamīmāṃsā*):** *athāto vratamīmāṃsā: prajāpatir ha karmāṇi sasṛje, tāny anyonyenāspardhanta---'vadiṣyāmy evāham' iti vāg dadhre, 'draakṣyāmy aham' iti cakṣuḥ, 'śroṣyāmy aham' iti śrotram; evam anyāni karmāṇi yathākarma; tāni śrameṇa mṛtyur upayeje, tāny āpnot, tāny āptvā mṛtyur avārundha; tasmād śrāmyaty eva vāk, śrāmyati cakṣuḥ, śrāmyati śrotram; athaitam eva nāpnot yo\'yaṃ madhyamaḥ prāṇaḥ\...*

Now begins the inquiry into vows (*athāto vratamīmāṃsā*): Father/Progenitor (*prajāpatiḥ*) created the active organs (*karmāṇi*). They competed with one another (*anyonyenāspardhanta*)---Speech (*vāk*) resolved: \"I will keep speaking\"; Sight (*cakṣuḥ*) resolved: \"I will keep seeing\"; Hearing (*śrotram*) resolved: \"I will keep hearing.\" So did the other organs according to their functions. Death (*mṛtyuḥ*) approached them in the form of fatigue (*śrameṇa*), overtook them (*tāny āpnot*), and having overtaken them, Death bound/held them back (*avārundha*). Therefore Speech tires (*śrāmyati*), Sight tires, Hearing tires. But Death could not overtake (*nāpnot*) this central vital lifeforce (*madhyamaḥ prāṇaḥ*)\...

**BU 1.5.22--23 --- Prāṇa as the Unbroken Vow (*Athāto pavamānaḥ*):** *tāni śerata: 'ayam eva naḥ śreṣṭho yaḥ saṃcaranṃś cāsaṃcaraṃś ca na vyathate, atho na riṣyati; hantāsyaiva sarve rūpaṃ prasajjāmā' iti; teta etasyaiva sarve rūpam abhavan, tasmād etenaivākhyāyante 'prāṇā' iti\... tasya ha vā etasya devatāyai apacitiṃ jigīṣati\... \'yadamūṁ rātriṃ paśyāmi, amūṁ rātriṃ paśyāmi\' iti, tasmād etad eva vidvān ekam eva vrataṁ cared: prāṇyāc caivāpānyāc ca, \'mā mā pāpmā mṛtyur āpnuvad\' iti\...*

The other organs recognized: \"This one (*ayam*) is indeed the greatest among us (*naḥ śreṣṭhaḥ*), who moving (*saṃcaran*) or remaining still (*asaṃcaran*) suffers no distress (*na vyathate*) nor perishes (*na riṣyati*). Come, let us all take on its form.\" Therefore, all of them took on its form, and they are named after it: *prāṇas* (*prāṇāḥ*)\... Therefore, let a person keep this single vow (*ekaṃ vrataṃ cared*): breathing in (*prāṇyāt*) and breathing out (*apānyāt*), saying, \"May evil, which is Death (*pāpmā mṛtyuḥ*), not overtake me (*mā mā āpnuvat*)\...\"

**(1). Senses Subject to Death (*Mṛtyu*) vs. Central Vital Lifeforce (*Madhyama Prāṇa*)**

The narrative of BU 1.5.21 outlines a fundamental functional hierarchy among the three internal foods (*Vāk*, *Manas*, *Prāṇa*):

- **The Weariness of Peripheral Organs:** Speech (*Vāk*), Sight (*Cakṣu*), Hearing (*Śrotra*), and Mind (*Manas*) operate in discrete, periodic bursts. Because their activities are outward-directed and subject to exhaustion (*śrama*), Death (*mṛtyu*) overtakes them, forcing them into states of cessation (sleep, unconsciousness, fatigue).

- **The Immunity of Madhyama Prāṇa:** The central vital lifeforce (*madhyamaḥ prāṇaḥ*) functions continuously without fatigue whether moving or resting (*saṃcaranṃś cāsaṃcaraṃś ca*). Physical death cannot overtake it. Thus, the other faculties merge their identity into *Prāṇa*, making *Prāṇa* the chief coordinator of all sensory functions (*prāṇāḥ*).

**(2). The Cycles of Prajāpati (The Sixteen Digits)**

Verses 1.5.14--15 describe how Father/Progenitor creates his progeny and designing their internal architecture, lifespans, and physical/mental dynamic functions.

**(1). The 16-Fold Blueprint of the Father/Progenitor**

The Father/Progenitor (*Prajāpati*) embodies the complete year (*saṃvatsara*), which serves as the temporal master-blueprint for all his descendants. He structures life into **16 dynamic parts (*kalāḥ*)**:

**15 Variable Digits (*Pañcadaśa Kalāḥ*):** These govern all active, changing functions of the living being---the physical body, external wealth, mental projections, speech, sensory activities, and bodily cycles. Just as the moon waxes and wanes through 15 nights, these functions naturally expand, contract, fatigue, and undergo change throughout a creature\'s lifetime.

**1st to 15th Phases (Life Activities):** Consuming food, growing, acting, losing physical strength, gaining or losing resources---all of these daily life activities are driven by these 15 fluctuating digits designed by the Father.

**16th Hidden Substrate (*Dhruvā Kalā*):** This is the core life-force (*Prāṇa*) that the Father places within his progeny. On the new moon night (*Amāvāsyā*), this 16th digit remains hidden inside water, seeds, and living beings, sustaining life behind the scenes when outer activities rest.

**(2). How the Father/Progenitor Designs the Human Individual**

In verse 1.5.15, the text applies this 16-fold design directly to how a human descendant operates:

**The Outer Shell (15 Digits):** the physical body, sensory faculties, speech, and material possessions are the 15 outer digits. They fluctuate continuously. If a person suffers losses, ages, or loses wealth, they are simply experiencing the natural waning of these 15 structural digits.

**The Axle/Hub of the Wheel (16th Digit):** The Father/Progenitor positioned **Prāṇa** as the central nave of the wheel. The text notes that even if a descendant loses all 15 outer digits (wealth, possessions, physical vigor), so long as the 16th digit (*Prāṇa*) remains intact, the individual continues to live and can regenerate their outer functions. The outer body and possessions are merely the perimeter; *Prāṇa* is the hub designed by the Father to hold the descendant together.

**3). The Jain (*Samaṇa*) Layer**

In the earliest Jain texts---primarily the **Ācārāṅga Sūtra** (Pāli/Prakrit: *Āyāraṁga Sutta*) and the **Sūtrakṛtāṅga Sūtra** (*Sūyagaḍaṁga*)---knowledge (*jñāna*) was understood as the removal of karmic veils through **primordial force** (*tapasā*). The focus was strictly on self-restraint and liberation from the world (*loka*).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.5.3 (Discrimination of Karmic Veils):**

*tammā beyaṁmi na majjhejja\... jeṇaṁ aṇāeyaṁ, jeṇaṁ aṇabhikkaṁtaṁ, taṁ saṁikkhae\...*

Therefore, one should not become intoxicated with worldly powers or higher perceptions\... Having burned away the veil through **primordial force** (*tapasā*), the *Samaṇa* clearly sees what is past and what is to come, yet remains unattached, knowing all worldly phenomena belong to the sphere of decay.

**Sūtrakṛtāṅga Sūtra 1.1.2.22 (Warning Against Superpowers):** *jāyā ya iddhi ya siloga-pūjā, taṁ kahissāmi ya laddha-jaṁtaṁ \| eayaṁ paḍibūhayaṁti mūḍhā, saṁsāra-taraṇaṁ na hu eya-mohaṁ \|\|*

Miraculous powers (*iddhi* / *ṛddhi*), fame, praise, and the acquisition of spells---the foolish celebrate these as achievements! But these do not cross over *saṃsāra*; they are merely delusion and worldly entanglement.

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.1.8.5 (The Ultimate Knowledge - Kevala):** *se kevalaṁ sarva-lokam-avekkhae, passatī iha-loyaṁ paraloyaṁ \| jāṇaṁ chāmaṁ samiyassa appaṇo, appāṇaṁ sarva-lokāṇaṁ-avekkhae \|\|*

He (**se**) who (**jaṁ**) has fully known (**abbhaññāsi**) the complete, absolute knowledge (**kevalaṁ**) and perceives (**avekkhae**) the entire cosmos / all worlds (**sarva-lokaṁ**), he (**se**) indeed (**vai**) knows (**jāṇai**) the primary cause (**mūlaṁ**)---that (**taṁ**) which is the root (**mūlaṁ**) of karma (**kammassa**)---and knowing (**ñāṇā**) this, he (**se**) abandons (**pahāya**) all attachment / worldly bondage (**saṅgaṁ**).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.3.4.1:** *se sāmāiyeṇaṃ paddha-kammassa viyaḍa-raeo, kevalaṃ sarva-lokam-avekkhae, saiyaṃ dhīro tam-āhu kevali-ṇāṇaṃ.*

He (*se*) through utter equanimity (*sāmāiyeṇaṃ*) has dismantled binding karma (*paddha-kammassa*) and shaken off all karmic dust (*viyaḍa-raeo*); he perceives (*avekkhae*) the entire world (*sarva-lokaṃ*) with absolute omniscience (*kevalaṃ*); the steadfast sage (*dhīro*) continuously (*saiyaṃ*) calls (*āhu*) that (*tam*) state the supreme knowledge of the Kevalin (*kevali-ṇāṇaṃ*).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.4.1.2:** *saṃkhāya logaṃ pahiyaṃ ca kaṃmaṃ, iha-meva kevalam-aṇuppattā-e, sa sarva-dukkha-prahīṇa-m-aṇusāsai.*

Having fully discerned (*saṃkhāya*) the world (*logaṃ*) and cast off (*pahiyaṃ*) all karma (*kaṃmaṃ*), right here in this very life (*iha-meva*) he attains (*aṇuppattā-e*) the absolute knowledge (*kevalam*); having completely destroyed (*prahīṇa*) all dukkha (*sarva-dukkha*), he (*sa*) instructs the world (*aṇusāsai*).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.5.6.4:** *n\'eva dīhe na hassa, na vaṭṭe na taṃse na caturaṃse na parimaṇḍale, na kāhaṃ na ṇīlaṃ na lohiyaṃ na haliddiyaṃ na sukkaṃ, na subbha-gandha na dubbha-gandha, na titta na kaḍuka na kasāya na amba na madhura, na khara na mauda na guru na lahu na sīda na uṇha na niddha na rukka, na kāo na puṇabbao na saṅgo na strī na purisa na napuṃsaka, saññā-pariṇṇā-vihāro, na saddho na rūpaṃ, sabbe sarā nivattāṃti, jatt\'ha ṭhio mukto.*

It is neither (*n\'eva*) long (*dīhe*) nor (*na*) short (*hassa*), nor (*na*) round (*vaṭṭe*), nor (*na*) triangular (*taṃse*), nor (*na*) square (*caturaṃse*), nor (*na*) circular (*parimaṇḍale*); nor (*na*) black (*kāhaṃ*), nor (*na*) blue (*ṇīlaṃ*), nor (*na*) red (*lohiyaṃ*), nor (*na*) yellow (*haliddiyaṃ*), nor (*na*) white (*sukkaṃ*); nor (*na*) sweet-smelling (*subbha-gandha*), nor (*na*) foul-smelling (*dubbha-gandha*); nor (*na*) bitter (*titta*), nor (*na*) pungent (*kaḍuka*), nor (*na*) astringent (*kasāya*), nor (*na*) sour (*amba*), nor (*na*) sweet (*madhura*); nor (*na*) hard (*khara*), nor (*na*) soft (*mauda*), nor (*na*) heavy (*guru*), nor (*na*) light (*lahu*), nor (*na*) cold (*sīda*), nor (*na*) hot (*uṇha*), nor (*na*) smooth (*niddha*), nor (*na*) rough (*rukka*); it has no (*na*) physical body (*kāo*), no (*na*) rebirth (*puṇabbao*), no (*na*) material attachment (*saṅgo*), and no (*na*) female (*strī*), male (*purisa*), or neuter gender (*napuṃsaka*); dwelling (*vihāro*) in complete comprehension of worldly designations (*saññā-pariṇṇā*), it is without (*na*) sound (*saddho*) and without (*na*) physical form (*rūpaṃ*); all (*sabbe*) verbal expressions (*sarā*) turn back (*nivattāṃti*), where (*jatt\'ha*) the liberated soul (*mukto*) stands transcended (*ṭhio*).

**Mukto**/ *Mukta (***Prakrit***)***: Sanskrit** *Muktaḥ:* \"to release,\" \"to unbind,\" \"to untie a knot,\" \"to shed,\" or \"to let go.\"

- *Mukto* does not mean a passive \"saved state\" or an abstract psychological feeling. It specifies the **mechanical severance** of the soul (*jīva*) from karmic matter (*pudgala*).

- When all karmic bonds (*bandha*) are snapped through **primordial force** (*tapasā*), the *jīva* is untied from the physical body, sensory faculties, and temporal cycles of the world (*loka*).

**Ṭhio /** *Ṭhiya:* **Sanskrit** *Sthitaḥ:* \"to stand firm,\" \"to remain motionless,\" \"to abide permanently,\" or \"to stay established without shifting.\"

- *Ṭhio* defines **absolute, un-shakable permanence**. In *saṃsāra*, living beings are perpetually moving, wandering (*saṃsaraṇa*), taking rebirth, and rising and falling across realms.

- Once liberated, the *jīva* stops wandering. It rises upward to the crest of the cosmos (*Siddha-śilā*) and stands (*ṭhio*) permanently in its own intrinsic, isolated nature (*kaivalya*), never again descending or moving under the force of cause and effect.

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.73 (The State of the Liberated):** *kaṃma-vippamukko ya ṇaṃ appā, aṇantā-ṇantaṃ sāmāiya-m-aṇuppatto, ṭhio a-loka-ss'anta-ya-m-āiccadi.*

The soul-spirit Ātmā (*appā*) being fully released from karma (*kaṃma-vippamukko*), having attained (*aṇuppatto*) infinite and uninterrupted equanimity (*aṇantā-ṇantaṃ sāmāiya*), stands (*ṭhio*) out of (a) the boundary of the world (*loka-ss'anta*) there (ya) He reaches (*āiccadi*) (*a-loka-ss'anta-ya-m-āiccadi*).

**Appā (Ātmā):** It denotes the **intrinsic subject**, the self, or the pure underlying spiritual agent when discussing agency, self-restraint, self-realization, or self-knowledge.
