**Jīva (𑀚𑀻𑀯):** Derived from the verbal root √jīv (to live, to sustain life). It denotes the **living entity** as an ontological substance (*dravya*), defined by life-force (*prāṇa*), sentience (*cetana*), and its structural opposition to non-living matter (*ajīva* / *pudgala*).

**Jīva** is the categorical substance term used when analyzing the mechanics of the universe (*loka*). It is one of the foundational *tattvas* (reals): *Jīva* (living substance) vs. *Ajīva* (non-living substance).

**Appā** is the internal, subjective term used when addressing discipline, self-exertion, and liberation. You restrain *yourself* (*appā*); you do not speak of restraining the abstract category of *jīva*.

**Jīva** can refer to the soul in any state---whether bound by karmic matter (*saṃsārī jīva*), embodied as a plant/animal, or liberated (*mukta jīva*).

**Appā** is used in texts to denote the direct internal locus of effort---the self that must be conquered (*appā jetavvo*), purified, or recognized as distinct from karmic dust.

**Self-Mastery using Appā (Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 9.34):** *appā ṇadi vetaraṇī, appā me kūḍa-sāmali \| appā kāma-duhā dhenu, appā me ṇaṃdaṇaṃ vaṇaṃ \|\|*

The self (*appā*) is the river Vaitaraṇī (hell-river); the self (*appā*) is the thorny Śālmali tree; the self (*appā*) is the wish-fulfilling cow; the self (*appā*) indeed is the joyful celestial garden (*ṇaṃdaṇaṃ vaṇaṃ*).

*(Here, **Appā** is the internal essence responsible for its own self-directed destiny, bondage, or liberation).*

**Categorical Reality using Jīva (Dasaveyāliya Sutta 4.10):** *jīve a-jīve ya viyāṇiyāṇam, saṁsaḍi-joge ya viyāṇiyāṇam \|*

Knowing clearly the living entity substance (*jīve*) and the non-living matter substance (*a-jīve*), and knowing clearly the combinations of worldly entanglement (*saṁsaḍi-joge*)\...

*(Here, **Jīva** is the formal substance classification contrasted against matter, not a reflexive personal self).*

**Conquering the Self (Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 1.15):** *appā jiaṃ tu sejjo, yaṃ jiaṃ itarā pajā \| jiya-ss'a jiao hoï, asaiṃ pabea-m-āiccadi \|\|*

Conquering (*jiaṃ*) oneself (*appā*) is far better (*sejjo*) than conquering (*yaṃ jiaṃ*) all other living beings (*itarā pajā*); he who has conquered (*jiao*) the self (*jiya-ss'a*) stands victorious (*hoï*) and does not enter repeated defeat.

**Jīva:** The living substance (*dravya*) that gets coated by physical karmic matter (*pudgala*) inside the world (*loka*).

**Appā:** The internal self/spirit that must apply **primordial force** (*tapasā*) to master its own senses, stop generating mental projections, and sever its connection to karmic bondages.

**Both terms are used together with liberated state**, but they express two different dimensions of the same reality at the moment of liberation:

**Appā (The Self/Subject):** Used when describing the **internal realization, agency, and direct experience** of liberation (*\"The self, purified, stands\...\"*).

**Jīva (The Entity/Substance):** Used when describing the **ontological result**---that the living substance (*jīva*), now stripped of karmic matter (*ajīva*), stands out of the cosmos.

**Appā (The Self Stands Liberated)**: focuses on the practitioner\'s inner spirit attaining unshakeable equanimity and standing firm:

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.73:** kaṃma-vippamukko ya ṇaṃ appā, aṇantā-ṇantaṃ sāmāiya-m-aṇuppatto, ṭhio a-loka-ss'anta-ya-m-āiccadi.

The self / soul-spirit (**appā**), being fully released from karma (**kaṃma-vippamukko**), having attained (**aṇuppatto**) infinite and uninterrupted equanimity (**aṇantā-ṇantaṃ sāmāiya**), stands firm (**ṭhio**) out of (a) the boundary of the world (*loka-ss'anta*) there (ya) He reaches (*āiccadi*) (**a-loka-ss'anta-ya-m-āiccadi**).

**Jīva (The Living Entity Stands Liberated):** analyzes the physical departure of the entity from karmic matter and its ascension (*Siddha-śilā*):

**Dravyasaṃgraha 14:** jīvo ṇik-kaṃmo hoūṇa ūdha-gamaṇo, ṭhio loga-sehar\'ae\...

The living entity (**jīvo**), becoming entirely free of karma (**ṇik-kaṃmo hoūṇa**), moves naturally upward (**ūdha-gamaṇo**), and stands firm (**ṭhio**) out of the crest of the world (**loga-sehar\'ae**).

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 36.64:** siddhā ya sa-ra-jā-mukka-jīvā, ṭhi-ā siddhi-pahe saiyaṃ\...

The perfected ones (**siddhā**) are living entities (**jīvā**) completely freed from karmic dust (**sa-ra-jā-mukka**), standing firm (**ṭhi-ā**) continuously (**saiyaṃ**) on the path of absolute perfection (**siddhi-pahe**).

**Appā ṭhio:** \"I, the purified self, stand untethered.\" (Subjective realization)

**Jīva ṭhio:** \"The living substance stands separated from dead matter (*ajīva*).\" (Objective baseline)

Both terms confirm that whether viewed as the internal self (**appā**) or the living entity (**jīva**), the liberated state requires total severance from the world (*loka*).

**Dasaveyāliya Sutta 4.28 (The Unshakeable Abiding):** *savva-raṃya-vippahiṇo, mukto saṃsāra-bhayao \| niccaṃ ṭhio samāhi-ṃmi, na ya puṇo-iccadi saṃsaḍiṃ \|\|*

Cast off from all karmic dust (*savva-raṃya-vippahiṇo*), liberated (*mukto*) from the dread of cycle of existence (*saṃsāra-bhayao*), permanently (*niccaṃ*) established (*ṭhio*) in utter samādhi (*samāhi-ṃmi*), he never again enters (*na ya puṇo-iccadi*) worldly entanglement (*saṃsaḍiṃ*).

Together, **Mukto Ṭhio** encapsulates the entire goal of the *Samaṇa* path:

**Mukto:** The complete unbinding from karmic matter through **primordial force** (*tapasā*).

**Ṭhio:** The unmoving, permanent posturing of the consciousness (*citta*) outside the world.

The **Ultimate Objective** for Jain *Samaṇa* tradition (**Ācārāṅga Sūtra** and **Uttarādhyayana Sūtra**): illustrating the complete burning away of karmic coverings via **primordial force** (*tapasā*).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.7.8.23:** *The classic reservoir simile demonstrating the mechanical burning away of accumulated karmic deposits.*

*jaha mahā-dahe gahiye, puṇṇa-puvve ya saṃvude \| taha tapsā jhūya-kammassa, nirvāṇaṃ paḍiyajjai \|\|*

Just as (*jaha*) a vast reservoir (*mahā-dahe*), when its prior inflow channels (*puṇṇa-puvve*) are completely shut (*saṃvude*), dries up as the old water evaporates, even so (*taha*), for one whose accumulated karma is thoroughly burned away (*jhūya-kammassa*) through **primordial force** (*tapsā*), absolute liberation (*nirvāṇaṃ*) is realized (*paḍiyajjai*).

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.27:** *Defining the exact operational function of primordial force (tapasā) as karmic eradication (nirjarā).*

*tapsā ṇaṃ purāṇa-kaṃma-nirjaraṇaṃ jaṇayai \| nirjiṇṇa-kaṃme ya ṇaṃ appā, a-leya-m-āiccadi \|\|*

Through **primordial force** (*tapsā*), one directly generates (*jaṇayai*) the complete purging and burning away (*nirjaraṇaṃ*) of ancient accumulated karma (*purāṇa-kaṃma*); and with all karma burned away (*nirjiṇṇa-kaṃme*), the self (*appā*), out of (a) the boundary of the world (loka-ss'anta) there (ya) He reaches (āiccadi).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.8.1.4:** *Restraint of the three instruments (body, speech, mind) combined with tapasā to destroy karmic coverings.*

*kāyeṇa ya vaeṇa ya manasa-o ya, sarvehiṃ saṃjaehiṃ \| tapsā dhūṇa-kammāṇi, saṁsāra-m-āiccadi \|\|*

By fully restraining (*saṃjaehiṃ*) all (*sarvehiṃ*) channels of body (*kāyeṇa*), speech (*vaeṇa*), and mental will (*manasa-o*), and by shaking off karmic coverings (*dhūṇa-kammāṇi*) through **primordial force** (*tapsā*), one completely transcends (*āiccadi*) the cycle of existence (*saṁsāra*).

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 30.2:** *The distinction between stopping new karma and burning old karmic matter.*

*saṃvareṇa ya kaṃmāṇi, ṇova-īyaṃti koya-i \| tapsā puṇo purāṇāṇi, jhāya-ṁti ya dhūya-ṁti ya \|\|*

Through restraint (*saṃvareṇa*), no new karmas (*kaṃmāṇi*) are permitted to enter (*ṇova-īyaṃti*); while through **primordial force** (*tapsā*), all old karmic deposits (*purāṇāṇi*) are scorched (*jhāya-ṁti*) and completely burned away (*dhūya-ṁti*).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.2.2.1 (Nibbida & Virāga):** *logam-avagāhiyā, ṇibbida-m-aṇuppatte \| viratto sarva-saṅgehiṃ, kaṃmaṃ dhūṇai ṭhāṇavā \|\|*

Having thoroughly comprehended the world (*logam-avagāhiyā*), he attains complete disenchantment (**ṇibbida-m-aṇuppatte**); faded of attraction to all attachments (**viratto sarva-saṅgehiṃ**), standing firm (*ṭhāṇavā*), he burns away all karmic deposits (*kaṃmaṃ dhūṇai*).

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.57--59 (The Triad Chain):** *ṇibbidā-ye ṇaṃ sabba-logeusu virajjai\... virāga-ye ṇaṃ saṃsaḍi-joge vaiccadi\... vimuttā-ye ṇaṃ a-leya-m-āiccadi\...*

Through disenchantment (**ṇibbidā-ye**), one becomes completely detached (*virajjai*) from all world structures; through the fading of worldly attachment (**virāga-ye**), one breaks free from worldly entanglements (*saṃsaḍi-joge*); through liberation from the world (**vimuttā-ye**), out of (a) the boundary of the world (loka-ss'anta) there (ya) He reaches (āiccadi).

Theravada tradition redefined the Three Knowledges (*tevijja*) as three direct, internal insights into the nature of existence. In the *Sutta Nipāta* (AN 3.59 / *Tikanipāta*, *Jāṇussoṇi Sutta*), the Brahmana Jāṇussoṇi asks the Buddha what qualifies a person as a true *tevijja* according to the Dhamma, explicitly contrasting it with the Vedas.

***Jāṇussoṇi:** \"How, Master Gautama, is one a master of the three knowledges (tevijjo) according to the discipline of the Noble Ones?\"*

**The Buddha:** \"Here, Brahmana, a *bhikkhu* recollects his manifold **past abodes**\... this is his **first knowledge** (*paṭhamā vijjā*) attained by him; ignorance is dispelled, knowledge arises\... Further, with the divine eye\... he sees beings passing away and bound for **rebirth** according to their actions\... this is his **second knowledge** (*dutiyā vijjā*) attained by him\... Further, by the destruction of the taints (*āsavānaṃ khayā*), he enters and dwells in the taintless **liberation of mind**\... this is his **third knowledge** (*tatiyā vijjā*) attained by him\... It is in this way, Brahmana, that one is a master of the three knowledges (*tevijjo*) in the discipline of the Noble Ones.\"

**4) The Pali Canon layer:** The Pali Canon's direct psychic power realization.

In the *Tevijja Sutta* (Dīgha Nikāya 13), young Brahmanas debate the correct path to union with Brahmā based on their respective Vedic traditions. The Buddha points out that none of their teachers---nor the original Vedic seers---have ever seen Brahmā directly.

**The Buddha:** \"So then, Vāseṭṭha, not even one of the Brahmanas learned in the Three Vedas has seen Brahmā face to face\... Just as a line of blind men, clinging to one another, the first sees nothing, the middle sees nothing, the last sees nothing---so it is with the talk of these Brahmanas learned in the Three Vedas; their talk turns out to be ridiculous, mere words, empty and vain.\"

The early *samaṇa* critique targets reliance on inherited text (*anussava*) or ritual authority, emphasizing that true knowledge must be directly experienced (*paccakkha*). Of course this is purely sectarian tension and pride as we can see. There is no way one Samanera for his first year can study the three knowledges prescribed by the Pali Canon, as in ***Theragāthā* 1.24 (Sugandha)** \"Three knowledges are attained (*tisso vijjā anuppattā*), The teaching of the Buddha has been done (*kataṃ buddhassa sāsanaṃ*).\" And also **Theragāthā 6.2 (Elder Sīha):** \"I used to be a scholar of the three Vedas (*tevijjo*), A Brahmana proud of my birth and learning. Now I am a true *tevijja*, Master of the three knowledges, bearer of the final body, Having destroyed all craving and uprooted all taints.\"

**Dhammapada 387--388 & 422:** \"He who knows his former existences, who sees heaven and hell, Who has reached the destruction of birth, a sage who has completed full understanding, Who has reached the culmination of all attainments--- Him I call a true Brahmana, a master of the three knowledges (*tevijja*).\"

**1). First Knowledge:** Recollection of Past Existences (*Pubbenivāsānussati*)

The practitioner directs the purified, motionless mind-heart (*citta*) to recollect former lives: one birth, two births\... hundreds of thousands of births, spanning cycles of cosmic expansion and contraction.

**What is seen:** The practitioner remembers precise details: *\"There I had such a name, belonged to such a clan, had such an appearance (evaṃvaṇṇo), such was my food (evaṃāhāro), such my experience of pleasure and pain (evaṃsukhadukkhappaṭisaṃvedī), and such the end of my life (evaṃāyupariyanto). Passing away from that state, I arose elsewhere\...\"*

**2). Second Knowledge: The Divine Eye / Death and Reappearance (*Dibba-cakkhu / Cutūpapāta-ñāṇa*)**

With the mind (*citta*) purified, bright, unblemished, rid of imperfection, malleable, wieldy, steady, and attained to imperturbability (emerging from the fourth jhāna), the practitioner directs it to the knowledge of the passing away and reappearance of beings (*cutūpapāta-ñāṇa*). **What is seen:** With the divine eye (*dibba-cakkhu*), the practitioner sees beings passing away (*cavamāne*) and reappearing (*upapajjamāne*): **The Spectrum of Beings:** Inferior (*hīne*) and superior (*paṇīte*), beautiful (*suvaṇṇe*) and ugly (*dubbaṇṇe*), fortunate (*sugate*) and unfortunate (*duggate*).

**Direct Observation of Kamma Mechanics:** The practitioner discerns how beings fare according to their actions (*yathākammūpage satte pajānāti*): *\"These beings---who were endowed with bodily misconduct (kāyasucaritena / kāyaduccaritena), verbal misconduct, and mental misconduct; who reviled the noble ones (ariyānaṃ upavādakā); who held wrong views (micchādiṭṭhikā) and acted under the influence of wrong views---upon the dissolution of the body, after death, have reappeared in a state of deprivation, an unhappy destination, a lower realm, even in hell (niraya). But these beings---who were endowed with good conduct of body, speech, and mind; who did not revile the noble ones; who held right views (sammādiṭṭhikā) and acted under the influence of right views---upon the dissolution of the body, after death, have reappeared in a happy destination, even in a heavenly world (sugatiṃ saggaṃ lokaṃ).\"*

**3). Third Knowledge: Destruction of the Taints (*Āsavakkhaya-ñāṇa*):**

With the mind (*citta*) purified, bright, unblemished, rid of imperfection, malleable, wieldy, steady, and attained to imperturbability in the fourth jhāna, the practitioner directs it toward the knowledge of the destruction of the taints (*āsavakkhaya-ñāṇa*).

**What is seen:** The practitioner directly discerns reality as it actually is (*yathābhūtaṃ*): **The Four Noble Realities:** *\"This is dukkha\" \"This is the origin of dukkha\" \"This is the cessation of dukkha\" \"This is the ancient path leading to the cessation of dukkha\".*

**The Direct Discerning of the Taints (*Āsava*):** *\"These are the taints\"* (*kāmāsava* / sensuality, *bhavāsava* / continuous becoming, *avijjāsava* / ignorance) *\"This is the origin of the taints\" \"This is the cessation of the taints\" \"This is the ancient path leading to the cessation of the taints\"*

**The Moment of Release (*Vimutti*):** Knowing and seeing thus, the mind (*citta*) is liberated from the taint of sensuality (*kāmāsavāpi cittaṃ vimuccati*), from the taint of continuous becoming (*bhavāsavāpi cittaṃ vimuccati*), and from the taint of ignorance (*avijjāsavāpi cittaṃ vimuccati*). When liberated, there comes the direct knowledge: *\"It is liberated\"* (*vimuttasmiṃ vimuttamiti ñāṇaṃ hoti*).

The practitioner directly knows: *Khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyā'ti. "Destroyed (khīṇā) is birth (jāti), fulfilled (vusitaṃ) is the brahmacariya (brahmacariyaṃ), done (kataṃ) is what had to be done (karaṇīyaṃ), there is no more (nāparaṃ) coming back to* here in this world. *(itthattāyā\'ti)."*

**(1). ittha:** i- (this, here) + suffix -ttha (indicating manner or place). **Meaning:** \"This present state,\" \"here in this manifest world.\" **Sanskrit Equivalent:** *ittham* -- \"thus,\" \"in this manner,\" \"in this world,\" \"here.\" **(2). -tta (Sanskrit:** *-tva*) (equivalent to English *-ness* or *-hood*). **Synthesis (*itthattā*):** *ittha* (this condition) + *-tta* (-ness) = **itthattā** Sanskrit: *ithatva*), meaning **\"this-world-ness,\" \"this state of manifest existence,\"** or **\"the state of being here.\" (3). -aya:** Indicates purpose, movement toward, or destiny: **\"for\...\"** or **\"towards\...\"**

#### 1.25. Nandiya

**Obhāsajātaṁ phalagaṁ, cittaṁ yassa abhiṇhaso;\
Tādisaṁ bhikkhumāsajja, kaṇha dukkhaṁ nigacchasī ti.**

*Whose (yassa) mind-heart (cittaṁ) is constantly (abhiṇhaso) radiant (obhāsajātaṁ) and gone to the fruit (phalagaṁ); such a one (tādisaṁ) having attack ed (āsajja) a bhikkhu (bhikkhum), O Dark One (kaṇha, an epithet for Māra), you will incur (nigacchasi) dukkha.*

**Commentary**: This verse represents a direct confrontation between a highly realized practitioner and Kaṇha (Māra, the personification of death).

1.  **The Luminescence of Realization (*Obhāsajātaṁ Cittaṁ*):** The mind (*citta*) that has completely eradicated *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* is no longer shrouded in darkness. It is naturally radiant (*obhāsajātaṁ*), freed from the obscurations of ignorance. This brightness reflects the purity of a mind that has transcended worldly attachments.

2.  **Attainment of Fruition (*Phalagaṁ*):** The term *phalagaṁ* specifies that the mind is permanently established in the fruit (*phala*) of liberation. It is not an intermittent state, but a constant (*abhiṇhaso*) reality.

3.  **Inviolability of the Purified Mind and Warning to Kaṇha:** Māra (*Kaṇha*) attempts to disrupt or harass those who strive on the path. However, attempting to attack (*āsajja*) an accomplished Bhikkhu whose *citta* is established in fruition brings severe kammic repercussions back onto Māra himself. Any effort by Māra to cause harm to such a being yields only *dukkha* for the aggressor, as a mind thoroughly cleansed of clinging cannot be pierced or shaken.

#### 1.26. Abhaya

**Sutvā subhāsitaṁ vācaṁ, buddhassādiccabandhuno;\
Paccabyadhiṁ hi nipuṇaṁ, vālaggaṁ usunā yathā ti.**

*Having heard (sutvā) the well-spoken (subhāsitaṁ) teachings (vācaṁ) of the Buddha (buddhassa), the Kinsman of Sun god ādicca (ādiccabandhuno); I penetrated (paccabyadhiṁ) indeed (hi) the profound reality (nipuṇaṁ), just as (yathā) one hits a hair-tip (vālaggaṁ) with an arrow (usunā).*

#### 1.27. Lomasakaṅgiya

**Dabbaṁ kusaṁ poṭakilaṁ, usīraṁ muñjapabbajaṁ;\
Urasā panudissāmi, vivekamanubrūhayan ti.**

*Pushing aside (panudissāmi) with my chest (urasā) the dabba-grass (dabbaṁ), kusa-grass (kusaṁ), poṭakila-reed (poṭakilaṁ), usīra-root (usīraṁ), and muñja-pabbaja grass (muñjapabbajaṁ); I will cultivate / develop (anubrūhayan) seclusion (vivekam).*

**Commentary**: This verse captures the resolute, unwavering determination of a yogi practitioner entering the wilderness to develop seclusion **viveka**.

**Physical Obstacles and the Symbolism of Wild Grasses (*Dabbaṁ Kusaṁ\...*):** The sharp, fibrous grasses---*dabba*, *kusa*, *poṭakila*, *usīra*, and *muñjapabbaja*---represent both the literal thickets of the jungle and the internal entanglements of sensory enticements and worldly attachments. Pushing through them \"with one\'s chest\" (*urasā*) signifies direct physical effort, fortitude, and fearlessness in facing painful sensations, hardship, and solitude.

**The Cultivation of Seclusion (*Viveka*):** ***Viveka*** (seclusion) functions on three progressive levels: **Kāya-viveka:** Bodily seclusion from human crowds and worldly distractions. **Citta-viveka:** Mental seclusion from defilements through concentrated meditation (*Samādhi*). **Upadhi-viveka:** Total seclusion from all substrate of existence and clinging.

#### 1.28. Jambugāmikaputta

**Kacci no vatthapasuto, Kacci no bhūsanārato;\
Kacci sīlamayaṁ gandhaṁ, Kiṁ tvaṁ vāyasi netarā pajā ti.**

*Are you perhaps (kacci no) devoted to clothes (vatthapasuto)? Are you perhaps (kacci no) delighted in ornaments (bhūsanārato)? Are you perhaps (kacci) \[prefer\] the fragrance (gandhaṁ) made of sīla (sīlamayaṁ)? Why (kiṁ) do you (tvaṁ) emit a fragrance (vāyasi), and not other (netarā) people (pajā)?*

**Commentary**: This verse highlights the contrast between worldly, sensory adornments and the intangible, unshakeable fragrance of ***sīla***.

**Sensory Adornments vs. The Fragrance of Sīla (*Vatthapasuto\... Sīlamayaṁ Gandhaṁ*):** Ordinary beings (*pajā*) seek beauty and attraction through external items---fine garments (*vattha*) and physical ornaments (*bhūsana*). In contrast, a practitioner purified in **Sīla** emits a \"fragrance of **Sīla**\" (*sīlamayaṁ gandhaṁ*) that emits even against the wind---a spiritual purity that attracts devas and refined minds without relying on material displays.

**The Inward Transformation:** The question asked in the verse reflects amazement at someone whose presence is uplifting and non-worldly. Physical ornaments are subject to decay, loss, and death; *sīla*, however, is the foundational stepping stone for *Samādhi* (concentration) and *Paññā* (wisdom), and liberation from the world (*vimutti*).

#### 1.29. Hārita (1st)

**Samunnamayamattānaṁ, usukārova tejanaṁ;\
Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna, avijjaṁ bhinda hāritā ti.**

*Straightening (samunnamayaṁ) your soul-spirit ātmā (attānaṁ), just as an arrow maker (usukāro) \[straightens\] an arrow (tejanaṁ); having made (karitvāna) the mind (cittaṁ) straight (ujuṁ), pierce through/shatter (bhinda) ignorance (avijjaṁ), O Hāritā (hāritā)!*

**Commentary**: The practice of **straightening the soul-spirit (*samunnamayaṁ attānaṁ*)**, making the **mind-heart straight (*cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna*)**, and **shattering ignorance (*avijjaṁ bhinda*)** is the central discipline of the ancient path. Ignorance (*avijjā*) is dense, crooked, and multi-layered; it cannot be pierced by a mind that is warped by impulse, hostility, or worldly distortion.

To shatter *avijjā*, the *citta* must be straightened like a fletcher's arrow (*usukāro va tejanaṁ*). This straightening is accomplished through a clear, progressive training (*sikkhā*) anchored in value, self-restraint, deep concentration, and penetrating wisdom. The process of aligning and straightening the *citta* follows a definite, sequential discipline:

**Foundation of Values & Self-Restraint (*Brahmavihāra* & *Brahmacariya*)**

- **Purifying the Base with Brahmavihāra:** Before the mind can achieve razor-sharp straightness, its emotional base must be cleansed of gross distortions. *Mettā* (kindness) unbends the mind by removing ill-will (*byāpāda*). *Karuṇā* (compassion) removes cruelty (*vihesā*). *Muditā* (Brahmic joy) removes envy and inner contraction (*arati*). *Upekkhā* (equanimity/transcendental elevation) removes bias, reactivity, and emotional volatility (*paṭigha* and *anurodha*).

- **Establishing Brahmacariya & Sīla:** *Brahmacariya* (the noble, pure life of restraint) acts as the outer casing that keeps the energy of the *citta* from leaking into low, power-based, or impulse-driven channels. Without strict *sīla*, any attempt to straighten the mind fails because new crookedness is continuously introduced through unskillful action.

The motif of \"straightening\" the mind like an arrow or reed, and using that straightness to cut through defilement, appears across the early discourses:

**Dhammapada 33--34 (Citta Vagga):** *Phandanaṁ capalaṁ cittaṁ, durrakkhaṁ dunnivārayaṁ; Ujuṁ karoti medhāvī, usukāro va tejanaṁ.* \"The flickering, fickle mind, difficult to guard, difficult to control---the wise one makes it straight, just as a fletcher straightens an arrow.\"

**Sutta Nipāta 2.6 (Dhammika Sutta):** Emphasizes that a bhikkhu must abandon crookedness of body, speech, and mind, walking the straight path (*ujumagga*) laid out by the *Tathāgata*, in stark contrast to the \"crooked path\" (*kummagga*) of impulse, sensory gratification, and speculative views. The *ujumagga* is defined across three distinct levels of alignment:

**Straightening Action (Kāya-Kamma & Vacī-Kamma):**

- Abandoning the physical crookedness of harm, theft, and misconduct.

- Eliminating verbal crookedness---false speech, divisive speech, harsh words, and idle chatter.

- Anchored strictly by *sīla* and *Brahmacariya* (the pure, restrained life), preventing energy from leaking into low, power-based, or sense-driven channels.

**Straightening the Mind (Mano-Kamma & Citta-bhāvanā):**

- Dispersing *vitakkā*---mental projections and coarse thoughts directed toward worldly objects.

- Overcoming the five hindrances (*nīvaraṇa*) to bring the mind into deep concentration (*Samādhi*). The **five hindrances (*pañca nīvaraṇā*)** are the primary mental obstructions that warp, darken, and bend the *citta*, preventing the attainment of deep concentration (*Samādhi*) and clear vision (*Paññā*):

<!-- -->

- **Kāmachanda (Sexual and Sensual Desire):** Sexual Attachment, longing, or craving for pleasant sensory objects (sights, sounds, smells, tastes, touches, or worldly thoughts).

- **Byāpāda (Ill-Will / Malice):** Hostility, aversion, resentment, or anger toward people, objects, or situations.

- **Thīna-Middha (Sloth and Torpor):** Sluggishness, heaviness, mental lethargy, and drowsiness that dull the *citta*.

- **Uddhacca-Kukkucca (Restlessness and Regret):** Agitation, mental scatteredness, worry, and remorse over past actions.

- **Vicikicchā (Skepticism / Doubt):** Indecision, lack of clarity, and vacillating doubt regarding the liberation path.

<!-- -->

- Cultivating the *Brahmavihāra* (*mettā, karuṇā, muditā, upekkhā*), the divine, ordered emotion.

- **Straightening Perception & View (*Sammā-Diṭṭhi*):** Seeing reality clearly without the warp of *avijjā* (ignorance).

In the Pāli canon, the metaphor of *uju* (straight/upright) is central to how transformation occurs:

- **The Fletcher Simile (*Usukāro va tejanaṁ*):** Just as a fletcher heats and bends a crooked reed until it is completely straight so it can fly true to its target, a practitioner aligns bodily conduct and mental focus until the *citta* is *ujubhūta* (become straight).

- **Direct Penetration (*Paṭivedha*):** A crooked arrow veers off course when shot; a bent mind misses ultimate truth. Only a straight mind (*uju-citta*) in deep concentration (*Samādhi*) possesses the sharp penetrative force needed to pierce through *avijjā*.

**Aṅguttara Nikāya 3.19 (Kaccāna Sutta):** Describes how a mind established in right view becomes *uju-bhūta* (become straight), direct, and free from the twists of delusion. **Key Themes of *Uju-bhūta*:**

- **Correcting the Distortion of Delusion:** Delusion (*moha*) causes the mind to perceive impermanent, worldly phenomena as permanent, stable, or desirable. Grounding the mind in *sammā-diṭṭhi* acts as a straightening alignment, removing the internal twists (*kuṭilatā*) to the worldly things caused by craving (*rāga*) and aversion (*dosa*).

- **Directness of the Citta (*Uju-bhūta*):** A mind that has become straight is unbent by personal preference, social pressure, or sensory temptation. It responds directly to ultimate truth without hesitation, rationalization, or hypocrisy.

- **Sustained Integrity (*Sīla* & *Brahmacariya*):** Internal mental straightness naturally manifests as outward rectitude. The practitioner's physical and verbal conduct become completely honest, transparent, and aligned with *Brahmacariya* (the pure, noble life).

**The Law of Values: Individual to Collective Scaling**

When the *citta* is straightened on an individual level through *Brahmavihāra* and *Brahmacariya*, it naturally projects outward into society, establishing a civilizational **Law of Values**.

**Individual Level:** Mettā, Karuṇā, Muditā, Upekkhā, that purifies the citta, removes reactivity, establishes an inner identity rooted in virtue.

**Collective Level:** the projection of individual values onto a community, this includes Justice, Fairness, Equality, Democracy; as well as Loyalty, Self-Sacrifice, Chivalry, Integrity, Responsibility, Discipline, Respect, Paññā. They not only scale upekkhā and mettā into collective stewardship, protecting the vulnerable and maintaining balance without favoritism, but also act as self-reinforcing safeguards that prevent societal collapse back into primitive, power-based behavior.

#### 1.30. Uttiya (1st)

**Ābādhe me samuppanne, sati me udapajjatha;\
Ābādho me samuppanno, kālo me nappamajjitun ti.**

*When affliction/illness (ābādhe) arose (samuppanne) in me (me), mindfulness (sati) arose (udapajjatha) in me (me); affliction (ābādho) has arisen (samuppanno) in me (me), \[now is\] the time (kālo) for me (me) not to be negligent (nappamajjitun).*

**Commentary**: The urgent awakening of mindfulness (*sati*) when confronted with bodily pain and physical breakdown. Instead of allowing bodily affliction (*ābādha*) to cause mental distress or despair, the practitioner uses physical suffering as an immediate catalyst for spiritual awakening and unshakeable urgency (*saṁvega*). Recognizing that physical strength is declining and death is an inevitable law of the physical realm, there is not much time left for heedlessness (*pamāda*): *now is the time to strive with absolute dedication, without delay.*

#### 1.31. Gahvaratīriya

**Phuṭṭho ḍaṁsehi makasehi, araññasmiṁ brahāvane;\
Nāgo saṅgāmasīseva, sato tatrādhivāsaye ti.**

*Touched/bitten (phuṭṭho) by gadflies (ḍaṁsehi) and mosquitoes (makasehi) in the forest (araññasmiṁ), in the vast wilderness (brahāvane); just as (iva) an elephant (nāgo) on the front line of battle (saṅgāmasīse), **mindful (sato)** there (tatra) one should **endure (adhivāsaye)**.*

**Commentary**: This verse highlights the heroic endurance (*khanti*) and unwavering mindfulness (*sati*) required of a practitioner dwelling in extreme, isolated conditions to attain final liberation.

**The War-Elephant Simile (*Nāgo Saṅgāmasīse*):** Just as a royal war-elephant entering the vanguard of battle endures incoming arrows, spears, and chaotic strikes without retreating or breaking rank, a bhikkhu in the wilderness must stand firm against physical discomforts. Gadflies and mosquitoes (*ḍaṁsehi makasehi*) represent bodily afflictions, harsh climates, and sensory irritations that test the practitioner\'s resolve.

**Patient Endurance Through Mindfulness (*Sato Tatrādhivāsaye*):** Endurance (*adhivāsana*) is not mere blind tolerance or passive stoicism. It is anchored in sharp mindfulness (*sato*). When physical pain or irritation arises from contact (*phassa*), the practitioner observes the sensation objectively without generating aversion (*dosa*) or mental disturbance.

#### 1.32. Suppiya

**Ajaraṁ jīramānena, tappamānena nibbutiṁ;\
Nimiyaṁ paramaṁ santiṁ, yogakkhemaṁ anuttaran ti.**

*From decaying/aging (jīramānena), to the nondecaying (ajaraṁ); from Tapas, the **primordial power from the soul-spirit attā**, (tappamānena), to Nibbāna (nibbutiṁ); replace (nimiyaṁ) \[for\] supreme (paramaṁ) peace (santiṁ), the unsurpassed (anuttaraṁ) ultimate sanctuary / safety from bondage (yogakkhemaṁ).*

**Commentary**: **Nimiya**

- **Root:** **mā** (to measure, mete out, fashion, construct).

- **Prefix:** **ni-** (down, into, exchange, back).

- **Pāli Verb:** *Nimināti* / *Nimmināti* (to swap, substitute, exchange).

- **Core Meaning:** The act of swapping one thing for another, replacing a lower state with a higher one, or effecting a total substitution. In this verse, it signifies leveraging a temporary, decaying/aging condition to step into an unconditioned, eternal reality.

In the **Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad 1.3.28**, this Vedic framework reaches its philosophical climax: *asato mā sad gamaya, tamaso mā jyotir gamaya, mṛtyor mā mṛtam gamaya.* From unreal (*asatas*) lead (*gamaya*) me (*mā*) to real (*sat*); from darkness (*tamasas*) lead (*gamaya*) me (*mā*) to light (*jyotis*); from death (*mṛtyos*) lead (*gamaya*) me (*mā*) to immortality (*amṛtam*).

- The movement from the unreal to the real (*asato mā sad gamaya*) and from death to the deathless (*mṛtyor mā\'mṛtaṁ gamaya*) is the ultimate expression of *ni-mā*.

- The transient, decaying body (*jīrṇa*) is recognized as an impermanent sheath. Through intense intentional invocation or inducement of this innate primordial power: *tapas*, the soul-spirit (*ātmā*) transcends the decaying vessel to abide in unshakeable peace.

**The Vehicle vs. The Power:** The decaying body (*jīramānena*) is merely the physical casing in the world of cause and effect. The practitioner induces the **tapas** power---the primordial force of the soul-spirit *attā*---as the essential catalyst to overcome and transcend this decaying framework.

**The Nature of *Nimiyaṁ*:** This is the complete substitution (*nimiyaṁ*) of the unstable, decaying worldly existence for **Nibbāna**---which is simultaneously *Ajaraṁ* (un-aging), *Nibbutiṁ* (unbound), *Paramaṁ Santiṁ* (supreme peace), and *Yogakkhemaṁ Anuttaraṁ* (unexcelled ultimate sanctuary).

#### 1.33. Sopāka (1st)

**Yathāpi ekaputtasmiṁ, piyasmiṁ kusalī siyā;\
Evaṁ sabbesu pāṇesu, sabbattha kusalo siyā ti.**

*Just as (**yathāpi**) toward an only child (**ekaputtasmiṁ**), toward a beloved one (**piyasmiṁ**), one should be skilled in goodness (**kusalī siyā**); even so (**evaṁ**) toward all living beings / breathing creatures (**sabbesu pāṇesu**), in all circumstances / universally (**sabbattha**), one should be skilled in goodness (**kusalo siyā**).*

**Commentary**: Clarification on ***mettā:* The Problem with the Maternal/Collective Archetype**

The conventional interpretation for this verse mirrors the famous maternal archetype found elsewhere in the *Karaṇīyamettā Sutta* (*Mātā yathā niyaṁ puttaṁ āyusā ekaputtamanurakkhe\...*). It denotes that the mind\'s protective, benevolent skill (*kusala*) toward an only, beloved child (*ekaputta*) must not remain a localized emotional attachment, but be elevated into an all-pervading, universal state. It implies a mind trained to respond with unwavering, uncorrupted goodness toward all living beings (*sabbesu pāṇesu*) without boundary or exception (*sabbattha*).

But framing this verse around a mother's instinct, relies on a biological and collective mechanism. As demonstrated in sociobiology (e.g., kin selection and evolutionary dynamics in books such as "The Selfish Gene" by Richard Dawkins), a mother\'s impulse to protect her child or an animal\'s self-sacrifice for its pack is driven by **collective consciousness, genetic preservation, and tribal instinct**.

This biological drive is strictly limited:

- It is bound by proximity, bloodline, and tribal affiliation.

- It operates automatically through the animalistic, collective machinery of the physical world (*loka*).

- It is inherently exclusive---protecting one\'s own offspring while remaining indifferent or hostile toward external threats.

**The True Perspective: *Brahmavihāra* as an Ordered Quality of the Individual Mind**

To extend *Mettā* toward **all living beings (*sabbesu pāṇesu*) in all circumstances, universally (*sabbattha*)** cannot be an expansion of biological instinct. It is an intentional, highly refined, and ordered quality of the **individual mind-heart/soul-spirit (*attā*)**.

**(1). *Kusala* as an Individual Sovereign Quality**

The verse states: **kusalo siyā** (\"one should be skilled/masterful in goodness\").

- This is an intentional cultivation executed by the individual *citta*.

- It is a **Brahma-attribute (*Brahmavihāra*)**---a divine state of high order, purity, and absolute self-mastery.

- Unlike biological emotion, which is reactive and impulse-driven, divine *Mettā* is a deliberate posture of the individual divine mind that refuses to be governed by worldly reactivity, aversion (*dosa*), or genetic bias.

**(2). Re-interpreting the *Ekaputta* Analogy**

Rather than serving as a biological model of maternal emotion, the phrase **Yathāpi ekaputtasmiṁ piyasmiṁ** serves purely as a measure of **intensity and singular focus**:

- It references the *uncompromising degree of dedication* one directs toward what is cherished.

- The practitioner takes that absolute degree of focus and applies it universally (*sabbattha*) through sovereign will, detaching it entirely from physical bloodlines or collective instincts.

By recognizing *Mettā* as a divine, individual mind-quality rather than a biological instinct, the practitioner breaks free from the collective machinery of *saṃsāra* which leads to **Nibbida (Disenchantment from the world),** **Virāga (Fading of worldly Passion) and** **Vimutti (Liberation from the world).**

#### 1.34. Posiya

**Anāsannavarā etā, niccameva vijānatā; Gāmā araññamāgamma,**

**tato gehaṁ upāvisi, Tato uṭṭhāya pakkāmi, anāmantetvā posiyo ti.**

*These \[sensory entanglements\] are best left unapproached (**anāsannavarā etā**), constantly indeed (**niccameva**) by one who clearly discerns (**vijānatā**); having gone from the village (**gāmā**) to the forest/ having retreated into the secluded wilderness (**araññamāgamma**), then he entered a house (**tato gehaṁ upāvisi**), rising up from there he departed (**tato uṭṭhāya pakkāmi**), without addressing/consulting those to be supported / household dependents (**anāmantetvā posiyo**).*

**Commentary**: This verse illustrates the uncompromising radicality of true renunciation (*nekkhamma*). It establishes the total severance of individual mind-sovereignty from domestic, social, and collective obligations (*posiyo*).

**(1). Discerning the Trap (*Anāsannavarā Etā*)**

The wise practitioner (*vijānatā*) continuously recognizes that worldly entanglements and sensory obligations (*etā*) are \"best left unapproached.\" They are traps of the physical world (*loka*) designed to pull the sovereign mind back into biological and social conditioning.

**(2). The Absolute Break (*Anāmantetvā Posiyo*)**

The act of departing without taking leave of/ addressing/ consulting those to be supported (*anāmantetvā posiyo*) is not mere coldness; it is the decisive breaking of the psychological leash. Consulting or taking leave invites emotional manipulation, guilt, and the collective pressure of the household matrix. Rising up instantly (*uṭṭhāya pakkāmi*) demonstrates the sovereign will of the individual soul-spirit (*attā*) asserting its complete independence over worldly duty.

This radical departure into total forest seclusion (*arañña*) fuels the essential operational mechanics of liberation: **Nibbida (Disenchanted from the world):** Seeing that social structures, domestic duties, and sensory entanglements are merely cycles bound to decay and death, the *citta* undergoes deep disenchantment with all the worldly (*loka*).

#### 1.35. Sāmaññakāni

**Sukhaṁ sukhattho labhate tadācaraṁ, Kittiñca pappoti yasassa vaḍḍhati;\
Yo ariyamaṭṭhaṅgikamañjasaṁ ujuṁ, Bhāveti maggaṁ amatassa pattiyā ti.**

*One desiring joy (**sukhattho**) gains joy (**sukhaṁ labhate**) practicing that (joy/path) (**tadācaraṁ**), and he attains fame/ noble reputation (**kittiñca pappoti**), increases honor/ respect (**yasassa vaḍḍhati**); who (**yo**) cultivates (**bhāveti**) the straight path (**añjasaṁ ujuṁ**), the Noble Eightfold Path (**ariyamaṭṭhaṅgikamaggaṁ**), for the attainment of the Deathless (**amatassa pattiyā**).*

**Commentary**: Joy (**sukha**) and Deathless (**amata**)

This verse outlines the immediate and ultimate fruit of walking the **ancient path to Nibbāna** (*ariyamaṭṭhaṅgikamañjasaṁ ujuṁ*). It bridges the practitioner\'s present spiritual well-being with the ultimate realization of the deathless.

**(1). The Direct Path (*Añjasaṁ Ujuṁ*):** The Noble Eightfold Path is described as **ujuṁ**---straight. It does not wander into the crooked, convoluted detours of worldly speculation, sensual gratification, or collective animalistic conditioning. It is an uncompromising road of individual detachment from the collective and purification of the individual soul-spirit **ātmā**:

**(2). Joy vs. Worldly Friction:** The joy (*sukhaṁ*) gained by practicing (*tadācaraṁ*) is not transient sensory pleasure. It is the unshakeable, noble ease born of inner order and purity. The honor/respect (*yasa*) and noble reputation (*kitti*) that grow (*vaḍḍhati*) represent the natural spiritual authority that radiates to the people in the world, from an individual mind (*attā*) aligned with divine truth, completely untouched by the chaos of the world.

**(3) The Deathless (*Amata*) as the Ultimate Destination:** In the verse, the phrase **amatassa pattiyā** (\"for the attainment of the Deathless\") explicitly defines the singular target of walking the straight Noble Eightfold Path. The realization of the **Deathless (*Amata*)** is the ultimate fulfillment of this ancient path. The term *Amata* (Sanskrit: *Amṛta*) literally means \"un-dying\" or \"immortal\"---signifying a realm and reality entirely beyond the reach of the universal law of decay and death.

a)  **Transcending the World of Death (*Loka*):** All manifest physical and mental phenomena in this world are governed by the laws of decay and death. To attain *Amata* is to step entirely outside of this cyclic existence with mortality.

b)  **The Nature of *Amata*:** *Amata* is not a temporary heavenly rebirth or a continued worldly manifest existence (*bhava*) subject to future downfall. It is an epithet for **Nibbāna-Dhātu**---the unconditioned, un-aging (*ajaraṁ*), and deathless realm.

c)  **The Active Realization:** By cultivating the straight path (**bhāveti maggaṁ**), the practitioner actively uses the temporary physical vessel to break through the illusions of reality and secure liberation from the world to gain permanent and peaceful life in the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

#### 1.36. Kumāputta

**Sādhu sutaṁ sādhu caritakaṁ, Sādhu sadā aniketavihāro;\
Atthapucchanaṁ padakkhiṇakammaṁ, Etaṁ sāmaññamakiñcanassā ti.**

*This is good (**sādhu**) for sacred learning/ scriptural knowledge (**sutaṁ**), This is good (**sādhu**) for putting it into practice (**caritakaṁ**), This is good (**sādhu**) for living without a fixed base (**aniketavihāro**) always / perpetually (**sadā**); investigating/ questioning the true purpose (**atthapucchanaṁ**), showing utmost reverence and alignment (**padakkhiṇa**) through action (**kammaṁ**)---this (**etaṁ**) is the true **sāmañña** / asceticism (**sāmaññam**) for one who possesses nothing (**akiñcanassā**).*

**Commentary**: True **Sāmañña** / Asceticism (**Sāmaññam**) for One Who Possesses Nothing (**Akiñcanassā**)

This verse establishes the complete structural architecture of true **sāmañña** (asceticism) who possesses nothing (**Akiñcana**), having stripped away all worldly attachments, false identities, and mental projections.

**(1). Sacred Learning / Scriptural Knowledge (*Sutaṁ*)**

True **sāmañña** / asceticism begins with sacred learning (**sutaṁ**)---receiving and piercing the uncorrupted world-transcending truth (*Dhamma*). It provides the essential cognitive map, pulling the individual mind-heart/soul-spirit (*attā*) out of worldly ignorance (*avijjā*) and laying the foundation for liberation from the world.

**(2). Putting It into Practice (*Caritakaṁ*)**

Knowledge without execution remains a mere intellectual trap of the world (*loka*). **Caritakaṁ** is the actual lived movement---taking sacred learning and actively translating it into spiritual exertion, moral grounding (*Sīla*), and *Samādhi* (concentration) meditation.

**(3). The Homeless Abiding Always / Perpetually (*Sadā Aniketavihāro*)**

The practitioner maintains a perpetual homeless abiding (**sadā aniketavihāro**). This is not about physical wandering or walking meditation as we see today, but the *citta*\'s absolute refusal to build a nest (*niketa*) within sensory experiences, physical property, or worldly identity. The mind remains continuously homeless within worldly conditioned existence.

**(4). Investigating / Questioning the True Purpose (*Atthapucchanaṁ*)**

Renunciation requires unrelenting analytical sharpness. **Atthapucchanaṁ** is the active, penetrating inquiry into ultimate spiritual purpose (*atthan*). The practitioner constantly cross-examines reality, testing every state of consciousness against the goal to ensure no subtle worldly attachment remains.

**5. Utmost Reverence and Alignment Through Action (*Padakkhiṇa-kammaṁ*)**

Internalized wisdom expresses itself as total alignment with the transcendent truth (**padakkhiṇa**) through concrete action (**kammaṁ**). Just as one circumambulates a sacred center keeping it to the right, every physical, verbal, and mental act of the practitioner is brought into strict, reverent subjection to the ultimate *Dhamma*.

#### 1.37. Kumāputtasahāyaka

**Nānājanapadaṁ yanti, Vicarantā asaññatā, Samādhiñca virādhenti; Kiṁsu raṭṭhacariyā karissati; Tasmā vineyya sārambhaṁ, Jhāyeyya apurakkhato ti.**

*They go to different districts and countries (**nānājanapadaṁ yanti**), wandering about (**vicarantā**) unrestrained/uncontrolled (**asaññatā**), and they ruin/ miss **samādhi** concentration (**samādhiñca virādhenti**); what good (**kiṁsu**) will this wandering through countries accomplish (**raṭṭhacariyā karissati**)? Therefore (**tasmā**), having discarded (**vineyya**) arrogance / spiritual conceit (**sārambhaṁ**), one should abide in **jhāna** meditation (**jhāyeyya**) without seeking prominence (**apurakkhato**).*

**Commentary**: **Critique of Ancient & Modern \"Tudong\" Wandering**

This verse serves as a direct, incisive critique of the modern spectacle surrounding so-called \"tudong\" bhikkhus---not only ancient but also nowadays those undertaking public cross-country marches through Thailand, Malaysia, Singapore, and Indonesia. It raises critical questions that every practitioner must confront:

Public Foot-Marches (Nānājanapadaṁ Yanti) ──►

Restless Spectacle (Asaññatā & Sārambhaṁ) │

▼ Ruin Concentration (Samādhiṁ Virādhenti) │

▼ Solitary Absorption (Jhāyeyya Apurakkhato) ──►

True Discipline & Pathway to Nibbāna

- **The Illusion of Observing the 13 Dhutanga Practices:**

The authentic ascetic practices (*dhutanga*) were designed strictly to reduce worldly clutter, eliminate conceit, and quiet mental projections (*vitakka*). When foot-marches across international borders turn into public media events, photogenic processions, and viral spectacles, they directly contradict the spirit of hidden, quiet renunciation. Are these journeys genuine observances of strict asceticism, or are they mere performance and spiritual pretension (*sārambhaṁ*)?

- **Abandoning a Fixed Base vs. Constant Motion:**

While leading a life without a fixed residence can break worldly attachments, aimless physical wandering (**vicarantā**) across entire nations without internal quietude simply exchanges one form of worldly restlessness for another. True solitude is found in the mind\'s detachment from the physical world, not in continuous geographic displacement.

- **What Do They Actually Accomplish? (Kiṁsu Raṭṭhacariyā Karissati?)**

The verse asks point-blank: *What good does wandering through countries do?* Physical distance traveled does not equal spiritual progress. If one moves across continents while remaining unrestrained (**asaññatā**) in the senses and mind, the endeavor yields no real spiritual fruit.

- **The Ruin of Concentration (Samādhiṁ Virādhenti):**

The most critical casualty of constant public traveling is Concentration (Samadhi). The noise, logistics, public interaction, and physical exhaustion of long-distance marches directly destroy the mental stillness required to enter deep jhāna. By prioritizing the outward journey, they miss the far more vital inward practice of silent, undisturbed meditation (**jhāyeyya apurakkhato**).

#### 1.38. Gavampati

**Yo iddhiyā sarabhuṁ aṭṭhapesi, So gavampati asito anejo;\
Taṁ sabbasaṅgātigataṁ mahāmuniṁ, Devā namassanti bhavassa pāragun ti.**

*He who by* *psychic power (**yo iddhiyā**) made the Sarabhū River (of Northern India) stand still / stop (**sarabhuṁ aṭṭhapesi**), he, Bhante Gavampati, disciples of Gautama the Supreme Arahant (**so gavampati**), is unattached / unbound / untethered to the physical world (**asito**) and unperturbed / immovable / free from inner motion or craving (**anejo**); him who has gone beyond all worldly attachments (**taṁ sabbasaṅgātigataṁ**), the great sage (**mahāmuniṁ**), the devas bow to (**devā namassanti**), the one who has reached the far shore (**pāragun**) of worldly existence (**bhavassa**).*

**Commentary**: Celebrating the Arahant Bhikkhu Gavampati

This verse celebrates the Arahant Bhikkhu Gavampati, illustrating how absolute mastery over the sovereign mind (*citta*) translates into total detachment from and power over the physical phenomena of this world.

**(1). Manifestation of Psychic Power Mastery (*Iddhiyā Sarabhuṁ Aṭṭhapesi*)**

When an Arahant halts the rushing waters of a physical river like the Sarabhū, it demonstrates that physical phenomena in this physical world are ultimately manifestations originating from or subordinate to the psychic power of the *citta*. Such supernormal displays (*iddhi*) are not performed for vanity or public acclaim (*sārambhaṁ*), but emerge naturally from absolute psychic mastery.

**(2). Total Inner Immovability of Craving (*Asito Anejo*)**

Although possessor of immense psychic mastery, Gavampati remains completely unattached (**asito**) and unperturbed (**anejo**). The word *anejo* signifies the complete absence of inner agitation, trembling, or craving (*eja*). He does not cling to supernormal powers, nor is his mind shaken by physical elements.

**(3). Transcendence of Worldly Existence (*Bhavassa Pāragū*)**

The verse emphasizes that Gavampati has crossed beyond all worldly attachments (**sabbasaṅgātigataṁ**) and reached the far shore of worldly existence (**bhavassa pāragū**). Worldly existence (*bhava*)---the endless cycle of involuntary reincarnation---holds no sway over one who has severed all ties to the world.

**(4). Homage from Heavenly Beings (*Devā Namassanti*)**

Because the Arahant Bhikkhu has conquered worldly existence and stands unshakeable in pure liberation, even the celestial beings (**devā**) bow in homage before him. The devas recognize that while they themselves still reside within the temporal celestial realms of *loka*, the *mahāmuni* has accomplished true liberation from the world.

#### 1.39. Tissa (1st)

**Sattiyā viya omaṭṭho, ḍayhamānova matthake;\
Kāmarāgappahānāya, sato bhikkhu paribbaje ti.**

*As if struck by a sword (**sattiyā viya omaṭṭho**), or as if one\'s head were on fire (**ḍayhamānova matthake**); for the complete abandoning of sexual (**kāma)** attachment (**rāga)** (**kāma-rāga-pahānāya**), mindful (**sato**), a **bhikkhu** should renounce household life (**paribbaje**).*

**Commentary**: This verse establishes the absolute urgency required to overcome the primary binding force of the sex realm (*kāma-dhātu*): sexual passion (*kāma-rāga*).

**(1). The Extreme Urgency of Emergency (*Sattiyā Viya Omaṭṭho, Ḍayhamānova Matthake*):** If a sword pierces a person\'s body or their head catches fire, they do not hesitate, delay, or negotiate; every unit of attention is immediately directed to putting out the flames or removing the blade. The verse uses this vivid metaphor to illustrate the attitude a practitioner must bring toward *kāma-rāga*. Sexual drive is not a benign instinct to be indulged or gently managed; it is an active fire burning the crown of the head.

**(2). Root Eradication of the Realm of Sex (*Kāmarāgappahānāya*):** Within cosmological structure, the lower sex realm (*kāma-dhātu*) is defined and sustained by the force of sex and sexual drive (*kāma*). To transcend the cycle of involuntary reincarnation, the complete eradication (**pahāna**) of *rāga* directed toward sexual drive is non-negotiable. Without severing this primary knot, the mind (*citta*) remains bound to rebirth in the sex realm.

**(3). Unwavering Awareness in Homeless Renunciation (*Sato Bhikkhu Paribbaje*):** To cut through such a deeply ingrained human instinct, the bhikkhu must live attentively (**sato**), continuously guarding the senses against mental projections (*vitakka*). Renouncing family life (**paribbaje**) signifies the physical and mental departure from worldly domestic life, cutting off the conditions that feed sexual attraction and worldly attachment.

#### 1.40. Vaḍḍhamāna

**Sattiyā viya omaṭṭho, ḍayhamānova matthake;\
Bhavarāgappahānāya, sato bhikkhu paribbaje ti.**

*As if struck by a sword (**sattiyā viya omaṭṭho**), or as if one\'s head were on fire (**ḍayhamānova matthake**); for the complete abandoning of passion for worldly existence (**bhava-rāga-pahānāya**), mindful (**sato**), a bhikkhu should renounce family life (**bhikkhu paribbaje**).*

**Commentary**: While the previous variant focused specifically on cutting *kāmarāga* (passion for sex / sexual drive within the sex realm), this parallel canonical verse expands the mandate to the overarching root of all rebirth: **bhava-rāga**---passion for worldly existence itself across all realms.

**(1). Imminent Danger of Worldly Existence (*Sattiyā Viya Omaṭṭho*)**

To remain attached to *bhava* (worldly existence, manifest in the world) is to stand with one\'s head ablaze. Every manifestation within *bhava*---whether in the physical realms, subtle fine-material realms, or formless realms---belongs to *loka* and is governed by the laws of decay and death (*dukkha*). Treating worldly existence as safe or desirable is like ignoring a sword piercing the body.

**(2). Root Eradication of Worldly Manifestation (*Bhavarāgappahānāya*)**

*Bhava-rāga* is the subtle attachment to worldly existing, becoming, or manifesting anywhere within the world. So long as the *citta* harbors even the faintest delight in worldly existence, it generates the mental projections (*vitakka*) and karmic force that drive involuntary reincarnation within this universe. Complete liberation requires the absolute eradication (**pahāna**) of *rāga* toward all levels of *bhava*.

**(3). Unwavering Guard of the Mind (*Sato Bhikkhu Paribbaje*)**

To cut through the deep-seated impulse for worldly existence, a bhikkhu must live with constant mindfulness (**sato**). Renounce family life (**paribbaje**) is both an outer renunciation of household ties and an inner detachment from all worldly attachments / passion.

#### 1.41. Sirivaḍḍha

**Vivaramanupatanti vijjutā, Vebhārassa ca paṇḍavassa ca;\
Nagavivaragato ca jhāyati, Putto appaṭimassa tādino ti.**

*Lightnings strike down into the mountain crevices (**vivaramanupatanti vijjutā**), of Mount Vebhāra and Mount Paṇḍava (**vebhārassa ca paṇḍavassa ca**); and gone into the mountain cave (**nagavivaragato ca**), he dwells in jhāna (**jhāyati**), son (**putto**) of the Incomparable, the Unshakable One (**appaṭimassa tādino ti**).*

**Commentary**: The verse highlights the universal characteristic of one who has realized complete detachment from physical phenomena:

**(1). Transience of Physical Phenomena**

The flashing lightning bolts (**vijjutā**) descending upon Mount Vebhāra and Mount Paṇḍava represent the chaotic, unstable movements of the physical world (*loka*). Because physical phenomena are mere physical manifestations in this world, they have no power to disturb the purified mind (**citta**) that has detached from sensory contact.

**(2). Absolute Mental Immovability**

By taking shelter in the mountain cave (**nagavivaragato**) and entering high **Samādhi**, Sirivaḍḍha abides in *jhāna* (**jhāyati**). He demonstrates that external physical storms cannot penetrate an inner stillness that has cut off mental projections (*vitakka*).

**(3). Lineage of the Unshakable**

He is called the son (**putto**) of Gautama the Supreme Arahant---the Incomparable One (**appaṭima**) who is *tādī* (steadfast, unshakable, completely unaffected by worldly change).

#### 1.42. Khadiravaniya

**Cāle upacāle sīsūpacāle (...), Patissatā nu kho viharatha;\
Āgato vo vālaṁ viya vedhī ti.**

*O Cāla, Upacāla, and Sīsūpacāla, do you dwell in continuously mindful (**patissatā nu kho viharatha**)? Like (**viya**) a hair-splitting archer (**vālaṁ vedhī**) has come to you (**āgato vo**).*

**Commentary**: **Patissatā -** Constant Mindfulness in Practice

Here Bhante Khadiravaniya Revata addresses his three young nephews, admonishing them to maintain constant mindfulness in their practice.

**Patissatā (pati + satā / adjective)**

- **pati-** (*prefix*): path, toward, established in.

- **satā** (*nominative plural masculine of sant / sara / smṛ*): mindful, acutely aware, present in discernment.

- **Meaning:** Established in mindfulness, acutely present and watchful.

Established Mindfulness (**patissatā**) is not a temporary technique but a continuous state of guarding the senses against mental projections (*vitakka*). By questioning his nephews, Bhante Khadiravaniya Revata reminds them that dwelling in seclusion requires total vigilance over every rising and falling movement within the world (*loka*).

An archer standing before you with a drawn bow represents an immediate life-or-death crisis, expects a practitioner to the goal of deathless. In texts like the *Maccudheyya Sutta* and *Dhammapada*, Māra (personification of death/impermanence) is often depicted as a swift archer whose arrows strike without warning. When Bhante Khadiravaniya Revata warns his nephews that \"an archer has come to you,\" it serves as an urgent wake-up call: death, decay, and the relentless law of impermanence (*anicca*) are actively targeting them. There is zero time for negligence or mental laxity.

#### 1.43. Sumaṅgala

**Sumuttiko sumuttiko, Sāhu sumuttikomhi tīhi khujjakehi;\
Asitāsu mayā naṅgalāsu mayā, Khuddakuddālāsu mayā.\
Yadipi idhameva idhameva, Atha vāpi alameva alameva;\
Jhāya sumaṅgala jhāya sumaṅgala, Appamatto vihara sumaṅgalā ti.**

*Completely freed, completely freed! (**sumuttiko sumuttiko**) Wonderful! I am completely freed from three crooked things (**sāhu sumuttikomhi tīhi khujjakehi**): from my sickle / scythe (**asitāsu mayā**), from my plow (**naṅgalāsu mayā**), and from my small spade (**khuddakuddālāsu mayā**)! Even if right here, right here (**yadipi idhameva idhameva**), or else enough, enough (**atha vāpi alameva alameva**)!*

*Dwell in jhāna (**jhāya**), **Sumaṅgala**, Dwell in jhāna (**jhāya**), **Sumaṅgala**! Live zealously (**vihara appamatto**), O **Sumaṅgala**!*

**Commentary**: In this verse, Bhante Sumaṅgala expresses his profound joy upon gaining complete freedom after a hard life as a farmer, celebrating his freedom from his three physical \"crookednesses\" (his sickle, his plow, and his spade). His declaration contrasts the heavy physical burdens of agricultural labor with the absolute lightness of mental freedom.

**(1). Freedom from Physical and Mental Crookedness**

The three crooked tools (**tīhi khujjakehi**)---the curved sickle, the heavy bent plow, and the hand spade---symbolize the grueling, cyclic effort to sustain the physical body within the world (**loka**). By casting them aside, Bhante Sumaṅgala not only renounces worldly poverty but eradicates the internal \"crookedness\" of craving (*taṇhā*) that ties the mind (*citta*) to labor and physical survival.

**(2). Total Sufficiency (*Alameva Alameva*)**

When he exclaims \"Enough, enough!\" (**alameva alameva**), he declares that he has completely finished with worldly existence. He needs no more physical possessions, no further agricultural harvests, and no future birth. His mind has achieved complete independence from external dependencies.

**(3). Self-Admonition to Immersion in jhāna** **(*Jhāya Sumaṅgala*)**

Addressing himself by name, Bhante Sumaṅgala commands his own mind to remain anchored in high Concentration (**Samādhi**) through *jhāna*. Physical seclusion from the field is only the outer frame; true release requires continuous internal zeal (**appamatto**) to prevent sensory distractions from re-entering the *citta*.

#### 1.44. Sānu

**Mataṁ vā amma rodanti, Yo vā jīvaṁ na dissati;\
Jīvantaṁ maṁ amma passantī, Kasmā maṁ amma rodasī ti.**

*They weep for one who is dead, O mother (**mataṁ vā amma rodanti**), or for one who, though living, is seen no more (**yo vā jīvaṁ na dissati**); seeing me alive, O mother (**jīvantaṁ maṁ amma passantī**), why, O mother, do you weep for me (**kasmā maṁ amma rodasī ti**)?*

**Commentary**: In this verse, Bhante Sānu addresses his mother, who was weeping and lamenting when he briefly considered disrobing and returning to the lay life. Bhante Sānu reasons with her, pointing out the irrationality of weeping for someone who is alive, standing right in front of her, and striving for spiritual liberation. Bhante Sānu's statement directly confronts emotional delusion (*moha*) and worldly attachment by examining the causes of grief.

**(1). Delusion of Worldly Attachment:** People weep due to grief born of possessiveness over temporary physical bodies. They mourn physical death (**mataṁ**) or physical separation (**jīvaṁ na dissati**). Bhante Sānu highlights how grief is rooted in emotional reactivity rather than wisdom, showing his mother that her tears stem from personal attachment rather than his actual well-being.

**(2). Spiritual Rebirth over Household Existence:** To return to lay life out of emotional obligation would be a spiritual \"death.\" Bhante Sānu emphasizes that remaining alive in the *Brahmacariya* (holy life) as a bhikkhu is true life. Weeping over a son who is actively seeking liberation from saṃsāra reflects a fundamental misunderstanding of what constitutes true protection and well-being.

**(3). Cutting Off Family Projections (*Vitakka*):** The *citta* of a practitioner must break free from household ties, familial expectations, and emotional demands. By questioning the rationale behind her grief, Bhante Sānu establishes the necessary mental boundary to protect his **Samādhi** and pursue total liberation.

#### 1.45. Ramaṇīyavihārin

**Yathāpi bhaddo ājañño, khalitvā patitiṭṭhati;\
Evaṁ dassanasampannaṁ, sammāsambuddhasāvakan ti.**

*Just as an excellent horse of noble breed (**yathāpi bhaddo ājañño**), having slipped (**khalitvā**), quickly regains its footing (**patitiṭṭhati**); so too is the disciple of the Rightly and Fully Awakened (**evaṁ sammā-sam-buddha-sāvakaṁ**), endowed with vision (**dassana-sampannaṁ**).*

**Commentary**: In this verse, Bhante uses the metaphor of a horse of noble breed to illustrate how a true disciple of Gautama Buddha, endowed with vision, immediately recovers their composure and mindfulness if they stumble or make a lapse in practice. A horse of noble breed may occasionally slip on rough terrain, but due to its innate strength and intelligence, it does not collapse. It rights itself instantaneously. Similarly, a disciple who is actively training in **Sīla**, **Samādhi**, and **Paññā** may experience a brief moment of heedlessness, but instantly catches the mind (*citta*) before it falls into full unwholesomeness.

For a disciple, \"slipping\" (**khalitvā**) refers to a moment of heedlessness---a sudden, unwholesome mental movement (*vitakka*) or a brief distraction by physical phenomena. A person lacking vision reacts to a slip with confusion, guilt, or deeper indulgence, causing them to fall completely into unwholesome states (*akusala*).

However, a practitioner endowed with vision (**dassana-sampanna**) possesses instant internal awareness. The moment a subtle slip occurs in the mind, their sharp discernment acts like the horse\'s innate balance: they recognize the movement, cut it off immediately, and restore Concentration (**Samādhi**) without delay. Because of this razor-sharp discernment, the noble disciple does not indulge in guilt, delusion, or prolonged unwholesome thoughts; the slip is noted, cut off, and correct alignment is restored immediately. Always maintains continuous mindful vigilance (**appamāda**) over the mental phenomena.

#### 1.46. Samiddhi

**Saddhāyāhaṁ pabbajito, Agārasmānagāriyaṁ;\
Sati paññā ca me vuḍḍhā, Cittañca susamāhitaṁ;\
Kāmaṁ karassu rūpāni, Neva maṁ byādhayissasī ti.**

*Out of faith I have renounced family life (**saddhāya ahaṁ pabbajito**), from home to homelessness (**agārasmā anagāriyaṁ**); my mindfulness and wisdom have grown (**sati paññā ca me vuḍḍhā**), and my mind-heart / **citta** is well-concentrated (**cittañca susamāhitaṁ**); make whatever physical forms you want to (**kāmaṁ karassu rūpāni**), you will never disturb me (**neva maṁ byādhayissasī**)!*

Commentary: In this verse, Bhante declares his total unshakeability in the face of tempting or distracting physical forms (***rūpāni***). Having renounced family life (**pabbajito**) out of faith (**saddhā**), nurtured mindfulness and wisdom (**sati paññā**), and unified / concentrated his mind (**samāhita/ samādahati**), he challenges worldly forms---or the tempter Māra---to present whatever enticing sights they wish, declaring that they can never disturb or agitate his purified mind. Bhante outlines the invulnerable progression of a practitioner, moving from initial faith to absolute mental mastery over all physical phenomena (**rūpāni**).

**(1). The Foundation of Renunciation (*Saddhā* & *Anagāriyaṁ*):** Renouncing family life and going forth from home to homelessness is not merely a physical change of residence; it is the deliberate departure from worldly dependencies. Faith (**saddhā**) serves as the initial spark that impels the practitioner to leave behind household attachments and devote themselves entirely to the ancient path to liberation.

**(2). Maturation of Mindfulness and Wisdom (*Sati Paññā ca Vuḍḍhā*):** As the training deepens through **Sīla**, **Samādhi**, and **Paññā**, mindfulness (**sati**) becomes continuous and wisdom (**paññā**) matures.

**(3). Immensity of Concentration (*Cittañca Susamāhitaṁ*):** When the mind (*citta*) is well-concentrated (**su-samāhita**), it reaches an unshakeable state that sensual objects, tempting visions, or terrifying physical forms (**rūpāni**) lose all power to agitate the mind.

#### 1.47. Ujjaya

**Namo te buddha vīratthu, vippamuttosi sabbadhi;\
Tuyhāpadāne viharaṁ, viharāmi anāsavo ti.**

*Homage to you, O Buddha, may homage be to you, O Hero / **vīra** (**namo te buddha vīra atthu**), you are fully liberated from everything in all respects (**vippamutto asi sabbadhi**); living following your footsteps (**tuyha apadāne viharaṁ**), I live without defilement / taintless (**viharāmi anāsavo**)!*

**Commentary**: **Heroism & Walking the Path**

In this verse, Bhante expresses his deep reverence to Gautama Buddha. He honors the Buddha as the Hero (**Vīra**) who is fully released from all ties to the world (**vippamutto sabbadhi**), and declares his walking in Gautama's footsteps and living without taints (**anāsavo**). This declaration encapsulates the complete realization of a noble disciple (**sāvaka**) following the ancient path taught by the Sugato.

**(1). The Epithet *Vīra* (Hero) and the Jain Parallel (*Mahāvīra*):** The title **Vīra** (\"Hero\" or \"Valiant One\") was a widely recognized term across ancient Indian Śramaṇa traditions for a spiritual conqueror. In Jainism, it became the primary name of the 24th Tīrthaṅkara (**Mahāvīra**---\"Great Hero\").

In early Pali literature, Gautama Buddha is frequently addressed as **Vīra** because spiritual victory in the Śramaṇa traditions was understood not as conquering external kingdoms, but as overcoming internal defilements: By conquering the entire realm of *Māra* (sensuality, worldly existence, and ignorance), Gautama Buddha was granted the epithet **Vīra**---the ultimate spiritual warrior who rediscovered the ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**(2). Living in the Footsteps (*Tuyhāpadāne Viharaṁ*):** Walking in Gautama Buddha\'s footsteps (**apadāna**) means to renounce the family life; to practise in seclusion for years to cultivate **Sīla, Samādhi and Paññā;** and ultimately after attaining the Deathless, share the Path that leads to the Deathless with people.

**(3). Living Without Taints (*Anāsavo*):** The term **anāsavo** defines the state of a fully purified Arahant. The *āsavas* (defilements/taints) are the underlying pollutants---sensual desire, attachment to worldly existence, wrong views, and ignorance---that bind the *citta* to the world (*loka*). Living **anāsavo** means the fuel for involuntary reincarnation is completely destroyed, leaving the mind-heart untouched by the universal law of death.

#### 1.48. Sañjaya

**Yato ahaṁ pabbajito, Agārasmānagāriyaṁ;\
Nābhijānāmi saṅkappaṁ, Anariyaṁ dosasaṁhitan ti.**

*From the time I (**yato ahaṁ**) became a renunciate (**pabbajito**), from home to homelessness (**agārasmā anagāriyaṁ**); I am not aware of having harbored any intention (**nābhijānāmi saṅkappaṁ**), that is ignoble or connected with ill-will (**anariyaṁ dosa-saṁhitan ti**).*

**Commentary**: ***Ariya*** (Sanskrit: *Ārya*)

In this verse, Bhante reflects upon his absolute purity of mind (***Ariya***) since he *became a renunciate (**pabbaja)***, declaring that from the very moment he left the household life, he has never harbored an ignoble thought (***anariya***) or infected by ill-will (***dosa***). Bhante's declaration highlights the ***Ariya*** way of life is required of a practitioner following physical renunciation - Samanera (***pabbaja***).

The historical evolution of the word ***Ariya*** (Sanskrit: *Ārya*)---from an active, functional standard of noble conduct into an exclusive, highly exalted badge of spiritual attainment (One who has attained mental purity and direct insight into Truth, as we see from today's Buddhism standard understanding)---represents one of the most significant semantic shifts in ancient Indian thought.

Analyzing this transformation reveals how the original, pragmatic definition found in the early *Rigveda* and preserved in early renunciate texts was gradually overlaid with institutional pride, scholastic categorization, and dogmatic exclusivity. Now let's reveal the true meaning of Noble Way of life taught by Gautama Buddha.

**(1). *Ārya* in the Earliest *Rigveda:*** In the earliest Mandala layers of the *Rigveda* (particularly Mandalas 2--7, the core \"Family Books\"), *Ārya* does not refer to a racial group that later and modern academia often depicted, but to a **community defined by specific religious, ethical, and social standards**.

An *Ārya* in the *Rigveda* is one who:

- Upholds **Ṛta** (the divine order, Brahmavihara values).

- Performs sacrifices (**Yajña**) with devotions and truthfulness (**Satya**).

- Cultivates noble character traits such as generosity, courage, and hospitality.

- Expresses devotion through poetic, structured speech (**Sūktas**).

Those who stood outside or opposed this ethical order were termed *Dasyu* or *Dāsa*, defined primarily by their lack of practice (*avrata*), absence of devotion (*adevayu*), or hostile speech.

**Verse Examples from the *Rigveda***

**Defining the *Ārya* by Moral & Divine Order (*Ṛta*)**

The *Ārya* is characterized by upholding *Ṛta* (cosmic truth/order) under the guardianship of deities like Varuṇa and Mitra:

**Rigveda 1.51.8:** *vijānīhy āryān ye cha dasyavo barhiṣmate randhayā śāsad avratān.* \"Discern well between the **Āryas** and those who are **Dasyus**; bring the lawless (*avratān*) under control for the sake of the sacred-grass performer.\"

**Key Meaning:** The distinction is operational and behavioral---the *Ārya* is bound by sacred vows and law (*vrata*), whereas the *Dasyu* acts without noble restraint (*avrata*).

***Ārya* as the Upholder of Light and Wisdom**

In Indra hymns, *Ārya* is linked with light, truth, and spiritual nobility over darkness and delusion:

**Rigveda 10.49.3:** *ahaṁ bhuvam vasunaḥ kāvyena maham ugraḥ kavir abhi śravobhiḥ, ahaṁ vedā asya nāma āryam ahaṁ dasyūñ chnamathāyābhi śuṣṇam.* \"By wisdom (*kāvya*) I have become the lord of treasures\... I make known the noble (*ārya*) name; I strike down the Dasyus\...\"

**Key Meaning:** Nobility (*āryam*) is directly tied to wisdom (*kāvya*), truth, and divine light, rather than mere physical lineage.

**Generosity and Moral Integrity (*Dāna* & *Satya*)**

In the *Rigveda*, noble living requires active generosity and truthfulness. A true *Ārya* does not hoard wealth:

**Rigveda 10.117.5:** *praṇīyārāyasaḥ śreyāṁsam atiyāt, moghaṁ annaṁ vindate apracetāḥ satyaṁ bravīmi vadha itsa tasya.* \"Let the wealthy man satisfy the needy\... In vain does the unthinking man acquire food; I speak the truth: it is verily his destruction.\"

**Key Meaning:** Wealth accumulated without generosity is deemed ignoble and self-destructive, establishing an early moral imperative for *Āryahood*.

**(2) The Historical Grounding: *Ariya* as an Active Vow of Conduct:** In the earliest strata of the *Rigveda* (Mandalas 2--7), *Ārya* functioned as an **active, behavioral standard**. It was defined by living in alignment with *Ṛta* (divine/moral order), keeping sacred vows (*vrata*), and acting with truthfulness (*satya*) and generosity (*dāna*).

When early practitioners went forth from the household life into homelessness (**agārasmā anagāriyaṁ**), they adopted this exact Vedic sense of *Ariya*: **a commitment to a noble way of life**.

In verses like *Theragāthā 1.48*, the practitioner states: *\"Since I became a renunciate\... I am not aware of having harbored any intention that is ignoble (anariya) or connected with ill-will (dosasaṁhita).\"*

Here, *anariya* (ignoble) is simply any action or thought driven by *dosa* (ill-will, malice, hatred). To live an *Ariya* life meant **living without malice, living honorably, and actively guarding one\'s conduct** from the very day of Samanera ordination (Novice). It was a lifestyle choice and an immediate standard of training---not a certificate of final enlightenment or achievement of complete mental purity.

**(3). The Later Distortion: Pride, Scholasticism, and Exclusion:** As centuries passed and monastic institutions grew, the living, practical standard of *Ariya* underwent a dramatic conceptual inflation, driven by several key factors:

**A. Institutional Pride and Exclusivism**

As the Buddhist monastic order evolved from a loose movement of forest-dwelling renunciates into large, wealthy, and organized monastic centers, a spirit of sectarian competition arose. Monastic communities sought to distinguish themselves from laypeople and competing ascetics. By elevating *Ariya* from a \"noble standard of training\" to a supernatural, hyper-exclusive spiritual rank, the institutional hierarchy asserted absolute authority over spiritual validation. But this is not limited to the mere shift of the meaning of *Ariya,* but instead a system degeneration from Arahanism to Buddhism, from Individual liberation path for renunciates to lay people's Mahayana movement, and more.

**B. The Scholastic Abhidharma Shift**

In later commentaries and Abhidharma treatises, the dynamic, active concept of the *Ariya-magga* (the Noble Path as a way of living) was frozen into rigid, technical taxonomies. The term *Ariya-puggala* (Noble Person) was mapped onto strict, ontological categories (Stream-enterer, Once-returner, Non-returner, Arahant) based on the exact count of eradicated fetters.

**The Loss of Practical Applicability:** In this scholastic shift, a novice or dedicated *bhikkhu* sincerely striving to live without ill-will was no longer considered *Ariya*; they were demoted to the status of a mere *puthujjana* (worldling/commoner).

**The Puffing-Up of Language:** By insisting that *Ariya* could *only* refer to someone who had attained transcendental direct insight into Truth, later dogmatists distorted the everyday reality of the renunciate\'s vow. They turned an attainable daily ethic into an unapproachable, lofty ideal, creating a vast spiritual gulf between the \"enlightened elite\" and the everyday practitioner.

**C. Separation from the Vedic Root & Polemical Weaponization Against *Samaṇa* Lineages**

By abandoning the original, accessible definition of *Ariya*---which defined any renunciate actively living a life of harmlessness, truthfulness, and moral vow (*vrata*)---later Buddhist monastic institutions achieved a double separation:

**a. Severing the Vedic Foundation**

In the early *Rigveda* and early renunciate strata, *Ārya* was a functional standard of noble living, *Ṛta* (divine order), and generosity. By re-engineering *Ariya* into an exclusive, internal status of total transcendental realization, later Buddhist dogmatists severed the word from its shared Indian heritage, denying that non-monastic or Vedic practitioners could embody true nobility of life through moral integrity alone.

**b. Declaring Sectarian Superiority Over other *Samaṇa* Lineages**

During the post-canonical and Abhidharma periods, Northern and Southern monastic centers were locked in intense competition with other *samaṇa* sects (such as the Jains) for royal patronage, lay support, and ideological dominance.

- **Monopolizing \"Nobility\":** Later Buddhist polemicists redefined *Ariya* so narrowly that only those following their specific doctrinal path (*Ariya-puggala*) could be classified as \"Noble.\"

- **Branding Others as *Anariya* (Ignoble):** *samaṇa* lineages---regardless of how rigorous their asceticism, how pure their moral conduct, or how dedicated their practice of non-violence (*ahiṁsā*) was---were systematically re-categorized as *anariya* (ignoble, worldly, or blind *puthujjanas*).

- **Fuelling Sectarian Warfare:** This intellectual pride and institutional \"puffing-up\" converted a universally accessible vow of harmlessness into an elitist badge. By claiming an absolute monopoly on the title *Ariya*, monastic scholastics created rigid dogmatic walls, turning what was once a shared ethic of noble renunciate conduct into a tool for sectarian exclusion and inter-tradition conflict.

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Dimension**                    **Early Vedic & Early Renunciate**                                                                         **Later Monastic & Scholastic Distortion**
  -------------------------------- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
