  **Primary Meaning**              An active, noble way of living; keeping vows (*vrata*); avoiding ill-will (*adosa*).                       An exclusive spiritual status reserved solely for those with transcendental realization within a specific Buddhist lineage.

  **Operational Definition**       *\"I do not harbor thoughts of ill-will.\"* (Immediate ethical practice shared among renunciates).         *\"I have permanently severed X number of mental fetters.\"* (Ontological state defined by scholastic taxonomy).

  **Relation to Other Lineages**   Recognizes any practitioner or *samaṇa* living a pure, harmless life as maintaining an *Ariya* standard.   Denigrates rival *samaṇa* traditions (Jains, Ājīvikas, Vedic ascetics) as inherently *anariya* (ignoble/unpurified).

  **Driven By**                    Moral integrity, active restraint, and practical mind-guarding.                                            Institutional pride, scholastic classification, sectarian warfare, and competition for patronage.
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

**Reclaiming the Universal Standard of the *Ariya* Life**

When we strip away centuries of scholastic inflation, sectarian pride, and polemical warfare, the original meaning of *Ariya* re-emerges clearly. It is not an elitist trophy meant to divide one *samaṇa* lineage from another, but an immediate, universal standard of daily mental discipline. A true *bhikkhu* or renunciate embodies the *Ariya* way of life from the very day of going forth by actively refusing to harbor *dosa* (ill-will), guarding their intention (**Sammā Saṅkappa**), and grounding themselves in **Sīla**, **Samādhi**, and **Paññā**.

#### 1.49. Rāmaṇeyyaka

**Cihacihābhinadite, sippikābhirutehi ca;\
Na me taṁ phandati cittaṁ, ekattānirataṁ hi me ti.**

*Resounding with the chirping of birds (cihacihābhinadite), and filled with the calls of cicadas (sippikābhirutehi ca); my mind-heart **citta** does not agitate at that (na me taṁ phandati cittaṁ), for (hi) my (me) established-in-oneness (ekattā-nirataṁ)!*

**Commentary**: **ekattā** (*noun / eka + -tta*): oneness, single-pointedness, unification. **nirataṁ** (*past passive participle of niramati / ni- +ram*): devoted to, delighted in, attached to, intent upon.

**Meaning:** Devoted to single-pointed unification / delighting in mental oneness / established in oneness. In this verse, Bhante describes his complete internal stillness while meditating in the deep forest. Surrounding him are the loud, persistent sounds of nature---chirping birds and singing cicadas---yet because his mind-heart (*citta*) is firmly unified in single-pointed concentration, it remains entirely unagitated, unshakeable, and still. Bhante presents a clear marker of authentic **Samādhi**: the complete immunity of the sovereign mind-heart (*citta*) to sensory distraction. It shows that true forest renunciation does not require absolute silence in the physical environment; rather, it requires that the *citta* become so thoroughly stilled and unified (**ekattānirataṁ**) that sound waves striking the ear cannot cause the mind to throb or tremble (**na phandati**).

#### 1.50. Vimala (1st)

**Dharaṇī ca siñcati, vāti Māluto, vijjutā carati nabhe;\
Upasamanti vitakkā, Cittaṁ susamāhitaṁ mama ti.**

*The earth is soaked with rain (**dharaṇī ca siñcati**), the wind blows (**vāti māluto**), and lightning moves across the sky (**vijjutā carati nabhe**); my discursive thoughts completely still (**upasamanti vitakkā**), for my mind-heart **citta** is perfectly concentrated (**cittaṁ susamāhitaṁ mama**)!*

**Commentary**: **Upasamanti & Samāhita**

Set against the backdrop of a roaring thunderstorm in the forest, Bhante describes the supreme inner tranquility: while rain pours down, wind howls, and lightning flashes across the sky, all mental proliferation (*vitakkā*) completely stills, and his mind (*citta*) remains profoundly unified and serene.

**Upasamanti (present verb):** Still completely / subside.

- (*3rd person plural present active of upasamati / upa- + sam*): they quiet down, become still, subside completely.

**Vitakkā (masculine noun / nominative plural):** Discursive thoughts / mental movements.

- **vitakkā** (*nominative plural of vitakka*): thoughts, discursive thinking, mental movement/applied thought.

**Susamāhitaṁ (su- + samāhitaṁ / compound adjective):** Thoroughly concentrated / perfectly unified.

- **su-**: well, perfectly, thoroughly.

- **samāhitaṁ** (*past passive participle of samādahati / saṁ- + ā- + dhā*): concentrated, collected, unified in Samādhi.

- **susamāhitaṁ** (*nominative singular neuter describing cittaṁ*): thoroughly concentrated / perfectly stilled.

#### 1.51. Godhika

**Vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ, Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā;\
Cittaṁ susamāhitañca mayhaṁ, Atha ce patthayasi pavassa deva ti.**

*The **Deva** rains like a beautiful melody (**vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ**); my **kuṭi (me kuṭikā)** is roofed (**channā**), comfortable (**sukhā**), and sheltered from the wind (**nivātā**); my mind-heart (**citta**) is perfectly unified (**su-samāhitañca**), belonging to me (**mayhaṁ**); so if you wish (**atha ce patthayasi**), **Deva**, rain forth! (**pavassa**)*

#### 1.52. Subāhu

**Vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ, Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā;\
Cittaṁ susamāhitañca kāye, Atha ce patthayasi pavassa deva ti.**

*The **Deva** rains like a beautiful melody (**vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ**); my **kuṭi (me kuṭikā)** is roofed (**channā**), comfortable (**sukhā**), and sheltered from the wind (**nivātā**); my mind-heart (**citta**) is perfectly unified (**su-samāhitañca**), in the body (**kāye**); so if you wish (**atha ce patthayasi**), **Deva**, rain forth! (**pavassa**)*

#### 1.53. Valliya (1st)

**Vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ, Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā;\
Tassaṁ viharāmi appamatto, Atha ce patthayasi pavassa deva ti.**

*The **Deva** rains like a beautiful melody* (**vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ**); my ***kuṭi (me kuṭikā)** is roofed (**channā**), comfortable (**sukhā**), and sheltered from the wind (**nivātā**); within it (**tassaṁ**) I live (**viharāmi**) zealous and vigilant (**appamatto**)*; *so if you wish (**atha ce patthayasi**), **Deva**, rain forth! (**pavassa**)*

**Commentary:** The word **appamatto** (the nominative singular masculine form of *appamāda*) is the absolute linchpin of Gautama's liberation path. Gautama himself emphasized the importance of appamāda in his parting words to his followers, as: \"Transient are all formations. Strive zealously!\" (appamādena sampādetha). It is not merely a state of watchfulness, but the active, unyielding engine of liberation itself---the razor-sharp boundary between falling into the cyclical vortex of saṃsāra and breaking free to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**The Etymological Urgency: Survival Against Death (*Pamādo Maccuno Padaṁ*)**

The *Dhammapada* serves as an unmatched, crystal-distilled summary of the Gautama's Dhamma. That the second chapter---the *Appamāda Vagga*---is placed right at the beginning of this collection is a deliberate, profound statement on the architecture of the liberation path toward the Deathless. Before one can master liberating wisdom (*paññā*) or deep *samādhi*, one must establish the unyielding engine of **appamāda** (zeal, diligence, heedfulness, and energetic vigilance).

**(1). The Strategic Placement of Chapter 2:** In the structure of the *Dhammapada*, Chapter 1 (*Yamaka Vagga*) sets the foundational cosmic law: *mind is the forerunner of everything* (*manopubbaṅgamā dhammā*). Right after establishing this premise, Chapter 2 introduces **appamāda** as the absolute prerequisite for mastering that mind.

Without *appamāda*, all philosophical knowledge remains theoretical, and the *citta* inevitably drifts back into the currents of worldliness (*loka*). As in the *Dhammapada* verse, *pamāda* represents the path of \"going to die\"---the state of negligence, heedlessness, and spiritual stupor. To be *pamattā* is to drift passively down the current of worldly phenomena (**loka**), completely helpless against the laws of cause and effect, aging and death (*maccu*).

The metaphor of a person drowning in a rushing river captures the exact operational essence of *appamāda*: **Pamāda (Going to Die):** The passive surrender to the current, allowing the waves of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* to pull one under. **Appamāda (Not to Die):** The immediate, explosive instinct for survival---the active, unwavering struggle to pull oneself out of the current and reach the secure shore of safety.

**(2). The Threshold Between Life and Death (*Amata vs. Maccu*):** *Dhammapada* Verse 21 establishes the foundational dichotomy: *Appamādo amatapadaṃ, pamādo maccuno padaṃ; Appamattā na mīyanti, ye pamattā yathā matā.*

***Zeal (Appamādo) is the foundation for the Deathless; apathy (pamādo) is the foundation for death. The zealous (Appamattā) do not die; the zealless (pamattā) are like the dead.***

Here, *pamāda* is not merely \"forgetfulness\"; it is spiritual drowsiness---a state of passive existence where one is constantly swept away by physical phenomena and mental projections. To be *pamattā* is to be functionally dead (*yathā matā*), living helplessly under the laws of cause and effect, old age, and mortality. Conversely, *appamāda* is the immediate, explosive instinct to swim against the worldly current, anchoring the *citta* in absolute awakeness.

**(3). The Internal Citadel (*Dīpa*)**

Verse 25 describes how the wise build a sanctuary that no flood can submerge: *Uṭṭhānenappamādena, saṃyamena damena ca;\
Dīpaṃ kayirātha medhāvī, yaṃ ogho n'ābhikīrati.*

***With zeal arisen, and thoughts well controlled, the wise make himself an island (Dīpa), which no flood can overwhelm.***

The physical world (*loka*) is subject to relentless storm-waves of change, decay, and sensory disturbance. The diligent practitioner (*bhikkhu*) uses *appamāda* to forge an internal citadel. By establishing unyielding mindfulness and guarding the sense-doors, external worldly storms cannot penetrate the inner shelter.

**(4). The Ascent to the Palace of Wisdom (Paññā-pāsāda)**

Verse 28 highlights the cognitive clarity born of non-negligence: *Pamādaṃ appamādena, yadā nudati paṇḍito; Paññāpāsādamāruyha, asoko sokiniṃ pajaṃ; Pabbataṭṭho'va bhūmaṭṭhe, dhīro bāle avekkhati.*

***When the wise person discards zealless by zeal, he, free from sorrow, ascends to the palace of wisdom and surveys the sorrowing ignorant folk as a mountaineer surveys those below.***

This is not pride or condescension, but the natural perspective of **transcendental release** (*upekkhā*). By overcoming the stupor of worldliness through continuous effort, the *citta* rises above the physical dimension of worldly phenomena with absolute clarity. The term **\"Palace of Wisdom\"** translates the Pali compound **Paññāpāsāda** (*paññā* = wisdom/discernment + *pāsāda* = mansion, palace, or elevated terraced structure).

It is a striking metaphor representing the elevated mental vantage point achieved by wise practitioner who has overcome worldly negligence (*pamāda*) through continuous zeal (*appamāda*).

In **MN 26 - *Ariyapariyesana Sutta* & DN 14 - *Mahāpadāna Sutta*:** Shortly after his Gautama's full awakening and liberation, Brahma Sahampati entreats the Buddha to teach the Dhamma using a nearly identical imagery: *\"Just as one standing on a rocky mountain peak surveys the people all around below, so let the Wise One, free from sorrow, ascend the terraced Mansion of Truth (Dhammamayaṁ pāsādaṁ) and look down upon the people submerged in grief, overcome by birth and decay.\"*

Just as a person standing high on a terraced palace (*pāsāda*) or mountain peak (*pabbataṭṭho*) gains an unobstructed, panoramic view of the people struggling in the low plains below (*bhūmaṭṭhe*), the practitioner who develops *Paññā* looks down upon the sorrows of worldliness (*loka*) with absolute, unshakeable transcendental release (*upekkhā*). When the *citta* ascends the *Paññā-pāsāda*, it clearly experiences complete disenchantment (*nibbida*) and fading of passion (*virāga*).

**(5). The Irresistible Force of the Zealous Bhikkhu**

Verses 31--32 depict the *bhikkhu* who delights in *appamāda*: *Appamādarato bhikkhu, pamāde bhayadassi vā; Saṃyojanaṃ aṇuṃ thūlaṃ, ḍahaṃ aggī'va gacchati. Appamādarato bhikkhu, pamāde bhayadassi vā; Abhabbo parihānāya, nibbānass'eva santike.*

***The zealous bhikkhu, realizing the danger of zealless, advances like fire, burning all fetters great and small. The zealous bhikkhu, realizing the danger of zealless, unable to regress, he is just in the vicinity of the nibbāna.***

*Appamāda* acts as an internal fire (*aggī*). It burns through subtle and coarse attachments alike, ensuring that the practitioner cannot fall back into spiritual stagnation and is brought directly to the threshold of Nibbāna.

**(6). The Evolutionary Continuum of Appamāda**

*Appamāda* is dynamic; it evolves naturally as practice deepens:

**Initial Drive (*Chanda*):** It begins as the initial spiritual urge, the energetic zeal to escape the suffering of involuntary reincarnation.

**Guarding Virtue (*Sīla*):** It matures into active vigilance over thoughts, speech, and physical actions.

**Internal Unification (*Samādhi*):** It becomes the unshakeable anchor of concentration within the body (**cittaṁ susamāhitañca kāye**).

**Penetrative Insight (*Paññā*):** It becomes the razor-sharp discernment that sees all conditioned formations (*saṅkhārā*) as impermanent, unsatisfactory, and non-self.

It is profoundly significant that when the Buddha reached his final moments before passing into Parinibbāna in the *Mahāparinibbāna Sutta* (DN 16), he distilled his entire forty-five years of teaching into this single, ultimate injunction: *Vayadhammā saṅkhārā appamādena sampādetha. **\"Transient are all conditioned formations. Strive zealously with diligence!\"***

All worldly conditioned things (*saṅkhārā*) are subject to the universal law of decay and death (*dukkha*). With *appamāda*, the *citta* may recondition / decondition itself to free from the worldly conditioning of *raga*, *dosa* and *moha*, so the internal soul-spirit *ātmā/ attā* may exit the universe to enter the other shore of the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

**(7). Early Veda, Upaniṣads and Jainism**

While the term **appamattā** (Sanskrit **apramattā** / **apramāda**) appears across ancient Indian literature, its status as the absolute core, indispensable driver of liberation from the world is a distinct hallmark of the Śramaṇa (Samana) traditions---most notably early Buddhism and Jainism---rather than the early Vedic texts.

In the earliest strata of Vedic literature (**Rigveda, Samaveda, Yajurveda, Atharvaveda**), the concept of *apramāda* is virtually absent as a central spiritual framework. Early Vedic practice focused primarily on *yajña* (sacrifice), *ṛta* (divine order), *tapas* (primordial force from the soul-spirit **ātmā**), and mantras. As Vedic evolved into the **Upaniṣads**, terms derived from *pramāda* / *apramāda* began to appear, but usually in reference to specific meditative focus or non-negligence in duties:

- **Mundaka Upaniṣad (2.2.4):** Uses the term *apramattena* in a famous archery metaphor describing meditation on *Om*:

*\"The Pranava (Om) is the bow, the arrow is the self (Atman), and Brahma is said to be the mark. It is to be hit by an undistracted/non-negligent person (**apramattena**); then one becomes united with it like the arrow with the target.\"*

- **Taittirīya Upaniṣad (1.11.1):** Features the famous ethical command to graduating students: *satyaṁ vada, dharmaṁ chara, svādhyāyān mā pramadaḥ* (\"Speak the truth, practice righteousness, do not zealless \[**mā pramadaḥ**\] in self-study\").

In contrast to the Vedic tradition, the **Jain tradition** placed an equally paramount importance on *apramāda*.

In Jainism, spiritual progress is measured along fourteen stages of spiritual evolution called *guṇasthānas*. The transition from the lower stages of bondage to high spiritual attainment hinges explicitly on overcoming *pramāda* (heedlessness/carelessness):

- **The 6th and 7th Guṇasthānas:**

**Pramattā-saṁyata (6th Stage):** The ascetic who observes vows but still suffers from *pramāda* (zealless).

**Apramattā-saṁyata (7th Stage):** The ascetic who completely eliminates *pramāda*, becoming **apramattā** (fully vigilant, zealous, and active in self-restraint).

- **The Uttarādhyayana Sūtra:** In Chapter 10 of this fundamental Svetambara Jain text, Mahāvīra repeatedly instructs his disciple Gautama with the famous refrain: *Samaṇa, mā pamāyae!*

  **\"O Samaṇa, do not be zealless even for a moment!\"**

For Jains, *pramāda* leads directly to the inflow (*āsrava*) of karmic matter onto the soul-spirit, while *apramāda* stops karmic inflow (*saṁvara*) and leads to liberation.

#### 1.54. Uttiya (2nd)

**Vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ, Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā;\
Tassaṁ viharāmi adutiyo, Atha ce patthayasi pavassa deva ti.**

*The Deva rains like a beautiful melody (vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ); my kuṭi (me kuṭikā) is roofed (channā), comfortable (sukhā), and sheltered from the wind (nivātā); within it (tassaṁ) I live (viharāmi) without a second companion / solitary (adutiyo); so if you wish (atha ce patthayasi), Deva, rain forth! (pavassa).*

#### 1.55. Añjanavaniya

**Āsandiṁ kuṭikaṁ katvā, ogayha añjanaṁ vanaṁ;\
Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Having constructed a kuṭi from a bench (āsandiṁ kuṭikaṁ katvā), and entered deep into the Añjana forest (ogayha añjanaṁ vanaṁ); the three Knowledges (tisso vijjā) have been attained (anuppattā), the teaching of the Buddha (buddhassa sāsanaṁ) has been done/ accomplished (kataṁ).*

#### 1.56. Kuṭivihārin (1st)

**Ko kuṭikāyaṁ bhikkhu kuṭikāyaṁ, Vītarāgo susamāhitacitto;\
Evaṁ jānāhi āvuso, Amoghā te kuṭikā kata ti.**

*Who (ko) in the kuṭi (kuṭikāyaṁ)? A bhikkhu is in the kuṭi (kuṭikāyaṁ), one whose craving/ attachment is gone (vītarāgo), with mind-heart fully concentrated (susamāhitacitto); know (jānāhi) thus (evaṁ), friend (āvuso), not in vain (amoghā) was your (te) kuṭi (kuṭikā) made (katā).*

#### 1.57. Kuṭivihārin (2nd)

**Ayamāhu purāṇiyā kuṭi, Aññaṁ patthayase navaṁ kuṭiṁ;\
Āsaṁ kuṭiyā virājaya, Dukkhā bhikkhu puna navā kuṭī ti.**

*They say (āhu) this (ayaṁ) is an old (purāṇiyā) **kuṭi**, you desire (patthayase) another (aññaṁ) new (navaṁ) kuṭi; relinquish (virājaya) desire (āsaṁ) for kuṭi (kuṭiyā), a new (navā) kuṭī again (puna) is dukkha (dukkhā), O bhikkhu.*

#### 1.58. Ramaṇīyakuṭika

**Ramaṇīyā me kuṭikā, Saddhādeyyā manoramā;\
Na me attho kumārīhi, Yesaṁ attho tahiṁ gacchatha nāriyo ti.**

*Delightful (ramaṇīyā) is my (me) **kuṭi** (kuṭikā), a gift given in faith (saddhādeyyā), mentally uplifting / pleasing to the mind-will (manoramā); there is no (na) need (attho) for me (me) with young women (kumārīhi), those who (yesaṁ) have need (attho), go (gacchatha) there (tahiṁ) to the women (nāriyo).*

#### 1.59. Kosalavihārin

**Saddhāyāhaṁ pabbajito, araññe me kuṭikā katā;\
Appamatto ca ātāpī, sampajāno patissato ti.**

*Out of faith (saddhāyā) I (ahaṁ) became a renunciate (pabbajito), in the forest (araññe) my (me) **kuṭi** (kuṭikā) was constructed (katā); zeal (**appamatto**) and (ca) possessing tapas / spiritual primordial force (**ātāpī**), full of clear comprehension (**sampajāno**), established in mindfulness (**patissato**).*

**Commentary**: The four terms **appamatto**, **ātāpī**, **sampajāno**, and **patissato** form a tightly integrated formula across the Pāḷi Canon, detailing the mental equipment required for deep contemplation and ultimate liberation.

**Appamatto** (*a + pamattā*): Derived from *a-* (non) + *pamattā* (past participle of *pamajjati*, to be zeal, spiritual drowsiness). Meaning zealous, earnest, diligent, vigilant, or non-negligent. *Appamāda* is the spiritual power of the mind, the power directly from the soul-spirit **ātmā,** which is multidimensional. It signifies the yigi practitioner connects/ have union with high dimensional existence and draws lifting power from the soul-spirit for the purpose of lifting the mind/soul-spirit out of the lower dimensional trap humans are in.

**Ātāpī** (*ā + √tap + -ī*): Derived from the prefix *ā-* (thoroughly) + verbal root *√tap* (the primordial force, cosmic generative power) + possessive suffix *-ī*. It denotes one who possesses cosmic generative power, spiritual power, soul-spirit power, or intense exertion. Because human soul-spirit **ātmā** is a fractal part of the cosmic source consciousness, so it inherits the generative/creative power of the source of universe. It is a power under *Appamāda.*

- In the early Vedic and Upanishadic traditions, *tapas* signified the cosmic, gestative fire (*Rigveda* 10.129) and the inner spiritual energy linked to *Brahman* (*Taittirīya Upanishad* 3.2.1). In the Jain tradition (*Tattvārtha Sūtra* 9.3), *tapas* represented the intense ascetic fire used to burn off karmic seeds (*nirjarā*) bound to the soul (*jīva*).

- Within the Pāḷi canonical framework, *ātāpī* functions as the dynamic, energetic factor (*sammā-vāyāma*) applied internally to burn away the defilements of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

**Veda: Cosmic Heat, Creation, and Ātman**

In the earliest Vedic literature, *tapas* is defined literally as \"heat\" or \"warmth\"---originally the gestative, incubating heat through which the universe was generated.

- **Rigveda 10.129 (Nāsadīya Sūkta):** In the creation hymn of the Rigveda, the original unmanifest cosmic unity comes into being purely through the power of heat: *\"That One which came to be, enclosed in nothingness, was produced through the power of tapas.\"* (*tapasas tan mahinājāyataekam*)

- **Rigveda 10.190.1:** Heat is framed as the primordial creative force preceding order and truth: *\"Order (ṛta) and Truth (satya) were born from tapas.\"* (*ṛtaṁ ca satyaṁ cābhīddhāt tapaso\'dhyajāyata*)

- **Upanishads (Taittirīya & Muṇḍaka Upanishads):** *tapas* was directly identified as the inner power and essence of the *Ātman* or *Brahman*.

  - In the *Taittirīya Upanishad* (3.2.1), Bhrigu approaches his father Varuṇa to learn about Brahman, and Varuṇa commands: *\"Seek to know Brahman by tapas; tapas is Brahman.\"* (*tapasa brahma vijijñāsasva, tapo brahma*)

  - Here, *tapas* is the primordial force generated by *samādhi,* allowing the soul (*Ātman*) to realize its intrinsic identity with the divine source (*Brahman*).

**Jain: Śramaṇa** **Fire to Burn Karma**

In Jainism---the oldest śramaṇa traditions---*tapas* (or *tapasya*) is defined literally as an internal \"fire\" generated to burn off accumulated karmic seeds (*karma-nirjarā*) attached to the soul (*jīva*).

**Tattvārtha Sūtra (Chapter 9, Sūtra 3):** The fundamental Jain text by Ācārya Umāsvāti explicitly defines *tapas* as the mechanism of burning away karma: *\"Nirjarā \[the shedding of karma\] occurs through tapas.\"* (*tapasā nirjarā ca*)

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra (Chapter 30):** The text classifies *tapas* training exercises into internal (*ābhyantara*) and external (*bāhya*). External exercises to induce *tapas* involve severe physical training (fasting, heat exposure, postures, near death experience) to induce the spiritual higher dimensional power into our mind and physical body to burn away the karmic seeds bound to the pure, eternal soul (*jīva*).

In the Jain framework, the soul (*jīva*) possesses infinite energy (*ananta-vīrya*), and *tapas* is the intense spiritual power required to purify the soul.

**The Primordial Lineage of Tapas:** Looking at these ancient sources, the root term **√tap** consistently carries its core meaning:

a)  **Cosmic Generative Power from Source of Universe:** The literal incubating and creative force of the universe in the Rigveda.

b)  **Essence of the Soul-Spirit *Ātman* /Brahman:** The human soul-spirit ātmā is a fractal part of the cosmic source, spiritual force in the Upanishads through which *Ātman* reveals itself.

c)  **Purifying Śramaṇa** **Fire:** The intense spiritual energy in Jainism that burns away karma seeds to liberate the soul *jīva*.

**Sampajāno** (*sam + pa + √ñā*): Present participle from *sam-* (rightly, thoroughly) + *pa-* (forth/completely) + *√ñā* (to know). It means possessing clear comprehension, and full discernment. While mindfulness (*sati*) registers the presence of mental and physical phenomena, *sampajañña* evaluates their nature, purpose, and suitability without delusion. It provides the cognitive clarity needed to see phenomena as they are: impermanent, conditional, and bound by cause and effect.

**Patissato** (*pa + tissato / patissata*): Derived from *pati-* (towards/established) + *sata* (past participle of *sarati*, to remember/recollect; related to *sati*). It means firmly established in mindfulness, recollected, or attentively present.

*Patissato* ensures that the mind remains continuously anchored in the awareness, grounded in the observation of physical and mental phenomena.

When these four factors act simultaneously, they create the inner environment needed to purify the mind (*citta*) and advance through *Sīla*, *Samadhi*, and *Paññā*.

#### 1.60. Sīvali

**Te me ijjhiṁsu saṅkappā, yadattho pāvisiṁ kuṭiṁ;\
Vijjāvimuttiṁ paccesaṁ, mānānusayamujjahan ti.**

*Those (te) aspirations (saṅkappā) of me (me) were fulfilled (ijjhiṁsu), for which (yad) purpose (attho) I entered (pāvisiṁ) my kuṭi; clear insight and liberation (vijjā-vimuttiṁ) I will attain (paccesaṁ), having completely uprooted (ujjahaṁ) the latent tendency to conceit (māna-anusayaṁ).*

**Commentary:** **Conceit (*māna*)** is considered one of the final and most stubborn obstacles to ultimate liberation. To understand how ancient traditions understood conceit (*māna*) and its direct opposition to ultimate liberation, we must look at how foundational texts in the Vedic/Upanishadic, Jain, and early Pali canons define it through specific verses and passages.

Across these traditions, *māna* is not treated merely as a social vice; it is identified as a fundamental metaphysical wall---the cognitive energy that constructs individual boundary, reinforces karmic weight, and obscures ultimate truth.

**(1). Vedic & Upanishadic Tradition: *Māna* and *Ahaṁkāra* as Obscuration**

In the Upanishads and later Vedāntic texts, *māna* (often functioning alongside *ahaṁkāra*---\"I-making\"---and *abhi-māna*---egoic claim) is the force that binds the soul-spirit *ātmā* to a worldly egoic identity.

To understand the relationship between **Ahaṁ** and **Ātman**, we must look at how the Vedic/Upanishadic systems (Advaita Vedānta) differentiates between the **egoic (\"I\")** and the **ultimate Reality/Self**.

- **Ahaṁ:** The first-person singular nominative pronoun in Sanskrit, meaning literally **\"I.\"** It is the psychological or linguistic reference point of self-awareness (\"I speak,\" \"I act,\" \"I know\").

- **Ātman:** Derived from roots meaning \"to breathe\" (*√an*), \"to move\" (*√at*), or \"to pervade\" (*√āp*). It refers to the **innate Self, true essence, or pure witness Consciousness** that underlies all individualized experience.

In classical Vedānta, the relationship between *Ahaṁ* and *Ātman* is understood through the distinction between the **Ego/Empirical Self** and the **Ultimate/ transcendental Self**:

- **Ahaṁ-kāra (The \"I-Maker\"):** When the pure multidimensional *Ātman* interacts with the physical world, in order to survive/live in this dimension, it produces *Ahaṁ-kāra* (the ego-sense). This creates the localized identity, the worldly identity: \"I am this body,\" \"I am this personality,\" \"I am happy/sad.\"

- **Ahaṁ-Spandana / Ahaṁ-Vṛtti:** The thought \"I\" (*Ahaṁ*) is the owner of worldly experience in the mind. All worldly thoughts (\"this,\" \"that,\" \"world\") depend on the primary reference point of \"I.\"

- **Direct Identity in Divine Identity, the Brahman Source of Humanity (Mahāvākya):** In the Upanishadic declaration **\"Ahaṁ Brahmāsmi\"** (*Brhadāraṇyaka Upanishad* 1.4.10 --- *\"I am Brahman\"*), *Ahaṁ* no longer refers to the lower physical dimensional identity. When ignorance (*avidyā*) is removed, the term *Ahaṁ* shifts its referent (*lakṣyārtha*) from the worldly identity ego (*Ahaṁ-kāra*) to the divine identity *Ātman/Brahman* itself which leads to liberation out of the world and universe.

**The Vedāntic Distinction:**

- **Empirical Worldly Identity *Ahaṁ*:** \"I am a person born in a specific time and place\" (Mind/Body identity).

- **Absolute *Ātman*:** ***Ahaṁ*** is the manifest of ***Ātman.*** ***Ātman*** is the embodied (*Sākṣin*) of ***Ahaṁ*** which includes all mental states, including the thought \"I.\"

**(2). Sāṁkhya & Yoga: The Reflected Light**

In the dualistic Sāṁkhya framework:

- **Ātman (Puruṣa):** Pure Consciousness.

- **Ahaṁkāra:** A material evolute (*prakṛti*) arising from the intellect (*buddhi*).

- **The Relationship:** *Puruṣa* (Ātman) illuminates *Prakṛti*. The intellect catches this light, and *Ahaṁkāra* falsely claims, \"I am the actor, I am the enjoyer.\" Liberation (*kaivalya*) occurs when *Puruṣa* disidentifies from *Ahaṁkāra*.

**(3). Jain Tradition: *Māna* as a Heavy Karmic Density**

In Jain philosophy, *māna* is categorized as one of the four cardinal *Kāṣāyas* (passions/pollutants) that cause the defilements (*āsrava*) and binding (*bandha*) of karmic seeds (*pudgala*) onto the pure soul (*jīva*). Jainism takes a remarkably literal, ontological view: conceit hardens the soul, making it impossible to shed karmic weight.

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.6 (Ācārya Umāsvāti):** In listing the internal purification required to stop and burn away karma (*nirjarā*), the text emphasizes *Mārdava* (humility/softness), which is the direct antidote to *Māna* (conceit): **uttama kṣamā mārdava ārjava sauca satya samyama tapas tyāga ākiñcanya brahmacaryāṇi dharmaḥ \|**

*Supreme (uttama-) forgiveness (kṣamā-), humility/gentleness (mārdava-), straightforwardness/honesty (ārjava-), purity/cleanliness (sauca-), truthfulness (satya-), self-restraint (saṁyama-), spiritual primordial force (tapas-), renunciation/giving up (tyāga-), non-attachment/poverty of possession (ākiñcanya-), and celibacy/chaste living (brahmacaryāṇi) is the dharma / law/ righteousness (dharmaḥ).*

This verse from Ācārya Umāsvāti's *Tattvārtha Sūtra* (Chapter 9, Sūtra 6) outlines the **Dasa-Lakṣaṇa Dharma**---the ten supreme virtues of Jainism. These ten qualities represent the precise internal spiritual conditions required to stop the defilements of karma (*saṁvara*) and burn away previously accumulated karmic seeds (*nirjarā*).

**Mārdava (Humility) as the Antidote to Māna:** Among these ten, *mārdava* (derived from *mṛdu*, meaning soft or pliable) is specifically listed to counter the hardening effect of *māna* (conceit/pride). While conceit stiffens the mind, creating a rigid identity out of worldly achievements or status, *mārdava* softens the consciousness, allowing it to disidentify from egoic structures and open up to ultimate purification of the innate true self, the soul-spirit *ātmā*.

**Tapas, Ākiñcanya, and Brahmacariya:** The inclusion of *tapas* (spiritual primordial force) works directly alongside *ākiñcanya* (having no possessions or sense of \"mine\") and *Brahmacariya* (pure, holy conduct). Together, they dismantle the psychological and physical attachments to worldly conditions.

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 9.54:** This canonical Jain text illustrates how *māna* destroys the spiritual merit accumulated through spiritual primordial force practices (*tapas*), directly blocking omniscience knowledge (*Kevala Jñāna*): **māṇeṇa hanii viṇayaṁ, koha-māṇa-mayāiyaṁ \|** **māṇaṁ chaḍḍittāṇa, viṇayaṁ payuñjae \|\|**

*By conceit (māṇeṇa) **viṇaya** is destroyed (hanii), \[which brings about\] anger (koha-), conceit (-māṇa-), illusion (-mayā-), and the rest (-iyaṁ); conceit (māṇaṁ) having completely thrown away (chaḍḍittāṇa), **viṇaya** one should practice (payuñjae).*

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 31.10:** (as well as related verses in Chapter 31, *Caraṇavihāro* / The Conduct of Righteous Living), which enumerates the eight places of pride (*aṭṭha mada-ṭṭhāṇāiṁ*) and warns that as long as conceit persists, the soul remains rigid and barred from liberation: ***Jāī-mayaṁ kula-mayaṁ, bala-mayaṁ rūpa-mayaṁ tahā \|* *Tava-mayaṁ suya-mayaṁ ca, lābha-mayaṁ assiriya-mayaṁ \|\|***

*Pride of birth (jāī-mayaṁ), pride of family status (kula-mayaṁ), pride of physical strength (bala-mayaṁ), pride of beauty/body (rūpa-mayaṁ); also (tahā), pride of spiritual primordial force/ psychic power (tava-mayaṁ), and pride of scriptural learning / intellectual power (suya-mayaṁ ca), and pride of material gains (lābha-mayaṁ), and pride of lordship, authority, social power (assiriya-mayaṁ).*

**(4). Pāḷi Canon: *Māna* as the Latent Fetter (*Mānānusaya*)**

In the Pāḷi Canon, *māna* is understood in its most refined psychological sense.

**Sutta Nipāta 4.14 (Māgaṇḍiya Sutta, v. 842):** The Buddha points out how *māna* keeps the mind trapped in conceptual debate, preventing true stillness: **\"Samaṁ\" ti rayo na upeti, \"Visamaṁ\" \"Seyyo\" ti vāpi na cappeti;** **Anupattiko so kassa vadeyya, \"Sacco\" ti vā \"Musā\" ti vā haye.**

*\[he\] does not undergo (na upeti) agitation/movement (rayo) \[towards thinking\] \"equal\" (\"Samaṁ\" ti), \"unequal\" (\"Visamaṁ\"), nor does \[he\] construct/conceive (na cappeti) \[ideas such as\] \"superior\" (\"Seyyo\" ti vāpi); without any clinging/underlying worldly attachment (anupattiko), to whom (kassa) would he (so) speak (vadeyya), saying \"True\" (\"Sacco\" ti vā) or \"False\" (\"Musā\" ti vā) indeed (haye)?*

One who is free from *worldly attachment (anupattiko)* will not have the concept of comparison, where conceit is born from, he does not harbour the worldly concept of \'equal,\' \'unequal,\' or \'superior.\' He would not even argue with anyone, saying \'this is true\' or \'that is false.\' This verse highlights the complete dismantling of the worldly based comparative ego-structure (*māna*) and dogmatic entanglements (*diṭṭhi*). When a person clings to opinions or mental formations, they enter into conflicts over what is \"True\" (*sacca*) and what is \"False\" (*musā*). The sage who has abandoned all clinging (*anupattiko*) has no subjective stance to defend vanity, and would only pursue liberation from the world.

**Samyutta Nikāya 22.89 (Khemaka Sutta):** In this crucial sutta, the Ven. Khemaka explains to his fellow bhikkhus how, even after lower fetters are abandoned, a subtle residual trace of conceit remains like the scent of soap on a washed cloth: **\"Arahanto na honsi\... api ca me āvuso, pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu \'asmi\' ti avigataṁ, \'ayamahamasmī\' ti na ca samanupassāmi\...\"** *\"Friends, I do not say I am an Arahant\... yet regarding the five aggregates of clinging, the subtle lingering sense \'I am\' (asmi-māna) has not yet fully disappeared, although I do not view any specific aggregate as \'This I am\'.\"*

**The Illusion of worldly egoic identity similar to the vedic "Aham", \"I Am\" (*Mānānusaya*):** Even when coarse attachments are abandoned through initial insights, the subtle worldly identity of \"I am\" (*asmi-māna*) remains embedded within mental phenomena.

**Obstacle to Clear Insight (*Vijjā*):** Clear insight requires seeing all worldly conditioned physical and mental phenomena not our true self, soul-spirit ***ātmā***. *Māna* re-establishes a sense of worldly identity and pwnership over worldly things including dogma and vanity. Thus any subtle movement of *māna* keeps the mind bound to the world.

**Obstacle to Liberation (*Vimutti*):** Full liberation from the world requires the complete end of worldly attachment. *Māna* acts as the implicit anchor on some subtle worldly identity / thing holding the mind back from total transcendence.

#### 1.61. Vappa

**Passati passo passantaṁ, apassantañca passati;\
Apassanto apassantaṁ, passantañca na passatī ti.**

*Sees (passati) the seer (passo) \[the worldly ego self\] seeing (passantaṁ), and (ca) he sees (passati) the one who does not see (apassantaṁ) \[the soul-spirit ātmā/attā\]; The one not seeing (apassanto) \[the soul-spirit ātmā/attā\] \[does not see\] the one who does not see (apassantaṁ), and (ca) he does not (na) see (passati) the one who sees (passantaṁ) \[the worldly ego self\].*

**Commentary**: **Ātmā/ Attā** and **its Liberation from Worldly Yoke**

- **Ātmā (Pāḷi: Attā):** The true self, the multidimensional soul-spirit entity that transcends physical body and mental formations.

- **Passati / Passo / Passantaṁ:** Verbs and participial forms from the root *√pas* (to see, perceive, behold). *Passo* is the worldly ego self; *passantaṁ* is the active, kinetic seeing of worldly phenomena.

- **Apassanto / Apassantaṁ:** The negative present active participle (*a-* + *passanta*). It designates the non-seeing state---the quiet, detached, non-perceiving nature of the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* relative to physical phenomena.

Transformation of consciousness when the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* is realized:

- **The Worldly Stage (*Passo* Seeing *Apassantaṁ*):** Initially, the worldly ego self (*passo*) is active, generating seeing and sensory processing (*passantaṁ*). Through contemplation on divine knowledge passed down from ancestral Brahma consciousness, this ego self (*passo*) perceives its own activity and beholds the silent presence of the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* (*apassantaṁ*), which remains non-seeing and detached from physical phenomena.

- **The Ultimate Shift (Focus Solely on the True Self):** Once the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* (*apassanto*) is realized as the true self, the shift is absolute. The non-seeing soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* does not gaze at the non-seeing state (*apassantaṁ*), nor does it look back at the false self---the worldly ego self (*passantaṁ*). Having realized its true nature, its vision remains exclusively anchored on the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā*. It turns away entirely and permanently from the false self and the world altogether, never looking back at the world with its egoic identity.

**The Path of Renunciation, Purification, and Liberation**

**Realizing the True Self & True Renunciation (*Nibbidā*):** The primary goal is to realize that the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* is the true self. The worldly ego self---along with all physical bodies, social statuses, and mental constructs---belongs strictly to this world (*loka*). They are conditioned by universal laws including the law of power and the law of decay and dissolution (*dukkha*). The true meaning of renunciation from the world is to completely discard and renounce the worldly egoic identity/self. Recognizing that the ego self belongs to the world leads directly to disenchantment (*nibbidā*) of the true self from the whole world.

**Purifying the Soul-Spirit *Ātmā* / *Attā* (*Virāga*):** Following disenchantment (*nibbidā*), the practitioner purifies the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā*, which is dispassion (*virāga*) or fading off worldly passions. Through *virāga*, the deep-seated defilements---the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---are cleansed from the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā*.

**Transcendence into the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*:** Once the *ātmā*/*attā* is purified and focused solely on its true nature, all worldly craving and clinging cease, permanently ending involuntary worldly reincarnation. Practicing *Brahmacariya* through the ancient path to Nibbāna (*Sīla*, *Samadhi*, and *Paññā*), the practitioner reaches the final exit threshold: **The Pure Abodes Brahma heavens for transcendence.** Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant, the purified *ātmā*/*attā* enters the deathless realm of *Nibbāna* (*Nibbāna-Dhātu*).

#### 1.62. Vajjiputta (1st)

**Ekakā mayaṁ araññe viharāma, Apaviddhaṁva vanasmiṁ dārukaṁ;\
Tassa me bahukā pihayanti, Nerayikā viya saggagāminan ti.**

*Alone (ekakā) we (mayaṁ) dwell (viharāma) in the forest (araññe), like (va) a log of wood (dārukaṁ) cast away (apaviddhaṁ) in the forest (vanasmiṁ); many (bahukā) are envious (pihayanti) of that (tassa) me (me), like (viya) those bound for hell (nerayikā) \[are envious of\] those going to heaven (saggagāminan).*

**Commentary**: **Arañña-vihāra**

This verse illustrates the absolute necessity of physical and mental renunciation to pull the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā* out of the collective consciousness for this ancient Individual liberation path:

1.  **The Image of the Discarded Log (*Apaviddhaṁ Dārukaṁ*):**

To the worldling immersed in social status, family ties, and collective identity, a bhikkhu sitting alone in the dense forest looks like a piece of dead wood thrown away in the bush (*apaviddhaṁ dārukaṁ*). The ego self of the worldling sees no value in physical seclusion because it thrives on social recognition, comparative status, and group belonging.

2.  **The Envy of the Worldlings (*Nerayikā Viya Saggagāminan*):**

Yet, those trapped in the fires of worldly anxiety, social competition, and sensory stimulation (*nerayikā*) secretly envy the profound peace of the solitary practitioner. Just as beings burning in hell look up with yearning toward those enjoying heavenly realms (*saggagāminan*), worldly people bound to collective noise look with longing at the utter serenity of the one who has renounced society.

**3). The Earliest Vedic Tradition: The *Keśin Sūkta* of the Rigveda**

The practice of physical dwelling in the forest (*araññavihāra* or *araññe*) as a necessary foundation for mental detachment and individual liberation is an ancient tradition across the Indian subcontinent. Long before Gautama Buddha walking the ancient path, earlier Vedic *Muni* tradition and the early *Samaṇa* ascetics including Jains all emphasized stepping completely outside societal structures to detach from the collective conscious containment and to purify the worldly attachment and defilements of the soul-spirit *ātmā*/*attā*.

The 10th Mandala of Rigveda contains the **Keśin Sūkta** (RV 10.136), dedicated to the *Muni*---the silent, long-haired forest ascetic. This text documents an early, independent stream of individual ascetics who lived outside agrarian village society.

The word *muni* comes from the root *man* (to think, reflect in silence). The *Muni* lives in wilderness environments (*araṇya*), abandoning social duties to transcend physical limits. This demonstrates that the path of individual forest seclusion existed long before the Upanishadic era.

**Rigveda 10.136.2:** *munayo vātaraśanāḥ piśaṅgā vasate malā \| vātasyānu dhrājiṁ yanti yaddevāso avikṣata \|\|*

**The *Munis* (munayo), wind-girded (vātaraśanāḥ), wear (vasate) soil-colored (piśaṅgā) dirt-stained garments (malā); following (anu) the swift course (dhrājiṁ) of the wind (vātasya) they go (yanti) where (yad) the gods (devāso) have entered before (avikṣata).**

This verse provides the ancient prototype for the physical and spiritual discipline of true renunciation: Whether naked (*vātaraśanāḥ*) or dressed in soil-stained garments (*piśaṅgā malā*) (exactly the same as Gautama Buddha's Bhikkhu renunciates), the *munis* discarded all village attire, social status, and collective identities. The true meaning of renunciation is to completely discard and renounce the worldly egoic identity/self, leading directly to disenchantment (*nibbidā*).

By following the course of the wind into forest solitude (*araññe*), away from societal triggers, the practitioner purifies the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*, which is dispassion (*virāga*). Through *virāga*, the underlying defilements---the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---are cleansed from the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*.

By walking where the gods/ devas (*devāso*) have entered before, the yogi practitioner seeks immortality / deathless, or complete liberation from the death in the world.

**Rigveda 10.136.4:** *antarikṣeṇa patati viśvā rūpāvacākaśat \| muniirdevasya devasya sakhtāyitā sukṛtyāya \|\|*

**Through the mid-air / middle realm (antarikṣeṇa) he flies / travels (patati) looking down upon (avacākaśat) all (viśvā) rūpā / fine form manifest (rūpā); the *muni* (muniir) becomes a friend / ally (sakhtāyitā) of every (devasya) single god/ deva (devasya), for holy action / transcendent realization (sukṛtyāya).**

This verse details the feats of the *muni* who has risen above the sex realm to reach *rūpa* realm: By flying through the mid-air / middle realm (*antarikṣeṇa*), the *muni* looks down upon all forms (*viśvā rūpā*) and make friends with all the gods/ devas, for the purpose of Transcendent Realization (Sukṛtyāya).

**4). The Early Jain (*Samaṇa*) Tradition: The Ācārāṅga Sūtra**

The Jain tradition represents the oldest *Samaṇa* lineage paths emphasizing absolute physical separation from society to untangle the soul (*jīva* / *ātmā*) from karmic seeds. The **Ācārāṅga Sūtra** (the oldest text of the Śvetāmbara canon, composed in Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit) lays out strict instructions for solitary forest dwelling.

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.8.1.3** (Prakrit): *Ego ahamsi ṇao ṇao, me ṇo vi vijjati kaci \| Egamāṇeṇa saṁvigne, araṇṇamhi viyārae \|\|*

**Alone (Ego) I am (ahamsi), known \[to myself\] (ṇao), discrete / wise (ṇao); to me (me) not (ṇo) even (vi) exists / belongs (vijjati) anyone / anything (kaci); with a solitary mind / self-awareness (Egamāṇeṇa), stirred by spiritual urgency (saṁvigne); in the wilderness / forest (araṇṇamhi) let him abide (viyārae).**

This ancient Jain Prakrit verse from the *Śramaṇa* canon establishes the essential foundation of true renunciation and individual liberation: The declaration *\"Ego ahamsi \... me ṇo vi vijjati kaci\"* (\"I am alone; nothing belongs to me, nor do I belong to anyone.\") lays bare the truth of existence. All physical bodies, social relationships, family ties, and worldly identities belong strictly to this world (*loka*). The true meaning of renunciation is to completely discard and renounce this worldly egoic identity. Recognizing that all worldly attachments are external and *dukkha* leads directly to spiritual urgency (*saṁvega*) and disenchantment (*nibbidā*) from the world. Driven by *saṁvega*, the practitioner enters forest seclusion (*araṇṇamhi*). Isolated from societal triggers, the practitioner purifies the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*, which is dispassion (*virāga*). Through *virāga*, the underlying defilements---the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---are cleansed from the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*.

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.8.3.2** (Prakrit): *Sūṇāgārehiṁ araṇṇehiṁ, vasahiṁ samayehiṁ va \| Niyattā-taṇho vivitto, jhāṇa-seṇiṁ aḍhirohae \|\|*

**In deserted houses / empty dwellings (Sūṇāgārehiṁ) or in forests (araṇṇehiṁ), in abodes / lodgings (vasahiṁ) or at meeting places (samayehiṁ va), with worldly craving ceased / restrained (Niyattā-taṇho), solitary / detached (vivitto), let him ascend (aḍhirohae) on the Jhāṇa meditation (jhāṇa-seṇiṁ).**

The phrase *Ego ahamsi* (\"I am alone\") underscores the fundamental premise of individual liberation: societal ties, social identities, and collective physical forms belong strictly to the world (*loka*). This verse sets out the essential *jhāna* meditation through spatial and internal seclusion:

Whether abiding in deserted houses (*sūṇāgārehiṁ*) or deep forests (*araṇṇehiṁ*), or in in normal house / lodgings during rain season (vasahiṁ) or at community center / meeting places (samayehiṁ va) during wandering, physical solitude provides the foundation to withdraw from external worldly distractions. Seeing the whole of the physical world as external allows the practitioner to completely discard and renounce the worldly egoic identity.

True solitude (*vivitto*) is realized when external worldly craving is thoroughly restrained or extinguished (*niyattā-taṇho*). By severing attachment to worldly phenomena, the practitioner purifies the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*, which is dispassion (*virāga*). Through *virāga*, the deep-seated defilements---the *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---are progressively cleansed from the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*. This creates conditions for ascending the stages of meditative absorption (*jhāṇa-seṇiṁ*). The yogi practitioner fulfills the ancient path through *Brahmacariya* by perfecting *Sīla*, *Samadhi*, and *Paññā*.

**5). Muni** and the **Samaṇa**

Both the **Muni** and the **Samaṇa** (Sanskrit: *Śramaṇa*) represent ancient lineages of individual seekers who withdrew from settled society into the wilderness (*arañña*) to master mind, body, and breath through *samādhi*.

While both operate within the broad framework of ancient *yoga* discipline (*yoga*, in its primordial sense of yoking or unifying the mind), they stem from distinct streams of ancient Indian antiquity and possess different etymological, structural, and methodological emphases.

Here is the breakdown incorporating the foundational verses from ancient Sanskrit and Pāḷi literature that explicitly characterize both the **Muni** and **Samaṇa** traditions.

**Verses of the Muni Tradition:** The *Muni* stream is anchored in the oldest layers of Vedic literature and early Upanishadic texts, highlighting absolute silence (*mauna*), wild hair (*keśīn*), wind-clad wanderings, and deep inward absorption.

**Rigveda 10.136.1--3 (*Keśin Sūkta*):** *keśyaghniṃ keśī viṣaṃ keśī bibharti rodasī \| keśī viśvaṃ svardṛśe keśīdaṃ jyotirucyate \|\| munayo vātaraśanāḥ piśaṅghā vasate malā \| vātāsyānu dhrājiṃ yanti yaddevāso avikṣata \|\| unmaditā maunena vayaṃ vātānātasthimā vayam \| śarīredasmākaṃ yūyaṃ martāso abhi paśyatha \|\|*

The long-haired one (keśī) bears (bibharti) fire (agniṃ), water (viṣaṃ), heaven and earth (rodasī); the long-haired one (keśī) \[is\] the whole (viśvaṃ) *sunlight* to look upon (svardṛśe), the long-haired one (keśī) is called (ucyate) light (jyotir).

The munis (munayo), wind-girded (vātaraśanāḥ), or wear (vasate) soil-colored (piśaṅghā) dirt-stained garments (malā); following (anu) the swift course (dhrājiṃ) of the wind (vātāsya) they go (yanti), where (yad) the gods (devāso) have entered before (avikṣata).

Exalted / enraptured (unmaditā) by deep inner silence (maunena), we (vayaṃ) have mounted (ātasthimā vayam) upon the winds (vātān); you (yūyaṃ) mortals (martāso) look upon (abhi paśyatha) our (asmākaṃ) bodies (śarīrā) only (it).

**Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upanishad 3.5.1:** *tasṁād brāhmaṇaḥ pāṇḍityaṁ nirvidya bālyena tiṣṭhāset \| bālyaṁ ca pāṇḍityaṁ ca nirvidyātha muniḥ \|\|*

Having declared (udīrayitvā) a true **samaṇa** as one with peaceful senses (santindriyaṃ), the **muni** does not (na) hesitate (cocchide), nor (na) does he act (samācaran) in such a \[worldly\] manner (tathā); being innerly tranquil (santantaro), and (ca) in the midst of worldly attachments / enticements (rañjanesu) he does not (na) form thoughts / mental proliferation (Ceti), him (taṃ) indeed (ve) they call (upante) both a **muni** and a **samaṇa**.

This verse synthesizes the *Samaṇa* and *Muni* streams into a unified archetype: **Stillness of the Senses & Discarding Worldly Action (*Nibbidā*):** The **muni** and **samaṇa** stills the sensory faculties (*santindriyaṃ*) and refuses to act in worldly ways (*na tathā samācaran*). Realizing that sensory engagements are purely external worldly phenomena leads directly to the complete discarding of the worldly ego self and brings about disenchantment (*nibbidā*). **Inner Tranquility & End of Mental Proliferation (*Virāga*):** Abiding as one who is innerly tranquil (*santantaro*), the **muni** and **samaṇa** remains entirely unagitated in the presence of worldly allurements (*rañjanesu*), without forming directed attention to worldly objects (*vitakkā*) or mental proliferation (*na Ceti*). By severing all engagement with worldly triggers, the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā* is purified through dispassion (*virāga*). Through *virāga*, the deep-seated defilements---the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---are thoroughly cleansed.

**Verses in the Pali Canon:** The *Samaṇa* stream preserved in early Pāḷi Canon, emphasizes active spiritual labor (*śram*), rigorous self-restraint to induce the primordial power of *tapasā*, to eradicate *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

**Dhammapada Verse 184:** *khantī paramaṃ tapo titikkhā, nibbāṇaṃ paramaṃ vadanti buddhā \| na hi pabbajito parūpaghātī, samaṇo hoti paraṃ viheṭhayanto \|\|*

*Patient endurance (khantī) \[and\] forbearance (titikkhā) \[are\] the highest (paramaṃ) practice to induce the primordial force (tapo), \"Nibbāna (nibbāṇaṃ) \[is\] supreme (paramaṃ),\" say (vadanti) the buddhā; whoever harms (parūpaghātī) another (para-), he is not (na hi) a renunciate (pabbajito), whoever oppresses / afflicts (viheṭhayanto) another (paraṃ) he is not (na) a samaṇa.*

This verse establishes the essential inner character of a *renunciate (pabbajito) and samaṇa*: **Patient Endurance (khantī) and Forbearance (titikkhā) as Highest Forms of Practice to Induce the Primordial Force (*Nibbidā*):** The practice of *khantī* (patient endurance) and titikkhā (forbearance) acts as the ultimate *tapo* (practice to induce primordial force), burning away internal agitation when encountering external worldly conflicts. **Harmlessness and Inner Purification (*Virāga*):** A true *samaṇa* or *pabbajito* abandons all physical and mental violence (*parūpaghātī*, *viheṭhayanto*). By severing harm and hatred toward external beings, the practitioner purifies the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*, entering the state of dispassion (*virāga*). Through *virāga*, the deep-seated defilements---the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---are thoroughly cleansed from the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā*.

**Dhammapada Verses 264--265** *na muṇḍakena samaṇo abbato alikaṃ bhaṇaṃ \| icchālobhasamāpanno samaṇo kiṃ bhavissati \|\| yo ca sameti pāpāni aṇuṃ thūlāni sabbaso \| samitattā hi pāpānaṃ \'samaṇo\'ti pavuccati \|\|*

*Not (na) by a shaved head (muṇḍakena) \[does one become\] a samaṇa \[if\] lacking discipline (abbato) \[and\] speaking (bhaṇaṃ) untruth / falsehood (alikaṃ); filled with (samāpanno) desire / craving (icchā-) and greed (lobha-), how (kiṃ) will he become (bhavissati) a samaṇa?*

*But (ca) whoever (yo) stills / quiets (sameti) evil / harmful deeds (pāpāni), small / subtle (aṇuṃ) \[or\] large / gross (thūlāni) entirely (sabbaso); because of the stilling (samitattā) indeed (hi) of evil deeds (pāpāni), he is called (pavuccati) a samaṇa.*

**Sutta Nipāta 1.3 (*Khaggavisāṇa Sutta*, Verse 55):** *sīho va saddesu asantasanto, vāto va jālesu asajjamāno \| paumaṃ va toyena alippamāno, eko care khaggavisāṇakappo \|\|*

*Like a lion (sīho va), unstartled / terrifyingly fearless (asantasanto) amid noises / sounds (saddesu), like the wind (vāto va), uncaught / unentangled (asajjamāno) in nets (jālesu), like a lotus (paumaṃ va), unsmeared / unstained (alippamāno) by water / mud (toyena), let one live (care) alone (eko), like the horn of a rhinoceros (khaggavisāṇakappo).*

**Verses in the Jain Samaṇa (Śramaṇa) Lineage**

In Jain canonical literature, **Muni** (silent, self-controlled sage) and **Samaṇa** (ascetic who has pacified the mind and practice dispassion) are central designations for one who has abandoned worldly ties, householder life, and internal defilements. In early Jain texts, these terms reflect strict inward containment, complete non-violence (*ahiṃsā*), and the total cessation of karmic influx.

***Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (Chapter 25, Verses 31--32):** *Na vi muṇḍieṇa samaṇo, na oṃkāreṇa baṃhaṇo \|* *Na muṇī raṇṇavāseṇaṃ, kusacīreṇa na tavassī \|\|* *Samayāe samaṇo hoi, baṃhacereṇa baṃhaṇo \|* *Ṇāṇeṇa ya muṇī hoi, taveṇa ya tavassī \|\|*

*Defining the true Samaṇa and true Brāhmaṇa by inner restraint rather than external birth or robes:* Not (Na) even (vi) by a shaved head (muṇḍieṇa) \[does one become\] a samaṇa, not (na) by \[reciting\] the Om (oṃkāreṇa) a brāhmaṇa; not (Na) a muṇī by forest residence (raṇṇavāseṇaṃ), by clothes of kusa grass (kusacīreṇa) not (na) an ascetic practitioner (tavassī).

By equanimity / tranquility (Samayāe) a samaṇa he becomes (hoi), by holy chaste life / spiritual purity (baṃhacereṇa) a brāhmaṇa; and (ya) by direct knowledge / wisdom (Ṇāṇeṇa) a muṇī he becomes (hoi), and (ya) by primordial force / inner spiritual power (taveṇa) an ascetic practitioner (tavassī).

**(1). Samayāe (***samatā***)**

- **Prakrit Form:** Instrumental singular feminine of *samayā* (Pāḷi: *samatā*; Sanskrit: *samatā*).

- **Etymological Roots:** Derived from Sanskrit **sama** (*adj.* \"even, equal, balanced, tranquil\") + feminine abstract nominal suffix **-tā** (which drops the intervocalic *-t-* in Prakrit, yielding *-ā* / *-āe* in the instrumental).

- **Semantic Analysis:** Denotes \"by equanimity,\" \"by impartiality,\" or \"by complete mental stillness.\" It signifies the internal equilibrium that defines a true *Samaṇa*---unshaken by pleasure or pain, gain or loss.

- **Parallel Verse (Dhammapada 142):** *alaṅkato ce pi samaṃ careyya, santo danto niyato brahmacārī \| sabbesu bhūtesu nidhāya daṇḍaṃ, so brāhmaṇo so samaṇo so bhikkhu \|\|*

> *"Even if (ce pi) wearing fine clothes (alaṅkato), if he should live (careyya) in tranquility / equanimity (samaṃ), peaceful (santo), self-controlled / restrained (danto), disciplined (niyato), walking in the holy chaste life (brahmacārī); having laid down (nidhāya) violence (daṇḍaṃ) toward all (sabbesu) living beings (bhūtesu), he (so) \[is\] a Brāhmaṇa, he (so) \[is\] a samaṇa, he (so) \[is\] a Bhikkhu."*

**(2). Baṃhacereṇa (***brahmacariya***)**

- **Prakrit Form:** Instrumental singular neuter of *baṃhacera* (Pāḷi: *brahmacariya*; Sanskrit: *brahmacarya*).

- **Etymological Roots:** Compound of two distinct Sanskrit roots: **Brahma-** (from root ***√bṛh***, \" holy, supreme, or pure\"). **Carya** (from root ***√car***, \"to live, move, practice, conduct oneself\").

- **Semantic Analysis:** Literally \"by living in the supreme/holy life of Brahma standard.\" It denotes the holy, chaste life of total sensory restraint, mental purity, and complete detachment from worldly sensory indulgence, which aligns with the Divine source of the cosmic Mankind race.

- **Parallel Verse (Sutta Nipāta 2.6 / Dhammapada 267):** yo \'dha puññañca pāpañca bāhetvā brahmacariyavā \| saṅkhāya loke carati sa ve \"bhikkhu\"ti vuccati \|\|

> Whoever (yo) here in this world (\'dha) having warding off / transcended (bāhetvā) merit (puññañca) and (ca) evil / demerit (pāpañca), living the holy chaste life (brahmacariyavā); with discernment (saṅkhāya) lives in the world (loke carati), he (sa) indeed (ve) is called (vuccati) \"Bhikkhu\".

**(3). Ṇāṇeṇa (***ñāṇa***)**

- **Prakrit Form:** Instrumental singular neuter of *ṇāṇa* (Pāḷi: *ñāṇa*; Sanskrit: *jñāna*).

- **Etymological Roots:** Derived from the PIE root \****ǵneh₃-*** / Sanskrit root ***√jñā*** (\"to know, perceive, discern\") + the nominal instrument/action suffix **-ana** (with the characteristic Prakrit retroflexion of *n* to *ṇ*).

- **Semantic Analysis:** \"By direct knowledge\" or \"by intuitive wisdom.\" Unlike *pāṇḍitya* (conceptual scholarship or intellectual learning), *ṇāṇeṇa* represents the silent, direct seeing of things as they truly are. It is the defining attribute of the *Muni*.

- **Parallel Verse (Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.5.5):** *se ṇāṇī se muṇī* \"He who possesses direct knowledge \[ṇāṇī\], he is muṇī.\"

**(4). Taveṇa (***tapas***)**

- **Prakrit:** Instrumental singular neuter of *tava* (Pāḷi: *tapo*/*tapa*; Sanskrit: *tapas*).

- **Etymological Roots:** Derived from the Sanskrit root ***√tap*** (\"to burn, emit heat, radiate energy, shine\").

- **Semantic Analysis:** \"By primordial force / cosmic generative power\" or \"by spiritual energy.\" It refers to the internal primordial or spiritual force that burns away deep-seated karmic influxes, sensual desires, and defilements. This force or power is derived directly from the soul-spirit ātmā, which is a fractal part of the source consciousness of the universe.

- **Parallel Verse (Dhammapada 184):** *khantī paramaṃ tapo titikkhā* (\"Patient endurance is the highest primordial force \[tapo\].\")

***Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (Chapter 16, Verse 1 / Chapter 19):** *Māṇī na labbhaī ṇāṇaṃ, kohī ṇāṇaṃ na viṃdaï \|* *Niggaṃtho hu suī hoi, muṇī moṇeṇa labbhaï*

One who is proud / conceited (māṇī) does not (na) acquire / attain (labbhaī) direct wisdom / knowledge (ṇāṇaṃ), one who is angry / wrathful (kohī) does not (na) gain (viṃdaï) direct wisdom (ṇāṇaṃ); the unattached one (niggaṃtho) indeed (hu) becomes (hoi) pure (suī), a *muṇī* is attained / realized (labbhaï) by inner reflection (moṇeṇa).

**Moṇeṇa (***mōna* / *moneyya***)**

- **Prakrit Form:** Instrumental singular neuter of *moṇa* (Pāḷi: *mōna* / *moneyya*; Sanskrit: *mauna* / *mauneya*).

- **Etymological Roots:** Derived from the Sanskrit root ***√man*** (\"to think, perceive, reflect, mind, ponder\") -\> primary derivative **muni** (\"silent sage, one who perceives directly\") -\> secondary abstract nominal derivative \****mauna*** / **moṇa** (formed via *vṛddhi* vowel lengthening of the root vowel *a* -\> *au* / *o*, plus the abstract suffix *-ya* / *-a*).

- **Grammatical Suffix:** The instrumental case ending **-eṇa** (Prakrit retroflexion of Skt. *-ena*) signifies the means by which an action is fulfilled: \"by/through interior silence\" or \"by/through inner reflection.\"

The term **moṇeṇa** does not mean muteness or refraining from vocal speech (*vāc*). Etymologically rooted in direct mental perception (*√man*), it denotes a profound state of inner clarity, inner reflection based on total sensory containment, and the absolute quietude of mental proliferation (*papañca*).

**A. *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* 1.3.2 (Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit):** *moṇeṇa suddhassa niggaṃthassa thiyappano \| ṇovammamaṇiyaṃ vijjā, jo imiṃ laddhu\'muṭṭhio \|\|*

Through inner reflection (moṇeṇa), one made pure (suddhassa), unattached (niggaṃthassa), firmly established in the true self / soul-spirit attā / appa / *Ātman* (thiyappano); known as unparalleled (ṇovammamaṇiyaṃ) clear insight (vijjā), who (jo) having attained (laddhu) this (imuṃ) remains actively striving / steadfast (\'muṭṭhio / samuṭṭhio).

**B. *Sutta Nipāta* 3.11 (*Nālaka Sūtra*, Verse 701):** *moneyyaṃ te upaññissaṃ, muni hutvā nadiṃ viya \| saṇantam ehi gacchantaṃ, tuṇhīm eti mahodadhi \|\|*

I shall declare to / teach (upaññissaṃ) you (te) inner reflection / wisdom (moneyyaṃ); having become (hutvā) a *muni* is like (viya) a river / watercourse (nadiṃ); \[the small stream\] goes along (gacchantaṃ) making noise / loud sounds (saṇantam), come! \[look!\] (ehi); \[while\] the great ocean / sea (mahodadhi) goes / flows (eti) silently / in deep stillness (tuṇhīm).

***Ācārāṅga Sūtra* (Book 1, Lecture 2, Lesson 3):** *Savve pāṇā savve bhūyā savve jīvā savve sattā na haṃtavvā\...* *Esa dhamme dhuve ṇicce taiyāhiṃ paveie, muṇīṇā sīibhūeṇaṃ.*

All (savve) living creatures (pāṇā), all (savve) entities (bhūyā), all (savve) living souls (jīvā), all (savve) sentient beings (sattā) \[should\] not (na) be slain / harmed (haṃtavvā); this (esa) \[is the\] eternal (dhuve) \[and\] unchangeable (ṇicce) law / Dhamma (dhamme) declared (paveie) by the *Protectors / Saviors / **Tāyi*** (taiyāhiṃ), by the muṇī who has become completely cooled / stilled / sītī (sīi-bhūeṇaṃ).

Commentary: **sīti** (and its related verbal/adjectival forms like *sītī-bhūta*, *sītibhāva*) literally translates to \"coolness\" or \"becoming cool,\" but carries deep metaphysical weight in early Pāḷi and Sanskrit literature.

**(1). Root & Etymological Progression**

- **Proto-Indo-European (PIE):** Derivatives trace back to the PIE root ***\*ḱey-*** or ***\*ḱey-d-***, meaning \"cold,\" \"cool,\" or \"to settle down/repose\" (cognate with Germanic/English *chill*, Old English *cēol*).

- **Sanskrit:**

  - Primary adjective: **śīta** (शीत) --- \"cold,\" \"cool,\" \"chilled.\"

  - Abstract/derivative forms: **śītī** (शीती) or **śītalatā** (शीफलता) --- \"coolness,\" \"absence of heat.\"

  - Verbal compounding root: **śītī-kṛ** (to cool down) or **śītī-bhū** (to become cool).

- **Pāḷi:**

  - Primary adjective: **sīta** (cool) / **sītala** (cool, refreshing).

  - Abstract form: **sīti** (cooling, tranquility) / **sītībhāva** (the state of being cooled).

**(2). Physical vs. Metaphysical Meaning**

In ancient Indian thought, heat (*santāpa*, *ḍaha*) represents the burning motion of the world (*loka*), because this world is established on top of the Fire element so when spirit manifest in this world it has to become soul by kindling, driven by the fires of **rāga**, **dosa**, and **moha**. Consequently, \"cooling\" (*sīti*) is not merely a physical sensation, but the structural cessation of these burning, worldly manifest. **Physical level:** Relief from the scorching sun or fever; refreshing water; shade. **Phenomenal level:** The calming of the burning, restless activity of worldly mind-phenomena (*mental phenomena*) as they are disengaged and left behind.

**(3). Usage in Pāḷi Literature**

In the Pāḷi suttas, **sīti** occurs most frequently in compound terms describing the complete extinction of worldly fires:

**A. Sītībhūta (sīti + bhūta = \"become cooled\"):** Used as an epithet for an Arahant or a purified mind that has quenched all worldly fever. **Formulaic phrase:** *"Nibbāto sītībhūto"* --- Unbound, become cool. It signifies that the fires of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* have burned out due to lack of fuel (craving and clinging), leaving the purified mind completely unbothered by worldly phenomena.

**B. Sītībhāva (sīti + bhāva = \"state of coolness\"):** Refers directly to the condition of absolute peace realized when worldly ties are abandoned. It describes the state prior to or upon reaching the threshold of final liberation from the world, where no heat of karma or rebirth can kindle again.

**Tathāgata vs. Tīrthaṅkara**

In Jain canonical literature, the exact conceptual equivalent to **Tathāgata**---one who is directed to go across to the \"other shore\" (*Nibbāna-Dhātu*)---is **Tīrthaṅkara** (or Prakrit: **Tittheyara**).

**(1). The Direct Functional Equivalent: Tīrthaṅkara**

**Tathāgata***:* **tathā** = *there / to that realm of Nibbāna-Dhātu*;

**gata** = *directed to go*

While *Tathāgata* stresses the movement toward and arrival at the unconditioned destination, Jainism uses **Tīrthaṅkara** to express the exact same crossing:

- Derived from **tīrtha** (\"ford,\" \"crossing-place,\" or \"bridge over the ocean of saṃsāra\") + **kara** (\"maker\" or \"builder\").

- **Meaning:** A *Tīrthaṅkara* is literally a \"Ford-Maker\"---one who has forged the crossing across the turbulent ocean of worldly reincarnation to reach the \"other shore\" (*Siddhasilā* / liberation), leaving behind the path for others to follow.

- **Correspondence:** Just as the Tathāgata travels the ancient path to *Nibbāna-Dhātu*, the Tīrthaṅkara crosses the ford of saṃsāra to the ultimate realm of liberated souls.

**(2). *Taiyāhiṃ* and *Tāyibhīḥ***

In the verse from *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* 1.2.3 (*taiyāhiṃ paveie*), scholars and traditional commentators read this Prakrit word in two distinct ways:

**A. Tāyi (Sanskrit: *Tāyin* / Instrumental plural: *Tāyibhīḥ*)**

- **Etymology:** Derived from the Sanskrit root ***√trā*** (\"to protect, preserve, save\") or ***√tāy*** (\"to expand, extend, guard\").

- **Meaning:** \"By the Protectors\" or \"By the Saviors.\" It refers to the liberated masters who protect living beings by revealing the eternal truth / Dhamma and showing the way out of the world.

- **Pāḷi Parallel:** The word *tāyi* appears in early Pāḷi suttas as an epithet for the Buddha (e.g., *Tādin* or *Tāyi*, \"the Protective/Unshakable One\").

Both Jainism and early Pali Canon, independently preserve **Tāyi** as an archaic title for a fully purified being who has overcome worldly ties (*rāga*, *dosa*, *moha*) and acts as a beacon for others to reach the ultimate threshold of liberation. There are explicit textual examples in both the Pāḷi Sutta Piṭaka and the ancient Prakrit Jain Āgamas where **Tāyi** (or **Tādin**) functions as a technical title for liberated masters, saviors, and fully purified beings.

**Pāḷi Canon (Theravāda Arahanist/ Buddhist Texts)**

In the Pāḷi Sutta Piṭaka, **Tāyi** (often occurring alongside or as a variant of **Tādin**) is a recognized epithet of Gautama the Supreme Arahant.

**a. Sutta Nipāta (Sn 1.10 --- *Āḷavaka Sutta*)**

When the yakkha Āḷavaka asks Gautama Buddha how one crosses the flood and is purified, the Buddha presents the path. Āḷavaka responds: **\"Kathaṃ su taratoghaṃ, kathaṃ su taratīṇṇavaṃ\...\"** *\"Atthaṃ hitāya lokassa, buddho uppajji **tāyi**.\"*

**Translation:** \"For the benefit and welfare of the world, the Buddha---the **Protector / Savior (Tāyi)**---has arisen.\"

Here, *tāyi* directly designates the master who protects beings by revealing the ancient path across the flood of saṃsāra.

**b. Dhammapada (Dhp 195--196)**

In the *Buddhavagga*, describing the merit of honoring those who are liberated and beyond worldly ties: \"Pūjārahe pūjayato, buddhe yadi va sāvake; Papañcasamatikkante, tiṇṇasokapariddave. Te tādise pūjayato, nibbute akutobhaye; Na sakkā puññaṃ saṅkhātuṃ, imettamapi kenaci.\"

**Translation:** \"He who pays homage to those worthy of homage---the Buddhas or their disciples---who have transcended worldly conceptualizations (*papañca*) and crossed beyond sorrow and lamentation; he who honors such **Saviors (Tādisa / Tāyi)**, who are cooled (*nibbute*) and free from all fear, his merit cannot be measured by anyone.\"

**c. Theragāthā & Udāna**

Throughout the *Theragāthā*, Arahants are repeatedly described as **tādino** or **tāyi**, and those who are completely unshakable by the eight worldly winds (*lokadhamma*), act as protectors / guardians of the truth / Dhamma.

**Ancient Jain Canonical Texts (Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit Āgamas)**

In the early layers of the Jain canon---most notably the *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* and *Sūtrakṛtāṅga Sūtra*---the Prakrit form **Tāyi** (instrumental plural: *tāyibhīḥ* / *taiyāhiṃ*) denotes the Tīrthaṅkaras and perfected Jinas.

**a. Ācārāṅga Sūtra (1.2.3 --- *Sastraparijñā*)**

In this foundational chapter regarding non-violence (*ahiṃsā*) and the realization of truth, the text states: \"Se bemi: Je ya atīyā, je ya paḍuppannā, je ya āgamissā arahantā vaḍḍhamāṇā, sabbe te eyaṃ kahaysu, eyaṃ pakahesu, eyaṃ pakahessanti\...\" **\"Taiyāhiṃ paveie eyaṃ\...\"**

**Translation:** \"I declare this: All the *Arahants* of the past, present, and future\... all of them have declared this, do declare this, and will declare this\... This was expounded by the **Saviors / Protectors (Taiyāhiṃ / Tāyibhīḥ)**.\"

Traditional Jain commentators (such as Śīlāṅka) gloss *taiyāhiṃ* directly as Sanskrit *tāyibhiḥ* (*\"by the Tāyins / Protectors\"*), defining a *Tāyin* as one who protects all living beings by teaching the path of escape from karmic bondage.

**b. Sūtrakṛtāṅga Sūtra (1.1.4 --- *Samaya*)**

Describing the noble character of the true Jina who has crossed the ocean of existence: **\"Tāyi samikkhaṁ dhammaṁ, anuttaraṁ thāṇaṁ saṁpattā\...\"**

**Translation:** \"The **Saviors (Tāyi)**, having realized the ultimate Dhamma through direct discernment, have attained the unsurpassed realm (*anuttaraṃ ṭhānaṃ*).\"

Here, *tāyi* refers explicitly to the liberated masters who discern the true nature of reality and transcend the physical universe.

**B. Tathāgata in Prakrit (*Tathāyaya* -\> *Taiya*)**

- In Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit, intervocalic consonants frequently elide (th -\> h or drops, g -\> y or drops). Thus, Sanskrit **Tathāgata** regularizes into Prakrit as **Tathāyaya**, **Tathāga**, or shortened into **Taiya** / **Tāi**.

- In this reading, *taiyāhiṃ* may be simply the Prakrit instrumental plural of **Tathāgata**: *\"Declared by the Tathāgatas.\"*

**C. Other Corresponding Epithets in Jainism**

Early Jain texts use three primary terms interchangeably with *Tīrthaṅkara*:

- **Pāragā** (Skt: *Pāragata*): **\"Gone to the Other Shore.\"** This matches *tatha-gata* almost identically. It signifies one who has completely crossed over the boundary of the world (*loka*) to the unconditioned deathless other shore.

- **Siddha**: **\"The Accomplished / Perfected One.\"** Refers to the soul (*jīva* / *attā*) that has completely cast off all karmic matter, exited the universe of decay and dissolution, and resides eternally in the realm of ultimate liberation.

- **Arhat** (Pali: Arahant, Prakrit: *Arihanta*): One who has eradicated all internal enemies (*rāga*, *dosa*, *moha*) and is bound for immediate exit from worldly rebirth.

**4). Semantic Comparison: Sīti vs. Nibbāna**

While **Nibbāna** (Sanskrit: *Nirvāṇa*) emphasizes the *unbound, release, liberation*, **Sīti** emphasizes the *cooling* and *stillness* remaining once the fire of worldly manifest is gone.

***Samaṇa Suttaṃ* (Compilation of Jain Agamas, Verse 135):** *Samido savvapāvehiṃ, samo savvesu bhūesu \|* *So samaṇo tti vuccai, saṃkilesavivajjio \|\|*

\"He who has quieted and eliminated all sinful influxes, who maintains complete equality toward all living beings, and who is entirely free from mental defilements (*saṃkilesa*)---he is called a **Samaṇa**.\"

**Muni vs. Samaṇa**

**Muni (The Seer of Inward Absorption):**

- **Etymology & Essence:** Derived from the root *man* (to think, reflect) and closely tied to wisdom, seer, and *mauna* (noble silence). The *Muni* is essentially the introspective seer.

- **Origin & Appearance:** Found in the oldest layers of the Vedic text (such as Rigveda 10.136, the *Keśin Sūkta*). They were solitary forest dwellers, known for long, unkempt hair (*keśīn*) and simple, earth-colored garments or nudity (*vātaraśanāḥ*).

- **Core Characteristics:** The *Muni* relies heavily on deep, inward reflection, transcendent states, and solitary absorption away from all human discourse and Vedic ritualism.

**Samaṇa / Śramaṇa (The Striver of Systematic Discipline):**

- **Etymology & Essence:** Derived from the root *śram* (to labor, exert, exert effort). The *Samaṇa* is literally \"one who strives\" or \"one who labors spiritually.\"

- **Origin & Context:** Prominently featured in the Non-Vedic *Śramaṇic* lineages (which include Sakyaputtiya/Early Buddhist, Jain/Nigaṇṭha, and Ājīvika streams).

- **Core Characteristics:** The *Samaṇa* emphasizes intentional, rigorous spiritual exertion, restraint, systematic mental training, and active doctrinal investigation into cause and effect (*kamma*) to burn away defilements.

**The Shared Goal: Primordial *Yoga* & *Samādhi***

Despite these distinct historical origins, both the *Muni* and the *Samaṇa* were ultimately practicing the ancient path of inward discipline:

a)  **Seclusion from the World (*Arañña*):** Both lineages recognized that village life (*grāma*) and collective social structures nourish the false egoic identity. Both withdrew into the wilderness to isolate the soul-spirit *ātmā/attā* from collective consciousness.

b)  **Mastery of Concentration (*Samādhi*):** Both used physical stillness, breath control, and sensory withdrawal to enter deep states of absorbed concentration (*samādhi*). For both, *samādhi* was not an end in itself, but the essential mental stability required to cut off worldly attachments.

c)  **Renunciation of Worldly Identity:** Whether designated as a *Muni* abiding in *mauna* or a *Samaṇa* exerting effort in *tapas*, both rejected social status, family lineage, and material ownership. Over time, these two streams deeply intermingled: Gautama the Buddha is frequently addressed in early Pāḷi texts both as *Samaṇa Gautama* and as *Sākyamuni* (the *muni* of the Śākya clan).

**Convergence of both Streams**

In early Pāḷi literature, both lineages synthesize in the figure of the fully liberated practitioner. A master is praised for uniting both the active exertion of the *Samaṇa* and the internal absorption of the *Muni*.

**Sutta Nipāta 1.12 (*Muni Sutta*, Verse 221):** *\"Having stilled the senses, proclaimed as a true **samaṇa**, the **muni** does not falter nor act with pride. Innerly tranquil, untouched by worldly attachment---him indeed they call both a **muni** and a **samaṇa**.\"*

The **Muni** is defined primarily through **Paññā** (wisdom, intuitive discernment, and clear insight, *mauna*/*ñāṇa*), whereas the **Samaṇa** is defined primarily through **Samadhi** (stilling, pacification of defilements, and rigorous ascetic exertion, *śram*/*sameti*).

- **Muni --- The Primacy of Wisdom (*Paññā* / *Ñāṇa*):**

The word *muni* is etymologically rooted in *man* (\"to think, ponder, perceive\") and *mauna* (\"profound interior silence\"). The *muni* is the silent observer who sees through the illusion of the world (*loka*). Rather than engaged in mere analytical thinking, the *muni* possesses direct intuitive vision (*ñāṇa*). This direct seeing triggers immediate disenchantment (*nibbidā*) from the world.

- **Samaṇa --- The Primacy of Stilling (*Samadhi* / *Tapas*):**

The word *samaṇa* (Sanskrit *śramaṇa*) carries a dual root resonance: *śram* (\"to exert oneself, perform ascetic effort\") and *sameti* / *śam* (\"to quiet, still, or pacify\"). The *samaṇa* represents the yogic engine of practice---the intense application of concentration (*Samadhi*), restraint (*Sīla*), and the burning away of karmic influx or defilements through primordial force (*tapas*).

**(1). *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* 25.31-32 (Jain Canon)**

This ancient Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit verse explicitly divides the spiritual duties, assigning **knowledge/wisdom to the Muni** and **stilling/ tranquility to the Samaṇa**: *Tāiṇo u pakuvvanti, na ya kopaṃ vaśaṃ ṇaya. \| Taṃsi bhikkhū na kuppijjā, jai icche saddhiṃ-āgami. 31 \|\| Samayāe samaṇo hoi, bambhacereṇa bambhaṇo \| Ñāṇeṇa ya muṇī hoi, taveṇa hoi tāvaso \|\| 32 \|\|*

The Saviors (*tāiṇo* / Pāḷi: *tāyino*) do not display anger, nor do they fall into the power (*vaśaṃ*) of wrath (*kopaṃ* / Pāḷi: *kopa*). On this, a bhikkhu (*bhikkhū* / Pāḷi: *bhikkhu*) should not become angry (*kuppijjā* / Pāḷi: *kuppeyya*), if he desires (*icche* / Pāḷi: *iccheyya*) to arrive at the destination (*āgami* / Pāḷi: *āgamituṃ*) together (*saddhiṃ* / Pāḷi: *saddhiṃ*) with them.

By equanimity/ tranquility (*samayāe* / Pāḷi: *samatāya*) one becomes a *samaṇa*, and by celibacy / *brahmacariya* one becomes a *brāhmaṇa*. By direct knowledge / *ñāṇa* one becomes a *muṇī*, and by inner spiritual power / *tapas,* one becomes an ascetic practitioner /*tāpasa*.

Here, *samatā* refers to the mental stillness of *Samadhi*, while *ñāṇa* refers to the penetrating insight of *Paññā*.

**(2). *Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad* 3.5.1**
