In the Upanishadic framework, intellectual learning (*pāṇḍityam*) and childlike simplicity (*bālyam*) are both transcended through disenchantment (*nirvidya*), yielding the state of the *Muni*: *Atha hainaṁ kaholaḥ kauṣītakeyaḥ papraccha; yājñavalkyeti hovāca, yadeva sākṣādaparokṣādbrahma, ya ātmā sarvāntaraḥ, taṃ me vyācakṣveti; eṣa ta ātmā sarvāntaraḥ; katamo yājñavalkya sarvāntaraḥ? Yo\'śanāyāpipāse śokaṃ mohaṃ jarāṃ mṛtyumatyeti. Etaṃ vai tamĀtmanaṃ viditvā brāhmaṇāḥ putraiṣaṇāyāśca vittaiṣaṇāyāśca lokaiṣaṇāyāśca vyutthāyātha bhikṣācaryaṃ caranti; tasmādbrāhmaṇaḥ pāṇḍityaṃ nirvidya bālyena tiṣṭhāset; bālyaṃ ca pāṇḍityaṃ ca nirvidyātha muniḥ; amānyaṃ ca maunaṃ ca nirvidyātha brāhmaṇaḥ; sa brāhmaṇaḥ kena syāt? Yena syāttenīdṛśa eva, ato\'nyadārtam. Tato ha kaholaḥ kauṣītakeya upararāma.*

*Then (atha / Pāḷi: atha) Kahola Kauṣītakeya questioned (papraccha / Pāḷi: pucchi) him; \"O Yājñavalkya,\" he said (hovāca / Pāḷi: avoca), \"That which is directly (sākṣāt / Pāḷi: sakkhī) and immediately (aparokṣāt) the brahma, the soul-spirit ātmā / attā, that is inner to all (sarvāntaraḥ / Pāḷi: sabbantara), explain (vyācakṣva / Pāḷi: vyākarohi) that to me.\"*

*\"This (eṣa / Pāḷi: eso) is your soul-spirit (ātmā / attā), which is inner to all.\"*

*\"Which one, O Yājñavalkya, is inner to all?\"*

*\"That which transcends / goes beyond / surpasses (atyeti / Pāḷi: aticcavati) hunger (aśanāyā), thirst (pipāsā), grief (śoka / Pāḷi: soka), delusion (moha / Pāḷi: moha), decay/old age (jarā / Pāḷi: jarā), and death (mṛtyu / Pāḷi: maccu). Having directly known (viditvā / Pāḷi: viditvā) this very soul-spirit (Ātmanaṃ / Pāḷi: attānaṃ), true brāhmaṇā.*

*Having completely risen above (vyutthāya / Pāḷi: vuṭṭhahitvā) the desire for offspring (putraiṣaṇā / Pāḷi: puttesanā), the desire for wealth (vittaiṣaṇā / Pāḷi: vittesanā), and the desire for worldly realms (lokaiṣaṇā / Pāḷi: lokesanā), then (atha / Pāḷi: atha) live (caranti / Pāḷi: caranti) the life of a bhikkhu / Alms / bhikkhā (bhikṣācaryaṃ / Pāḷi: bhikkhācariyaṃ).*

*Therefore (tasmāt / Pāḷi: tasmā), let a brāhmaṇa, having become thoroughly disenchanted with / having relinquished (nirvidya / Pāḷi: nibbinditvā) scholarly erudition/ book-learning (pāṇḍityaṃ / Pāḷi: paṇḍiccaṃ), should desire to remain (tiṣṭhāset / Pāḷi: tiṭṭheyya) in child-like innocence/ simplicity /guilelessness (bālyena / Pāḷi: bālyena);*

*having become thoroughly disenchanted with / having relinquished (nirvidya / Pāḷi: nibbinditvā) both child-like innocence / simplicity and scholarly erudition, then (atha / Pāḷi: atha) one becomes a muni; having become thoroughly disenchanted with (nirvidya / Pāḷi: nibbinditvā) pride / non-conceptualization / non-reflectiveness (amānyaṃ) and being muni/ muna (maunaṃ / Pāḷi: muna), then (atha / Pāḷi: atha) one is brāhmaṇa.*

*How does that brāhmaṇaḥ live/act? By whatever means he lives, he is indeed such an elevated one. Everything other than (ataḥ anyat) this true transcendent soul-spirit (ārtam / Pāḷi: ātta ) is subject to dukkha (distress, suffering and death). Thereupon Kahola Kauṣītakeya became silent /* *ceased questioning (upararāma / Pāḷi: uparami).\"*

While the *Muni* represents the seer, silent wisdom (*Paññā*) and the *Samaṇa* represents the active, stilling yogic exertion (*Samadhi*), **all true Munis are Yogis, and all fully accomplished Samaṇas become Munis, and they become Bhikkhus and Brahmana.**

**Arañña vs. Āśrama**

In ancient Indian and Śramaṇa literature, **Arañña** and **Āśrama** represent fundamentally different spaces, social structures, and spiritual orientations. While both exist outside the dense center of village life, their functions in early ascetic traditions diverge significantly.

**Primary Meaning**

**Arañña:** Uncultivated wilderness, forest, or jungle beyond human habitation.

**Āśrama:** A settled hermitage, institution, or stage of life (*āśrama-dharma*).

**Social Structure**

**Arañña:** Total absence of social organization, domesticity, or domestic ritual.

**Āśrama:** Communal living under a guru/teacher, often with students, family, or domestic fires.

**Spiritual Function**

**Arañña:** Direct solitude for solitary bhikkhus and munis seeking total liberation (*Nibbāna*).

**Āśrama:** A settled sanctuary for study, Vedic/ascetic rituals, or communal discipline.

**Relation to Civilization**

**Arañña:** The absolute boundary of the uncultivated.

**Āśrama:** An extension of human settlement into nature; a managed enclave.

**Arañña**

**Etymology & Scope:** Derived from the root meaning wild, foreign, or distant from human dwelling. In Pāḷi, *Arañña* denotes the deep forest or uncultivated wilderness beyond the perimeter of villages (*gāma*) and towns (*nigama*).

**Ascetic Context:** In the Śramaṇa traditions---especially early Buddhism and Jainism---*Arañña* is the primary physical setting for intense, solitary meditation (*Dhyāna*/*Jhāna*).

**The Solitary Ideal:** In canonical texts, dwelling in the wilderness (*āraññika*) is praised because it removes the practitioner from the social entanglements, sensual temptations, and distractions of domestic life. It is the realm of the solitary *muni* who has cut ties with society to realize the cessation of craving (*kāma-chanda*) and reach the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

**Character:** Unmanaged, untamed, and indifferent to human needs.

**Āśrama**

**Etymology & Scope:** Derived from the Sanskrit root śram (\"to exert oneself,\" \"to labor,\" or \"to perform asceticism\"). An *Āśrama* is a place of spiritual exertion or a structured hermitage.

**Social Context:** Historically tied to the Brahmanical tradition, an *Āśrama* often functioned as a settled community or gurukula. It frequently included a head teacher (guru or ṛṣi), disciples, livestock, and sacrificial fires (*agnyādhāna*).

**Institutional Role:** Unlike the solitary wilderness, an *āśrama* is an established institution where scriptures are recited, rituals are performed, and lifestyle guidelines are maintained. Later, the term also came to denote the four stages of Brahmanical life (*brahmacarya*, *gṛhastha*, *vānaprastha*, *saṁnyāsa*).

**Character:** Semi-cultivated, structured, and communal.

While an **Āśrama** brings a protected, organized sanctuary *into* nature where tradition and communal spiritual labor are sustained, **Arañña** represents the raw, unconditioned wilderness where the individual practitioner steps entirely outside of human institutions, turning inward to achieve complete liberation.

Early Śramaṇa also held the balance between physical seclusion (*arañña*) and practical shelter (*vihāra* / *āśrama*-like dwellings), especially during seasonal shifts like the rainy season retreat (*vassa*). However, the realization of *samādhi* and liberation (*Nibbāna*) is fundamentally tied to **the degree of mental and physical seclusion (*viveka*)** that an environment affords.

**(1). The Real Purpose of *Arañña***

The deep wilderness (*arañña*) is repeatedly praised in *Theragāthā* ---not because living outdoors is inherently sacred, but because of what the wilderness **eliminates**:

- **Absence of Social Distraction:** The wilderness cuts off the sensory triggers of town life (*gāma-saṁsagga*)---social chatter, domestic duties, visual enticements, and praise or blame.

- **Forced Self-Reliance:** Facing the wild (wild beasts, cold, solitude) shatters complacency, inducing spiritual urgency (*saṃvega*) and turning the mind inward.

- **Natural Quietude:** The physical stillness of the forest naturally aids the calming of mental formations, allowing the practitioner to enter the *jhānas* without external interference.

For the early *munis*, *arañña* was the ideal laboratory for burning away subtle attachments.

**(2). The *Vassa* Lodges and *Vihāras*: Early \"Āśrama-like\" Dwellings**

During the three-month rain retreat (*vassa*), the Buddha permitted and encouraged *bhikkhus* to reside in sheltered dwellings---ranging from simple huts (*kuṭī*) and leaf-huts (*paṇṇasālā*) to donated monastic parks (*ārāma* or *vihāra* like Veluvana and Jetavana).

These rain dwellings served functions very similar to an *āśrama*: They provided physical safety, protection from severe weather, and basic survival needs. They were located **\"neither too far from the village nor too close\"** (*nāccāsannaṁ nātidūre*)---close enough for daily alms-round (*piṇḍapāta*), but distant enough to preserve quietude.

The canonical standard was not suicidal exposure to harsh elements, but maintaining an environment conducive to spiritual labor (*brahmacariya*).

**(3). Impact on Realization: *Arañña* vs. *Āśrama***

From the perspective of the *Theragāthā* elders, the physical structure (whether a cave, a tree root, a modern Canadian home, or an *āśrama*-style lodge) is secondary to the **mental environment** it fosters.

**Wilderness (*Arañña*)**

- **Primary Danger:** Physical hardship, danger from elements/wildlife.

- **Primary Benefit:** Instant physical seclusion (*kāyaviveka*); rapid cutting of sensory distraction.

- **Impact on *Samādhi*:** Direct and sharp; minimal external noise to disturb deep absorption (*jhāna*).

- **Impact on *Nibbāna*:** Facilitates deep disenchantment (*nibbidā*) with worldly phenomena (*loka*).

**Settled Lodge / Hermitage (*Āśrama* / *Vihāra*)**

- **Primary Danger:** Social entanglement (*saṁsagga*), complacency, and institutional attachment.

- **Primary Benefit:** Physical stability, protection from extreme climate, structured environment for practice.

- **Impact on *Samādhi*:** Achievable if strict discipline and silence are maintained; vulnerable to communal chatter.

- **Impact on *Nibbāna*:** Fully possible, provided the lodge does not become a new \"home\" or source of attachment.

**(4). The Core Principle: Internal Seclusion (*Citta-Viveka*)**

The *Theragāthā* makes a clear distinction between three types of seclusion: **Kāya-viveka:** Physical seclusion (living away from crowds). **Citta-viveka:** Mental seclusion (a mind free from the five hindrances and *rāga, dosa, moha*). **Upadhi-viveka:** Complete seclusion from all substrates of existence (realization of *Nibbāna*).

Living in a modern building code-compliant dwelling or an *āśrama*-style hermitage does **not** block *samādhi* or *Nibbāna*, provided the practitioner guards the senses and avoids recreating domestic entanglements inside those walls. If bhikkhu's mind (*citta*) is free from craving (*kāmacchanda*) and social noise, that very lodge functions as his *arañña*, opening directly onto the path of ultimate sanctuary (*Yogakkhema*) of the deathless.

#### 1.63. Pakkha

**Cutā patanti patitā, giddhā ca punarāgatā;\
Kataṁ kiccaṁ rataṁ rammaṁ, sukhena anvāgataṁ sukhan ti.**

*Passed away / departed from a state of existence (cutā), those fallen ones (patitā) fall down (patanti); greedy / attached / obsessed with worldly sensuality (giddhā), and come back again (ca punarāgatā); Done ought to be done (kataṁ kiccaṁ), delighted in the delightful (rataṁ rammaṁ), through joy/ bliss (sukhena), joy / bliss has been attained (anvāgataṁ sukhaṁ).*

**Commentary**: Inner Joy (*sukha*) is Part of the Path

1.  **The Worldly Trap (*Saṃsāra*):**

The first line describes the plight of beings who remain attached to the world (*loka*). Being greedily attached (*giddhā*) to worldly conditioned phenomena---which are **not our true self / soul-spirit, nor part of our true self/ soul-spirit, nor belonging to our true self/ soul-spirit** ---they continually fall back into realms of suffering and dissolution (*dukkha*). Because they fail to recognize that all worldly phenomena are subject to the universal law of decay and death, they remain trapped in involuntary reincarnation in this world.

2.  **The Completion of the Task (*Katakiiccatā*):**

The second line expresses the triumphant realization of an Arahant. Through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the practitioner fulfills the ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna via *Sīla*, *Samadhi*, and *Paññā*. The task is done (*kataṁ kiccaṁ*).

3.  **Reaching Bliss Through Bliss (*Sukhena anvāgataṁ sukhaṁ*):**

Unlike external worldly happiness that creates bondage to the world, that leads to mere suffering and involuntary reincarnation, inner joy and bliss is independent (*anissito*) from the world that creates no worldly bondage. The individual liberation path leverages the sublime bliss in *Sīla*, *Samadhi*, and *Paññā* ---specifically reaching **The Pure Abodes Brahma heavens with Brahmavihara,** transcends all worldly suffering and realize the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

#### 1.64. Vimalakoṇḍañña

**Dumavhayāya uppanno, jāto paṇḍaraketunā;\
Ketuhā ketunāyeva, mahāketuṁ padhaṁsayī ti.**

*Arisen (uppanno) from her who is named after a tree (dumavhayāya), born (jāto) with a white banner / noble lineage, with purity and royalty (paṇḍara-ketunā); The destroyer of banner / pride / conceit (māna) (ketuhā), by very banner / noble lineage \[of Gautama the Supreme Arahant\] (ketunāyeva), has utterly destroyed (padhaṁsayi) the great banner (of pride/ conceit / māna) (mahāketuṁ).*

**Commentary**: ***ketu: Banner of Nobility, Conceit or Dhamma***

In ancient Magadha and North Indian kingdoms, physical banners (*ketu* or *dhaja*) were carried by ruling monarchs, military commanders, and noble houses to symbolize authority and royal descent. **White (*Paṇḍara*) as Purity and Royalty:** White was traditionally the royal color denoting purity, sovereignty, and high status (similar to the royal white parasol, *setacchattā*). A family or lineage described as carrying a \"white banner\" (*paṇḍaraketu*) belonged to a high-caste, elite, or noble household (*kula*), distinguishing them from common or dishonorable origins.

**Double Meaning (Literary Wordplay on *Ketu* (Lineage Banner vs. Mental Conceit):** The verse intentionally sets up a contrast between two types of \"banners\" (*ketu*):

**The Worldly White Banner (*Paṇḍaraketu*):** The physical birth, noble titles, and social status of noble heritage.

**The Banner of Conceit (*Mahāketu*):** The underlying mental tendency (*mānānusaya*) to harbor mental delusion of conceit (*māna*) based on birth, bloodline, or worldly gains.

**The Noble Dhamma Children of Gautama Lineage (Gotra) of the Supreme Arahant:** The supreme \"banner\" of noble lineage from Gautama's Dhamma for liberation from the world. By establishing the true \"banner\" of Gautama's Dhamma, the Bhikkhu completely demolished the grand illusion of ego---the \"great banner\" (*mahāketuṁ*).

#### 1.65. Ukkhepakatavaccha

**Ukkhepakatavacchassa, Saṅkalitaṁ bahūhi vassehi;\
Taṁ bhāsati gahaṭṭhānaṁ, Sunisinno uḷārapāmojjo ti.**

*That which was accumulated (saṅkalitaṁ) over many years (bahūhi vassehi) by Bhante Ukkhepakatavaccha (Ukkhepakatavacchassa); Seated comfortably (sunisinno) with sublime spiritual joy (uḷāra-pāmojjo), he speaks / expounds (bhāsati) that (taṁ) to householders (gahaṭṭhānaṁ).*

**Commentary**: ***Pāmojja* (or *Pāmujja*):** \"delight,\" \"joy,\" \"gladness,\" or \"spiritual rapture.\"

***Prāmodya* in the Ancient Vedic Tradition**

In classical Vedic Sanskrit and early post-Vedic literature (such as the Epics and Classical Sanskrit treatises), the root \$\\sqrt{mud}\$ and its derivatives (*prāmodya*, *pramoda*) are used to describe intense joy, celebration, and spiritual satisfaction.

1.  **Vedic Verbal Forms (mud):**

In the **Rigveda** and **Atharvaveda**, the verb *modate* and the noun *muda* refer to delight associated with alignment with Branma, source of cosmic Mankind, divine order (*Ṛta*).

2.  **Upanishadic Contexts (*Pramoda* / *Prāmodya*):**

In the **Taittirīya Upaniṣad** (2.5), within the description of the *Ānandamaya Koṣa* (the sheath composed of bliss), emotional states of joy are classified into hierarchical stages:

- **Priya** (fondness / basic pleasure)

- **Moda** (delight upon obtaining a desired object)

- **Pramoda** (extreme joy / supreme delight)

- **Ānanda** (transcendental bliss)

Here, *pramoda* / *prāmodya* represents a high-level mental elevation that prepares the mind for deep inward transcendental bliss.

***Prāmodya* / *Pāmojja* in the Jain Tradition**

In Jainism---which shares the Middle Indo-Aryan linguistic heritage of Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit---the term appears both phonetically as **Pāmoccha** / **Pāmojja** and in Sanskritized Jain texts as **Prāmodya**.

a.  **Prāmodya as a Fundamental Virtue (*Bhāvanā*):**

In the **Tattvārtha Sūtra** (Chapter 7, Sutra 11) by Ācārya Umāsvāti, *prāmodya* is prescribed as one of the four essential mental attitudes (*bhāvanās* or *brahmavihāras*) to purify the soul: *Maitrī* (Benevolence toward all living beings); **Prāmodya** (Delight / appreciation for the Brahma values / virtues); *Kāruṇya* (Compassion for suffering beings); *Mādhyasthya* (Transcendental equanimity toward the refractory or intractable).

In Jain practice, cultivating *prāmodya* specifically means experiencing pure joy upon witnessing Brahmic virtue (*guṇa*) in others, counteracting jealousy (*īrṣyā*) and pride (*māna*).

b.  **Ardhamāgadhi Canon:**

In the Jain Āgamas (such as the *Sūyagaḍaṃga* and *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra*), spiritual gladness (*pāmojja*) arises when a samana successfully casting off worldly attachments and householder ties (*gahaṭṭha*), realizing freedom.

Spiritual joy (*pāmojja* / *prāmodya*) functions as an essential transitional catalyst along the contemplative path. It emerges during the initial stages of moral purification and mental calm, but it is progressively transcended and abandoned as the mind ascends into deeper, peaceful state.

**Presence of Joy (*Pāmojja* Present):**

**Pre-Jhāna / Moral Purification:** Arises when contemplating one\'s own purified virtue (*sīla*) or when abandoning the five mental hindrances (*nīvaraṇāni*).

**First Jhāna Preparation:** Serves as the immediate proximate cause (*upanisā*) for rapture/ happiness (*pīti*) and bodily tranquility (*passaddhi*).

**Absence of Joy (*Pāmojja* Transcended): Third Jhāna:** Physical rapture (*pīti*) and mental excitement subside into serene happiness (*sukha*) and transcendental equanimity (*upekhā*). **Fourth Jhāna:** All mental pleasure, pain, joy, and grief are entirely abandoned, leaving pure, purified peace rooted in equanimity (*upekhā-sati-pārisuddhi*).

***Dīgha Nikāya* 2 (Sāmaññaphala Sutta):** Tassime pañca nīvaraṇe pahīne attāni samanupassato pāmojjaṁ jāyati, pamuditassa pīti jāyati, pītimanassa kāyo passambhati, passaddhakāyo sukhaṁ vedeti, sukhino cittaṁ samādhiyati.

In him (*tassa*) seeing (*samanupassato*) these (*ime*) five (*pañca*) hindrances (*nīvaraṇe*) abandoned (*pahīne*) in himself (*attāni*), joy (*pāmojjaṁ*) is born (*jāyati*); in one who is joyful (*pamuditassa*), rapture (*pīti*) is born (*jāyati*); in one with a rapturous mind (*pītimanassa*), the physical body (*kāyo*) becomes tranquil (*passambhati*); one whose physical body is tranquil (*passaddhakāyo*) experiences (*vedeti*) happiness (*sukhaṁ*); the mind (*cittaṁ*) of one who is happy (*sukhino*) becomes concentrated (*samādhiyati*).

This standard formula describes the emergence of *pāmojja* upon overcoming the five hindrances, leading directly into concentration (Samadhi).

***Samyutta Nikāya* 45.8 (Vibhaṅga Sutta):** Sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṁ atthaṅgamā; adukkhamasukhaṁ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṁ catutthaṁ jhānaṁ upasampajja viharati.

With the abandoning (*pahānā*) of pleasure (*sukhassa*) and (*ca*) the abandoning (*pahānā*) of pain (*dukkhassa*), with the previous (*pubbe*) disappearance (*atthan-gamā*) of mental joy (*somanassa*) and mental grief (*domanassānaṁ*), he enters (*upasampajja*) and dwells (*viharati*) in the fourth (*catutthaṁ*) jhāna, which is neither-painful-nor-pleasant (*adukkham-asukhaṁ*), purified in mindfulness through transcendental equanimity (*upekkhā-sati-pārisuddhiṁ*).

This formula describes the transcendence of all coarse joy, mental excitement, and pleasure in the Fourth Jhāna.

**Ancient Vedic Tradition: *Taittirīya Upaniṣad* 2.1-6:** *Brahmavid āpnoti param. Tad eṣābhyuktā: satyaṁ jñānam anantaṁ brahma, yo veda nihitaṁ guhāyāṁ parame vyoman, so\'śnute sarvān kāmān saha brahmanā vipaściteti. Tasmād vā etasmād Ātmana ākāśaḥ sambhūtaḥ, ākāśād vāyuḥ, vāyor agniḥ, agneḥ āpaḥ, adbhyaḥ pṛthivī, pṛthivyā oṣadhayaḥ, oṣadhībhyo\'nnam, annāt puruṣaḥ. Sa vā eṣa puruṣo\'nnarasamayaḥ. Tasyedam eva śiraḥ, ayaṁ dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ, ayam uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ, ayam ātmā, idaṁ pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā. Tadapyeṣa śloko bhavati.*

The knower of Brahma (brahmavid) reaches (āpnoti) the Other Shore (param). Brahma is truth/reality (*satyaṁ*), knowledge (*jñānam*), and infinite (*anantaṁ*). Whoever (*yo*) knows (*veda*) \[Brahma\] hidden (*nihitaṁ*) in the secret cavity (*guhāyāṁ*) of the highest (*parame*) space (*vyoman*), he (*saḥ*) fulfills (*aśnute*) all (*sarvān*) desires (*kāmān*) together with (*saha*) the wise (*vipaścitā*) *brahmanā*. From that (*tasmād*) very (*vā*) self / soul-spirit (*etasmād Ātmanaḥ*), space (*ākāśaḥ*) was born (*sambhūtaḥ*); from space, air (*vāyuḥ*); from air, fire (*agniḥ*); from fire, water (*āpaḥ*); from water, earth (*pṛthivī*); from earth, plants (*oṣadhayaḥ*); from plants, food (*annam*); from food, man (*puruṣaḥ*). That (*sa*) person (*puruṣaḥ*) indeed (*vā eṣa*) consists of the essence of food (*anna-rasa-mayaḥ*).

Of that body, this (*idam*) is the head (*śiraḥ*), this is the right (*dakṣiṇaḥ*) wing/side (*pakṣaḥ*), this is the left (*uttaraḥ*) wing (*pakṣaḥ*), this is the central torso (*ātmā*), and this is the lower foundation (*pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā*).

*Annād vai prajāḥ prajāyante, yāḥ kāśca pṛthivīṁ śritāḥ. Atho annenaiva jīvanti, athainad api yantyantataḥ\... Tasmād vā etasmād annarasamayāt, anyo\'ntara ātmā prāṇamayaḥ. Tenaiṣa pūrṇaḥ. Sa vā eṣa puruṣavidha eva. Tasya puruṣavidhatām, anvayaṁ puruṣavidhaḥ. Tasya prāṇa eva śiraḥ, vyāno dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ, apāna uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ, ākāśa ātmā, pṛthivī pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā. Tadapyeṣa śloko bhavati.*

Different (*anyaḥ*) from this (*etasmāt*) self made of food essence (*anna-rasa-mayāt*), there is another inner (*antaraḥ*) self made of vital energy (*prāṇa-mayaḥ*). By that (*tena*), this outer sheath is filled (*pūrṇaḥ*). That inner self (*saḥ*) takes the human shape (*puruṣa-vidhaḥ*) of the former.

Of that vital energy self, the in-breath (*prāṇaḥ*) is the head (*śiraḥ*), the pervasive breath (*vyānaḥ*) is the right wing (*dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ*), the out-breath (*apānaḥ*) is the left wing (*uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ*), space (*ākāśa*) is the central trunk (*ātmā*), and earth (*pṛthivī*) is the base (*pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā*).

*Prāṇaṁ devā anu prāṇanti, manuṣyāḥ paśavaśca ye\... Tasmād vā etasmāt prāṇamayāt, anyo\'ntara ātmā manomayaḥ. Tenaiṣa pūrṇaḥ. Sa vā eṣa puruṣavidha eva. Tasya puruṣavidhatām, anvayaṁ puruṣavidhaḥ. Tasya yajur eva śiraḥ, ṛg dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ, sāmottaraḥ pakṣaḥ, ādeśa ātmā, atharvāṅgirasaḥ pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā. Tadapyeṣa śloko bhavati.*

Different (*anyaḥ*) from this (*etasmāt*) self made of vital energy (*prāṇa-mayāt*), there is another inner (*antaraḥ*) self made of mental phenomena (*mano-mayaḥ*). By that (*tena*), this energy sheath is filled (*pūrṇaḥ*). It takes the same human shape (*puruṣa-vidhaḥ*).

Of that mental self, the *Yajur Veda* (sacrificial formula) is the head (*śiraḥ*), the *Ṛg Veda* (verse) is the right wing (*dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ*), the *Sāma Veda* (chant) is the left wing (*sāmottaraḥ pakṣaḥ*), injunctions (*ādeśaḥ*) form the central trunk (*ātmā*), and the hymns of the Atharvans (*atharvāṅgirasaḥ*) form the base (*pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā*).

*Yato vāco nivartante, aprāpya manasā saha, Ānandaṁ brahmaṇo vidvān, na bibheti kadācaneti. Tasyaitad eva śārīram ātmā, yaḥ pūrvasya. Tasmād vā etasmān manomayāt, anyo\'ntara ātmā vijñānamayo. Tasyaitad eva śārīram ātmā, yaḥ pūrvasya. Tasmād vā etasmād vijñānamayāt, anyo\'ntara Ātmanandamayaḥ. Tasya priyam eva śiraḥ, modo dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ, pramoda uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ, ānanda ātmā.*

From where (*yato*) words (*vāco*) turn back (*nivartante*), without reaching (*aprāpya*) together with (*saha*) the mind-will/ mental phenomena (*manasā*), knowing (*vidvān*) the bliss (*ānandaṁ*) of Brahmana, one does not (*na*) fear (*bibheti*) at any time (*kadācana*). Different (*anyaḥ*) from this (*etasmāt*) mental phenomena self (*manomayāt*), there is another inner (*antaraḥ*) self made of intellect/understanding/consciousness (*vijñāna-mayaḥ*).

Of that intellect /understanding/consciousness self, faith (*śraddhā*) is the head (*śiraḥ*), divine order (*ṛtam*) is the right wing (*dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ*), truth (*satyam*) is the left wing (*uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ*), *yoga* is the central trunk (*ātmā*), and the great principle (*mahaḥ*) is the base (*pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā*).

*Vijñānaṁ yajñaṁ tanute, karmāṇi tanute\'pi ca\... Tasyaitad eva śārīram ātmā, yaḥ pūrvasya. Tasmād vā etasmād vijñānamayāt, anyo\'ntara Ātmanandamayaḥ. Tenaiṣa pūrṇaḥ. Sa vā eṣa puruṣavidha eva. Tasya puruṣavidhatām, anvayaṁ puruṣavidhaḥ. Tasya priyam eva śiraḥ, modo dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ, pramoda uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ, ānanda ātmā, brahma pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā. Tadapyeṣa śloko bhavati.*

Different (*anyaḥ*) from this (*etasmāt*) intellect/ understanding/ consciousness self (*vijñānamayāt*), there is another inner (*antaraḥ*) self made of bliss (*ānandamayaḥ*).

Of that bliss self, love/fondness (*priyam*) is the head (*śiraḥ*), joy (*modaḥ*) is the right wing (*dakṣiṇaḥ pakṣaḥ*), intense delight (*pramodaḥ*) is the left wing (*uttaraḥ pakṣaḥ*), bliss (*ānandaḥ*) is the central trunk (*ātmā*), and *brahma* is the ultimate base (*pucchaṁ pratiṣṭhā*).

\[ 2.1 Annamaya Koṣa \] \--\> Physical Body (food)

\[ 2.2 Prāṇamaya Koṣa \] \--\> Vital Energy Body

\[ 2.3 Manomaya Koṣa \] \--\> Mental Phenomena Body (Mind/Thought)

\[ 2.4 Vijñānamaya Koṣa \] \--\> Intellect / Understanding Body

\[ 2.5 Ānandamaya Koṣa \] \--\> Bliss / Joy Body (Pramoda/Moda)

*Asanneva sa bhavati, asad brahmeti veda cet. Asti brahmeti chedveda, santamenam tato vidur iti. Tasyaitad eva śārīram ātmā, yaḥ pūrvasya. Athāto\'nupraśnāḥ: utāvidvānamuṁ lokaṁ pretya kaścana gacchātī3, āho vidvān amuṁ lokaṁ pretya kaścit samaśnutā3 u. So\'kāmayata, bahu syāṁ prajāyeyeti. Sa tapo\'tapyata. Sa tapastaptvā, idaṁ sarvam asṛjata, yad idaṁ kiṁ ca. Tat sṛṣṭvā, tadevānuprāviśat. Tad anupraviśya, sac ca tyac cābhavat, niruktaṁ cāniruktaṁ ca, nilayanaṁ cānilayanaṁ ca, vijñānaṁ cāvijñānaṁ ca, satyaṁ cānṛtaṁ ca, satyam abhavat. Yad idaṁ kiṁ ca, tat satyam ity ācakṣate. Tadapyeṣa śloko bhavati.*

He becomes non-existent (*asanneva sa bhavati*) if he knows Brahma as non-existent (*asad brahmeti veda cet*). If he knows that Brahma exists (*asti brahmeti veda*), then sages know him as truly existent (*santam enam tato viduḥ*). It \[Brahma\] desired (*saḥ akāmayata*): \"Let me become many (*bahu syāṁ*), let me be born (*prajāyeyi*).\" It performed *tapas* (*sa tapo\'tapyata*)---generating primordial force. Having exerted that primordial force (*tapas taptvā*), it created (*asṛjata*) all (*idaṁ sarvam*), what exists (*yad idaṁ kiṁ ca*). Having created it, it entered into it (*tat eva anuprāviśat*).

#### 1.66. Meghiya

**Anusāsi mahāvīro, sabbadhammāna pāragū;\
Tassāhaṁ dhammaṁ sutvāna, vihāsiṁ santike sato;\
Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Mahāvīro (the great hero), who has gone to the beyond / other shore (pāragū) of all phenomena (sabbadhammāna), instructed (anusāsi); having heard (sutvāna) his (tassa) Dhamma, I (ahaṁ) dwelt (vihāsiṁ) near him (santike), mindful (sato); the three knowledges / clear insights (tisso vijjā) have been attained (anuppattā), the instruction of the Buddha (buddhassa sāsanaṁ) has been fulfilled (kataṁ).*

**Commentary**: **Mahāvīra** 大雄

The usage of the title **Mahāvīro** (\"Great Hero\") in early Pāḷi verses---and its enduring legacy in Chinese Buddhism as the **Daxiong Baodian** (大雄寶殿, \"Hall of the Great Hero\") to host Buddha statues---is clear evidence of a shared, ancient spiritual foundation that predates later sectarian divisions.

The fact that Gautama the Supreme Arahant was addressed as Mahāvīro demonstrates that early practitioners operated within a unified culture of liberation. Rather than representing distinct, hostile religions or spiritual lineages, these lineages shared a common goal: transcending the worldly realms (*loka*) and escaping the cycle of involuntary reincarnation.

The modern narrative that paints Buddhism and Jainism as fundamentally opposed systems is the product of centuries of sectarian pride, academic over-analysis, and dogmatic infighting. Over the last two millennia, both traditions underwent significant degradation. As sectarian tension grew, the original, uncompromising pursuit of liberation for renunciates was diluted, and the cohesive ancient renunciants fragmented into rigid, institutionalized \"isms\".

The term *Mahāvīro* standing prominently over the main hall of temples today serves as an undeniable reminder of this original unity. It points back to a time before sectarian dogma distorted the core message: the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* to transcend all worldly phenomena.

To overcome two thousand years of degeneration and sectarian strife, returning to this foundational source is essential. Uniting under the banner of **Arahanism** restores the primordial path to liberation from the world and entering the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*---reclaiming the uncompromised standard established by the Great Hero himself.

#### 1.67. Ekadhammasavanīya

**Kilesā jhāpitā mayhaṁ, bhavā sabbe samūhatā;\
Vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro, natthi dāni punabbhavo ti.**

*The defilements / impurities (kilesā) belong to me (mayhaṁ) are burned up / incinerated / completely consumed (jhāpitā), all (sabbe) worldly existence / manifests (bhavā) are completely uprooted (samūhatā); the continuous cycle of birth and saṁsāra / reincarnation (jāti-saṁsāro) is finished / destroyed (vikkhīṇo), now (dāni) there is no (natthi) more manifest / worldly existence/ becoming (puna-bhavo).*

**Commentary**: **kilesā (**defilements / impurities**)**

The technical framework of kilesa (or *kleśa*) belongs entirely to the ancient Samaṇa (Śramaṇa) culture rather than early Vedic orthodoxy. And the concept of t**he Incineration of Defilements (*Kilesā jhāpitā*):** The impurities of the ***citta*** are not merely suppressed; through the primordial force *tapas*, all ***Kilesā*** are completely burned up. Because these defilements are the bondage of the ***citta*** to this world, their utter destruction leaves nothing to bind the ***citta*** to the world.

**kilesa** (Sanskrit: **kleśa**; Prakrit: **kilesa**) derives from the Verbal Root: klis: to afflict, torment, stain, soil, cause anguish. *kilesa* or *kleśa* literally translates to **\"stain,\" \"dirt,\" \"pollution,\"** or **\"that which torments/afflicts.\"**

**Verbal Noun:** *Kiliṣṭa* (Sanskrit) / *Kiliṭṭha* (Pāḷi) means \"polluted, defiled, corrupted.\"

Etymologically, a *kilesa* is not a passive flaw or an abstract idea; it is a **corrupting pollution** that actively torments the *citta* (mind-heart). It sticks to the mind-heart like soot or rust on bright metal, causing the *citta* to be bound to worldly phenomena (*loka*) through *rāga* (craving and attachment) and *dosa* (fear and anger). Since the concept of **kilesā is** Samaṇa (Śramaṇa) rather than Vedic, the can connect the dots that the pollution is at ***ātmā*** level since the soul-spirit ***ātmā*** is enveloped inside the *citta* as inner essence.

**Kilesa Across Ancient Samaṇa** **Lineages**

**A. The Pāḷi Canon & Early Arahanism**

In the Pāḷi Sutta Piṭaka, *kilesa* and its close derivative *upakkilesa* refer to the corruptions of the *citta* that cloud direct knowledge (*abhibhū* / *paññā*).

**The Primary Roots (Akusala-Mūla):** The canonical core of all defilements is summarized as the three poisons:

i.  **Rāga** (Sensual Passion / Craving / Attachment / Attraction)

ii. **Dosa** (Fear / Anger / Aversion / Hatred / Malevolence)

iii. **Moha** (Ignorance / Delusion)

**The Ten Defilements (*Dasa Kilesa-Vatthūni*):** Formulated systematically in the *Dhammasaṅgaṇī* and *Vibhaṅga* (Abhidhamma) and synthesized in the *Visuddhimagga*: **Lobha** (Greed / Attachment), **Dosa** (Hatred / Anger), **Moha** (Delusion), **Māna** (Conceit / Ego-Pride), **Micchādiṭṭhi** (Wrong Views / Misinterpreting the world), **Vicikicchā** (Skeptical Doubt / Hesitation), **Thīnaṁ** (Sloth / Mental Torpor), **Uddhaccaṁ** (Restlessness / Agitation), **Ahirikaṁ** (Shamelessness before unwholesomeness), **Anottappaṁ** (Recklessness / Fearlessness of karmic consequences).

**B. Yoga & Shramanic Traditions**

In the early Shramanic and Yogic ascetic strata (later codified in Patañjali\'s *Yoga Sūtras* 2.3), the *kleśas* are enumerated as the **Five Afflictions (*Pañca-Kleśa*)** that bind the self to *saṃsāra*:

**Avidyā (**Ignorance / Primary Delusion (the root of all other four *kleśas*)**), Asmitā (**Egoism / False identification of the worldly mind/body with the true identity**), Rāga (**Passionate Attachment / Clinging to pleasurable worldly experiences**), Dveṣa (**Aversion / Resistance and hostility toward pain or unpleasant objects**), Abhiniveśa (**Clinging to physical existence / Fear of death / Involuntary survival drive**).**

**C. Jaina Āgamas**

In ancient Prakrit Jain texts, *kilesa* translates directly as the burning affliction of **Kaṣāya** (passions/soils). Jainism categorizes *kilesa* into **4 Primary Kaṣāyas** (Defilements): **Krodha** (Anger), **Māna** (Pride), **Māyā** (Deceit), and **Lobha** (Greed).

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 8.12 (Kāyavihāṇiyā / Leaf of the Tree Chapter):** *Kohaṁ māṇaṁ ca māyaṁ ca, lobhaṁ ca tivihaṁ taha;* *Jahijja kāsayaṁ savvaṁ, bhaṇiyaṁ jiṇasāsaṇe.*

\"Anger (*koha* / *krodha*), conceit / pride (*māṇa*), deceit / Illusion (*māya*), and greed (*lobha*)---one should give up all these four *kaṣāyas*, as declared in the teaching of the Jinas.\"

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 28.11:** *Kodhāpaitteṇa ya māṇadūsio, māyāe diṭṭho ya aṇeaga-roge;* *Lobheṇa griddho vi ya savva-sokko, na vindaī santi-samādhi-mūlaṁ.*

\"Afflicted by anger, corrupted by pride, struck by the many diseases of deceit, and greedy through covetousness, one never finds the root of peace and *samādhi*.\"

**Sūtagadaṁga Sūtra 1.1.4.11:** *Kodho ya māṇo ya vadhāi chaṭṭho, māyā ya lobho ya punabbhavāya;*

\"Anger, pride, deceit, and greed lead directly to *punabbhava* (renewed worldly existence / involuntary rebirth).\"

**(1). Krodha (Anger / Hostility):** corresponds directly to **Dosa** (aversion/hatred). It is the violent, repulsive energy that arises when the mind encounters an unwanted worldly object or experience. It operates through resistance, irritation, ill-will, and the urge to destroy or push away. *Krodha* creates intense agitation and fiery turbulence within the *citta*. It burns up mental peace and solidifies the illusion of an enemy, binding the mind tightly to the objects it hates through a chain of reactive karma.

**(2). Māna (Pride / Conceit):** is the defensive ego-structure that compares itself in worldly way to others and seeks to elevate itself within the worldly hierarchy (*loka*). It manifests as arrogance, superiority, vanity, or even self-pity/inferiority (which is simply inverted pride). It relies on identifying with temporary, impermanent phenomena---such as physical strength, beauty, wealth, intellect, or status. *Māna* prevents the *citta* from seeing that all worldly phenomena are not part of our true self. By building a false sense of worldly ego identity around worldly attributes, it prevents true disenchantment and detachment from the world.

**(3). Māyā (Deceit / Illusion):** is crookedness, hypocrisy, self-deception, and the deliberate distortion of truth to manipulate outcomes or maintain a false image. It operates through dishonesty, pretense, hidden motives. It is the refusal to face things as they truly are. *Māyā* clouding direct vision (*paññā*) is a primary manifestation of **Moha** (delusion). Because it relies on misrepresenting reality, it locks the mind in an intricate web of falsehoods, making it impossible for the *citta* to perceive the unconditioned truth or develop genuine mindfulness (*sati*).

**(4). Lobha (Greed / Covetousness):** corresponds directly to **Rāga** (sensual passion/attraction/clinging). It is the insatiable hunger of the mind to possess, consume, and hold onto worldly objects and experiences. It manifests as covetousness, attachment, possessiveness, and the desperate craving for continuous sensory stimulation or existence (*bhava*). *Lobha* is the ultimate \"glue\" of *saṃsāra*. It supplies continuous fuel (*upādāna*) to the cycle of rebirth. As long as *Lobha* is present, the *citta* continuously reaches out to seize new bodies and new forms of existence across the realms.

In ancient Samaṇa texts (such as the *Tattvārtha Sūtra* and *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra*), these four defilements are compared to **resin, oil, or mud**:

a)  **Viscosity / Stickiness:** When the body is clean, dust does not stick to it; but if the body is smeared with oil, every particle of dust in the air adheres to it. Similarly, when the *citta* acts with *Krodha, Māna, Māyā,* or *Lobha*, it becomes \"sticky.\"

b)  **Attraction of Karmic Matter:** This stickiness causes subtle karmic particles / seeds (*kārmaṇavargaṇā*) to adhere to the *citta* or the soul (*jīva*).

c)  **Involuntary Reincarnation:** These attached defilements create the momentum that pulls the *citta* or the soul (*jīva*) into new physical births, locking it into the laws of aging, decay, and death (*dukkha*).

The precise physical metaphor of *kaṣāya* acting as a sticky substance (like resin or oil on skin) that attracts and binds fine subtle material particles (*kārmaṇavargaṇā*) to the soul (*jīva*) is detailed in the **Tattvārtha Sūtra** (composed in early Sanskrit/Prakrit tradition by Ācārya Umāsvāti) and the Prakrit **Dravyasaṁgraha** of Nemicandra.

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 8.2 & 8.3:** *Sakaṣāyatvāj jīvaḥ karmaṇo yogyān pudgalān ādatte sa bandhaḥ. Prakṛti-sthiti-anubhāva-pradeśās tadvidhayaḥ.*

\"Because of possessing *kaṣāyas* (passions/defilements), the soul *jīva* assimilates matter (*pudgala*) fit for karma. That is binding (*bandha*). This bondage is categorized fourfold according to type (*prakṛti*), duration (*sthiti*), intensity (*anubhāva*), and quantity of karmic matter (*pradeśa*).\"

**Dravyasaṁgraha (Prakrit) 33 (Metaphor of Sticky Matter):** *Jaha tel-abbhattā-sarīro, reṇūhiṁ pusiya-gatto ya;* *Ghaṭṭai kaṣāehiṁ jo, so bajjhai kamma-reṇūhiṁ.*

\"Just as (jaha) a person whose body is smeared with oil (tel-abbhattā-sarīro) and then (ya) that person's limbs/body are touched by dust (pusiya-gatto reṇūhiṁ), whoever (jo) acts with defilements (ghaṭṭai kaṣāehiṁ), that person (so) becomes bound (bajjhai) by the dust particles of karma (kamma-reṇūhiṁ).\"

***Kilesā* *Jhāpitā* Burned Up**

The phrase **Kilesā jhāpitā mayhaṁ** (\"My defilements are burned up\") uses metallurgical and ascetic metaphors common to all ancient Sramaṇa lineages. Burning defilements is not a metaphor for suppression; it is an energetic, direct destruction of the fuel (*upādāna*) that powers *saṃsāra*.

Gautama's teachings in early Pali Canon emphasizes Nibbāna as the ultimate goal of the ancient path, one important condition is the total incineration of the defilements:

- **Through Sīla & Samādhi:** Restraining the senses breaks the outward expression of *Krodha* and *Lobha*, while deep absorption (*jhāna*) temporarily suppresses all four. When the *citta* enters deep states of absorption (the *jhānas*), the five hindrances (*nīvaraṇa*)---which are the active manifestations of *kilesa*---are completely suspended.

- **Through Disenchantment:** By observing all physical and mental phenomena with clarity, the *citta* recognizes that everything in this world is subject to decay and death. Seeing that, the *citta* turns away from them. the citta experiences complete disenchantment, severs all craving (taṇhā), and stops supplying fuel to the kilesas. Without fuel, the kilesas are incinerated (jhāpitā), leaving no trace behind.

- **Incineration (*Jhāpitā*):** Deprived of fuel, all four defilements---*Krodha, Māna, Māyā,* and *Lobha*---are completely burned up. When they are thoroughly eradicated, the cycle of birth (*jātisaṁsāro*) comes to an absolute end, allowing the purified *citta* to transcend this universe and realize final liberation in **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

In ancient Jain Prakrit canons, the incineration of defilements (*kilesā* / *kaṣāyas*) is through internal spiritual fire, the primordial force of *tapasā* and total detachment. The ancient Samana path relies on **tapas** (primordial force / intense spiritual heat) generated through absolute sense-restraint (*sīla*), unyielding endurance, and unbroken focus. *Tapas* incinerates the latent tendencies (*anusayas*) of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* stored within the mental continuum. Here are key verses from primary Jain texts that directly describe burning up (*jhāpita* / *dhadha*) defilements and karmic fuel.

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.27 (Burning Defilements Through Tapas):** *Tavassāhaṇae ṇaṁ bhaṁte! Jīve kiṁ jaṇayai?* *Tavassāhaṇae ṇaṁ kamma-ravam viśohae, kammaṁ ca ṇassa jhāpaī;* *Jhāpa-kamme ṇaṁ jīve sidhi-gai-ṇāmamohe viya-ragae;* *Siddhiṁ gachhaï, savva-dukkhāṇaṁ aṁtaṁ kareï.*

This verse directly addresses burning up past karma and defilements through the primordial force of *tapas*: "By the practice of tapas (tavassāhaṇae ṇaṁ), **bhaṁte/ bhante**, what does the soul (jīve) produce (kiṁ jaṇayai)? By the practice of tapas (tavassāhaṇae ṇaṁ), it purifies the dust of karma (kamma-ravam viśohae), and its karma is burned up / incinerated (kammaṁ ca ṇassa jhāpaī); having burned up karma (jhāpa-kamme ṇaṁ), the soul (jīve) becomes free from attachment (viya-ragae), reaches the state of perfection (siddhiṁ gachhaï), and makes an end of all dukkha (savva-dukkhāṇaṁ aṁtaṁ kareï)."

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 30.7 (The Fire of Meditation Incinerates Karma):** *Jahā mahā-kiyā-saṁgho, hava-vāheṇa jhāpaī;* *Evaṁ kamṁāṇi taviṇā, jhāṇa-sighi-samāhiṇā.*

Here, meditative absorption (*dhyāna*) is explicitly described as a fire that burns up defilements and karma like dry wood: "Just as (jahā) a large heap of dry wood (mahā-kiyā-saṁgho) is incinerated/burned up (jhāpaī) by a raging fire (hava-vāheṇa), even so (evaṁ) all kammas (kamṁāṇi) by one who possesses ***tapas*** (**taviṇā**) using the fire (sighi) of **jhāṇa** and **samāhi**/ **samādhi**."

**Sūtagadaṁga Sūtra 1.15.11 (Incinerating the Four Kaṣāyas):** *Kodhaṁ māṇaṁ ca māyaṁ ca, lobhaṁ ca tivihaṁ taha;* *Dhadha-bīya-sāmāṇiyaṁ, na rohaï puṇa-bbhave.*

This verse details the destruction of the four primary defilements (*krodha, māna, māyā, lobha*) so they can never sprout again: "Anger (kodhaṁ), pride (māṇaṁ), deceit (māyaṁ), and greed (lobhaṁ)---when these are completely scorched/burned like roasted seeds (dhadha-bīya-sāmāṇiyaṁ), they do not sprout (na rohaï) into renewed worldly existence / manifest (puṇa-bbhave)."

**Dravyasaṁgraha 48 (The Incineration of All Fuel):** *Jaha jhāṇālaṁki-siha, kamma-dhāṇaṁ dahadi koḍi-koḍīṇam;* *Taha jhāṇāṇala-siha, kilesa-bhāraṁ dahadi sassa.*

This text describes how the inner fire severs attachment and consumes the sticky material binding the mind**:** "Just as (jaha) the peak of a natural fire\'s flame (jhāṇālaṁki-siha) burns up heaps of fuel (dahadi), even so (taha) the flame of the fire of **jhāṇā** (jhāṇāṇala-siha) completely burns up (dahadi) the massive weight of defilements (kilesa-bhāraṁ) of the practitioner (sassa).

In Jaina doctrine, *jhāṇa* (dhyāna/meditation) and *samāhi* (samādhi/concentration) are not separate from *tapas*---rather, *dhyāna* is explicitly classified as the highest form of **abhyantara tapas** (internal **tapas**). Through *jhāṇa*, the internal fire of *tapas* is ignited, which burns away accumulated karmic seeds (*karmanirjarā*). The following key verses and passages across canonical and post-canonical Jain literature demonstrate this structural identity between *dhyāna*, *tapas*, and karmic destruction:

**(1). Tattvārtha Sūtra (Chapter 9)**

*Ācārya Umāsvāti explicitly defines dhyāna as internal tapas and establishes it as the cause of karma destruction.*

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.19:** *uttarottaraṁ bahuphalaṁ tapaḥ*

\"Each subsequent form of internal *tapas* yields exponentially greater spiritual fruit \[karmic destruction\].\" *(Note: The six internal tapas are expiation, reverence, service, study, renunciation, and **dhyāna**, making dhyāna the pinnacle of tapas.)*

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.29:** *uttamasamhananasyaikāgracintānirodho dhyānam āmuhūrtāt.* \"*dhyāna* is the concentration of thought on a single object by a person of robust constitution, lasting up to a *muhūrta* (48 minutes).\"

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.39:** *tapasā nirjarā ca.* \"Karmic burnout (*nirjarā*) is accomplished specifically through *tapas* \[of which *dhyāna* is the highest\].\"

**(2). Uttarādhyayana Sūtra (Chapter 29)**

*In the canonical Mūlasūtra, Lord Mahāvīra explains how jhāṇa and meditation operate as tapas to eliminate karma.*

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.27:** *jhāṇeṇaṁ kammabaṁhāṇaṁ nigghāḍaṇaṁ karei.* \"Through *jhāṇa*, the practitioner shatters and destroys the bondage of karmas.\"

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.8:** *taveṇaṁ appāṇaṁ sohei.* \"Through *tapas*, the self / *ātmā / attā* / soul-spirit (*appā*) purifies itself of karmic influx.\"

**(3). Dravyasaṁgraha (Ācārya Nemicandra)**

*This text highlights how pure dhyāna (śukla-dhyāna) acts as the direct fire that burns the karmic forest.*

**Dravyasaṁgraha 47:** *jhāṇaggiṇā daḍḍhūṇa kammakakkhaṁ.* \"Having burned down the dense forest of karmas with the blazing fire of *jhāṇa* (*jhāṇaggi* / *dhyānāgni*)\...\"

**(4). Jñānārṇava (Ācārya Śubhacandra)**

*A classical Jaina treatise dedicated specifically to meditation and yoga, using the metaphor of fire (tapas/dhyāna) burning karma.*

**Jñānārṇava 28.14:** *dhyānameghānilenetthaṁ karmadhūliḥ praṇīyate \| dhyānāgninā ca tatkṣaṇaṁ bhasmīkriyate pātakam \|\|*

\"By the wind-like storm of *dhyāna*, the dust of karma is blown away; and by the fire of *dhyāna*, all past karmic impurities are instantly reduced to ashes.\"

**Tapas is twofold:** External (*bāhya*, e.g., fasting/body posture) and Internal (*abhyantara*, e.g., study/absorption). **Dhyāna/Jhāṇa is the apex of Tapas:** It is the internal transformation where focus becomes motionless, directing the soul\'s energy inwards. **The Fire Mechanism:** *samāhi* turns this internal energy into a \"blazing fire\" of *jhāṇa* (*jhāṇa-sighi* / *dhyānāgni*), which severs karmic bondage (*nirjarā*).

**Jain Dhyāna/Jhāṇa Training**

In early Śvetāmbara and Digambara canonical texts---most notably the *Tattvārtha Sūtra* (Chapter 9) by Ācārya Umāsvāti (Umāsvāmi) around 2nd to 3rd CE and the *Jñānārṇava* by Ācārya Śubhacandra roughly 1000--1100 CE---the systematic entry into meditative absorption (*Dhyāna*) is structured around four preliminary prerequisites, followed by four progressive stages of concentration.

**Prerequisites for Entering Dhyāna:** Before entering deep meditative absorption, the practitioner must establish specific internal and external conditions:

- **Samyaktva (Right View/Faith):** Meditation is only classified as true *Dhyāna* (specifically *Dharma-dhyāna* or *Śukla-dhyāna*) if it is anchored in clear discernment between the spirit/soul (*jīva*) and non-spirit/matter (*ajīva*).

- **Kṣāyopaśama / Manaḥ-śuddhi (Mental Purity):** The mind must be free from intense passions (*kaṣāyas*: anger, pride, deceit, and greed) and unwholesome reflections (*Ārta-dhyāna* and *Raudra-dhyāna*).

- **Kāyotsarga (Relinquishment of the Body):** The physical prerequisite where the practitioner completely relaxes the body, standing or sitting in absolute stillness, severing attachment to physical form.

- **Prāṇāyāma / Svāsocchvāsa-nirodha (Breath Regulation):** Steadying the breath to tranquilize the physical energy channels (*prāṇa*), allowing the mental activity (*mano-yoga*) to settle.

**The Four Preparatory Phases (*Dhyāna-Pīṭhikā*):** In texts like the *Jñānārṇava*, the entrance into object-based meditation (*Dharma-dhyāna*) is achieved through four sequential contemplations (*Piṇḍastha-dhyāna*):

1.  **Pārthivī Dhāraṇā (Earth Contemplation):** Visualizing the vast, peaceful ocean of milk and a gold lotus at its center, bringing initial stability to the mind.

2.  **Āgneyeī Dhāraṇā (Fire Contemplation):** Visualizing a fire rising from the navel to burn away all karmic matter (*karmic particle influx*) surrounding the soul.

3.  **Śvasanā / Vāyavī Dhāraṇā (Wind Contemplation):** Visualizing strong winds blowing away the ash of consumed karma.

4.  **Varuṇī Dhāraṇā (Water Contemplation):** Visualizing a downpour of rain washing the soul completely clean, revealing its inherent radiance.

**Stages of True Dhyāna (*Dharma* & *Śukla*):** Once the mind is settled, meditation progresses into two higher forms:

Stage 1: **Dharma-Dhyāna** *(Virtuous Meditation):* Calming the mind and achieving complete detachment from worldly phenomena.

**(1). Ājñā-vicaya:** Reflecting on the profound truths of the teachings.

**(2). Apāya-vicaya:**Reflecting on the causes of karmic bondage and how to remove.

**(3). Vipāka-vicaya:** Reflecting on the nature of karmic fruition.

**(4). Saṃsthāna-vicaya:** Reflecting on the structure of the cosmos and the transient nature of the world (*loka*).

Stage 2: **Śukla-Dhyāna** *(Pure/Bright Meditation)*: Complete destruction of karmic forces, leading directly to liberation.

**(1). Pṛthaktva-vitarka-vicāra:** Conceptual reflection with shifting objects/words.

**(2). Ekatva-vitarka-avicāra:** Single-point concentration on a single aspect without shifting.

**(3). Sūkṣma-kriyā-pratipāti:** Subtle mental/physical activity remaining.

**(4). Samuchinna-kriyā-nivarti:** Total cessation of all physical, verbal, and mental activities.

Through this systematic inward withdrawal, the practitioner stops the influx of new karma (*saṃvara*) and burns away existing karmic seeds (*nirjarā*), transcending the worldly realm.

In Chapter 9 of the *Tattvārtha Sūtra*, Ācārya Umāsvāti systematically defines meditative absorption (*Dhyāna*), its structural prerequisites, its four classification categories, and its role in shedding karma (*nirjarā*).

**Prerequisites and Definition of Dhyāna**

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.27-29:** *uttamasamhananasyaikāgracintānirodho dhyānamānūrtāt \|\| 27 \|\| ārtaraudradharmyaśuklāni \|\| 28 \|\| pare hetuḥ saṁsārasya \|\| 29 \|\|*

*Dhyāna* is the concentration of thought on a single object to the exclusion of all others, achievable by a practitioner with the highest physical constitution, lasting for a duration up to an *antarmuhūrta* (under 48 minutes). It is classified into four types: Distressed (*Ārta*), Hostile (*Raudra*), Virtuous (*Dharmya*), and Pure (*Śukla*). The first two types (*Ārta* and *Raudra*) are the causes of worldly bondage and continued reincarnation.

**Virtuous Meditation (Dharma-Dhyāna)**

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.37 (Four Types of Dharma-Dhyāna):** *ājñā-apāya-vipāka-saṁsthāna-vicayāya dharmyam \|\| 37 \|\| śukle cā dye pūrvavidaḥ \|\| 38 \|\|* *pṛthaktva-ekatva-vitarka-sūkṣma-kriya-pratipāti-vyuparata-kriyā-nivartīni \|\| 39 \|\|*

*Dharma-dhyāna* consists of contemplation on: scriptural authority (*Ājñā*), eradication of karmic causes (*Apāya*), the fruition of karma (*Vipāka*), and the structure of the cosmos (*Saṃsthāna*). The first two stages of Pure *Śukla-dhyāna* are attainable by those well-versed in the ancient scriptures (*Pūrvas*). The four stages of Pure Meditation (*Śukla-dhyāna*) are: (1) Analytical concentration with shifting modes (*Pṛthaktva-vitarka-vicāra*); (2) Single-pointed concentration without shifting (*Ekatva-vitarka-avicāra*); (3) Subtle activity without regression (*Sūkṣma-kriyā-pratipāti*); (4) Total cessation of all activities (*Vyuparata-kriyā-nivarti*).

Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.44 (Ultimate Goal of Pure Dhyāna): *tryekaśeṣe sarvathā \|\| 44 \|\|*

Upon reaching the final stages of Pure Meditation, all destructive karmas (*ghātī-karma*) are completely eradicated, leading directly to Omniscience (*Kevala-jñāna*) and final liberation from the world.

#### 1.68. Ekudāniya

**Adhicetaso appamajjato, Munino monapathesu sikkhato;\
Sokā na bhavanti tādino, Upasantassa sadā satīmato ti.**

*For one possessing a higher mind (adhicetaso), who is diligent / exert zeal (appamajjato/ appamāda), for the muni training (sikkhato) in the ways of (pathesu) munihood (mona); sorrows / grief / mental distress (sokā) do not (na) manifest (bhavanti) for such a steadfast one (tādino), for one who is fully calmed (upasantassa), always (sadā) possessing continuous mindfulness (satīmato).*

**Commentary**: ***Adhicitta*** training and ***Muni*** training

This verse (found in texts *Sutta Nipāta* 4.15 / *Itivuttaka 45*) outlines the inner posture of the practitioner to achieve complete release:

**1). Itivuttaka 45: *Vitakka Sutta* / Thoughts**

In **Itivuttaka 45**, the prose preceding the verse explicitly addresses the problem of **unwholesome mental proliferation (*vitakkā*)**---specifically, how an un-concentrated mind is continuously invaded and scattered by worldly thoughts.

The Buddha addresses the bhikkhus about two distinct types of thoughts (*vitakkā*) that torment the untrained mind:

- **Duggaha-vitakka (Harmful/Sensory Thoughts):** Thoughts connected to sensual desire, ill-will, and harm.

- **Khemānupassī (Protection/Restraint):** The necessity of recognizing how these thoughts lead to agitation, grief, and bondage to *saṃsāra*.

The text explains that when a practitioner lives without guarding the senses, the mind continuously generates *vitakkā* (directed attention toward worldly objects). This continuous mental movement creates friction, which inevitably leads to *soka* (sorrow and distress).

Immediately after explaining how coarse thoughts destroy mental peace, the Buddha delivers the verse: *Adhicetaso appamajjato, Munino monapathesu sikkhato; Sokā na bhavanti tādino, Upasantassa sadā satīmato.*

The verse is given as the **antidote**. The Buddha is saying: *\"If you want to stop the onslaught of unwholesome thoughts and the sorrow they produce, you must abandon ordinary sensory thinking and establish the higher mind (adhicitta) through Jhāna.\"*

**2). Sutta Nipāta Snp 4.15 / *Attādaṇḍa Sutta* --- The Rod / Violence**

In the **Attādaṇḍa Sutta** of the *Sutta Nipāta* (one of the oldest strata of the Pāḷi Canon), the context is even more dramatic.

The Sutta opens with the Buddha describing his own pre-awakening observation of the world: He sees human beings fighting, struggling like fish in little water, driven by fear and violence (*attādaṇḍā bhayaṁ jātaṁ*). He sees people frantically searching for a safe refuge in the world, but finding none, because **the entire world (*loka*) is burning with conflict, aging, and death**. He identifies the cause of this universal panic: an internal arrow/dart (*salla*) stuck in the heart of beings. That dart is **craving (*taṇhā*) and defilements (*kilesā*)**.

After establishing that the entire external world is a site of conflict and sorrow (*soka*), the Sutta turns inward to explain how the sage (*muni*) pulls out the internal dart: The practitioner must stop looking for safety in external worldly objects. The practitioner must turn inward, cultivate the higher mind (*adhicetaso*), and remain stilled in absorption.

The verse is introduced to state: Once the internal arrow of craving is pulled out through *samādhi* and *adhicitta*, the practitioner becomes **Tādī** (steadfast/unshakeable). External worldly chaos can no longer penetrate or cause grief to such a mind.

The ordinary mind is constantly stirred up by *vitakkā* (worldly thoughts) and *kilesā* (defilements), making it vulnerable to fear, grief, and sorrow (*soka*). The practitioner trains in the paths of sagehood / *munihood* (*monapatha*), withdraws attention from worldly objects, and enters **Adhicitta** (the 4 Jhānas). The mind becomes **Upasanta** (fully stilled) and **Tādī** (unshakable). As a structural certainty, sorrow can no longer arise (*sokā na bhavanti*).

***Adhicitta*** training

To see the *adhicitta* training, we must look directly at the prose definition in the **Sikkhā Sutta (AN 3.88 / AN 3.89)**, which establishes that *adhicitta* is structurally identical to the four *jhānas*: *Katamā ca, bhikkhave, adhicittasikkhā? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ... dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ... tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ... catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, adhicittasikkhā. \"And what, bhikkhus, is the training in higher mind (adhi-citta-sikkhā)? Here, secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, a bhikkhu enters and dwells in the first jhāna... second jhāna... third jhāna... fourth jhāna. This is called the training in higher mind.\"*

Based on the canonical definitions (such as in the *Samaññaphala Sutta* and *AN 3.88*), a mind becomes an **adhicitta** when it undergoes three specific structural transformations:

1.  **Complete Seclusion from Worldly Agitation (*Vivicca kāmehi*):** The ordinary mind is constantly pulled by sensory inputs and worldly phenomena. An *adhicitta* is defined by turning away from these inputs---withdrawing *vitakkā* from worldly object so the mind is no longer scattered.

2.  **Purification from the Five Hindrances (*Vigatūpakkilesa*):** The mind drops coarse defilements: sensual desire (*kāmachanda*), ill-will/anger (*byāpāda* / *krodha*), sluggishness (*thīna-middha*), restlessness (*uddhacca-kukkucca*), and doubt (*vicikicchā*).

3.  **Establishment in the Four Jhānas (*Samāhita / Āneñjappattā*):** The mind progresses through stillness until it reaches the 4th Jhāna, defined by eight structural markers: **Samāhita** --- Steadfast and concentrated. **Parisuddha** --- Cleaned of dirt/defilements (*kilesā*). **Pariyodāta** --- Unclouded and bright. **Anaṅgana** --- Free of blemishes. **Vigatūpakkilesa** --- Freed from subtle disturbances. **Mudubhūta** --- Pliable. **Kammaniya** --- Workable. **Āneñjappattā** --- Completely immovable by worldly impacts (*loka-dhamma*).

An **adhicitta** is **a mind in the state of Jhāna / Samādhi**. It is not a moral feeling or an abstract status---it is a mind whose attention has been withdrawn from lower worldly phenomena (*loka*), cleansed of *kilesā*, and stilled into one-pointed absorption (*ekāgratā*).

**Muni training** (*monapathesu sikkhato*)

**1). The Pāḷi Canon: The Practical Blueprint of *Monapatha***

The Pāḷi Canon contains an entire discourse dedicated explicitly to defining **monapatha** (the state and training of a *muni*): the **Mona Sutta** (*Aṅguttara Nikāya* 3.122 / *Sutta Nipāta* 3.11 *Nālaka Sutta*).

In **AN 3.122**, the Buddha divides the training of a *muni* into three distinct operational faculties---the **Threefold Silence** (*tīṇi moneyyāni*):

- **Kāya-moneyya (Bodily Quietude):** Abstaining from physical violence, misconduct, and sensory dissipation; guarding physical posture and actions.

- **Vacī-moneyya (Verbal Quietude):** Abstaining from false speech, divisive speech, harsh words, and idle chatter (*samphappalāpa*); maintaining complete noble silence (*ariyo tuṇhībhāvo*) or speaking only Dhamma.

- **Mano-moneyya (Mental Quietude / Adhicitta):** The direct internal mechanism---withdrawing *vitakkā* from worldly objects, abandoning the five hindrances, entering the *jhānas* and realizing total destruction of taints (*āsavakkhaya*). Through the destruction of the taints (*āsavā*), a bhikkhu enters and dwells in the taintless liberation of mind (*cetovimutti*) and liberation through wisdom (*paññāvimutti*), having realized it directly for himself in this very life.

*Aṅguttara Nikāya* AN 3.122 **Mona Sutta:** *\"Tīṇimāni, bhikkhave, moneyyāni. Katamāni tīṇi? Kāyamoneyyaṃ, vacīmoneyyaṃ, manomoneyyaṃ. Katamañca, bhikkhave, **kāyamoneyyaṃ**? Idha, bhikkhave, ekacco pāṇātipātā paṭivirato hoti, adinnādānā paṭivirato hoti, kāmesumicchācārā paṭivirato hoti. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, kāyamoneyyaṃ. Katamañca, bhikkhave, **vacīmoneyyaṃ**? Idha, bhikkhave, ekacco musāvādā paṭivirato hoti, pisunāya vācāya paṭivirato hoti, pharusāya vācāya paṭivirato hoti, samphappalāpā paṭivirato hoti. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, vacīmoneyyaṃ. Katamañca, bhikkhave, **manomoneyyaṃ**? Idha, bhikkhave, ekacco āsavānaṃ khayā anāsavaṃ cetovimuttiṃ paññāvimuttiṃ diṭṭheva dhamme sayaṃ abhiññā sacchikatvā upasampajja viharati. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, manomoneyyaṃ. Imāni kho, bhikkhave, tīṇi moneyyānīti.\"*

\"Bhikkhus, there are these three forms of silence (*moneyyāni*). Which three? Bodily quietude (*kāyamoneyya*), verbal quietude (*vacīmoneyya*), and mental quietude (*manomoneyya*). \"And what, bhikkhus, is **bodily quietude**? Here, a bhikkhu abstains from taking life, abstains from taking what is not given, and abstains from sexual misconduct. This is called bodily quietude. \"And what, bhikkhus, is **verbal quietude**? Here, a bhikkhu abstains from false speech, abstains from divisive speech, abstains from harsh speech, and abstains from idle chatter (*samphappalāpa*). This is called verbal quietude. \"And what, bhikkhus, is **mental quietude**? Here, through the destruction of the taints (*āsavā*), a bhikkhu enters and dwells in the taintless liberation of mind (*cetovimutti*) and liberation through wisdom (*paññāvimutti*), having realized it directly for himself in this very life. This is called mental quietude. \"These, bhikkhus, are the three forms of silence.\"

The Buddha closes the sutta with verse that synthesizes the three faculties into the definition of a true *muni*: \"Kāyamuniṃ vacīmuniṃ, manomuniṃ anāsavaṃ; Muniṃ moneyyasampannaṃ, āhu \'sabbapahāyin\'ti.\"

\"Silent in body, silent in speech, silent in mind, free from taints--- the *muni* perfect in silence is called \'one who has abandoned everything\'.\"

**Verses from Snp 3.11 Nālaka Sutta**

The second section of the Nālaka Sutta contains the *Moneyya-paṭipadā* (the explicit guidelines for the practice of sagehood) delivered directly to Nālaka, emphasizing extreme sensory restraint, solitary living, and equanimity toward praise or blame.

**Sn 698 (Equanimity in Speech and Blame):** \"Samānaṃ kabbasaṃ kubbe, tulyaṃ gāme aninditaṃ; Manasā nānukampetha, na ca rosaye kañci naṃ.\"

"Maintain equanimity when hearing harsh words or praise in the village. Do not let the mind become perturbed, and do not harbor anger toward anyone."

**Sn 701 (Sensory Guarding and Alms Collection):** \"Sakkārasakkāraṃ tulayitvā, piṇḍāya caranto muni; Gāme na ramatī gāme, na lipyati gāmesu cārī.\"

"Weighing honor and dishonor equally as he walks for alms, the *muni* takes no delight in the village, remaining unsmeared while moving through the world."

**Sn 702--703 (Directing Attention Away from Worldly Objects):** \"Monāpathaṃ anuppatto, kāmehi na vilumpati; Santino santipadasya, vineyya manasi kathaṃ.\"

"Having attained the paths of sagehood/ munihood (*monapatha*), he is not stolen away by sensual pleasures. Peaceful, residing in the state of peace, he eliminates all worldly mental proliferation (*vitakkā*) and internal questioning."

**Sn 712 (The Mind of the Silent Sage):** \"Nadiyo kāsubbhe yanti, sanantā yanti mahodadhī; Yadūnakakaṃ taṃ saṇati, yaṃ pūraṃ santameva taṃ.\"

"What is half-empty makes noise; what is full is completely still. The fool is like a half-filled water jar; the wise *muni* is like a full pool of water, silent and deep."

**Sn 713 (Noble Silence / *Ariyo Tuṇhībhāvo*):** \"Yadaññātamhi bhāsati, ñāṇavā so tathā bhasi; Yo jānaṃ saṃyamo hoti, sa ve munīti vuccatī.\"

"When the sage speaks, he speaks only deep truth based on direct knowledge. But knowing the true nature of things, he mostly practices self-restraint and noble silence---he indeed is called a *muni*."

*Sutta Nipāta* **Snp 1.12 Muni Sutta:** The Muni Sutta is a fundamental text detailing the *muni* as one who has cut off sensory attachments, left home, abandoned all worldly mental proliferation, and remains unshakeable (*tādī*), it focuses on the internal detachment, sensory control, and solitary nature of the sage who has cut through worldly ties.

**Sn 207 (Solitude and Renunciation):** \"Santhavāto bhayaṃ jātaṃ, niketā jāyate rajo; Aniketamasanthavaṃ, etaṃ ve munidassanaṃ.\" **"**From companionship fear arises; from home life dust \[defilement\] is produced. Freedom from home, freedom from association---this indeed is the vision of a *muni*."

**Sn 211 (Mastery Over Sensory Stimuli):** \"Phussāphussaṃ yathā tattha, virājetvā na rajjati; Yo na rajjati saṃrattho, sa ve munīti vuccatī.\"

"Having faded out the stain (*virājetvā*) of sensory contact and non-contact (*phussāphussaṃ*) just as (*yathā*) it occurs there (*tattha*), he is not stained (*na rajjati*); he who (*yo*) is not stained (*na rajjati*) among the thoroughly dispassioned (*saṃrattho*), he (*sa*) indeed (*ve*) is called (*vuccatī*) a muni (*munīti*).

**Sn 212 (Unshakeable Mind - *Tādī*):** \"Usabho pavaro munī saṅghātā, yo nābhībhavatī ca kiñci loke; Tamaniñjamasaṅkhataṃ vimuttaṃ, kaṭhalaṃ majjhagataṃva nītacittaṃ.\"

**"**Like a noble bull, the *muni* conquers all and is conquered by nothing in the world. Immovable, unconditioned, and liberated, his mind remains completely balanced amidst all worldly conditions."

**Sn 221 (Abandoning Worldly Mental Proliferation):** \"Sabbābhibhuṃ sabbaviduṃ sumedhaṃ, sabbesu dhammesu anupalittaṃ; Sabbañjahaṃ taṇhakkhaye vimuttaṃ, taṃ vāpi dhīrā muni vedayanti.\"

**"**The wise declare him to be a *muni* who has overcome all, who knows all, who remains unsmeared by all worldly phenomena (*dhammā*), who has abandoned everything, and who is liberated through the destruction of craving."

**2). Jain Āgamas: *Mano-Gupti*, *Sāmāyika*, and Internal *Tapas***

In the Ardhamāgadhī Jain Canon, the training of the *muni* (Prakrit: *moṇappa* or *mauna*) is defined as the absolute cessation of mental, verbal, and physical activity (*yoga*) that causes karmic binding.

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra (Ahyāro 1.1--1.8):** The oldest Jain text, describing the *muni* who practices total non-harm (*ahiṃsā*), severe bodily restraint, and absolute mental stillness (*uvasaṃtacitta*). It outlines how a sage remains unmoved by heat, cold, hunger, and physical afflictions.

***Ahyāro 1.3.1.3* (The Tranquilized** **Mind and Equanimity):** *Uvasaṃtacitte je aniangāre, sīyaṃ ca teosiṃ ca adhiyāsaye;* *Aghāyaī na haniā saṃvude, eyaṃ monaṃ samaiyāyāi.*

"The homeless one (*aniangāre*) who is of tranquilized mind / mind freed from mental vibrations (*uvasaṃtacitte/ upasāntacitta*), let him endure (*adhiyāsaye*) cold (*sīyaṃ*) and (*ca*) heat (*teosiṃ ca*); non-violent (*aghāyaī*), restrained / guarded at the sense doors (*saṃvude*), not harm (*na haniā*); this (*eyaṃ*) is sagehood / munihood (*monaṃ*), taught (*āyāi*) as equanimity (*samaiyā / sāmāyika*)."

**Key Characteristics of the Stilled/ Tranquilized** **Mind *Uvasaṃtacitte* (*Upasāntacitta*)**

- **Total Restraint (*Saṃvuda*):** Guarded at the sense doors so that external sensory contact does not agitate the mind.

- **Unshakeable Equanimity (*Sāmāyika* / *Samayam*):** Free from oscillation (*avehaṅmāṇa*), remaining identical in heat, cold, praise, or insult.

- **Complete Non-Violence (*Ahiṃsā*):** Because the mind is stilled and freed from *rāga* (attachment) and *dosa* (aversion), it no longer generates harm (*na haniā*) toward any living entity.

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra (Chapter 2, Verse 24):** *Vandana-pūjaṇa-lāha-ggahaṇa-aṇayāe, taha ya gārāha-paribhava-sahe;* *Uvasaṃtacitte sayaṃ aṇelise, muṇī sa kammaṃ chaḍayāi samiyāye.*

Describes the *muni* who has stilled the mind (*upasāntacitta*) in the face of praise, censure, and physical affliction: "Without desire (*aṇayāe*) for praise (*vandana*), veneration (*pūjaṇa*), or gain (*lāha-ggahaṇa*), and (*taha ya*) enduring blame (*gārāha*) and insult (*paribhava*); the one of stilled mind (*uvasaṃtacitte*), ever (*sayaṃ*) peerless (*aṇelise*), that *muni* (*muṇī sa*) sheds (*chaḍayāi*) kamma (*kammaṃ*) through equanimity (*samiyāye*).

**Sūtagadaṃga Sūtra (1.14.12):** *Uvasaṃtacitte je saṃvud\'indie, cha pajjave jīva-nikāya-saṃvude;* *Na vi kammaṃ ciṇāi saṃvude, sa ya muṇī pariyaeṇa-muccai.*

Describes how the *muni* who stills the mind transcends the world (*loka*) and remains untouched by karma: "He who is of stilled mind (*uvasaṃtacitte*), whose senses are guarded (*saṃvud\'indie*), who restrains harm toward all six classes of living beings (*cha pajjave jīva-nikāya-saṃvude*); the guarded one (*saṃvude*) accumulates no (*na vi*) kamma (*kammaṃ*), and that *muṇī* (*sa ya muṇī*) by his pure practice (*pariyaeṇa*) is liberated (*muccai*).

In early Jain scriptures such as the *Ācārāṅga Sūtra*, the state of being *uvasaṃtacitte* (*upasāntacitta*)---a stilled or tranquilized mind---describes the **ethical and behavioral baseline of the homeless one (*aniangāre*) or *bhikkhu*** who has abandoned worldly ties.

- **Practical Endurance:** It refers directly to the psychological stability required to endure physical extremes---such as heat, cold, hunger, and physical afflictions---without anger or mental agitation.

- **Restraint (*Saṃvuda*):** It is the complete halting of harmful impulses toward living beings (*ahiṃsā*).

- **Equanimity (*Sāmāyika*):** It is maintaining an unshakeable, unbending mind when facing dualities, insult, or physical suffering.

This stilled mind is a condition of moral discipline, sense-restraint, and freedom from passion (*kāsaya*), operating continuously in daily homeless life.

***Ahyāro 1.7.8.1* (Endurance of Physical Afflictions):** *Phusantaṃ sīyaṃ adhiyāsayei, a-haii-māṇo anighāiya-ppao;* *A-cchambhī a-vehaṅmāṇa-citte, mahā-muni samayam anuvaehai.*

"Enduring (*adhiyāsayei*) the contact with (*phusantaṃ*) cold (*sīyaṃ*), with unbroken resolve (*a-haii-māṇo*) and giving no cause for grief (*anighāiya-ppao*); Unwavering (*a-cchambhī*), with an unagitated mind (*a-vehaṅmāṇa-citte*), the great *muni* (*mahā-muni*) remains established in (*anuvaehai*) equanimity (*samayam / sāmāyika*)."

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra (Chapter 29 - *Sammattā-parakkama \"The Right Exertion\"*):** Outlines 73 distinct spiritual exerts (*parākrama*). **Step 28 (Withdrawal of the Mind / *Cittassa Ovahāṇa*)**, which specifically details the withdrawal of mental vibrations (*cittassa ovahāṇeṇaṃ*) generates one-pointedness (*ekāgratā*), and the destruction of kamma (*nirjarā*) through internal *tapas*.

**73 Steps of *Samyaktva-Parākrama***

1.  *Saṃvega* (Longing for Liberation): *Saṃvegeṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Saṃvegeṇaṃ aṇaṃtayārayam aṇuyaya-ppaṇiyaṃ dhammaṃ saḍḍahai; aṇuyaya-ppaṇiyaṃ dhammaṃ saḍḍahamāṇe aṇaṃta-royaṃ kiṭṭi-kammaṃ karei.*

"By the urgent longing for liberation (*saṃvegeṇaṃ*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By the urgent longing for liberation (*saṃvegeṇaṃ*), one places faith / trusts (*saḍḍahai*) in the supreme, unexcelled Dhamma (*aṇuyaya-ppaṇiyaṃ dhammaṃ*) that cuts off endless wandering (*aṇaṃtayārayam*); Placing faith / trusts (*saḍḍahamāṇe*) in the supreme, unexcelled Dhamma (*aṇuyaya-ppaṇiyaṃ dhammaṃ*), one accomplishes (*karei*) purification and end of Samsara (*aṇaṃta-royaṃ kiṭṭi-kammaṃ*)."

2.  *Nirveda* (Disenchantment with the World): *Ṇivveeṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Ṇivveeṇaṃ divva-māṇusa-tesia-vesieesu kāmeesu ṇivveaṃ jaṇayai; kāmeesu ṇivvia-citte ṇaṃ jīve aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ kammaṃ ṇijjārei.*

"By disenchantment (*ṇivveeṇaṃ*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By disenchantment (*ṇivveeṇaṃ*), one produces (*jaṇayai*) detachment (*ṇivveaṃ*) toward sensory pleasures (*kāmeesu*), whether divine, human, or animal (*divva-māṇusa-tesia-vesieesu*); With a mind disenchanted (*ṇivvia-citte*) with sensory pleasures (*kāmeesu*), the soul (*jīve*) sheds (*ṇijjārei*) karma (*kammaṃ*) not previously experienced/matured (*aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ*)."

3.  *Dharmāstikāya-śraddhā* (Faith in the Dhamma): *Dhamma-saḍḍhaṇayāe ṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Dhamma-saḍḍhaṇayāe ṇaṃ aṇaṃta-royaṃ kiṭṭi-kammaṃ karei; aṇaṃta-royaṃ kiṭṭi-kammaṃ kareṃte ṇaṃ aṇuyaya-ppaṇiyaṃ dhammaṃ saḍḍahai.*

"By faith in the Dhamma (*dhamma-saḍḍhaṇayāe*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By faith in the Dhamma (*dhamma-saḍḍhaṇayāe*), one accomplishes (*kREi*) purification and end of Samsara (*aṇaṃta-royaṃ kiṭṭi-kammaṃ*); To accomplish (*kareṃte*) purification and end of Samsara (*aṇaṃta-royaṃ kiṭṭi-kammaṃ*), one places firm faith (*saḍḍahai*) in the supreme, unexcelled Dhamma (*aṇuyaya-ppaṇiyaṃ dhammaṃ*)."

4.  *Guru-paryupāsanā* (Attending upon the Teacher): *Guru-pariuvāsaṇayāe ṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Guru-pariuvāsaṇayāe ṇaṃ viṇaya-paḍivattiṃ jaṇayai; viṇaya-paḍipatte ṇaṃ jīve viṇaya-mūlaṃ dhammaṃ ahikārei.*

"By attending upon the Teacher (*guru-pariuvāsaṇayāe*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By attending upon the Teacher (*guru-pariuvāsaṇayāe*), one produces (*jaṇayai*) the practice of humble discipline (*viṇaya-paḍivattiṃ*); Endowed with humble discipline (*viṇaya-paḍipatte*), the soul (*jīve*) masters (*ahikārei*) the Dhamma, which has discipline as its root (*viṇaya-mūlaṃ dhammaṃ*)."

5.  *Alocanā* (Confession of Faults to the Teacher, Transparent Disclosure / Inspection, Laying bare all transgressions before the Guru without concealment or distortion.): *Āloaṇayāe ṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Āloaṇayāe ṇaṃ māya-māṇa-māya-ṃjūhaṇayāe ajjava-bhāvaṃ jaṇayai; ajjava-bhāvaṃ uvagao ṇaṃ jīvo aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ kammaṃ ṇijjārei.*

"By transparent disclosure (*āloaṇayāe*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By transparent disclosure (*āloaṇayāe*), through the removal of deceit, pride, and hidden motives (*māya-māṇa-māya-ṃjūhaṇayāe*), one produces (*jaṇayai*) straightness and honesty of mind (*ajjava-bhāvaṃ*); Having attained straightness and honesty (*ajjava-bhāvaṃ uvagao*), the soul (*jīvo*) sheds (*ṇijjārei*) karma (*kammaṃ*) not previously experienced/matured (*aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ*)."

6.  *Nindana* (Internal Self-Censure, Censure of one\'s own transgressions performed privately within one\'s own consciousness (*citta*).): *Ṇindaṇayāe ṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Ṇindaṇayāe ṇaṃ paścāttāpaṃ jaṇayai; paścāttāpeṇaṃ viśuddhiṃ uvagaacchai.*

"By self-censure (*ṇindaṇayāe*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By self-censure (*ṇindaṇayāe*), one produces (*jaṇayai*) genuine remorse (*paścāttāpaṃ*); through genuine remorse (*paścāttāpeṇaṃ*), one attains (*uvagaacchai*) purity (*viśuddhiṃ*)."

7.  *Garhaṇa* (Confession before the Guru): *Garihaṇayāe ṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Garihaṇayāe ṇaṃ apurisa-bhāvaṃ jaṇayai; apurisa-bhāveṇaṃ ṇaṃ maḍuyattāṃ uvagaacchai, maḍuyattāṃ uvagao ṇaṃ aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ kammaṃ ṇijjārei.*

"By formal confession (*garihaṇayāe*), O *bhaṃte*, what (*kīvaṃ*) does one produce (*jaṇayai*)? By formal confession (*garihaṇayāe*), one produces (*jaṇayai*) freedom from egoic self-assertion / stubborn pride (*apurisa-bhāvaṃ*); Through freedom from egoic self-assertion / stubborn pride (*apurisa-bhāveṇaṃ*), one attains (*uvagaacchai*) mildness and humility (*maḍuyattāṃ*); Having attained mildness and humility (*maḍuyattāṃ uvagao*), one sheds (*ṇijjārei*) karma (*kammaṃ*) not previously experienced / matured (*aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ*)."

28. **Cittassa Ovahāṇa / Citta-nirodha (Withdrawal of Mental Vibrations)**

*Cittassa ovahāṇeṇaṃ bhaṃte! kīvaṃ jaṇayai? Cittassa ovahāṇeṇaṃ ekagga-mmaṇaṃ jaṇayai; ekagga-mmaṇe ṇaṃ je bhāve maṇasā abhiṇivehaï, te bhāve maṇasā saṃvuruṇai; saṃvuda-citte ṇaṃ aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ kammaṃ ṇijjārei.*

\" bhaṃte, what (kīvaṃ) does one produce (jaṇayai) by the withdrawal of mental vibrations (cittassa ovahāṇeṇaṃ)?\" \"By the withdrawal of mental vibrations (cittassa ovahāṇeṇaṃ), one produces (jaṇayai) one-pointedness of mind (ekagga-mmaṇaṃ); One who possesses a one-pointed mind (ekagga-mmaṇe), whatever (je) worldly phenomena (bhāve) one settles upon (abhiṇivehaï) mentally (maṇasā), those (te) phenomena (bhāve) one shuts down / restrains (saṃvuruṇai / saṃvara) mentally (maṇasā); One whose mind is completely guarded/restrained (saṃvuda-citte), one burns away / destroys (ṇijjārei) those karma (kammaṃ) not previously experienced / deep-seated latent karmic matter (*sancita-karma*) that has not yet ripened into fruit (aṇāṇuya-puvvaṃ).\"

73. *Nirvāṇa-mārga-samāpti* (Completion of the Path to Liberation)

**Tattvārtha Sūtra (Chapter 9):** Defines the training of the *muni* through the three **Guptis** (restraints of mind, speech, and body---*mano-vacana-kāya-gupti*) and the six forms of internal *tapas*, culminating in *śukla-dhyāna* (pure *dhyāna*).

***Sūtra 9.4* (Definition of the Three Guptis):** *Samyag-yoga-nigraho guptiḥ.*

**"**The proper (*samyag*) restraint (*nigraho*) of activity \[of mind, speech, and body\] (*yoga*) is guarding (*guptiḥ*)."

***Sūtra 9.19--9.20* (Internal Tapas and Pure Meditation):** *Uttaraṃ chaḍ-bhedam: prāyaścitta-vinaya-vaiyāvṛttya-svādhyāya-vyutsarga-dhyānāny uttamam.* *Śukle cāddye pūrva-vidaḥ.*

Higher \[tapas\] (*uttaraṃ*) is sixfold (*chaḍ-bhedam*): penance / atonement / purification of faults (*prāyaścitta*), reverence / humility (*vinaya*), service to teachers and fellow (*vaiyāvṛttya*), self-study of scriptures (*svādhyāya*), renunciation / abandoning attachment to body and mental objects (*vyutsarga*), and *Jhāna* (*dhyānāny*). And (*ca*) the first two (*āddye*) stages of pure \[*Jhāna*\] (*śukle*) belong to the knower of the early scripture (*pūrva-vidaḥ*).

**3). Ancient Vedic Texts: *Mauna* and the *Muni* Lineage**

Rigveda contains an extraordinary historical snapshot of the original Sramaṇa-like *muni* tradition, which later matured in the Upanishads.

**Rigveda 10.136 (The *Keśin* / *Muni* Sukta):** is the earliest and most direct textual snapshot of the *Muni* tradition within the Vedic Saṃhitās. This text disproves the prevailing academic stereotype that the early Vedic or Brāhmaṇa tradition was exclusively focused on Vedic priests performing sacrificial rituals (*yajña*). Instead, it demonstrates that *Munis* engaged in silent contemplation, breath and energy control, vows of celibacy or wandering, and direct inner transformation. These elements directly prefigured the Śramaṇa movements (Jainism, Buddhism, and Ājīvikism) as well as the contemplative interiorization found in the Upanishads, such as the *Bṛhadāraṇyaka* and *Śvetāśvatara* Upanishads.

Furthermore, early tradition records that the foundational King Bharata---whose name became the original designation for the entire subcontinent (*Bhāratavarṣa*) long before modern terms like \"India\" or \"Hindu\" were introduced---was himself trained by *Muni* living in the forest.
