These forest-dwelling *Munis* were highly venerated and held such spiritual authority that they could influence or even decide the fate of a kingdom. This places the roots of the *Muni* tradition hundreds of years prior to the so called "Śramaṇa movements" by academia surrounding the era of Gautama Buddha.

**Rigveda 10.136.2 (The Outer Appearance and Wandering):** *munayo vātaraśanāḥ piśaṅgā vasate malā \| vātāsyānu dhrājiṃ yanti yad devāso avikṣata \|\|* \"The *Munis*, wind-girt \[or naked\], wear soiled, reddish garments; following the swift course of the wind, they go where the gods have entered.\"

**Rigveda 10.136.3 (Ascension of Consciousness over the Physical Body):** *unmaditā mauneyana vātāṁ ā tasthimā vayaṃ \| śarīred asmākaṃ yūyaṃ martyāso abhi paśyatha \|\|* \"transcending ordinary mind by silence (*mauneya*), we have mounted upon the winds! You mortals behold only our physical bodies \[while our consciousness transcends\].\'\"

**Rigveda 10.136.4 (Flight in the Inner Realm):** *antarikṣeṇa patati viśvā rūpābhicākaśat \| munir devasya-devasya saukṛtyāya sakhā hitaḥ \|\|* \"He flies through the mid-air, surveying all forms (*rūpa*) below; the *Muni* is a devoted friend to every god / deva for the accomplishment of good works.\"

**Self-Mastery vs. Ritual Sacrifice:** Unlike the mainstream academia's stereotype for Vedic priests (*ṛtvik*, *hotṛ*) that totally depended on fire altars (*agnyādheya*) and external offerings to deities, the *Muni* depended on internal spiritual power of *tapas*, silence (*mauna*), and breath-energy to achieve direct, ecstatic transcendence. The description ***Vātaraśanā*** (\"wind-girt\") directly connects with early Jain traditions (where *Vātaraśana* Śramaṇas are described as ancient nude or wind-clad ascetics) and points toward the shared tradition with other non-Vedic lineage of spiritual renunciation (*sannyāsa* / *śramaṇa*).

**Purity and Body Disenchantment:** In RV 10.136.3 (*śarīred asmākam\...*), there is an explicit dualistic separation between the transcendent consciousness and the physical body (*śarīra*). The physical body is viewed merely as an outer husk left behind for mortals to see---a worldview that became central to the Upanishadic distinction between the transient realm (*loka*) and the liberated self / soul-spirit, as well as the Śramaṇa focus on detachment from the physical body.

**Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upanishad (3.5.1):** Explicitly defines how one becomes a *muni*: *\"Pāṇḍityaṃ nirvidya bālyena tiṣṭhāset; bālyaṃ ca pāṇḍityaṃ ca nirvidyātha muniḥ\...\"* \"Having transcended scholarly learning, one should live like a child \[without worldly artifice\]. Having transcended both scholarly learning and child-like simplicity, **then one becomes a Muni \[a silent sage\]**.\"

**Maitrī Upanishad (6.28):** Discusses *mauna* (noble silence) as the prerequisite for direct absorption into higher truth, shutting off the senses to fix the mind within.

#### 1.69. Channa

**Sutvāna dhammaṁ mahato mahārasaṁ, Sabbaññutaññāṇavarena desitaṁ; Maggaṁ papajjiṁ amatassa pattiyā, So yogakkhemassa pathassa kovido ti.**

*Having heard (sutvāna) the Dhamma of great grandeur (mahato) and great taste \[of liberation\] (mahārasaṁ), taught (desitaṁ) by the best / supreme (varena) knowledge (ññāṇa) of omniscience / all-knowingness (sabbaññuta); I have entered upon the path (maggaṁ papajjiṁ) for the attainment of the deathless (amatassa pattiyā), He \[the Teacher / Tathāgata\] who is skilled (so kovido) in the path to reach the ultimate safety / security (yogakkhemassa pathassa).*

**Commentary**: **Amata** and **Khema** for **Nibbāna**

In the *Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta* (SN 43), the Buddha explicitly lists 33 epithets for the Unconditioned (*Asaṅkhata*), explicitly including both *Amata* and *Khema*. Both terms emphasize that Nibbāna is not a temporary state of quietude within the world, but the permanent, unconditioned sanctuary (*Khema*) where death holds no sway (*Amata*).

**1). Amata (The Deathless)**

- **Literal Meaning:** *A-* (non/without) + *mata* (dead/dying), deathless.

- **Philosophical:** Because all worldly phenomena (*loka*) are subject to arising, altering, and passing away (*dukkha*), Nibbāna is named *Amata* because it stands entirely outside the jurisdiction of the universal law of decay and death.

- **Canonical Usage:** It is often referred to as the deathless realm of Nibbana (*Amatā-dhātu*) or the goal of the path (*Amatassa patti*).

**2). Khema / Yogakkhema (Union with Nibbāna / Absolute Safety)**

- **Literal Meaning:** *Khema* denotes peace, sanctuary, security, or safety---a direct name for *Nibbāna*. *Yoga* yuj) signifies yoking, or spiritual union.

- **Philosophical Import:** *Yogakkhema* expresses the direct union (*yoga*) of the purified mind (*citta*) with the ultimate sanctuary (*khema*) of *Nibbāna-dhātu*. It represents the complete exit from the world (*loka*) into permanent, unassailable safety.

- **Canonical Usage:** The Tathāgata is titled *Yogakkhemassa pathassa kovido* (\"The Master Teacher Skilled in the Path to Union with Nibbāna\"), pointing the way to *Anuttara Yogakkhema*---the supreme, unsurpassable attainment of ultimate peace and sanctuary.

#### 1.70. Puṇṇa (2nd)

**Sīlameva idha aggaṁ, paññavā pana uttamo;\
Manussesu ca devesu, sīlapaññāṇato jayan ti.**

*Sīla* alone (*eva*) is supreme here in this world (*idha aggaṁ*), but one possessing *pañña* (wisdom) (*paññavā pana*) is the highest (*uttamo*); Among cosmic mankind (*manussesu*) and devas (*ca devesu*), victory is attained by means of *Sīla* and *pañña* (*sīla-paññāṇato jayan-ti*).

**Commentary**: ***Sīla*** and ***pañña** for **devas*** and cosmic **mankind** to Transcend Realms of Existence.

1.  **Sīla as the Foundation:** *sīla* establishes the baseline of purity, restraining unwholesome physical and verbal actions. It serves as the indispensable ground (*agga*) upon which all spiritual progress rests.

2.  **Paññā as the Ultimate Liberator:** While *sīla* purifies conduct, it is wisdom (*paññā*) that penetrates reality, eradicates *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, and realizes true release. Thus, the wise practitioner (*paññavā*) is named *uttamo*---the highest.

3.  **True Victory (*Jaya*):** Victory over the lower realms and worldly bondage (*saṃsāra*)---whether among cosmic mankind race (*manussesu*) or devas in the heavenly realms (*devesu*)---is not achieved through power or status, but exclusively through the dual cultivation of *sīla* and *paññā*.

#### 1.71. Vacchapāla

**Susukhum-anipuṇattha-dassinā, Matikusalena nivātavuttinā;\
Saṁsevita-vuddha-sīlinā, Nibbānaṁ na hi tena dullabhan ti.**

*By one who sees the extremely subtle, profound ultimate goal (susukhum-anippuṇ\'attha-dassinā), skilled in discernment (mati-kusalena), living with humility and gentleness (nivāta-vuttinā); By one who associates with those mature in sīla (saṁsevita-vuddha-sīlinā), Nibbāna is surely not hard to attain (nibbānaṁ na hi tena dullabhan-ti).*

**Commentary**: *Nibbāna* is Surely Not Hard to Attain By Those ...

1.  **Vision of the Subtle Goal (*Susukhum-anippuṇ\'attha-dassī*):** *Nibbāna* as the ultimate goal (*attha*) is exceedingly subtle (*sukhuma*) and profound. It cannot be apprehended through coarse, worldly cognition, but must be directly discerned through deep, refined penetration.

2.  **Skilled Discernment (*Matikusala*):** Sharp, wholesome intelligence (*paññā*) is required to discern the unconditioned and deathless sanctuary of *Nibbāna* by recognizing how the universal law of decay and death have conditioned all worldly phenomena (*loka*).

3.  **Ego-less Humility (*Nivātavutti*):** True spiritual progress demands total freedom from worldly egoic pride. Humility softens the mind, enabling the practitioner to yield to truth and renunciation, abandon all worldly clinging.

4.  **Noble Association (*Saṁsevitavuddhasīlī*):** Walking the path requires seeking out, serving, and learning from those who are fully mature and established in *sīla*, drawing inspiration from their purified conduct.

5.  **Certainty of Realization (*Nibbānaṁ na hi tena dullabhaṁ*):** For a practitioner endowed with these qualities---discerning the subtle goal, skilled in wisdom, grounded in humility, and devoted to noble fellowship---attaining *Nibbāna* is not an elusive or improbable feat, but a clear and certain outcome.

#### 1.72. Ātuma

**Yathā kaḷīro susu vaḍḍhitaggo, Dunnikkhamo hoti pasākhajāto;\
Evaṁ ahaṁ bhariyāyānitāya, Anumaññaṁ maṁ pabbajitomhi dānī ti.**

*Just as a young bamboo shoot (yathā kaḷīro susu) grows up at its top (vaḍḍhitaggo) and becomes hard to extricate (dunnikkhamo hoti) once it branches out (pasākhajāto); Even so (evaṁ), before my wife was brought home / before the marriage/domestic life was fully established (bhariyāyānitāya), I (ahaṁ) have obtained permission (anumaññaṁ maṁ), now (dānī) I have renounced worldly life (pabbajit-omhi).*

**Commentary**: **Renunciation** (***pabbajja***)

1.  **The Analogy of the Bamboo Shoot (*Kaḷīra*):** A young, tender bamboo shoot can easily be pulled out of the ground. Once it grows tall and spreads side-branches (*pasākhajāto*), its roots interweave with the earth, making it practically impossible to extract (*dunnikkhamo*).

2.  **Entrapment of Household Ties:** Worldly commitments---specifically marriage and family life---act like side-branches that root a person deeper into the world (*loka*). The longer one lingers in domestic life, the more dense the network of craving, responsibility, and attachment becomes.

3.  **Decisive Renunciation (*Pabbajjā*):** Bhante recognized the danger of delay. By seeking permission and renouncing worldly life (*pabbajjā*) before domestic bonds could fully entangle him, he severed the root of future bondage, ensuring a clear path toward total liberation from the world and enter the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

#### 1.73. Māṇava

**Jiṇṇañca disvā dukhitañca byādhitaṁ, Matañca disvā gatamāyusaṅkhayaṁ; Tato ahaṁ nikkhamitūna pabbajiṁ, Pahāya kāmāni manoramānī ti.**

*Having seen an old person (jiṇṇañ-ca disvā), and one afflicted with suffering and sickness (dukhitañ-ca byādhitaṁ), And having seen a dead person (matañ-ca disvā) whose life-forces had reached their end (gatam-āyusaṅkhayaṁ); Thereupon (tato), having departed from home (ahaṁ nikkhamitūna), having abandoned (pahāya) sexual pleasures (kāmāni) delightful to the mind (manoramānī), I renounced worldly life (pabbajiṁ).*

**Commentary: Spiritual Awakening and Renunciation**

1.  **The Catalyst of Impermanence:** Encountering old age (*jiṇṇa*), illness (*byādhita*), and death (*mata*) shatters the illusion of physical stability. One thus renounces the external world (*loka*) and turns inward.

2.  **Urgency and Disenchantment (*Saṃvega*):** Beholding a corpse whose vital force is completely exhausted (*gatam-āyusaṅkhaya*) induces spiritual urgency. It drives the practitioner to turn away from transient existence and seek the deathless sanctuary (*Nibbāna*) where the law of death holds no sway (*Amata*).

3.  **Abandonment of Sexual/Sensual Craving:** *kāma* is sex. Renouncing sex is a prerequisite stage to transcend the lower and base sex realm*.* Craving for sexual/sensual delight is the main tether to *saṃsāra* for us humans because we are basically sex realm being; only through the Rupa realm, the middle way among the three realm model of our world, that we can gain ultimate liberation from the world.

4.  **Decisive Departure (*Nikkhamitūna Pabbajiṁ*):** True renunciation involves both internal abandonment and external action. Departing from the household life (*nikkhamitūna*) and renunciation (*pabbajiṁ*) represents the decisive exit from worldly entanglements onto the ancient path to the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

#### 1.74. Suyāmana

**Kāmacchando ca byāpādo, Thinamiddhañca bhikkhuno;\
Uddhaccaṁ vicikicchā ca, Sabbasova na vijjatī ti.**

*Sexual/Sensual desire (kāmacchando ca) and ill-will (byāpādo), sloth and torpor (thina-middhañ-ca) in a bhikkhu (bhikkhuno); Restlessness (uddhaccaṁ) and doubt (vicikicchā ca), in every way / entirely (sabbasova) not exist / found (na vijjatī-ti).*

**Commentary**: Five Mental Hindrances (*nīvaraṇāni*)

1.  **Eradication of the Five Hindrances (*Nīvaraṇa*):** The verse lists all five classic obstacles to *samādhi* and *paññā*:

    *Kāma-chanda* (Sexual/Sensual Desire) & *Byāpāda* (Ill-will)

> *Thina-middha* (Sloth/Torpor) & *Uddhacca* (Restlessness) --- Impairing mental clarity and stability: *thina* (*stāna*) = sloth, sluggishness, mental inertia. *middha* = torpor, drowsiness, heavy inactivity. *uddhacca* = Restlessness, agitation, mental flurry.
>
> *Vicikicchā* (Doubt) --- Stemming from *moha*. Skeptical doubt, indecision, uncertainty regarding the Dhamma.

2.  **Permanent Abandonment (*Sabbasova na vijjati*):** The phrase *sabbasova na vijjati* emphasizes that these mental phenomena are not merely temporarily suppressed, but completely eradicated at their roots.

3.  **Purity of the Mind (*Citta*):** When these five hindrances cease to exist, the mental phenomena are no longer corrupted by worldly defilements. The mind (*citta*) transcends worldly agitation (*loka*), opening directly into the deathless.

#### 1.75. Susārada

**Sādhu suvihitāna dassanaṁ, Kaṅkhā chijjati buddhi vaḍḍhati;\
Bālampi karonti paṇḍitaṁ, Tasmā sādhu sataṁ samāgamo ti.**

*This is good (sādhu) seeing (dassanaṁ) those well-established in noble life (suvihitānaṁ); doubt is destroyed (kaṅkhā chijjati) and Intellect / discernment (buddhi) grows (vaḍḍhati); Even a fool / lacking discernment transforms into a wise / possessed of spiritual insight (bālam-pi karonti paṇḍitaṁ); therefore (tasmā), it is good (sādhu) for association with (samāgamo) the virtuous / peaceful / purified ones (sataṁ).*

**Commentary**: **Fellowship Leads to Transformation (*Sataṁ Samāgama*)**

Even a fool / lacking discernment (*bāla*) entangled in worldly delusion can be transformed into a wise / possessed of spiritual insight (*paṇḍita*) through the elevating influence of noble association (*kalyāṇamittatā*). Thus, meeting with the virtuous / peaceful / purified ones (*sataṁ samāgamo*) is a good thing *sādhu*---supremely good.

#### 1.76. Piyañjaha

**Uppatantesu nipate, nipatantesu uppate;\
Vase avasamānesu, ramamānesu no rame ti.**

*One should land/stay low while others are flying up (uppatantesu nipate), and one should ascend/soar while others are falling down (nipatantesu uppate); One should dwell among those lacking a true home (vase avasamānesu), and one should not delight among those who indulge/take pleasure (ramamānesu no rame-ti).*

#### 1.77. Hatthārohaputta

**Idaṁ pure cittamacāri cārikaṁ, Yenicchakaṁ yatthakāmaṁ yathāsukhaṁ; Tadajjahaṁ niggahessāmi yoniso, Hatthippabhinnaṁ viya aṅkusaggaho ti.**

*In the past (pure), this mind-heart (idaṁ cittam) wandered about (acāri cārikaṁ) wherever it wished (yenicchakaṁ), wherever it desired (yatthakāmaṁ), as it pleased (yathāsukhaṁ); Today (tad-ajja), I shall master/restrain it (ahaṁ niggahessāmi) with wise attention (yoniso), just as a mahout with a hook (aṅkusaggaho) restrains a wild elephant in rut (hatthippabhinnaṁ viya).*

**Commentary**: The imagery of the elephant iron hook (*aṅkusa*) can sound severe to modern sensibilities, but in the canonical context, it is a precise metaphor for **inner precision and firm determination**, not self-torture or physical cruelty.

**(1). Metaphor vs. Method: Firmness is Not Cruelty**

In ancient Indian training, a wild, rutting elephant could not be guided by vague, gentle wishes; it required a clear, unyielding boundary.

**What the Iron Hook Represents:** The iron hook symbolizes **vigilance and wise attention (*yoniso manasikāra*)**. It is the immediate, sharp recognition that cuts off a destructive thought or directed attention (*vitakkā*) the second it strays toward harmful worldly objects.

**(2). The Modern Shift: The Softening of Determination**

Modern spiritual practice often tilts toward comfort, self-soothing, and low expectations, whereas the ancient Śramaṇa and *Muni* tradition was built on radical urgency (*saṃvega*).

**The Softened Approach:** Today, people often prioritize gentle acceptance without the critical second step: **active abandonment (*pahāya*)** of unwholesome states.

**The Loss of Sharpness:** Without a firm standard of discipline (*sīla*) and mental restraint, practice easily degrades into passive watching rather than active mastery (*niggahessāmi*).

**(3). The Central Role of *Appamāda* (Earnest Zeal)**

Gautama Buddha declared *appamāda* to be the single most important internal quality for liberation, is directly aligned with the canonical texts:

*\"Just as the footprint of an elephant footprint covers that of all other animals, so does* **earnest zeal** *(appamāda) encompass all wholesome qualities.\"* (SN 3.17)

**What *Appamāda* Means:** it is **unwavering earnest zeal**---the continuous awareness of danger in worldly existence (*saṃsāra*) paired with the zeal to transcend it.

**Why Today\'s Mind Struggles:** Modern culture minimizes the perception of existential danger (*dukkha*) through constant distraction and comfort. When the sense of urgency (*saṃvega*) is low, *appamāda* drops, making the rigorous discipline of the ancient liberation path feel unnecessarily harsh.

The ancient masters were not being cruel; they were being **realistic about the wildness of the unmastered/undisciplined mind**.

Training the mind requires gentle patience with oneself, but an absolute, unyielding firmness toward the defilements. The \"iron hook\" is simply the unwavering decision to stop letting the mind wander aimlessly into worldly reincarnation, keeping it steadily aligned toward the ultimate sanctuary of the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

#### 1.78. Meṇḍasira

**Aneka-jāti-saṁsāraṁ, sandhāvissaṁ anibbisaṁ;\
Tassa me dukkhajātassa, dukkhakkhandho aparaddhoti.**

*Through many births I wandered (aneka-jāti-saṁsāraṁ), continuously running about / transmigrates (sandhāvissaṁ) without finding \[the builder of the house / body\] (anibbisaṁ); For me (tassa me) born into this suffering (dukkha-jātassa), this mass of suffering (dukkha-khandho) has been destroyed (aparaddho).*

**Commentary:** **The Search for the House-Builder**

1.  **The Trajectory of Involuntary Wandering (*Aneka-jāti-saṁsāraṁ Sandhāvissaṁ*):** Wandering through countless births is the consequence of an unpurified *citta*. As long as the *citta* is defiled, the mind-heart continuously transmigrates through the world (*loka*), bound by cause, effect, and continuous rebirth.

2.  **The House as the Physical Vessel (*Dukkha-khandho*):** The \"house\" constructed across *saṃsāra* represents the body, bound with decay and death - suffering (*dukkhakkhandha*).

3.  **The Mind-Heart (*Citta*) and the Inner Essence Soul-Spirit (*Attā*):** When the mind-heart (*citta*) is unpurified and blinded by defilements, its inner essence soul-spirit (*attā*) acts as the builder, generating the karmic momentum that constructs future bodies. Rebirth is driven by this unpurified mind-heart *citta* seeking manifest in the world.

4.  **Purification and the End of Construction (*Aparaddho*):** Liberation from the world is realized when the *citta/ attā* is fully purified through the total eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. Once purified, the builder no longer constructs a new house or body. The causal chain of rebirth is severed, the entire mass of suffering (*dukkha-khandho*) is destroyed (*aparaddho*), and the purified mind transcends worldly existence to reach the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

**Dhammapada Verses 153-154**

This verse about finding the house-builder relate to the full verses of Dhammapada 153-154. It express the discovery of the Citta and *ātmā / attā,* how its defilements lead to countless births and saṃsāra, and how its purification dismantles the mechanism of involuntary reincarnation.

*Anekajāti saṃsāraṃ, sandhāvissaṃ anibbisaṃ; Gahakāraṃ gavesanto: dukkhā jāti punappunaṃ. / 153 / Gahakāraka diṭṭho'si, puna gehaṃ na kāhasi; Sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, gahakūṭaṃ visaṅkhataṃ; Visaṅkhāragataṃ cittaṃ, taṇhānaṃ khayam ajjhagā. / 154 /*

Through many births in saṃsāra I wandered, not finding the house-builder. Painful is birth again and again. O house-builder, you are seen! You will not build this house again. All your rafters are broken, your roof-ridge is destroyed. The mind-heart (citta), having reached the Unconditioned, has attained the complete eradication/extinction of cravings (*taṇhā*).

Commentary: For countless lifetimes, the mind-heart (*citta*) together its essence of soul-spirit *ātmā / attā* / *appā* has wandered through saṃsāra, unknowingly trapped in the cycle of birth and death. Searching for the house-builder, it has suffered again and again.

At last, the house-builder (*gahakāraka*), the mind-heart itself, is seen and the mechanism forcing constructing each new body (geham) is understood. With this realization, the conditioning cycle is broken---the framework of worldly craving and attachment that sustained worldly rebirth is dismantled. The foundation of worldly existence is shattered.

Purified and freed from all worldly conditioning (*visaṅkhāra*), the mind-heart will no longer construct another body. With worldly craving uprooted, it enters the Unconditioned state and attains ultimate liberation from the world, never to re-enter the world of birth and death again after the dissolution of the final body.

**Jain Canonical Texts (Ardhamāgadhi Prakrit)**

In the Jain Śramaṇa tradition, the equivalent Prakrit verbal form **saṁdhāvai** (from Sanskrit *saṁdhāvati*) is also used to describe the soul\'s (*jīva*) wandering through the four states of existence (*gati*).

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 3.1-3 (Uttajjhāyā):** *Cattāri paramaṅgāṇi, dullahāṇīha jaṃtuṇo; Māṇusattāṃ suī saddhā, saṃjamaṃmi ya vīriyaṃ. Kamma-ppaḍa-baddhāṇaṁ, jīvāṇaṁ saṁsarāṇa ya; Nāṇā-gati-upa-pannaṇaṁ, māṇusattāṁ su-dullahaṁ. Tiriccha-gati-mappāṇaṁ, kallaṁ patto hi maṇuya-taṁ; Kayāī su-kadayāṇaṁ, kammāṇaṁ phalam-utta-maṁ.*

Four (*cattāri*) ultimate factors (*paramaṅgāṇi*) are difficult to obtain (*dullahāṇi*) here in this world (*iha*) for a living being (*jaṃtuṇo*); Human / mankind state (*māṇusattāṁ*), hearing of the sacred teaching (*suī*), faith (*saddhā*), and (*ya*) energy (*vīriyaṁ*) in self-restraint (*saṃjamaṁmi*).

For soul *jīvā* (*jīvāṇaṁ*) bound by karmic attachments (*kamma-ppaḍa-baddhāṇaṁ*) and wandering in saṃsāra (*saṁsarāṇa ya*), Reborn across various states of existence (*nāṇā-gati-upa-pannaṇaṁ*), the human / mankind state (*māṇusattāṁ*) is extremely rare (*su-dullahaṁ*).

From the animal/sub-human state of existence (*tiriccha-gati*), a soul-spirit *Ātman* / *appā* (*appāṇaṁ*) reaching (*patto*) the status of humanity (*maṇuya-taṁ*) at the proper time (*kallaṁ*); Reaps (*hi*) the time (*kayāī*) the higher fruit (*phalam-utta-maṁ*) of well-performed noble deeds (*su-kadayāṇaṁ kammāṇaṁ*).

**Jīva vs. Appā / Ātmā**

**Jīva:** Emphasizes the **ontological substance (*dravya*)** defined by life, consciousness (*cetanā*), and vital functions (*prāṇa*). It emphasizes the part when the spirit enters the world built on the four great elements transformed into soul.

**Appā / Ātmā:** It is the overall soul-spirit as a whole. It emphasizes the **subjective identity, inner essence, and spiritual agent**. It is the self-referential \"I\" or inner self that is capable of purification, self-control (*saṁyama*), and realizing ultimate liberation (*mokṣa*).

**The Four States of Existence (*Catu-Gati*) in Jain Agamas**

When Verse 3.2 refers to \"various states of existence\" (*nāṇā-gati*), it points to the foundational Jain cosmological framework of the four realms through which the unpurified *jīva* transmigrates:

A.  **Deva-gati:** Celestial / Heavenly state.

B.  **Manushya-gati:** Human / mankind state (highlighted in 3.1 as the sole gateway to final liberation).

C.  **Tiryak-gati:** Animal, plant, and sub-human state.

D.  **Naraka-gati:** Hellish / Infernal state.

Verse 3.1 isolates **human/mankind existence** (*māṇusattāṁ*) as the essential starting point for spiritual self-restraint (*saṃjama*), Verses 3.2 and 3.3 explicitly frame this human rebirth as a rare escape from the continuous reincarnation (*saṃsāra*) of the *jīva* through these four *gatis*.

While the Jain Śramaṇa tradition consistently uses **4 states (*caturgati*)**, whereas early Buddhism explicitly uses **5 destinations (*pañca-gatayo*)** in the core Pāḷi Suttas, which later expanded to **6 destinations** in certain Buddhist traditions by separating Asuras into their own distinct realm.

5 Gatis (*Pañca-gatayo*) of Early Pāḷi Canon:

A. *Deva* (Heavenly / Deva realm)

B. *Manussa* (Human realm)

C. *Petta-visaya* (Ghost realm / Hungry ghosts)

D. *Tiracchāna-yoni* (Animal realm)

E. *Niraya* (Hell realm)

6 Gatis of Mahāyāna Tradition: Adds *Asura* (Demigods / Titans) as a standalone 6th realm instead of grouping them under *Deva* or *Petta*.

**Sūuyagaḍaṃgasiṁha (Sūtrakṛtāṅga Sūtra 1.1.2.11):** *Anāiyaṃ aṇavagaggaṃ, saṃsāraṃ moga-m-āhuyaṃ; Sattā-ṭṭhāṇāṇi **saṃdhāvai**, punabbhava-parammukho.*

Beginningless (*anāiyaṃ*) and endless (*aṇavagaggaṃ*), ***saṃsāra*** is taught (*āhuyaṃ*) as delusion (*moga-m-*); One continuously runs through / transmigrates (*saṃdhāvai*) the seven places/states (*sattā-ṭṭhāṇāṇi*), until turning one\'s back on / facing away from (*parammukho*) repeated manifestation / worldly existence (*parammukho*).

**The Seven Places / Categories of Existence (*Sattā-Ṭṭhāṇāṇi*)**

**Seven fundamental categories of living embodied existence** (*kāya-sthāna* / *jīva-sthāna*) through which the unpurified soul (*jīva* / *appā*) continuously transmigrates (*saṃdhāvai*) while bound in saṃsāra. Ancient Jain taxonomy classifies all embodied living entities into seven distinct structural realms based on their physical vehicle and sensory apparatus:

a)  **Pudhavī-kāya (Earth-bodied beings):** Living entities whose physical body is composed of earth/minerals (one-sensed: touch).

b)  **Āu-kāya (Water-bodied beings):** Living entities whose physical body is composed of water/liquids (one-sensed: touch).

c)  **Teu-kāya (Fire-bodied beings):** Living entities whose physical body is composed of fire/heat (one-sensed: touch).

d)  **Vāu-kāya (Air/Wind-bodied beings):** Living entities whose physical body is composed of air/wind (one-sensed: touch).

e)  **Vaṇassai-kāya (Plant-bodied beings):** Living entities inhabiting flora, vegetation, and subtle plant life (one-sensed: touch).

f)  **Taasa-kāya (Mobile / Sub-human animal beings):** Beings possessing two to **five senses** that can move independently (insects, worms, fish, birds, four-legged animals---representing the *Tiriccha-gati*).

g)  **A-taasa / Amāṇusa-Maṇuya-Deva-Nāraya (Human, Heavenly, and Infernal beings):** Beings with **mind/reasoning** faculties (*saññī*) inhabiting the remaining realms of existence (Humans, Devas, and Hell-beings).

While **Jainism (*Sūtrakṛtāṅga*)** uses *Sattā-ṭṭhāṇāṇi* to classify the **physical types of bodies/existences** (*kāya*) in which a *jīva* gets trapped across the material universe. **Early Buddhism (*Dīgha Nikāya* / *Samyutta Nikāya*):** Uses the term **Sattā Viññāṇa-ṭṭhiti** (\"Seven Stations of Consciousness\"), which classifies existence by the **seven ways consciousness lands and settles** in the physical and mental realms: Diverse Bodies, Diverse Perceptions: Beings with different physical forms and different mental perceptions (e.g., human beings, some devas, and some beings in the lower realms). Diverse Bodies, Unanimous Perception. Unanimous Bodies, Diverse Perceptions (like Tesla Optimus Bot). Unanimous Bodies, Unanimous Perception. The Sphere of Boundless Space. The Sphere of Boundless Consciousness. The Sphere of Nothingness.

#### 1.79. Rakkhita

**Sabbo rāgo pahīno me, sabbo doso samūhato;\
Sabbo me vigato moho, sītibhūtosmi nibbuto ti.**

*All (sabbo) worldly craving and attachment (rāgo) has been abandoned (pahīno) by me (me), all (sabbo) fear and anger / hatred (doso) has been uprooted (samūhato); All (sabbo) my (me) ignorance and delusion (moho) has vanished (vigato), I am (asmi) freed from the burning fires of saṃsāra / worldly existence / manifest (sītībhūto), unbound (nibbuto).*

**Commentary**: **Etymology of *Nibbuto***

**Pāḷi:** *Nibbuto* (Nominative singular masculine) **Sanskrit:** *Nirvṛta* **Grammatical Form:** Past Passive Participle of *nibbāti* (*ni-* + vā or vṛ)

**Root Derivation & Linguistic Pathways:** In Pāḷi philology, *nibbuto* represents a conflation of two primary Indo-Aryan root structures:

**vā (To Blow): Prefix:** *Ni-* (out, away, off, back). **Root:** vā (to blow, emit air). **Process:** *Ni-* + vā + *-ta* -\> *Nibbāta* / *Nibbuta*. **Core Meaning:** \"Blown out,\" \"quenched,\" \"extinguished\" (like a flame when its fuel supply is exhausted).

**vṛ (To Cover / Enfold):** **Prefix:** *Ni-* (out of, un-). **Root:** vṛ (to cover, enclose, bind, entangle). **Process:** *Ni-* + vṛ + *-ta* -\> *Nir-vṛta* -\> *Nibbuta*. **Core Meaning:** \"Un-covered,\" \"un-bound,\" \"dis-entangled,\" \"freed from confinement.\"

While older 19th-century European scholarship/ academia primarily stressed the passive metaphor of *extinction* or *cooling* (deriving strictly from vā), modern comparative philology and ancient commentators recognize that both semantic streams---\"blown out\" (vā) and \"unbound / uncovered\" (vṛ)---coexist within the Pāḷi term. And here my position is with "unbound", which spiritually correct.

**Confinement vs. Release:** The prefix *ni-* acts as a negative/privative reverser. Where a bound worldly state is enmeshed (*vaṭṭa*) by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the *nibbuto* individual has completely severed these ties, becoming entirely unentangled and unbound.

**Fuel vs. Binding:** Just as a fire goes out when it no longer clutches at fuel (*upādāna*), the *citta* becomes *nibbuto* when all worldly clinging and craving cease. Because *upādāna* in Pāḷi means both \"fuel\" and \"clinging/binding,\" the concepts of \"extinguished\" and \"unbound\" merge naturally.

**Semantic Components of *Nibbuto:***

**Cooling (*Sītībhāva*):** The cessation of the scorching heat generated by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

**Unbinding (*Mokkha / Visaṃyoga*):** Complete liberation from the bondage of cause and effect, kamma, and involuntary rebirth.

**Transcendence:** Leaving behind all worldly phenomena (*loka*) to reach the unconditioned sanctuary of the deathless Nibbāna realm (*Nibbāna-Dhātu*).

#### 1.80. Ugga

**Yaṁ mayā pakataṁ kammaṁ, appaṁ vā yadi vā bahuṁ;\
Sabbametaṁ parikkhīṇaṁ, natthi dāni punabbhavo ti.**

*Whatever (yaṁ) kamma (kammaṁ) has been done (pakataṁ) by me (mayā), whether (yadi) little (appaṁ vā) or (vā) much (bahuṁ); All this (sabbametaṁ) is completely exhausted (parikkhīṇaṁ); now (dāni) there is no (natthi) next / renewed worldly existence / manifest (puna-bhavo).*

**Commentary:** **Exhaustion of Kamma:** Early Pāḷi Canon vs. Jainism

The concept of \"exhausting kamma\" exists in both early Pāḷi Canon and Jainism, resting on a shared understanding that past karmic imprints must be overcome to end worldly manifestation (*loka*). While both paths utilize spiritual exertion (*tapas*), they frame the nature of kamma and the process of final release through distinct termimology.

**Comparative Framework: Exhaustion of Kamma**

**Nature of Kamma**

- **Jain (*Sūtrakṛtāṅga*, *Tattvārtha*):** Karmic matter (*paudgalika-karma*) represents a subtle, non-physical material dimension that forms an imprint upon the living soul (*jīva* / *appā*).

- **Early Pāḷi Canonical (*Suttas*):** Volitional intention (*cetanā*) and mental constructions (*saṅkhāra*) leave karmic seeds or imprints upon the *citta*.

**Method of Exhaustion**

- **Jain (*Sūtrakṛtāṅga*, *Tattvārtha*):** *tapas*: Burning off and consuming old accumulated karmic imprints through intense spiritual energy (*nijjarā*) while shutting down incoming karmic inflow (*saṁvara*).

- **Early Pāḷi Canonical (*Suttas*):** *tapas* & *paññā*: Applying *tapas* to burn away the underlying root fires---*rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---so that accumulated karmic seeds lose their potency (*vipāka*) and cease to yield renewed worldly existence (*punabbhava*).

**Key Mechanism**

- **Jain (*Sūtrakṛtāṅga*, *Tattvārtha*):** Purifying and exhausting the imprints bound to the conscious self (*jīva* / *appā*) through spiritual exertion, severing its bondage to physical manifestation.

- **Early Pāḷi Canonical (*Suttas*):** Depriving the karmic seeds on the *citta* of their craving (*taṇhā*) and ignorance (*avijjā*), rendering them completely barren so no future body (*geha*) or worldly manifestation can be constructed.

**Final Realization & Release**

- **Jain (*Sūtrakṛtāṅga*, *Tattvārtha*):** Complete liberation (*mokṣa*) of the *jīva*, transcending all physical boundaries to reside in the realm of liberated souls (*siddhasilā*).

- **Early Pāḷi Canonical (*Suttas*):** Complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*; upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant, the purified *citta* transcend into *Nibbāna-dhātu*.

**Textual Evidence in the Pāḷi Canon**

While the Early Pāḷi Suttas clarify how spiritual exertion (*tapas*) operates, they explicitly describe the exhaustion and rendering ineffective of past kamma once the roots of defilement are severed.

**1). The Seed Metaphor: Ratanasutta (Sutta Nipāta 235 / Khuddakapāṭha):** *Khīṇaṁ purāṇaṁ navaṁ natthi sambhavaṁ,*

*Virattācittā\'āyatike bhavasmiṁ; Te khīṇabījā aviṛūḷhichandā,*

*Nibbanti dhīrā yathāyaṁ pradīpo.*

The old (*purāṇaṁ*) \[kamma\] is exhausted (*khīṇaṁ*), there is no (*natthi*) new (*navaṁ*) arising (*sambhavaṁ*); Their mind-hearts (*citta*) are detached (*virattācittā*) from future (*āyatike*) worldly existence / manifest (*bhavasmiṁ*); They (*te*) whose karmic seeds (*bīja*) are exhausted (*khīṇabījā*), with no growth (*aviṛūḷhi*) of desire (*chandā*); The wise (*dhīrā*) are unbound (*nibbanti*), just as (*yathā*) this (*ayaṁ*) lamp (*pradīpo*).

**2). Rendering Kamma Ineffective: Aṅguttara Nikāya 3.33 (Nidāna Sutta)**

In this discourse, the Buddha explains how past actions lose their capacity to generate rebirth when the roots (*nidāna*) are destroyed: \"Bhikkhus, actions (*kamma*) performed out of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---when *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* are abandoned, uprooted like a palm tree with its base cut off, made non-existent, incapable of future arising---that kamma is discarded, exhausted, and rendered powerless to yield rebirth.\"

The Sutta provides a precise agricultural simile: \"Just as seeds that are unbroken, unrotted, unweathered, sound, and lying in fertile soil would grow if watered---yet if a person burns those seeds with fire (*tapas*), reduces them to ashes, and scatters the ashes in a strong wind, those seeds are destroyed at the root, rendered incapable of ever sprouting again.\"

**3). Depriving Kamma of Rebirth Potential: Devadaha Sutta (MN 101)**

Addressing Jain practitioners (*Nigaṇṭhas*), the Sutta contrasts the emphasis on *tapas* with the complete burning off of root conditions:

**Jain View:** \"By burning off past kamma through primordial force (*tapasā*) and preventing new kamma, old kamma is exhausted (*nijjiṇṇa*).\"\[comment: this is actually the same as Gautama Buddha's teaching on *Nibbida* -- preventing new kamma, and *Viraga* - old kamma be exhausted, the two steps before *Vimmuti*: liberated from the world\].

**Early Pāḷi Canon:** When a practitioner cuts off craving (*taṇhā*) and ignorance (*avijjā*) through *tapas* and *paññā*, past kamma cannot find a footing to produce a future body (*geha*), and becomes *ahosi-kamma* (inoperative) because the fuel that allows kamma to ripen into renewed worldly existence (*punabbhava*) has been completely eradicated.

In early Pāḷi literature, when a verse states *sabbametaṁ parikkhīṇaṁ* (\"all this kamma is completely exhausted\"), it means that by destroying the root causes---*rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---through the application of *tapas* and *paññā*, the karmic momentum on the *citta* is rendered barren. Without craving (*taṇhā*) to supply fuel and without ignorance (*avijjā*) to provide context, past *kamma* can no longer construct a future physical body. The accumulated weight of past kamma is thereby *parikkhīṇaṁ*---thoroughly exhausted and deprived of any power to cause renewed manifestation (*punabbhava*). Upon the final dissolution of the body, the purified *citta* leaving behind the worldly realms (*loka*) for *Nibbāna-dhātu*.

#### 1.81. Samitigutta

**Yaṁ mayā pakataṁ pāpaṁ, pubbe aññāsu jātisu;\
Idheva taṁ vedanīyaṁ, vatthu aññaṁ na vijjatī ti.**

*Whatever (yaṁ) harmful kamma (pāpaṁ) has been committed (pakataṁ) by me (mayā), in the past (pubbe) in other (aññāsu) lifetimes (jātisu); Right here (idheva) that (taṁ) must be experienced (vedanīyaṁ); another (aññaṁ) basis (vatthu) \[for future rebirth\] does not (na) exist (vijjatī ti).*

**Commentary**: **Vedanīyaṁ:** Future passive participle / gerundive (*vedeti* + *-anīya*). \"Must be experienced/felt.\"

This directly demonstrates the shared Śramaṇa ethos underlying both early Jainism (*Sūtrakṛtāṅga*) and the Early Pāḷi Canon. When stripped of modern academic divisions, the core logic expressed in this verse reveals a unified Śramaṇa framework regarding the mechanics of liberation:

**Inescapable Exhaustion:** Both traditions hold that accumulated karmic imprints must be experienced and exhausted (*parikkhīṇaṁ*).

**The Present Body as the Arena:** The verse explicitly asserts that the current physical vessel (*idheva*) serves as the final ground where these past imprints ripen and dissolve, precisely because the path of *tapas* has severed the generation of new kamma.

**Destruction of the Substrate (*Vatthu*):** Once the current physical body breaks down, because there is no remaining substrate (*vatthu*) or fuel of craving (*taṇhā*) left to construct a new house/body (*geha*), no further worldly existence / manifest/ becoming (*punabbhava*) can occur.

In both early Jain and early Buddhist contexts, the practitioner relies on the same *tapas* to burn away defilements, face the ripening of past kamma without generating new attachment, and allow the living soul or mind (*jīva* / *appā* / *attā* / *citta*) to be completely freed and unbound (*nibbuto*). The verse simply reflects that single, uncompromising Śramaṇa principle of total karmic purification.

#### 1.82. Kassapa

**Yena yena subhikkhāni, sivāni abhayāni ca;\
Tena puttaka gacchassu, mā sokāpahato bhava ti.**

*Wherever (yena yena) places are bountiful in alms (subhikkhāni), safe / peaceful / auspicious (sivāni), and (ca) free from fear (abhayāni); There (tena), O son (puttaka), go (gacchassu)! Do not (mā) be one (bhava) struck down by grief (sokāpahato).*

#### 1.83. Sīha

**Sīhappamatto vihara, rattindivamatandito;\
Bhāvehi kusalaṁ dhammaṁ, jaha sīghaṁ samussayan ti.**

*Like a lion (sīha), dwell (vihara) zealous (appamatto), night and day (rattindivaṁ) untiring (atandito); Develop (bhāvehi) wholesome (kusalaṁ) dhamma (dhammaṁ), abandon (jaha) swiftly (sīghaṁ) the physical body (samussayaṁ) \[immediately severing the mental attachment and psychological ownership over the body\].*

**Commentary**: The popular image of the male lion as a lazy king who just lets the lionesses do all the work is actually a big myth---field biology shows male lions are active, brutal hunters in their own right.

While lionesses do most of the open-plains daytime hunting for the pride, male lions are far from free-riders:

**Nighttime Apex Hunters:** Male lions specialize in nocturnal hunting in dense vegetation or bush where their heavy, muscular build gives them a massive advantage. They single-handedly target massive, dangerous prey that lionesses usually avoid, such as full-grown buffalo, giraffes, and young hippos.

**Brutal Territorial Defense:** The primary role of the male is pride protection. They patrol territory borders spanning dozens of square miles, mark boundaries, and engage in violent, often fatal fights with nomadic male coalition rivals and hyena clans to protect the pride\'s cubs and territory.

**Dominance at the Kill:** When a male joins a pride kill, his immediate takeover is a structural hierarchy that ensures the strongest defender of the territory remains fully nourished and battle-ready.

In ancient Śramaṇa literature, the lion (*sīha*) is precisely for its raw power, absolute fearlessness (*abhaya*), untamed independence, and unyielding posture when facing danger.

#### 1.84. Nīta

**Sabbarattiṁ supitvāna, divā saṅgaṇike rato;\
Kudāssu nāma dummedho, dukkhassantaṁ karissatī ti.**

*Having slept (supitvāna) all night long (sabbarattiṁ), delighting (rato) in crowd association / social chatter and worldly company / social entanglement (saṅgaṇike) by day (divā); When indeed (kudāssu nāma) will such foolish one (dummedho) make (karissati) an end (antaṁ) to dukkha (dukkhassa ti)?*

**Commentary**: In the Early Pāḷi Canon, a *bhikkhu* / *dhīra* treats sleep not as a luxury or passive indulgence, but as a carefully controlled physical necessity to maintain the body while keeping the mind (*citta*) in a state of continuous vigilance (*appamāda*). The Suttas explicitly lay out the ideal sleep routine, the exact time allocated, and how specialized ascetic practices (*dhutaṅga*) handle sleep.

**(1). The Middle Watch Practice**

The canonical standard for a practitioner\'s daily and nightly routine is detailed across numerous discourses (such as the *Aṅguttara Nikāya* and *Majjhima Nikāya*, e.g., MN 107 *Gaṇakamoggallāna Sutta*). The night is divided into **three watches** (*yāma*):

**First Watch of the Night (Approx. 6:00 PM -- 10:00 PM):** The practitioner purifies the mind (*citta*) from obstructive states while walking up and down (*caṅkama*) and sitting (*nisajjā*).

**Middle Watch of the Night (Approx. 10:00 PM -- 2:00 AM):** The practitioner sleeps in the **Lion's Posture** (*sīhaseyya*): lying on the right side, placing one foot over the other, mindful and fully aware (*sato sampajāno*), having noted in mind the exact time to wake up (*uṭṭhānasaññaṁ manasi karitvā*).

**Manner of Mind:** Even during these 4 hours, sleep is not a state of dull sluggishness (*thīna-middha*). By adopting the lion\'s posture with mindfulness and a predetermined time to rise, the practitioner ensures the mind does not sink into deep, unwholesome stupor (*sabbarattiṁ supitvāna*), keeping the spiritual energy (*tapas*) active.

**Last Watch of the Night (Approx. 2:00 AM -- 6:00 AM):** The practitioner rises, resumes walking and sitting meditation, and continues purifying the mind of defilements (*rāga, dosa, moha*).

**(2). Do Dhutaṅga Practitioners (Dhutaṅgaers) Sleep?**

Practitioners who undertake *dhutaṅga* practices, especially those who adopt the specific **Nesajjik\'aṅga** (the sitter\'s practice) refrain from **lying down**.

Both the Vedic/Yogic and Jain traditions directly affirm that intense *samādhi*, *tapas*, and the attainment of higher spiritual energy alter physical bodily needs, explicitly reducing or entirely transcending the need for sleep (*nidrā*).

The rationale in the *Nesajjik\'aṅga* (the sitter\'s practice) directly mirrors these ancient Indian traditions. When a practitioner generates *samādhi* and internal spiritual energy through sustained practice, the coarse physical need for lying down to rest disappears. The body remains sustained, energized, and purified by the refined force of meditative absorption rather than tamasic slumber.

**a. Jain Traditions & Canonical Texts**

In Jainism, ordinary sleep (*nidrā*) is classified as a sub-type of **Darśanāvaraṇīya Karma** (the karma that obscures perception). Sleep is explicitly viewed as an impurity of the *jīva* (soul) that causes dullness and obstructs spiritual awareness. The perfected sage (*kavalī* or high-level *sādhaka*) conquers sleep through *tapas* and deep absorption, reaching a state where no physical sleep is required.

**The *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* (Book I):** Describing the strict life of Mahāvīra during his years of severe *tapas*, the text notes that he transcended the natural limits of the body. It records that he remained vigilant day and night, standing or sitting in *dhyāna*, completely abandoning ordinary sleep.

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra 1.5.1.3 (Describing Mahāvīra's Awakening):** *Niyayaṁ jāgaraṁ samāhiṁ, niddaṁ pi teṇa jitā; Rattindivaṁ pi appamatto, jhānaṁ ca samāhiṁ ca anuvaṭṭide.* \"Established in *samādhi*, by him sleep was completely conquered (*niddaṁ pi teṇa jitā*). Night and day vigilant, he remained continuously absorbed in *jhāna* and *samādhi*.\"

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 28.11:** *Niddā-tanda-parissaya-ji-o, samāhi-jhoṇa-samappio; Suhada-muttā-bhāva-gatā, ṇo vi ya niddaṁ uveli tā.*

\"Having conquered sleep (*niddā*) and drowsiness (*tanda*), fully absorbed in *samādhi* and *dhyāna*, established in the liberated nature of the self (*appā*), such a one does not undergo sleep.\"

**The Digambara Tradition (*Tattvārtha Sūtra* & *Samayasāra*):** When an ascetic sheds karmic bondage through intense *dhyāna* and ascends the *guṇasthānas* (stages of spiritual development), physical compulsions diminish. Upon attaining **Kevala Jñāna** (omniscience/supreme *samādhi*), a *Kevalin* transcends physical limitations: they do not experience hunger, thirst, or sleep, remaining constantly absorbed in the supreme nature of the soul.

In Jain ascetic discipline, posture controls like *kāyotsarga* (standing or sitting motionless) are used to burn karma (*nirjarā*) through *kāyakleśa* (austere control of the physical body). Refraining from lying down prevents the karmic influx of sloth (*pramāda*) and *nidrā*.

**b. Vedic, Upanishadic, and Yogic Texts**

In the Vedic and Yogic traditions, sleep is governed by **Tamas** (the quality of inertia, darkness, and heaviness). *Samādhi* generates intense **Sattwa** (purity/clarity) and spiritual power of **Tapas** or **Ojas**, which neutralizes *Tamas* and renders ordinary sleep unnecessary.

**The *Pātañjala Yoga Sūtras*:** In Sūtra 1.10 (*\'abhāva-pratyayālambanā vṛttir nidrā\'*), Patanjali defines sleep (*nidrā*) merely as a mental modification (*vṛtti*) based on the cognition of absence or inertia. Because *samādhi* requires the complete cessation of all *vṛttis* (*\'yogaś citta-vṛtti-nirodhaḥ\'*), a practitioner who masters high concentration operates beyond the *nidrā-vṛtti*.

In Chapter 3 (*Vibhūti Pāda*), the text explains that through *saṃyama* (mastery of concentration and *samādhi*) over specific *prāṇic* centers, the Yogi gains complete mastery over physical exhaustion and bodily limits, acquiring *siddhis* (supranormal capabilities).

**The Upanishads & Advaita Texts:**

Texts such as the *Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad* map human consciousness across four realms: *Jāgrat* (waking), *Svapna* (dreaming), *Suṣupti* (deep sleep), and **Turīya** (the Fourth---pure transcendent consciousness). Deep *samādhi* enters or reflects *Turīya*. A sage established in *Turīya* achieves what is termed **Nidrā-less Sleep** or **Jāgrat-Suṣupti** (waking sleep), where the physical body is revitalized directly by *prāṇa* and pure awareness without needing tamasic physical sleep.

**The *Bhagavad Gītā* (Chapter 6, Verse 17):**

The text highlights that for one who has mastered Yoga (*yuktāswapnāva-bodhasya*), sleep and waking become fully regulated and transformed through spiritual absorption.

**Yogatattvopaniṣad 109--110 (Conquest of Sleep via *Samādhi* Power):** *Nidrā tandra-bhayaṁ nāsti samādhi-sthasya yoginaḥ; Prāṇa-siddhiṁsamāpnoti kevalaṁ dhāraṇābhyāsa-yogataḥ.* \"For the Yogi established in *samādhi*, there is no sleep (*nidrā*), no drowsiness (*tandrā*), and no fear; by the power of sustained concentration (*dhāraṇā*), he attains complete mastery over life-force (*prāṇa-siddhi*).\"

**Hatha Yoga Pradipika 4.49 (Absorption Beyond Waking & Sleep):** *Na jāgarti na svapati cittaṁ yasya vinirmalam; Samādhau vartamānasya kevalaṁ mukta eva saḥ.* \"He whose *citta* is completely purified neither wakes \[in the worldly sense\] nor sleeps (*na svapati*). Remaining purely in *samādhi*, he is truly liberated.\"

**Siva Samhita 3.56 (Elimination of Bodily Fatigue and Sleep):** *Sveda-kampa-tathā-nidrā-tṛṣṇā-dahā-bhavanti na; Abhyāsa-balataḥ samyak yoginaḥ siddha-rūpiṇaḥ.* \"Sweat, trembling, sleep (*nidrā*), thirst, and physical burning no longer occur for the Yogi who has attained perfection (*siddha*) through the power of practice.\"

#### 1.85. Sunāga

**Cittanimittassa kovido, Pavivekarasaṁ vijāniya;\
Jhāyaṁ nipako patissato, Adhigaccheyya sukhaṁ nirāmisan ti.**

*Skilled (kovido) in the sign (nimittassa) of the mind (citta), having known (vijāniya) the taste (rasaṁ) of the supreme seclusion (paviveka); absorbed in jhāna (jhāyaṁ), discerning / prudent (nipako), established in mindfulness (patissato); He would attain (adhigaccheyya) unworldly / free from worldly bait / transcendent (nirāmisaṁ) bliss (sukhaṁ).*

**Commentary**: ***citta-nimitta**,* ***samādhi-nimitta***

*Nimitta* (Sanskrit: *nimitta* / Prakrit: *nimitta* or *ṇimitta*) serves across early Śramaṇa, Upanishadic, and Yogic traditions as the specific internal marker, mental image, or focus point utilized to transition the mind (*citta* / *jīva*) into deep *jhāna* / *dhyāna* / *samādhi*. By using the ***citta-nimitta*** (or ***samādhi-nimitta***), the practitioner withdraws attention from physical phenomena, cuts off worldly engagement, and locks the mind into *jhāna* / *dhyāna*.

**1). Pāḷi Canon: The Mark of Concentration for *Jhāna***

In the Early Pāḷi Suttas, the *nimitta* represents the stabilized mental image or mark of focus that arises as sensory input fades, allowing the practitioner to enter the *jhānas*.

**AN 5.28 (Samādhisaṅkhāra Sutta / Pañcaṅgika Sutta):** Describes five factors of noble right concentration. The fifth factor specifically involves mastering the *nimitta* of reviewing (*paccavekkhaṇā-nimitta*) or the *samādhi-nimitta*, stating that the *samādhi-nimitta* must be thoroughly grasped (*sugahitaṁ*), well-attended to (*sumanasikataṁ*), well-sustained (*supadhāritaṁ*), and well-penetrated by wisdom (*supaṭividdhaṁ*).

"Again further, O bhikkhus, for a bhikkhu (puna caparaṁ, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno), the samādhi-nimitta is thoroughly grasped (sugahitaṁ hoti), well-attended to (sumanasikataṁ), well-sustained (supadhāritaṁ), well-penetrated with wisdom (supaṭividdhaṁ paññāya). Just as, O bhikkhus, one person might review another (seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, aññataro aññataraṁ avekkheyya), standing, he might review one sitting (ṭhito vā nisinnaṁ avekkheyya), or sitting, he might review one lying down (nisinno vā nipannaṁ avekkheyya); even so, O bhikkhus, for a bhikkhu (evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno), the samādhi-nimitta is thoroughly grasped (sugahitaṁ hoti), well-attended to (sumanasikataṁ), well-sustained (supadhāritaṁ), well-penetrated with wisdom (supaṭividdhaṁ paññāya)."

**AN 9.35 (Gāvī Sutta):** Details how an inexperienced meditator attempts to enter the first jhāna without properly securing, mastering, and attending to the *nimitta* (the internal theme/basis of concentration), resulting in failing to attain the first jhāna and losing the foundation altogether.

"here a certain bhikkhu (idh\'ekacco bhikkhu) foolish, inexperienced, unskilful with fields (bālo avyatto akhettaññū), unskilful with entering and abiding in the first jhāna (akusalo paṭhamassa jhānassa samāpattiyā), secluded from sensual pleasures (vivicceva kāmehi), secluded from unwholesome states (vivicca akusalehi dhammehi), enters and abides in the first jhāna (paṭhamaṁ jhānaṁ upasampajja viharati), with *vitakka* and *vicāra* (savitakkaṁ savicāraṁ), born of seclusion (vivekajaṁ), filled with *pīti* and *sukha*. He does not attend to that *nimitta* (so taṁ nimittaṁ na āsevati), does not develop it (na bhāveti), does not make much of it (na bahulīkaroti), does not thoroughly establish it (na supaṭiṭṭhitaṁ karoti)."

**AN 3.100 (*Paṁsudhovaka Sutta*):** "When these coarse impurities are abandoned, eradicated, and made non-existent (tassa te olārikā upakkilesā pahīnā honti vyanti-katā anabhāva-katā), Subtle impurities still remain---such as thoughts about family, country, or reputation (atha sukkhamā upakkilesā avasiṭṭhā honti jñātitvitakkā janapadavitakkā anavaññattipatisaṁyuttā vitakkā). That, too, a dedicated practitioner abandons, dispels, eradicates, and makes non-existent (taṁ enam bhikkhu pajahati vinodeti vyanti-karoti anabhāvaṁ karoti).

When those are abandoned, there remain only thoughts on wholesomeness (tasmiṁ pahīne atha vitakkāvasiṭṭhā honti dhammapatisaṁyuttā). That samādhi is not yet peaceful, nor refined, nor has it attained full tranquility (so hoti samādhi na ceva santo na ca paṇīto na ca paṭipassaddhaladdho), It is maintained by forceful suppression (na ca ekodibhāvāpanno sasankhāraniggayhavāritagato).

But there comes a time when the mind settles internally, grows still, becomes unified, and stabilizes (hoti so samayo yaṁ taṁ cittaṁ ajjhattāṁ eva santiṭṭhati sannisīdati ekodi hoti samādhiyati). That samādhi is then peaceful, refined, tranquil, and unified (so hoti samādhi santo paṇīto paṭipassaddhaladdho ekodibhāvāpanno), It is no longer maintained by forceful suppression (na ca sasankhāraniggayhavāritagato).

That nimitta of adhi-samādha which arises---there the mind settles (tassa yaṁ hoti adhisamādha-nimittaṁ, tasmiṁ cittaṁ saṇṭhāti), It stands still, becomes unified, and enters samādhi (sannisīdati ekodi hoti samādhiyati)."

The Buddha uses the refined gold analogy for the mind. When coarse impurities are removed, the practitioner focuses on the *adhisamādhi-nimitta* (the sign of higher *samādhi*) to enter jhana.

**2). Jain Canon: *ṇimitta* / *Alambaṇa* in *Dhyāna***

In early Jain texts (such as the *Sūtrakṛtāṅga*, *Ācārāṅga*, and later analytical works like the *Tattvārtha Sūtra*), the transition into *śukla-dhyāna* (pure, luminous absorption) requires a central focus point (*ālambaṇa* or *ṇimitta*).

**Ācārāṅga Sūtra (Book 1, Lecture 5):** Notes that the ascetic fixes the internal vision on a single inner mark (*ṇimitta*) to withdraw the *jīva* (soul) from the ten sensory doors. By fixing the mind on this internal point, the practitioner enters unbroken *jhāna*, preventing the influx of new karma.

"Seeing thus, the great muṇī (evaṁ passaṁ, mahāmuṇī) fixes the mind upon a single *ṇimitta* (ege ṇimitte cittaṁ dhārei). He withdraws the soul *jīva* from the ten doors of the senses (dasa-dārohiṁ jīvaṁ paṭisaṁharai); standing firm on that *ṇimitta* alone (tasmiṁ eva ṇimitte ṭhio), he lets no outer attachments enter (na bāhiram-āsaṁsaṁ āvajjai).

Restraining the channels of sensory contact (sotādīhiṁ saṁvuo), he prevents the influx of new karmic particles (asavaṁ nivārei). Unbroken in meditation, absorbed in unwavering jhāṇa (anichimiyāe jhāṇa-samāhīe), the muṇī becomes free from karmic ties (muṇī kamma-vippamukko hohi\'ti).

**Tattvārtha Sūtra 9.27--9.29 (Acharya Umāsvāti):** Details how *Dhyāna* requires fixing thought on a single *ālambaṇa* (object/sign) for up to an *antarmuhūrta* (under 48 minutes). This steady focus on the *ṇimitta* serves as the bridge to burn karmic imprints (*nirjarā*) and achieve total spiritual purification.

**"**Concentration of thought on a single object of focus (uttama-saṁhana-nasyai\'kāgracintānirodho dhyānām ā-antarmuhūrtāt). For a person of the best physical constitution, the stoppage of thought on a single support/mark (uttama-saṁhananasya ekāgra-cintā-nirodho) is dhyāna, lasting up to an antarmuhūrta (ā-antarmuhūrtāt).

The first two types of meditation are causes of karmic bond (pare hetū bandhasya). The former two---distressed and wrathful meditation---are the cause of bondage (pare hetū bandhasya).

The remaining two types lead to the shedding of karma and liberation (pare tu nirjarā-hetū). But the latter two---righteous and pure meditation---are the causes of karmic destruction/shedding (pare tu nirjarā-hetū)."

**3). Vedic & Yogic Texts: *Nimitta* in *Dhāraṇā* and *Dhyāna***

In the Upanishadic and Yogic streams, the *nimitta* refers to the internal luminous signs (*lakṣaṇa* / *nimitta*) that appear as *prāṇa* and *citta* draw inward during *dhāraṇā* (concentration), signaling the immediate entry into *dhyāna* and *samādhi*.

**Pātañjala Yoga Sūtras 1.35:** *Viṣayavatī vā pravṛttir utpannā manasaḥ sthiti-nibandhinī.*

\"Or, the manifestation of extraordinary sensory signs (*viṣayavatī pravṛttiḥ / nimitta*) holds the mind steady in absorption.\"

Patanjali notes that concentrating on an internal sign (such as inner light, sound, or a specific mental mark) stabilizes the *citta* and seals concentration.

While YS 1.35 itself uses *viṣayavatī pravṛttiḥ*, Vyāsa\'s primary Sanskrit commentary (*Bhāṣya*) directly introduces the exact word **nimitta** to define it: *Devatā-viṣayam vā saṁvit-samutpādena nimittaṁ karoti.*

*\"Or, by generating direct perception regarding a deva, it acts as a **sign (nimitta)**.\"*

**\...Etayā dhāraṇā-kṛtāyām saṁśaya-viparyayo nivartate, saṁśaya-nivṛttau ca samādhi-bhūmāv eṣa nimittī-bhavati.**

*\"When this* **dhāraṇā** *(concentration) is performed, doubt and misconception cease; upon the cessation of doubt, this becomes the **direct sign (nimittī-bhavati)** for the stages of samādhi.\"*

Vyāsa defines the internal sensory perception (*pravṛtti*) as a **nimitta**---a diagnostic mark that anchors the mind and confirms entry into *samādhi*.

**Maitrī Upaniṣad 6.20**

This Upaniṣad explicitly uses the word **nimitta** for the internal vision of light during inner practice: *Atha ya eṣo\'ntar-hṛdaya-jyotir-aṇur\... tasya nimittāny upalabhante. \"Now, regarding this subtle light within the heart\... its **nimittās** are perceived.\"*

Describes the internal perception of the subtle light within the heart (*hṛdaya-jyotis*) and explicitly terms these emerging internal phenomena as its *nimittāni* (signs/marks).

**Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 2.11 (Signs Preceding Absorption):** *Nīhāra-dhūmārka-anilānalānāṁ khadyota-vidyut-sphaṭika-śaśīnām;* *Etāni rūpāṇi puraḥsarāṇi brahmhaṇy abhivyakti-karāṇi yogē.*

\"Mist, smoke, the sun, wind, and fire (nīhāra-dhūma-arka-anila-analānām); Fireflies, lightning, crystal, and the moon (khadyota-vidyut-sphaṭika-śaśīnām)---These visual forms are the precursors (etāni rūpāṇi puraḥsarāṇi), Manifesting the reality of Brahman in yoga practice (brahmaṇy abhivyakti-karāṇi yoge).\"

The text explicitly identifies these internal visual/mental signs as indicators that *samādhi* is near.

#### 1.86. Nāgita

**Ito bahiddhā puthu aññavādinaṁ, Maggo na nibbānagamo yathā ayaṁ;\
Itissu saṅghaṁ bhagavānusāsati, Satthā sayaṁ pāṇitaleva dassayan ti.**

*Outside of this teaching, among the many teachers of other doctrines (ito bahiddhā puthu aññavādinaṁ), There is no path leading to Nibbāna like this one (maggo na nibbānagamo yathā ayaṁ); Thus the bhagava instructs the Saṅgha (itissu saṅghaṁ bhagavānusāsati), The Teacher himself showing it clearly, as if holding it in the palm of his hand (satthā sayaṁ pāṇitaleva dassayan).*

**Commentary**: Contrast of Nibbāna: Simple Reality vs. Modern Confusion

The simile of showing the path to Nibbāna **"as if holding it in the palm of his hand"** (*pāṇitaleva dassayaṁ*) directly addresses the modern landscape of confusion, speculative mythology, and convoluted interpretations.

Over the centuries, the core teaching of the path to Nibbāna has often been obscured by mythical embellishments, ritualistic additions, and overly complex scholastic systems that push liberation into a distant, near-impossible realm.

**The Classical Reality (*Pāṇitaleva*):** The simile of holding an object in the palm of one\'s hand signifies **absolute directness, simplicity, and immediate accessibility**. A fruit placed in the palm requires no speculation, complex dogma, or esoteric initiation to be seen---it is simply observed as it is. The ancient path to Nibbāna is equally direct: it is outside the universe through the Rupa realm.

The uniqueness of the path lies precisely in its unvarnished directness. It simply relies on the systematic cessation of worldly craving and clinging, and the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

Teaching the path plainly and directly, as I am doing here, reflects the exact methodology of Gautama Buddha, our Teacher (*Satthā*) did. So the practitioner can walk the ancient path to Nibbāna with total clarity.

#### 1.87. Paviṭṭha

**Khandhā diṭṭhā yathābhūtaṁ, bhavā sabbe padālitā;\
Vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro, natthi dāni punabbhavo ti.**

*The aggregates (khandhā) are seen (diṭṭhā) as they truly are (yathābhūtaṁ), all (sabbe) worldly existences (bhavā) are shattered (padālitā); the birth and saṁsāra (jātisaṁsāro) is completely exhausted (vikkhīṇo), now (dāni) there is no (natthi) further worldly existence / manifest / becoming (punabbhavo).*

**Commentary: The Nature of Aggregation and Universal Conditioning**

**Inherent Inscription of Decay:** The five aggregates (*khandhā*) are conditioned, from the very moment of their aggregation, universal laws---most notably the laws of decay and death---are woven directly into their structural existence. This is the core mechanism of the conditioning process: anything formed by aggregation is inherently bound, dictated, and subjected to the laws of the universe (*loka*).

**Direct Comprehension (*Yathābhūtaṁ*):** Seeing the aggregates \"as they truly are\" means recognizing their conditioned, impermanent nature triggers the internal turning away---allowing the *citta* to cease identifying with what is bound to decay.

**The Origin of Aggregation:** Aggregates do not coalesce out of absolute nothingness; they are aggregated from primary cosmic substrates, subtle elements of existence, and primordial consciousness.

**The Process of Un-aggregating (*Padālitā*):** To \"shatter\" all worldly existences / manifest means reversing the aggregation process at its root. Through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the binding force that pulls the soul-spirit *attā* into the worldly manifest. When the conditioned aggregates are completely relinquished, the underlying purified, unconditioned essence (*attā*) is fully disentangled from the cosmic web, stepping beyond the reach of the universal laws.

**Exhaustion of the Universal Loop:** The cycle of birth and *saṁsāra* (*jātisaṁsāra*) is maintained solely as long as the soul-spirit *attā* remains misidentified with its aggregated wrappers. Once the conditioned nature of the five aggregates is seen with unclouded vision, the fuel driving involuntary reincarnation in the universe is thoroughly exhausted (*vikkhīṇo*).

**Transcendence Beyond the Universe:** \"Now there is no further worldly existence / manifest\" (*natthi dāni punabbhavo*). With the five aggregates abandoned, the liberated, purified spirit is no longer bound by the laws of the universe. It exits the cycle of worldly birth and *saṁsāra* entirely, achieving final release into the deathless realm of Nibbāna (*Nibbāna-dhātu*).

#### 1.88. Ajjuna

**Asakkhiṁ vata attānaṁ, uddhātuṁ udakā thalaṁ,\
Vuyhamāno mahogh eva, saccāni paṭivijjhahan ti.**

*I was truly able (asakkhiṁ vata) to rescue my soul-spirit (attānaṁ), to raise (uddhātuṁ) from the water / the flood/ocean of saṃsāra (udakā) onto dry land / the firm, unshakeable ground, a direct epithet for Nibbāna-dhātu) (thalaṁ), as if (eva) being swept away (vuyhamāno) by a great flood (mahogheva); by fully realizing (paṭivijjhahan) the Truths (saccāni).*

#### 1.89. Devasabha (1st)

**Uttiṇṇā paṅkapalipā, pātālā parivajjitā;\
Mutto oghā ca ganthā ca, sabbe mānā visaṁhatā ti.**

*Crossing over (uttiṇṇā) the mire and the swamp (paṅkapalipā), avoiding (parivajjitā) the bottomless abyss (pātālā); freed (mutto) from the flood (oghā) and from the ties (ganthā), all (sabbe) conceits (mānā) are completely destroyed (visaṁhatā).*

#### 1.90. Sāmidattā

**Pañcakkhandhā pariññātā, tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā;\
Vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro, natthi dāni punabbhavo ti.**

*The five aggregates (pañcakkhandhā) are fully understood (pariññātā), they remain (for the remainder of the physical lifespan) (tiṭṭhanti) with their roots severed (chinnamūlakā); the birth and saṁsāra (jātisaṁsāro) is completely exhausted (vikkhīṇo), now (dāni) there is no (natthi) further worldly existence / manifest (puna-bhavo).*

#### 1.91. Paripuṇṇaka

**Na tathā mataṁ satarasaṁ, Sudhannaṁ yaṁ mayajja paribhuttaṁ;\
Aparimitadassinā gotamena, Buddhena desito dhammo ti.**

*Not (na) that (tathā) delicious (mataṁ) possessing a hundred flavors (satarasaṁ), is fine food (sudhannaṁ) which (yaṁ) was consumed (paribhuttaṁ) by us today (mayajja); as the Dhamma taught (desito dhammo) by Gautama (gotamena), the Buddha (buddhena) of limitless vision (aparimitadassinā).*

#### 1.92. Vijaya

**Yassāsavā parikkhīṇā, āhāre ca anissito;\
Suññato animitto ca, vimokkho yassa gocaro;\
Ākāseva sakuntānaṁ, padaṁ tassa durannayan ti.**

Whose defilements (yassāsavā) are completely destroyed (parikkhīṇā), and who is not dependent (anissito) on \[worldly\] food (āhāre); whose realm (yassa gocaro) is liberation (vimokkho) through emptiness (suññato) and the signless (animitto); like the track (padaṁ) of birds (sakuntānaṁ) in the sky (ākāseva), his (tassa) is hard to follow (durannayaṁ).

**Commentary**: **Later Addition and Textual Corruption**

This verse is a later addition to the Pali Canon and presents corrupted concepts and false claims of liberation path. It contradicts the original, direct teaching of Gautama Buddha and should be set aside.

**1). \"Emptiness\" and \"The Signless\" are Later Additions:** The insertion of *suññato* (emptiness) and *animitto* (the signless) in this verse is a later edit and is wrong.

**Extremely Rare in Main Nikāyas:** Among the more than 9,000 suttas in the main Nikāyas, emptiness (*suññatā*) and the signless (*animitta*) are barely mentioned as paths to liberation.

**Distorted Meaning:** In the few suttas where emptiness is mentioned (such as MN 121), it only means something very simple and practical: the mind being empty of the village, the town, or specific external distractions during concentration. It has nothing to do with the later Mahāyāna concept that denies the existence of the human nature, essence, or soul (*attā*).

**Scholastic Insertion:** This verse inserts these abstract categories to fit later commentarial theories. Readers can safely ignore this verse entirely, and possibly the verses after this.

**2). Claiming the Path is \"Hard to Follow\" is Completely Wrong**

The concluding line stating that the path of the liberated being is \"hard to follow\" is false and directly contradicts the Buddha\'s own words.

**The Path is Simple and Direct:** In the numerous suttas, the Buddha explicitly states that he teaches with an open palm (*pāṇitaleva*), showing the path clearly as if holding an object in the palm of his hand.

**Contradiction:** Calling the path \"hard to follow\" creates unnecessary confusion and hides the direct methodology. The path to Nibbāna is straightforward: seeing the five aggregates (*khandhā*) as worldly constructs bound by death, abandoning them, and letting the purified soul-spirit exit the universe, that's it.

#### 1.93. Eraka

**Dukkhā kāmā eraka, Na sukhā kāmā eraka;\
Yo kāme kāmayati, Dukkhaṁ so kāmayati eraka;\
Yo kāme na kāmayati, Dukkhaṁ so na kāmayati erakā ti.**

*Sexual pleasures (kāmā) are dukkha (dukkhā), O Eraka (eraka), Sexual pleasures are not (na) happiness (sukhā), O Eraka (eraka); whoever (yo) desires (kāmayati) Sexual pleasures (kāme), he (so) desires (kāmayati) dukkha (dukkhaṁ), O Eraka (eraka); whoever (yo) does not desire (na kāmayati) Sexual pleasures (kāme), he (so) does not desire (na kāmayati) dukkha (dukkhaṁ), O Eraka (erakā).*

#### 1.94. Mettaji

**Namo hi tassa bhagavato, sakyaputtassa sirīmato;\
Tenāyaṁ aggappattena, aggadhammo sudesito ti.**

*Homage (namo) indeed (hi) to him (tassa), the bhagavato, the glorious (sirīmato) son of the Sakyas (sakyaputtassa); by him (tena), who has reached the supreme state (aggappattena), this supreme Dhamma (aggadhammo) is clearly and well-taught (sudesito).*

#### 1.95. Cakkhupāla

**Andhohaṁ hatanettosmi, kantāraddhānapakkhando;\
Sayamānopi gacchissaṁ, na sahāyena pāpenā ti.**

*I am blind (andhohaṁ), my eyes are destroyed (hatanettosmi), traversing (pakkhando) a desolate wilderness path (kantāraddhāna); even crawling (sayamānopi) I will go forward (gacchissaṁ), not (na) with an evil companion (sahāyena pāpena).*

#### 1.96. Khaṇḍasumana

**Ekapupphaṁ cajitvāna, asīti vassakoṭiyo;\
Saggesu paricāretvā, sesakenamhi nibbuto ti.**

*Having offered (cajitvāna) a single flower (ekapupphaṁ), for eighty (asīti) ten-millions of years (vassakoṭiyo); having enjoyed bliss (paricāretvā) in heavens (saggesu), by the remainder \[of that merit\] (sesakena) I am now fully unbound (amhi nibbuto).*

#### 1.97. Tissa (2nd)

**Hitvā satapalaṁ kaṁsaṁ, Sovaṇṇaṁ satarājikaṁ;\
Aggahiṁ mattikāpattāṁ, Idaṁ dutiyābhisecanan ti.**

*Having abandoned (hitvā) a bronze bowl (kaṁsaṁ) worth a hundred pala (satapalaṁ), golden (sovaṇṇaṁ) and inlaid with a hundred lines (satarājikaṁ); I took up (aggahiṁ) an earthen alms bowl (mattikāpattāṁ), this (idaṁ) was my second coronation / royal consecration (dutiyābhisecanaṁ).*

#### 1.98. Abhaya

**Rūpaṁ disvā sati muṭṭhā, Piyaṁ nimittaṁ manasikaroto;\
Sārattācitto vedeti, Tañca ajjhosa tiṭṭhati;\
Tassa vaḍḍhanti āsavā, Bhavamūlopagāmino ti.**

*Having seen (disvā) a visual form (rūpaṁ) with mindfulness lost (sati muṭṭhā), attending to (manasikaroto) a pleasant (piyaṁ) sign (nimittaṁ); with an infatuated mind (sārattācitto) he experiences (vedeti), and having clung to that (taṁ ca ajjhosa), he abides (tiṭṭhati); for him (tassa) the defilements (āsavā) increase (vaḍḍhanti), leading to the root of becoming / manifest / worldly existence (bhavamūlopagāmino).*

#### 1.99. Uttiya (3rd)

**Saddaṁ sutvā sati muṭṭhā, Piyaṁ nimittaṁ manasikaroto;\
Sārattācitto vedeti, Tañca ajjhosa tiṭṭhati;\
Tassa vaḍḍhanti āsavā, Saṁsāraṁ upagāmino ti.**

*Having heard (sutvā) a sound (saddaṁ) with mindfulness lost (sati muṭṭhā), attending to (manasikaroto) a pleasant (piyaṁ) sign (nimittaṁ); with an infatuated mind (sārattācitto) he experiences (vedeti), and having clung to that (taṁ ca ajjhosa), he abides (tiṭṭhati); for him (tassa) the corruptions (āsavā) increase (vaḍḍhanti), leading toward saṃsāra (saṃsāraṁ upagāmino).*

#### 1.100. Devasabha (2nd)

Sammappadhānasampanno, Satipaṭṭhānagocaro;\
Vimuttikusumasañchanno, Parinibbissatyanāsavo ti.

*Endowed with right efforts (sammappadhānasampanno), having mindfulness as his domain (satipaṭṭhānagocaro); covered with the flowers of liberation (vimuttikusumasañchanno), he will attain final Parinibbāna without defilements (parinibbissatyanāsavo).*

#### 1.101. Belaṭṭhānika

**Hitvā gihittaṁ anavositatto, Mukhanaṅgalī odariko kusīto;\
Mahāvarāhova nivāpapuṭṭho, Punappunaṁ gabbhamupeti mando ti.**

*Having abandoned household life (hitvā gihittaṁ) but unfulfilled in detachment (anavositatto), with a face like a plow (mukhanaṅgalī), gluttonous (odariko), and lazy (kusīto); like a great boar (mahāvarāhova) fattened on fodder (nivāpapuṭṭho), the fool (mando) enters the womb (gabbhamupeti) again and again (punappunaṁ).*

#### 1.102. Setuccha

**Mānena vañcitāse, Saṅkhāresu saṅkilissamānāse;\
Lābhālābhena mathitā, Samādhiṁ nādhigacchantī ti.**

*Cheated by conceit (mānena vañcitāse), being defiled (saṅkilissamānāse) in conditioned phenomena (saṅkhāresu); crushed by gain and loss (lābhālābhena mathitā), they do not attain (nādhigacchanti) samādhi.*

#### 1.103. Bandhura

**Nāhaṁ etena atthiko, Sukhito dhammarasena tappito;\
Pitvā rasaggamuttamaṁ, Na ca kāhāmi visena santhavan ti.**

*I have no need for this (nāhaṁ etena atthiko), satisfied (sukhito) and nourished (tappito) by the taste of the Dhamma (dhammarasena); having drunk (pitvā) the supreme taste of the ultimate (rasaggamuttamaṁ), I will never (na ca kāhāmi) make friends with (santhavaṁ) poison (visena).*

#### 1.104. Khitaka

**Lahuko vata me kāyo, Phuṭṭho ca pītisukhena vipulena;\
Tūlamiva eritaṁ mālutena, Pilavatīva me kayo ti.**

*Truly my body is light (lahuko vata me kāyo), touched (phuṭṭho ca) by abundant joy and happiness (pītisukhena vipulena); like cotton blown (tūlamiva eritaṁ) by the wind (mālunā / mālutena), my body seems to float (pilavatīva me kāyo).*

#### 1.105. Malitavambha

**Ukkaṇṭhitopi na vase, ramamānopi pakkame;\
Na tvevānatthasaṁhitaṁ, vase vāsaṁ vicakkhaṇo ti.**

*Though dissatisfied, let him not dwell there (ukkaṇṭhitopi na vase), though finding delight, let him depart (ramamānopi pakkame); certainly not where it is unbeneficial (na tvevānatthasaṁhitaṁ), let the wise person (vicakkhaṇo) dwell in a residence (vase vāsaṁ).*

#### 1.106. Suhemanta

**Sataliṅgassa atthassa, satalakkhaṇadhārino;\
Ekaṅgadassī dummedho, satadassī ca paṇḍito ti.**

*Of a matter possessing a hundred characteristics (sataliṅgassa atthassa), bearing a hundred marks (satalakkhaṇadhārino); one who sees only a single aspect is a fool (ekaṅgadassī dummedho), while one who sees all a hundred aspects is a wise person (satadassī ca paṇḍito).*

#### 1.107. Dhammasava

**Pabbajiṁ tulayitvāna, agārasmānagāriyaṁ;\
Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Having weighed (tulayitvāna) the renunciation (pabbajiṁ) from the house (agārasmā) to the homeless life (anagāriyaṁ); the three knowledges (tisso vijjā) have been attained (anuppattā), the teaching (sāsanaṁ) of the Buddha (buddhassa) has been fulfilled (kataṁ).*

#### 1.108. Dhammasavapitu

**Sa vīsavassasatiko, pabbajiṁ anagāriyaṁ;\
Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Being 120 years old (sa vīsavassasatiko), I renounced household life (pabbajiṁ anagāriyaṁ); the three knowledges (tisso vijjā) have been attained (anuppattā), the teaching (sāsanaṁ) of the Buddha (buddhassa) has been fulfilled (kataṁ).*

#### 1.109. Saṅgharakkhita

**Na nūnāyaṁ paramahitānukampino, Rahogato anuvigaṇeti sāsanaṁ;\
Tathāhayaṁ viharati pākatindriyo, Migī yathā taruṇajātikā vane ti.**

*Indeed, he does not ponder in seclusion (na nūnāyaṁ\... rahogato anuvigaṇeti) the teaching (sāsanaṁ) of him who has absolute compassion for the highest good (paramahitānukampino); thus he dwells (tathāhayaṁ viharati) with his sense faculties un-guarded/exposed (pākatindriyo), like a young fawn (migī yathā taruṇajātikā) in the forest (vane).*

#### 1.110. Usabha (1st)

**Nagā nagaggesu susaṁvirūḷhā, Udaggameghena navena sittā;\
Vivekakāmassa araññasaññino, Janeti bhiyyo usabhassa kalyatan ti.**

*Trees growing securely (nagā\... susaṁvirūḷhā) on mountain peaks (nagaggesu), sprinkled (sittā) by a fresh (navena) rain-cloud (udaggameghena); for one desiring seclusion (vivekakāmassa) and perceiving the forest (araññasaññino), it produces (janeti) an increase (bhiyyo) of excellence and delight (kalyataṁ) for the noble bull/elephant (usabhassa).*

#### 1.111. Jenta

**Duppabbajjaṁ ve duradhivāsā gehā, Dhammo gambhīro duradhigamā bhogā; Kicchā vutti no itarītareneva, Yuttaṁ cintetuṁ satatamaniccatan ti.**

*Difficult is the renunciation (duppabbajjaṁ), and hard to dwell in are houses (duradhivāsā gehā); deep is the Dhamma (dhammo gambhīro), and wealth/enjoyments are hard to attain (duradhigamā bhogā); painful is the livelihood (kicchā vutti), and not by just anything (no itarītareneva); fitting it is to reflect constantly (yuttaṁ cintetuṁ satataṁ) on impermanence (aniccatanti).*

#### 1.112. Vacchagotta

**Tevijjohaṁ mahājhāyī, cetosamathakovido;\
Sadattho me anuppatto, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Possessing the three true knowledges (tevijjohaṁ), a great master of jhānas (mahājhāyī), skilled in concentration (cetosamathakovido); my own true goal has been attained (sadattho me anuppatto), the teaching (sāsanaṁ) of the Buddha (buddhassa) has been fulfilled (kataṁ).*

#### 1.113. Vanavaccha (2nd)

**Acchodikā puthusilā, gonaṅgulamigāyutā;\
Ambusevālasañchannā, te selā ramayanti man ti.**

*With clear water (acchodikā) and broad rocks (puthusilā), frequented by gibbons and deer (gonaṅgulamigāyutā); covered with moss and aquatic plants (ambusevālasañchannā), those mountains (te selā) delight my mind (ramayanti maṁ).*

#### 1.114. Adhimutta (1st)

**Kāyaduṭṭhullagaruno, hiyyamānamhi jīvite;\
Sarīrasukhagiddhassa, kuto samaṇasādhutā ti.**

*With a heavy and unrefined body (kāyaduṭṭhullagaruno), as life is waning/diminishing (hiyyamānamhi jīvite); of one greedy for physical comfort (sarīrasukhagiddhassa), where is the goodness for samaṇa (kuto samaṇasādhutā).*

#### 1.115. Mahānāma

**Esāvahiyyase pabbatena, Bahukuṭajasallakikena;\
Nesādakena girinā, Yasassinā paricchadenā ti.**

*This mountain is surrounded (esā\... paricchadenā) by trees and shrubs such as kuṭaja and sallaki (bahukuṭajasallakikena); by this hunter\'s mountain (nesādakena girinā), renowned and adorned (yasassinā).*

#### 1.116. Pārāpariya (1st)

**Chaphassāyatane hitvā, guttadvāro susaṁvuto;\
Aghamūlaṁ vamitvāna, patto me āsavakkhayo ti.**

*Having abandoned the six fields of contact (chaphassāyatane hitvā), with guarded doors and thoroughly restrained (guttadvāro susaṁvuto); having vomited out the root of misery (aghamūlaṁ vamitvāna), the destruction of defilements has been attained by me (patto me āsavakkhayo).*

#### 1.117. Yasa

**Suvilitto suvasano, sabbābharaṇabhūsito;\
Tisso vijjā ajjhagamiṁ, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan ti.**

*Well anointed, well dressed, adorned with all ornaments (suvilitto suvasano, sabbābharaṇabhūsito); the three knowledges have been attained by me (tisso vijjā ajjhagamiṁ), the teaching (sāsanaṁ) of the Buddha (buddhassa) has been fulfilled (kataṁ).*

#### 1.118 Kimbila (1st)

**Abhisattova nipatati, Vayo rūpaṁ aññamiva tatheva santaṁ;\
Tasseva sato avippavasato, Aññasseva sarāmi attānan ti.**

*Like a curse it falls (abhisattova nipatati), age and form (vayo rūpaṁ) as if different yet just the same (aññamiva tatheva santaṁ); while he himself remains unchanged and does not depart (tasseva sato avippavasato), I remember myself as entirely different (aññasseva sarāmi attānaṁ).*

#### 1.119. Vajjiputta (2nd)

**Rukkhamūlagahanaṁ pasakkiya, Nibbānaṁ hadayasmiṁ opiya;\
Jhāya gotama mā ca pamādo, Kiṁ te biḷibiḷikā karissatī ti.**

*Having resorted to the root of a tree or a dense forest (rukkhamūlagahanaṁ pasakkiya), placing Nibbāna within your heart (Nibbānaṁ hadayasmiṁ opiya); meditate, Gautama, let there be no zeallessness (jhāya gotama mā ca pamādo), what will hollow mockery do to you (kiṁ te biḷibiḷikā karissatī).*

#### 1.120. Isidattā
