**Pañcakkhandhā pariññātā, tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā;\
Dukkhakkhayo anuppatto, patto me āsavakkhayo ti.**

*The five aggregates are fully comprehended (pañcakkhandhā pariññātā), they stand with their roots severed (tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā); the destruction of dukkha has been attained (dukkakkhayo anuppatto), the destruction of defilements has been attained by me (patto me āsavakkhayo).*

## PART II: Therīgāthā: Verses of the Elder Bhikkhunis

Step into the living words of the Elder Bhikkhunis.

The **Therīgāthā**---the *Verses of the Elder Bhikkhunis*---preserves the voices of women who entered the Bhikkhuni Saṅgha of **Gautama the Supreme Arahant** (*Mahā Arahant*), undertook the holy life (*brahmacariya*), confronted the conditions of worldly existence, practiced Gautama's true Dhamma, and realized the final goal of liberation from the world. Together with the *Theragāthā*, it forms one of the most precious records of the early Arahanist Saṅgha: not merely a collection of Gautama's teachings about liberation, but the testimony of those who experienced the Path to Liberation themselves.

The *Therīgāthā* is traditionally organized into **sixteen chapters (nipātas)**, arranged primarily according to the number of verses attributed to the individual elders. Within these chapters are the voices of bhikkhunis whose lives could scarcely have been more varied. Some had known wealth and privilege; others poverty and hardship. Some had experienced marriage and motherhood; others had lived through bereavement, separation, aging, or profound personal loss. Some had entered the bhikkhuni life after lives of privilege, while others came seeking freedom after experiences of extraordinary difficulty.

Yet beneath these different circumstances runs a single direction:

**away from worldly bondage and toward liberation from the world.**

The power of the *Therīgāthā* lies partly in the immediacy of these voices. The bhikkhunis do not speak about Gautama's Dhamma as distant observers. They speak as women who had themselves encountered the forces that bind beings to worldly existence. Their verses frequently begin from intensely personal circumstances---grief, desire, attachment, family, the body, youth, beauty, aging, loss, fear, or the impermanence of everything once regarded as secure---and then turn those very experiences into occasions for seeing Gautama's Dhamma of liberation for renunciates.

This gives the *Therīgāthā* a distinctive character alongside the *Theragāthā*.

The Elder Bhikkhus frequently speak from the perspective of **renunciation and mastery**: leaving the household life, entering solitude, overcoming the mind\'s disturbances, conquering Māra, developing concentration, and completing the training. The Elder Bhikkhunis also speak of these things, but their verses often reveal with particular clarity **what had to be relinquished in order to become free from worldly bondages**.

For many of the bhikkhunis, the doorway into the Dhamma is strikingly personal.

A woman who has lost a child encounters the impermanence of life.\
A woman who once depended upon beauty confronts aging.\
A woman bound by sensual attachment discovers the cost of clinging.\
A woman who has experienced grief sees the unsatisfactory nature of worldly existence.\
A woman who has lived under the expectations of family and society discovers another possibility: the freedom of the holy life.

Their stories therefore bring the abstract language of Gautama's Dhamma of liberation into immediate human experience.

The *Therīgāthā* repeatedly demonstrates how **the very experiences that appear to define a person\'s worldly identity can become objects of insight**. Motherhood, marriage, beauty, youth, social status, suffering, grief, and the body are not transformed into permanent sources of meaning. Instead, they become occasions for seeing their worldly-conditioned, changing, and unreliable nature.

This is one of the most powerful features of these verses.

The bhikkhunis do not escape liberation by escaping their human stories. They pass through those stories with increasing clarity and learn to see beyond them.

Their verses can therefore be deeply intimate without being merely autobiographical. A personal story becomes a Dhamma teaching. A memory becomes an examination of worldly attachment. A loss becomes an insight into impermanence of the worldly. An encounter with desire becomes an opportunity for renunciation for sensual cravings. A reflection on the body becomes a means of seeing beyond identification with the body.

Again and again, the personal becomes universal.

This is also why the *Therīgāthā* should not be reduced to a collection of \"women\'s poetry\" in the modern literary sense. Its central concern is not the celebration of female identity. Its central concern is **freedom from bondage to the world**.

The women whose voices we hear here did not enter the bhikkhuni Saṅgha merely to acquire a new social identity. They entered the holy life to undertake the training that leads beyond the world. Their verses repeatedly return to the decisive movement from household life toward renunciation, from worldly attachment toward release from the worldly bondage, from confusion toward clarity, and from the worldly-conditioned toward the unconditioned-by-the-world.

In this respect, the *Therīgāthā* stands beside the *Theragāthā* as an equal testimony to the possibility of liberation from the world.

Yet the two collections should not be read as identical.

The *Theragāthā* often gives us the voice of the forest-dwelling elder, the renunciant celebrating solitude, discipline, meditation, and the conquest of the inner enemy. The *Therīgāthā* often allows us to hear more directly the voice of someone whose renunciation has emerged through the intimate circumstances of family, relationships, motherhood, beauty, aging, grief, and loss.

The difference is not a difference in the **goal**. It is a difference in the **human doorway through which the Path is described**. Both collections lead beyond the world. Both testify to the same possibility of liberation from the universe containing billions of worlds. Both speak of the destruction of sensual craving and the completion of the holy life. Both ultimately point toward the **Deathless Realm of Nibbāna**.

But the *Therīgāthā* makes especially visible something that can easily be overlooked in discussions of early Buddhism: the Path to liberation was not confined to one type of human experience. Gautama's Dhamma of Liberation was encountered within forests and villages, monasteries and households, solitude and family life, privilege and hardship, youth and old age, joy and devastating loss.

The Elder Bhikkhunis brought all of these experiences to the Path---and then learned to go beyond them.

Their verses therefore deserve to be read progressively: first as the words of individual women with particular histories; then as testimony to the transformation of those histories through Gautama's Dhamma; and finally, as declarations of a freedom that transcends the universe where each journey began. This volume follows that movement.

We will explore the historical setting of the early bhikkhuni Saṅgha, the distinctive literary character of the *Therīgāthā*, and Gautama's Dhamma for Liberation from the world embedded within its verses. We will encounter the women behind the verses, their struggles and turning points, their encounters with worldly attachment and loss, and the wisdom that transformed their lives.

But above all, we will listen to what they themselves say.

Their words remind us that liberation does not begin with having a perfect life. It begins when one sees clearly the nature of the life one has been living---and becomes willing to leave worldly-bondage behind.

The women whose voices survive in the *Therīgāthā* did exactly that. They left behind what had bound them. They entered the Path to Liberation from the world. They practiced. They saw. They were liberated from the world.

And now, across more than two millennia, their voices remain.

**These too are the voices of liberation.**

### 1. Ekanipāto --- Chapter of the Ones 

#### 1.1. Aññatarā: An Unknown Therī

**Sukhaṁ supāhi therike, Katvā coḷena pārutā;**

**Upasanto hi te rāgo, Sukkhaḍākaṁ va kumbhiyan ti.**

*Sleep with joy / bliss (sukha), O Therī (sukhaṁ supāhi therike), having made yourself robed (katvā coḷena pārutā); indeed, your worldly craving and attachment (raga) is pacified (upasanto hi te rāgo), like dried vegetables in a pot (sukkhaḍākaṁ va kumbhiyaṁ).*

**Commentary: Keys to Liberation**

**1). *Joy and Bliss Even in Sleep as a Marker of Transcendence***

**The State of Unshakeable Bliss:** Experiencing true joy and bliss (*sukha*) even in sleep (*supāhi*) reflects a mind (*citta*) entirely liberated from the anxious turbulence of the worldly matrix (*loka*). Ordinary beings remain bound by restless craving (*taṇhā*) and fear, tossing about in mental agitation.

**2). *The Complete Pacification of Rāga***

**The Extinction of worldly craving and attachment:** The absolute pacification of *rāga* (*upasanto hi te rāgo*) removes the foundational ties that drives involuntary reincarnation (*jātisaṁsāra*).

**The Simile of Dried Vegetables:** Comparing this extinguished state to dried greens in a cooking pot (*sukkhaḍākaṁ va kumbhiyaṁ*), as dried herbs are rendered incapable of sprouting or burning, a mind purified of **worldly craving and attachment** remains forever inert to worldly entanglements.

**3). *The Dual Keys to Complete Liberation from the World***

These two elements---sustaining unshakeable inner bliss (*sukha*) detached from sensory inputs, and achieving the absolute pacification of *rāga*---serve as the indispensable keys for anyone seeking complete liberation from the world and entering the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

#### 1.2. Muttā

**Mutte muccassu yogehi, cando rāhuggahā iva;**

**Vippamuttena cittena, anaṇā bhuñja piṇḍakanti.**

*Muttā, become free (muccassu) from the bonds / yokes / fetters \[sensory ties and attachments to the world\] (yogehi), like the moon from the grip of Rāhu \[the mythological demon who seizes the moon\] (cando rāhuggahā iva); with a completely liberated (vippamuttena) mind-heart (cittena), debtless / free from karmic debt, enjoy almsfood (anaṇā bhuñja piṇḍakaṁ).*

#### 1.3. Puṇṇā

**Puṇṇe pūrassu dhammehi, Cando pannaraseriva;**

**Paripuṇṇāya paññāya, Tamokhandhaṁ padālayā ti.**

*Puṇṇa, Fill yourself (pūrassu) with dhammas (dhammehi) in fullness (puṇṇe), like the moon on the fifteenth day (cando pannaraseriva); with completely fulfilled paññā (paripuṇṇāya paññāya), shatter (padālayā) the aggregates of ignorance (tamokhandhaṁ).*

#### 1.4. Tissā

**Tisse sikkhassu sikkhāya, mā taṁ yogā upaccaguṁ;**

**Sabba-yoga-visaṁyuttā, cara loke anāsavā ti.**

*Tissā, train yourself (sikkhassu) in the training (sikkhāya), let not (mā) the yokes, bonds, or fetters \[sensory attachments tying consciousness to the world\] (yogā) overcome (upaccaguṁ) you (taṁ); unyoked (visaṁyuttā) from all (sabba) yokes/ bonds/ fetters (yoga), live in the world (cara loke) without defilements (anāsavā).*

#### 1.5. Tissā (2nd)

**Tisse yuñjassu dhammehi,khaṇo taṁ mā upaccagā;**

**Khaṇātītā hi socanti,nirayamhi samappitā ti.**

*Tissā, yoke yourself (yuñjassu) with dhammas (dhammehi), let not the opportune moment slip away from you (khaṇo taṁ mā upaccagā); for those who have missed the opportune moment (khaṇātītā) indeed grieve (hi socanti), cast down (samappitā) into the nether world (nirayamhi).*

**Commentary**: ***Niraya*** in Vedic and Early Samana Cosmology

**1). The Vedic/Brahmanical 3-World Model (*Tīṇi Lokāni*)**

The term *niraya* (or *naraka*) finds its deepest historical roots in the older Vedic framework, which divided existence primarily into three realms: **Bhūḥ** (the earthly sphere), **Bhuvaḥ** (the atmospheric/intermediate realm, acts as the span immediately above the earth and below the higher luminous spaces), and **Svaḥ** (the heavenly sphere). Within this tripartite division and associated ancient texts, sub-terrestrial realms of torment, *niraya (or naraka) is positioned **beneath** or strictly outside the accessible tiers of the earth. While Bhūḥ represents the physical plane of human and terrestrial existence, niraya constitutes the subterranean regions of darkness and torment (the deepest subterranean pits, separate from the surface domain of mortals),* functioned as destinations for moral transgression, distinct from the ascending paths of ancestors (*pitṛyāna*) and gods (*devayāna*).

**The Path of the Ancestors (*Pitṛyāna*):** Associated with the lunar path, the realm of the ancestors (*Pitṛloka*) is traditionally mapped to the intermediate or atmospheric sphere (*Bhuvaḥ* / the moon), where departed souls temporarily reside before returning or ascending further, based on accumulated merit.

**The Path of the Gods (*Devayāna*):** Associated with the solar path (Sun as portal for ascension), *devayāna* leads directly to the higher luminous heavens and ultimate transcendence (*Svaḥ* or the solar realms), completely bypassing the lower cycles of return.

**2). The Jain Tradition's Four Realms (*Catu-gati*)**

In Jain cosmology, the wheel of *saṁsāra* divides souls (*jīva*) trapped in bodily forms into four main states of existence (*gati*):

**Hellish Beings (*Nārakī, or*** *niraya in this verse***):** Inhabitants of the subterranean hells who endure extreme physical and mental suffering as a direct karmic consequence of intense violence and cruelty.

**Sub-human/Animal Realm (*Tiryanch*):** Encompassing animals, birds, insects, and even plants, characterized by subjugation, lack of higher moral reflection, and constant vulnerability.

**Human/ Mankind Realm (*Manuṣya*):** The realm of conscious moral choice, suffering, and the unique capacity to practice rigorous asceticism (*tapas* / primordial force) and attain liberation (*mokṣa*).

**Celestial Beings (*Deva*):** Denizens of the upper heavens who enjoy high status and refined sensory pleasure, yet remain bound to the cycle of rebirth because their karmic fuel will eventually exhaust.

3). **The Pāḷi Canon's Five Destinies (*Pañcagati*)**

In the Nikāya cosmology of the world (*loka*), *saṁsāra* is classified into five distinct destinations (*pañcagati*):

**Hell (*Niraya*):** The subterranean state of intense *dukkha* and torment resulting from unpacified demerit and unbridled *dosa*.

**Animal Womb (*Tiracchānayoni*):** The realm driven primarily by instinct, predation, *moha*, and subjection to exploitation within the physical world.

**Ghost Realm (*Pettivisaya* / *Petaloka*):** The state of perpetual hunger, thirst, and deprivation, bound by intense unfulfilled craving (*taṇhā*).

**Human Realm (*Manussa*):** The mixed sphere of pleasure and pain, serving as the critical threshold where moral agency and ascetic practice can be undertaken.

**Heavenly Realm (*Deva*):** The luminous realms of sensory or refined pleasure resulting from wholesome deeds (*kusala-dhammā*), yet still entirely conditioned and decay-bound.

4). While the early Pāḷi Nikāyas primarily utilized the fivefold classification (*pañcagati*), later Buddhist commentarial traditions and Mahāyāna cosmological texts systematically expanded *saṁsāra matrix* into **six realms** (*cha-gati* or *ṣaḍ-gati*), b**y** changing the **Asuras** from an overlap with the *deva* into an independent tier of existence, which is of course untrue, full of prejudice and manipulative.

#### 1.6. Dhīrā

**Dhīre nirodhaṁ phusehi,Saññāvūpasamaṁ sukhaṁ;**

**Ārādhayāhi nibbānaṁ,Yogakkhemamanuttaran ti.**

*O Dhīrā, you experience the cessation (nirodhaṁ phusehi), the pacification / calming / complete subsiding of perception (saññā) (saññāvūpasamaṁ) is a bliss (sukhaṁ); attain (ārādhayāhi) nibbāna, the unsurpassed (anuttaraṁ) union with the security / safety* *\[khema =Nibbāna\] (yoga-khemaṁ).*

**Commentary**: Cessation of Perception (*Saññā*) in Advanced Samadhi

Within advanced mental discipline, the complete calming and subsiding of perception (*saññāvūpasamaṁ*) represents the critical threshold where ordinary sensory labeling halts. Because perception (*saññā*) binds the *citta* to the worldly matrix (*loka*), its cessation allows the mind to experience true, unconditioned bliss (*sukhaṁ*).

**1). Pāḷi Canon Texts (*Saññāvedayitanirodha*)**

**The Pinnacle of Samadhi:** In the early Pāḷi suttas (such as the *Mahāvedalla Sutta* and *Saṅghāramātā Sutta*), the ultimate attainment in *samadhi* is **Saññāvedayitanirodha** (the cessation of perception and feeling).

This advanced state is described as the point where mental activities completely subside. It serves as an immediate stepping stone for the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, enabling the purified spirit (*attā*) to execute its Sovereign Exit out of the universe entirely, to realize absolute freedom in Nibbāna-dhātu.

**MN 44 *Mahāvedalla Sutta*:** *"Yadā, āvuso, bhikkhuno saññā-vedayita-nirodhaṁ samāpannassa cittaṁ hoti, so 'saññāvedayitanirodhaṁ samāpajjissāmī'ti vā na saññāṇī hoti\... atha kho naṁ dhamma paṭivijjhanti."* When a bhikkhu has attained the cessation of perception and feeling, his mind tends toward stillness. Far beyond ordinary mental labeling (*saññā*), this advanced concentration (*samadhi*) halts all conditioned aggregates (*khandhā*).

**2). Vedic and Upanishadic Texts (*Turiya* and *Amūrtam*)**

In the ancient Upanishadic framework (such as the *Māṇḍukya Upanishad*), the state beyond waking, dreaming, and deep sleep is **Turiya** (the fourth) or an unmanifested state where all objective perception (*saññā*) ceases.

While older Vedic texts mapped sacrificial ascendancy through the tripartite *loka*, the advanced contemplative tradition recognized that true immortality (*amṛta*) requires the total cessation of worldly sensory fluctuations, and the total withdrawal, pacification, and structural cessation of *saṁjñā* (cognitive perception and mental labeling) as the prerequisite for crossing beyond conditioned existence.

The *Maitrī Upanishad* addresses the active suppression and final cessation of mental activity and sensory labeling (*saṁjñā*): *\"Dhyāna-nirdhūta-tasyaiva karmaṇaḥ śubha-pāpataḥ \| Ātmā-saṁstho 'malaḥ śāntaḥ param-Ātmanam aśnute \|\| indriyāṇāṁ nirodhena mana-saṁsāra-kṣayāt \| bhūtānāṁ ca parijñānāñ-liṅga-bhaṅgāc ca jāyate.\"*

Through *dhyāna*, having shaken off (*nirdhūta*) *karma*---both good and evil (*śubha-pāpataḥ*)---established in the *Ātmā* (*Ātmā-saṁsthaḥ*), stainless (*amalaḥ*), and peaceful (*śāntaḥ*), he attains (*aśnute*) the Supreme Self (*param-Ātmanam*). Through the cessation (*nirodha*) of the senses (*indriyāṇāṁ*), through the destruction of the mind\'s *saṁsāra* (*mana-saṁsāra-kṣayāt*), and through the complete comprehension / knowledge (*parijñānāt*) of beings (*bhūtānāṁ*) and the breaking (*bhaṅga*) of subtle signs (*liṅga*), it is accomplished (*jāyate*). --- *Maitrī Upanishad (6.24)*

**3). Jain Texts (*Śukla-dhyāna* and *Ayoga-kevali* State)**

In Jain epistemology, advanced ascetic practice culminates in **Śukla-dhyāna** (pure, bright meditation), which systematically stills all mental activities, fluctuations, and perceptual activities (*saññā*). Just that neither the Vedic corpus nor the Jain canonical texts contain a direct technical equivalent to the Pāḷi *saññāvedayitanirodha* (the intentional meditative cessation of perception).

By completely halting all internal cognitive activity and purging karmic influxes through intense ascetic discipline, the soul (*jīva*) sheds all worldly ties, reaching the final motionless state (*ayogī*) that breaks free from the fourfold cycle of *saṁsāra* to attain permanent liberation (*mokṣa*).

***Tattvārtha Sūtra* (Chapter 9, Sūtras 42--44) --- *The Progression to Absolute Cessation*:** *"Sūkṣma-kriyānivṛttiḥ\... vyuparata-kriyō 'nāvṛttis tu."*

**Sūkṣma-kriyānivṛttiḥ** (*comp. nom. sg.*): The cessation (*nivṛtti*) of subtle physical and mental activities (*sūkṣma-kriyā*).

**vyuparata-kriyaḥ** (*comp. nom. sg.*): One whose activities have completely ceased or subsided (*vyuparata* + *kriyā*).

**anāvṛttiḥ tu** (*nom. sg. + particle*): And non-return (to worldly existence).

Through the cessation of subtle activities (*sūkṣma-kriyānivṛttiḥ*), the ascetic enters the state where all mental and cognitive fluctuations have completely subsided (*vyuparata-kriyaḥ*), leading to absolute, non-returning liberation (*anāvṛttiḥ tu*).

***Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (Chapter 29: The True Path of Concentration) --- *Silencing the Mind and Perception:*** *"Jāeṁti khayaṁ dosā, appaṇo jhāṇamuttamaṁ \|* *jaṁ ṇaṁ jhāyati ṇiccāto, ṇibbuvī pāvae ṭhāṇaṁ \|\|."*

*"*All *dosā* go to destruction (*khayaṁ*) through the supreme *jhāṇa* (*jhāṇam-uttamaṁ*) of the self (*appaṇo*). Constantly contemplating this, the ascetic becomes entirely unbound (*ṇibbuvī*), attaining the ultimate, unconditioned station (*ṭhāṇaṁ*)."

#### 1.7. Vīrā

**Vīrā vīrehi dhammehi,bhikkhunī bhāvita-indriyā;**

**Dhāreti antimaṁ dehaṁ,jetvā māraṁ savāhinin ti.**

*Vīrā, by heroic (vīrehi) dhammas (dhammehi), the bhikkhunī, whose sensory faculties (indriya) are completely cultivated and restrained (bhāvita) \[sensory inputs no longer food to fuel worldly cravings (rāga)\], bears (dhāreti) her final body (antimaṁ dehaṁ), having conquered (jetvā) Māra with his army (māraṁ savāhinin).*

Commentary: ***Vīra*, *Bhāvita*, and the Final Body**

**1). *Vīra Dhammas* --- The Heroic Dhammas:** The root ***vīra*** (hero, warrior, valiant one) shares an Indo-European pedigree signifying strength, vital energy, and martial mastery (*vīro* in Latin, *vīra* in Vedic/Sanskrit). In the Pāḷi Canon, the Buddha and advanced ascetics are frequently styled as *vīra* (conquerors) because spiritual liberation is framed not as passive quietude, but as an ascetic warfare against *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. While *vīra* appears often as an epithet for the Buddha or Arahants (e.g., *Itivuttaka 112*, *Theragāthā*), the specific formulation coupling *vīra* with *dhamma* (*vīrehi dhammehi*---by heroic, warrior-like qualities) highlights the uncompromising, ascetic vigor required to dismantle worldly attachments.

**2). *Bhāvita-indriyā* --- Cultivated and Restrained Faculties:** Derived from the causative verb *bhāveti* (from the root *bhū*, to become or to bring into being), ***bhāvita*** literally means \"caused to become,\" developed, cultivated, or matured. In ancient Indian philology, it signifies profound mental and sensory transformation---fundamentally restructuring the psychological apparatus. **The Critical Role in Liberation:** *Bhāvita-indriyā* (one whose sensory faculties are fully cultivated) represents the essential mechanism where sensory inputs (*indriya*) no longer project outward to fuel worldly cravings (*rāga*). By mastering the senses, the ascetic starves the worldly identity of egoic food.

**3). *Jetvā Māraṁ Savāhinin* --- Conquering Māra and His Army:** *Māra* personifies the entirety of the conditioned world, death, and sensory temptation. Conquering Māra along with his army (*savāhiniṁ*) signals the total eradication of all mental defilements and karmic debts.

**4). *Dhāreti Antimaṁ Dehaṁ* --- Bearing the Final Body:** The Arahant now bears (*dhāreti*) her final physical body (*antimaṁ dehaṁ*). Because all karmic fuel for involuntary reincarnation (*jātisaṁsāra*) has been permanently cut, no subsequent form will be generated for *samsāra*. **The Sovereign Exit:** Upon the natural dissolution of this final physical body, the purified spirit (*attā*) executes its final Sovereign Exit out of the universe entirely, passing through the Pure Abodes realm, into the deathless realm of Nibbāna (*Nibbāna-dhātu*).

#### 1.8. Mittā

**Saddhāya pabbajitvāna, mitte mittaratā bhava;**

**Bhāvehi kusale dhamme, yoga-khemassa pattiyā ti.**

*Out of faith (saddhāya), having renounced household life (pabbajitvāna), O Mittā, be (bhava) one who delights in noble friends (mittaratā); cultivate (bhāvehi) wholesome dhammas (kusale dhamme) for the attainment (pattiyā) of union with the security/ safety \[khema=Nibbāna\] (yoga-khemassa).*

#### 1.9. Bhadrā

**Saddhāya pabbajitvāna, bhadre bhadraratā bhava;**

**Bhāvehi kusale dhamme, yoga-khemam-anuttaran ti.**

*Out of faith (saddhāya), having renounced household life (pabbajitvāna), O Bhadrā, be one who delights in what is good, noble, and auspicious (bhadraratā); cultivate (bhāvehi) wholesome dhammas (kusale dhamme) for the union with the unsurpassed security/ safety \[khema=Nibbāna\] (yoga-khemam-anuttaran).*

#### 1.10. Upasamā

**Upasame tare oghaṁ, maccudheyyaṁ suduttaraṁ;**

**Dhārehi antimaṁ dehaṁ, jetvā māraṁ savāhinin ti.**

*O Upasamā, cross over (tare) the flood (oghaṁ), the realm of Death (maccudheyyaṁ), very difficult to cross (suduttaraṁ); bear (dhārehi) your final body (antimaṁ dehaṁ), having conquered (jetvā) Māra with his army (māraṁ savāhinin).*

#### 1.11. Muttā (2nd)

**Sumuttā sādhumuttāmhi, tīhi khujjehi muttiyā; Udukkhalena musalena, patinā khujjakena ca; Muttāmhi jātimaraṇā, bhavanetti samūhatā ti.**

*Completely liberated (sumuttā), truly released am I (sādhumuttāmhi), through release (muttiyā) from three burdens (tīhi khujjehi): by the mortar (udukkalena), by the pestle (musalena), and by the husband who is deformed/crippled (patinā khujjakena ca); I am released (muttāmhi) from birth and death (jātimaraṇā), and the conduits, the lead-in to future becoming / worldly existence (bhavanetti) are completely uprooted (samūhatā).*

#### 1.12. Dhammadinnā

**Chandajātā avasāyī, Manasā ca phuṭā siyā;**

**Kāmesu appaṭibaddhacittā, Uddhaṁsotāti vuccatī ti.**

*Born with will (chanda-jātā), resolute (avasāyī), and must be (siyā) imbued / permeated (phuṭā) with mind-will (manasā ca); whose mind-heart cittā is unattached (appaṭibaddha cittā) to sexual/ sensual desires (kāmesu), she is called (vuccati) \"one whose stream flows upward\" (uddhaṁsotā).*

#### 1.13. Visākhā

**Karotha buddha-sāsanaṁ, yaṁ katvā nānutappati;**

**Khippaṁ pādāni dhovitvā, ekamante nisīdathā ti.**

*Practice (karotha) the Buddha\'s instruction (buddha-sāsanaṁ), having done that (yaṁ katvā), one does not regret (nānutappati); quickly (khippaṁ) having washed your feet (pādāni dhovitvā), sit down in a secluded spot (ekamante nisīdatha).*

#### 1.14. Sumanā

**Dhātuyo dukkhato disvā, mā jātiṁ punarāgami;**

**Bhave chandaṁ virājetvā, upasantā carissasī ti.**

*Having seen (disvā) the elements (dhātuyo) as dukkha (dukkhato), do not come back again (mā punarāgami) to birth (jātiṁ); having faded away desire (chandaṁ virājetvā) in becoming / worldly existence (bhave), you will live (carissasi) in the supreme peace \[Nibbāna\] (upasantā).*

#### 1.15. Uttarā

**Kāyena saṁvutā āsiṁ, vācāya uda cetasā;**

**Samūlaṁ taṇha-mabbuyha, sītibhūtāmhi nibbutā ti.**

I was (*āsiṁ*) rstrained (*saṁvutā*) by body (*kāyena*), by speech (*vācāya*), and (*uda*) by mind (*cetasā*); having pulled out worldly craving completely with its root (*samūlaṁ taṇha-mabbuyha*), attained coolness/ Nibbāna (*sītibhūtāmhi*), unbound/ attained Nibbāna (*nibbutā*).

#### 1.16. Vuḍḍhikā

**Sukhaṁ tvaṁ vuḍḍhike sehi, katvā coḷena pārutā;**

**Upasanto hi te rāgo, sītibhūtāsi nibbutā ti.**

*Lie down happily (sukhaṁ tvaṁ sehi), O Vuḍḍhikā, having made yourself robed (katvā coḷena pārutā); your rāga is completely pacified (upasanto hi te rāgo), you attained coolness/ Nibbāna (sītibhūtāmhi), unbound/ attained Nibbāna (nibbutā).*

#### 1.17. Dhammā

**Piṇḍapātaṁ caritvāna,daṇḍamolubbha dubbalā;**

**Vedhamānehi gattehi,tattheva nipatiṁ chamā;**

**Disvā ādīnavaṁ kāye,atha cittaṁ vimucci me ti.**

*Having gone about for alms (piṇḍapātaṁ caritvāna), feeble (dubbalā) and leaning on a staff (daṇḍamolubbha); with trembling limbs (vedhamānehi gattehi), right there I fell down to the ground (tattheva nipatiṁ chamā); having seen the danger (disvā ādīnavaṁ) in the body (kāye), then my citta was liberated (atha cittaṁ vimucci me).*

#### 1.18. Saṅghā

**Hitvā ghare pabbajitvā,hitvā puttaṁ pasuṁ piyaṁ;**

**Hitvā rāgañca dosañca,avijjañca virājiya;**

**Samūlaṁ taṇhamabbuyha,upasantāmhi nibbutā ti.**

*Having given up household life and renounced (hitvā ghare pabbajitvā), having given up beloved son/ children and livestock (hitvā puttaṁ pasuṁ piyaṁ); having completely faded away rāga, dosa, and also avijja-ignorance (hitvā rāgañca dosañca, avijjañca virājiya); having uprooted craving completely with its root (samūlaṁ taṇha-mabbuyha), I am n the supreme peace \[Nibbāna\] (upasantāmhi) and unbound/ attained Nibbāna (nibbutā).*

## About the Author

**Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera** is a Canadian Theravāda bhikkhu, meditation teacher, religious scholar, author, and founding Ācariya of **Arahanism---the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**. His work is dedicated to preserving, examining, and transmitting Gautama\'s original liberation teaching, with particular emphasis on restoring renunciation and Arahantship to the centre of religious life. Rooted in the Theravāda tradition, Arahanism upholds the Sutta and Vinaya foundations of the Pāḷi Canon while critically examining subsequent doctrinal developments and interpretations in light of what it understands as Gautama\'s original teaching.

Ordained as a bhikkhu in 2013 at Pa-Auk Forest Monastery and Meditation Center in Myanmar, Bhante Mudita undertook intensive training in *samatha--vipassanā* meditation, Pāḷi Canon study, and monastic discipline. He subsequently spent five years practising within the Thai forest tradition, deepening his training through solitude, simplicity, renunciation, and sustained contemplative practice. Returning to Canada in November 2024, he dedicated himself to the systematic teaching and development of Arahanism.

His scholarship encompasses early Buddhist literature, canonical studies, meditation, renunciant spirituality, and comparative religious research. Through translations, extensive commentaries, and doctrinal studies, he examines the teachings and spiritual experiences preserved in ancient religious texts. His particular interest in the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* arises from their importance as first-person testimonies of early Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs who undertook the renunciant life and pursued liberation. Rather than approaching these verses merely as historical or literary compositions, he seeks to understand their practical significance: how these elders trained, overcame worldly attachments, cultivated the mind, and described their progress towards liberation from worldly bondage.

His broader publications include studies of Gautama\'s Liberation Path, the *Dhammapada*, the *Suttanipāta*, meditation and Samādhi, the *Bhagavad-Gītā*, and early Christianity. Across these diverse subjects, his research is guided by an interest in renunciation, spiritual discipline, and the ultimate purpose of religious practice.

At the heart of his teaching is the progressive cultivation of **Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā**, leading towards **Nibbidā, Virāga, and Vimutti**. Through ethical discipline, restraint of the senses, concentration, and liberating wisdom, the practitioner works towards the complete eradication of *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (worldly survival instincts of fear, anger, aversion, hatred and hostility), and *moha* (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the path to liberation from worldly bondage). Within Arahanism, this training culminates in the liberation of *citta* from worldly bondage and the realization of *Nibbāna-dhātu*, the Deathless realm.

As Founder and Spiritual Head of the **Arahanist Order of Canada**, Bhante Mudita seeks to establish a living Canadian Theravāda lineage dedicated to canonical study, monastic discipline, meditation, progressive renunciation, and the training of future generations of renunciants. His work also encompasses religious education, scholarly publication, and the open digital preservation of Arahanism literature through international research and archival platforms.

His life\'s work is dedicated to preserving and transmitting **the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**, and to encouraging sincere practitioners to study the testimonies of the ancient elders, undertake the discipline of renunciation, and pursue the ultimate goal of the path: liberation from worldly bondage and the realization of the Deathless.
